                                   vi
Preface

This book originated in my studies of German intellectual history,
in particular of the ‘panentheist’ philosopher, Karl Christian Friedrich
Krause, and the ways he shaped radical free-thinking in nineteenth-
century Spain. Yet the more I began empirical research into Spanish
radicalism, the more I recognised the need for a ‘bottom-up’ study of
the masses and their relationship to radicalism rather than a top-down
study of the elites. Very quickly I realised that ideas exist only in the
‘context of their context’, and that no satisfactory study of ideas could
be sensibly sustained without any empirical study of their reception and
impact. I also discovered that my early interest in Spain’s 1850s and
1860s was being pulled back to the events of Spain’s early nineteenth
century. Time and again I had to explain such events as the failure of
the revolution in 1848 and its success in 1854, the Loja rising, and the
evolution of the Spanish Democrat Party with reference to the chaos
and revolution wrought by the Peninsular War of 1808–14 and hard-
ened by the bloody struggles between Left and Right during 1820–23
and, especially, the First Carlist War of 1833–40.
  I noticed that the First Carlist War was a comparative blind-spot in
the English-language scholarship. Thanks to the feedback I received at
various international conferences, the untiring inspiration and encour-
agement from Professor Charles Esdaile, and the particular interest of
the ‘War, Culture and Society’ editorial board at Palgrave Macmillan,
I was able to produce this monograph.
  I should offer a note of explanation with regard to my use of lan-
guages. A glance at twenty-ﬁrst-century maps of the Basque country,
Catalonia and Galicia will reveal the respective use of Basque, Catalan
and Gallego names for places which, in the nineteenth century, were
generally written in Castilian. Largely for the beneﬁt of my Anglophone
audience, I have used Castilian place names instead of their regional
equivalents (which I use more sparingly). Hence my readers will not
be arrested by the sight of ‘Bilbo’ instead of ‘Bilbao’. Equally, I have
privileged the use of Castilian over Anglicised Spanish place names.
‘Corunna’ and ‘Saragossa’, for example, must look quaint to twenty-ﬁrst
readers who will have at least some familiarity with Spanish geography,
and I think I am not making too many cognitive demands by using
La Coruña and Zaragoza, respectively.

                                   viii
1
Introduction, History and Sources




Tragedy invites study, and wars, especially civil wars, are tragic by their
very nature. National, imperial and civil wars dominated Spain dur-
ing the revolutionary and counter-revolutionary turbulence of 1808–48,
pitting Patriots against Frenchmen, americanos against peninsulares, and
Liberals against Carlists. These interminable conﬂicts have shaped works
of ﬁction. Benito Pérez Galdós’s Episodios Nacionales serialised calamities
from the Battle of Trafalgar to the Carlist War and beyond, weaving ﬁc-
tional characters into real historical events in a didactic construction
of national truth. Political elites contested the legitimate nature of the
state but generally had no interest in social change. Instead, they relied
upon a surfeit of army veterans who ‘pronounced’ against governments
in return for pay and promotion, precious commodities in a bankrupt
economy. The predominantly rural masses were integrated into political
programmes as a last resort. Yet the inability of elites to rescue Spain
from its post-imperial crisis condemned their people to two generations
of upheaval and war, to the extent that upheaval has been seen as the
normal, if problematic, functioning of politics. The longest nineteenth-
century Spanish conﬂict, the First Carlist War (1833–40), began with a
dynastic dispute. But it developed into an unlikely agent of modernity
on account of the unequal demands it imposed on the people who, in
response, and for the ﬁrst time in modern Spanish history, found their
own voice, agendas and solutions.
   It is surprising that Spain’s First Carlist War remains Europe’s most
neglected nineteenth-century civil war in the English scholarship. This
lacuna is unworthy of the conﬂict’s importance. It was both the
last great European conﬂict of the pre-industrial age and, before the
nineteenth-century military revolution, an iconic moment in the pan-
European struggle between liberalism and absolutism, and a key stage in

                                    1
2   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


the history of modern Spain. Yet, unlike the Peninsular War (1808–14),
the First Carlist War is something of a Cinderella for monographs and
transnational histories alike.1 The conﬂict’s Spanish historiography is far
better, thanks to such scholars as Jordi Canal, Josep Clemente, Alfonso
Bullón de Mendoza, Ramón Urquijo Goitia, Pedro Rújula and the late
Julio Aróstegui, to say nothing of the wealth of local and regional stud-
ies. But it is surprising that even the Spanish historiography was late in
applying modern historical methods to the conﬂict; rather, until the
1970s, the war, like the broader issues of Carlism and Liberalism in
general, was the battleground of ideologically committed panegyrists
and detractors. Antonio Pirala’s exhaustive six-volume study, written
between the 1850s and 1870s, remained authoritative for more than
a century as a consequence. But the Spanish innovation has not yet led
to a monograph of the conﬂict in English. In principle, then, there is a
need for a new study in English which will examine the whole of the
war and integrate the military narrative into wider questions concern-
ing politics and society. This monograph shows how the First Carlist
War created a radical movement within the ranks of Spanish liberalism
which thereafter never ceased to exert pressure on its more moderate
(and distinctly elitist) movement.
   Spain’s Carlist Wars make interesting and exciting reading, especially
the ﬁrst and most prolonged of these civil wars, the First Carlist War
of 1833–40. The background to this conﬂict is extremely complex, but,
in brief, as a result of the Peninsular War (1808–14) Spain had been
plunged into a far-reaching political, social and economic crisis. The
only way forward from this situation was a comprehensive programme
of reform that extended and deepened the changes already made in
the reigns of Charles III (1759–88) and Charles IV (1788–08). Initiated
by the progressive group of liberales that had dominated the Cortes of
Cádiz (1810–14), and picked up in more timid fashion by the counsellors
of Ferdinand VII after 1814, this programme of reform was challenged
by elements in the Church and the nobility who had hoped rather
to ‘colonise’ Ferdinand VII and to make him not only turn back the
clock in respect of the changes made in the period of 1808–14, but
reverse those made in the period of enlightened absolutism as well. For
a long time it seemed that they would ultimately reverse the tide of
reform – the heir to the throne, Ferdinand’s younger brother, Prince
Charles (‘Don Carlos’: hence the word ‘Carlist’) was very much under
their thumb – but the last months of Ferdinand’s life saw a compli-
cated series of events threaten their dominance, and no sooner had he
died than they staged an armed revolt aimed at ensuring their triumph
                                          Introduction, History and Sources   3


by force. This civil war has attracted nothing like the critical attention
lavished upon its iconic successor a century later. In some ways this sit-
uation is surprising, as, for a start, the enduring stereotype of the ‘Two
Spains’ – which for so long was deemed central to understanding mod-
ern Spain – might have seemed an equally compelling paradigm for the
First Carlist War: whereas the Spanish Civil War saw Nationalists and
Republicans ﬁghting for their respectively hegemonic visions of Spain,
the First Carlist War offers a comparable struggle between legitimist
Carlists and modernising Liberals which took twice as long to resolve
as its successor, exacted relatively more casualties and even anticipated
the International Brigades (some 18,000 French, British, Belgian and
Portuguese volunteers for liberty). One major difference lay in the rever-
sal of the term ‘national’. Whereas in the 1830s it was the progressive
Liberal side which was ‘national’, in the 1930s the Francoist coalition,
heirs of the vanquished Carlists, claimed this title. Spanish nationalism
had evolved from Left to Right, from a civic force of nation-building
into a conservative force of National Catholicism.2
   It was a dynastic dispute about who should succeed to the throne in
1833 that provided the trigger for war, but the latter’s roots in reality
stretched far wider. The ﬁrst root was contested legitimacy, which was
no arcane matter but an issue that shaped all sorts of popular militancy.
Spaniards had traditionally held their monarchy in great reverence:
unlike the French and English, they have never killed their king. The
illegitimacy of Napoleon’s seizure of their throne in 1808 in the name
of his brother, Joseph, had been the formal casus belli for the Peninsular
War. But to the question of legitimacy needs to be added the question
of absolutist royal will. In 1789 Ferdinand’s father, Charles IV, had used
a secret session of the Spanish Cortes to issue a decree opening up the
royal succession to female as well as male heirs. This broke with the
Salic Law which the Bourbon victor of the 1701–13 War of the Spanish
Succession, Philip V, had conferred on his new dynasty and which
effectively excluded female heirs from the succession by exhausting
all legitimate male bloodlines to the throne. Any doubts surrounding
the validity of the Salic Law had long remained moot: Ferdinand’s ﬁrst
three marriages bore him either no children or resulted in miscarriages
and infant mortalities, which could only mean that the King’s younger
brother, Don Carlos, would succeed him. But the healthy birth of the
royal daughter, Isabella, born in October 1830, revived the question of
the succession. Don Carlos did not challenge his brother openly, and
much of the running was made by Ferdinand’s wife, María Francisca,
who was at odds with his sister-in-law, Luisa Carlota. Bourbon women
4   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


had always dominated when their menfolk were weak. But now this
tradition was given new form, as the dynastic triumph of the women,
María Cristina and her young daughter Isabella, was countered by the
Carlist faction in sexist propaganda.3 As a leading Carlist propagandist
pronounced, ‘Phillip V knew how nature demands that a man should
command and a woman obey, and that there is barely one out of a
million of the fairer sex ﬁt for something greater than homespinning.’4
Despite this sexism, the war would place women into roles ‘naturally’
reserved for men. Pre-war court politics foreshadowed this. From 1830,
after the birth of Isabella and then a second daughter, it became possi-
ble for the Cristino faction to win the succession. Under pressure from
María Cristina, whom events would throw into the arms of the Lib-
eral reformists, Ferdinand timidly reissued the 1789 decree, now termed
the Pragmatic Sanction, thereby announcing Isabella’s succession to the
throne in the event of his death. Pro-Carlist court pressure succeeded
in prevailing upon Ferdinand brieﬂy to revoke the Sanction.5 But the
Carlist recovery was by no means assured. In 1832 the King lapsed
into what was thought to be terminally poor health, and his powers
were passed to the regency of his wife who presided over a purge of
Carlists from the army and administration. During a brief Indian sum-
mer in the King’s health, three months before his death, the Cristino
faction again secured the King’s approval for the re-abolition of the
Salic Law. Thus, Carlist hopes of a smooth transition were dashed: the
royal will was that the infant Isabella’s minority reign would be exer-
cised by her mother’s regency when the King’s death ﬁnally came in
September 1833.
   Don Carlos, devout and tenacious, refused to recognise the Isabeline
succession: the recent legitimist rising known as the Agraviados had
taught him the wisdom of awaiting events. But he also refused openly
to challenge the King whilst the monarch still lived. This legitimist pro-
priety did not have to matter as such. Early nineteenth century Spain
had been littered with examples of pronunciamientos, or military coups,
being mounted in order to change politics and, in the case of young
Prince Ferdinand and his elderly father, Charles IV, in 1808, even to
force a royal abdication. And Don Carlos suffered no lack of proxies
prepared to do his bidding, including several in some of the highest
positions of authority in the Bourbon state. The purge of these Carlists
during the last months of Ferdinand’s life was explained by Liberal his-
torians with references to the ‘illegitimacy’ of the Carlist dynastic claim.
The dual issue of the secret decree of 1789 and the deﬁnitive expres-
sion of royal will of 1833 supported their case. According to the Liberal
historian, Antonio Pirala, the Cristino case may have been impolitic
                                         Introduction, History and Sources   5


but was by no means illegitimate. Not only had the Cortes effectively
legitimised a female succession as early as 1789, but the Salic Law in
any case was the worst example of ‘foreign despotism’ to contravene
centuries of Spanish tradition, and which had been instigated by the
Bourbons in 1713 solely in order to settle European fears of a formally
integrated Franco-Spanish monarchy.6 One of the ironies of the dynas-
tic dispute was that modernising Liberals invoked medieval Spanish
tradition whereas medievalist Carlists invoked the eighteenth-century
Bourbons, the very dynasty whose enlightened absolutism created the
conditions for nineteenth-century liberalism to emerge.
   Certainly, nineteenth-century Liberals used Spanish tradition in other
contexts for political ends. Most markedly, the 1820s radical-liberals
named their secret society the Comuneros in honour of the 1520s
Comuneros revolt of Castile, the founding myth for Spanish Liber-
als who viewed it as the ﬁrst modern defence of popular sovereignty.
Historicism came easily to nineteenth-century Spanish Liberals, many
of whom (Count Toreno, Agustín de Argüelles and Alcalá Galiano, to
name a few) were themselves historians. But despite their erudition,
their works are unhelpfully partisan and self-exculpatory with regard
to the early nineteenth-century crisis that unleashed the Carlist War.7
This is not only because an equally strong case could be – and was –
made by Carlist historians for Don Carlos’s claim,8 but also because
most nineteenth-century histories lacked the analytical and contextual
rigour demanded by modern scholarship. An adequate understanding
of the background to the First Carlist War needs to integrate archival
research with the advances made in Spanish social history since the
mid-twentieth century.


The First Carlist War: history and sources

Whereas most publications relating to the First Carlist War appeared
during the conﬂict itself and its immediate aftermath, the highest qual-
ity of publications has appeared since the 1970s.9 That said, the most
important single body of work remains Antonio Pirala y Criado’s six
volumes, written over two decades from the 1850s. Pirala was a posi-
tivist progresista (Spanish ‘Whig’) and sometime ﬁction writer who, in
his youth, ﬂirted with democracy in response to failure of the 1848
revolution in Spain. His politics moved to the Right in proportion
to his advancing age and career. But he remained a Liberal, thinking
the Carlists were wrong even though he was objective and unusu-
ally knowledgeable about them. His even-handedness was commended
by the Carlist side. He was bequeathed private Carlist correspondence
6   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


by the Carlist commander-in-chief who, in 1839 made peace, Rafael
Maroto.10 When Pirala, in the 1870s, began his ﬁve-volume Historia
Contemporánea, dealing with the Second Carlist War (1872–76), he was
granted privileged access to Carlist papers.11 Yet Pirala’s prolix narra-
tive means that he was often cited but seldom read. As a positivist
he expressed immutable moral norms and was constrained to give
meaning to the political and social radicalisation unleashed by the
war. In committing details of massacres, violence and riots to paper,
Pirala conforms to a peculiarly nineteenth-century humanitarian liter-
ary model designed to make readers feel part of national suffering.12
Pirala was not the ﬁrst to use this technique. As the historian of
Aragonese Carlism, Pedro Rújula, has pointed out, three Radical-Liberals
who had exercised public roles in Aragón during the war (Francisco
Cabello, Francisco Santa Cruz and Ramón María Temprado) were quick
to write a leftist history of the conﬂict which amassed details of heroism
and massacres in order to cast the Liberal militia as the martyred van-
guards of a civil society whose memory must be avenged not just against
any resurgent Carlists, but also against the conservatives (moderados)
who brutally disbanded the militia whilst these authors were writing
in 1843.13 But Pirala, less radical in his liberalism, provided the clas-
sic nineteenth-century expression of the humanitarian narrative. Yet
he was not without his shortcomings. In particular, he neglected the
Cristino ‘home front’, especially Andalucía where Carlism was admit-
tedly at its weakest, but where so much of the social and political
radicalism was nonetheless produced by the war.
   Pirala’s failure to integrate the Cristino experience of civil war into
the Liberal revolution has still not been fully addressed. At the turn of
the current century Jordi Canal lamented that there was still no reliable
synthesis of the First Carlist War.14 Certainly, no adequate attempt has
been made to undertake this in the English language, but it is interest-
ing to reﬂect upon how even the more sophisticated Spanish scholarship
presents challenges and controversies. The Spanish historiography may
be divided into ﬁve broad categories. The ﬁrst comprised nineteenth-
century dynastic and classical military histories, contemporary (and
generally inferior in quality) to Pirala’s volumes.15 The second comprised
the panegyrics from Franco-era traditionalists who depicted Carlism as
an organic Christian good resisting the onslaught of godless and artiﬁ-
cial Spanish liberalism.16 The First Carlist War was but one protracted
episode in the wider war between Christianity and the Anti-Spain that
was joined in 1808 and won by the crusaders only in 1939. The most
impressive work in this vein was the 30-volume history of traditionalism
                                           Introduction, History and Sources   7


edited by Melchor Ferrer from the 1940s, which was meant to be the
Carlist answer to the Liberal Pirala, but which in fact lacked the lat-
ter’s balance and command of primary sources.17 The third category
comprised the ‘Navarra School’ of Pamplona-based neo-traditionalists,
led by Federico Suárez Verdeguer. These scholars sustained a far more
sophisticated right-wing analysis based on modern empirical research.18
Their contention that Spain remained royalist, or apolitical, throughout
this period rendered Liberalism an artiﬁcial and arrogant innovation.
As the best neo-traditionalist scholar of the First Carlist War, Alfonso
Bullón de Mendoza, put it, the Cristino Liberals were waging war against
their own people.19
   Even though both the traditionalists and neo-traditionalists sympa-
thised with historical Carlism, they were alienated by the leftward shift
of ‘Carlism 1968’, which inspired a new generation of neo-Carlist schol-
arship. The neo-Carlists are the fourth category, and their revisionism
is impossible to understand without reference to the socialist ‘self-
management’ (autogestión) doctrine advocated from the late 1960s by
the Carlist ‘Prince’ Carlos Hugo de Borbón Parma and his followers. The
neo-Carlists resented how the Francoist ‘families’ of neo-traditionalists
and National Catholics had hijacked their movement since 1937 in
the name of the ‘Movement’, and the neo-Carlist movement accord-
ingly faced repression under the last years of the Franco dictatorship.20
Their most strident scholar was Josep Carles Clemente. Clemente argued
that early Carlism was an inchoate social protest against nineteenth-
century oligarchs who were using the Liberal property revolution to
marginalise villagers’ livelihoods and erode the communitarian model
enshrined in the Basque fueros (devolved economic and political pow-
ers under the Spanish crown)21 Carlism predominated in the Basque
country, he explained, because since the eighteenth century landhold-
ing there had become concentrated in fewer, more exploitative, hands,
causing rents to rise whilst agricultural prices remained depressed, and
to varying degrees this class conﬂict pertained elsewhere in Spain.22
Clemente thus turned Marxist social history against its ‘correct’ con-
stituency, representing Carlism as a ‘social dissidence’ springing from
a form of ‘feudal socialism’ under attack from the centralising and
capitalist drive of Spain’s modernising Liberals;23 a neo-Carlist interpre-
tation that was expanded upon by other scholars equally exasperated
by Spain’s oligarchical liberalism.24 But Clemente’s ire targeted not the
Marxists but the traditionalists who had hijacked the true historical
essence of Carlism, or as he complained in the 1977 publication Nosotros
los carlistas: ‘ﬁrst came the volunteers to raise their standard of King and
8   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Fueros, and later came the traditionalists with an agenda of their own,
adding God and Fatherland to our popular movement and turning it
into a political philosophy of traditionalism’.25
   Clemente’s neo-Carlism did not hold the ﬁeld for long, however:
in the early 1990s neo-traditionalism counter-attacked with Alfonso
Bullón de Mendoza’s doctoral thesis, quickly turned into a published
monograph. Bullón de Mendoza found Clemente’s neo-Carlism, along
with conventional Marxist analyses, to be ‘wilful history’ (historia voli-
tiva), left-wing propagandistic distortions foundering upon their lack of
‘objective facts’. Thus, there was not a single coherent Carlist manifesto
offering social justice, and episodes of villagers and combatants assert-
ing their ‘moral economy’ came as much from Liberal as Carlist quarters
(i.e. the side was irrelevant). Meanwhile, the Basque fueros on which
Clemente based part of his argument, were no pre-capitalist model of
social harmony but simply a different model of social exploitation.26
Bullón de Mendoza’s thesis provoked a furious debate with the neo-
Carlists, which was happily sustained more by evidence rather than
assertion. We may add to this by considering how Carlist War newspa-
per extracts qualify Clemente’s ‘social dissidence’ thesis. On one level,
Clemente’s thesis does indeed reﬂect the fears of 1830s Cristino elites
who saw Carlists as reactionary levellers bent on smashing the progress
and hierarchy of respectable society, or, as a moderado newspaper in 1837
put it: ‘Carlism could never be attractive to people who have something
to lose because it is in fact a kind of pure democracy, demagoguery, and
anarchy.’27 This would support the ‘social dissidence’ thesis, until we
ﬁnd that the same Cristino elites also despaired of the excesses made by
the ‘democratic rabble’ on their own side.28 The inference must be that
the First Carlist War should be studied not from the perspective of elite
discourse and ideology but from the perspective of contingency. This
book aims to take this perscpective, using a chronological organisation
to enable the simultaneous analysis of concurrent developments which
shaped the background and the course of the conﬂict. This approach, it
is hoped, will avoid the constrictive, jargon-prone methodologies of so
much of the ideological scholarship.
   A narrative study can make an original contribution for two reasons.
First, the 1990s ‘end of history’ made Clemente’s focus on class conﬂict
seem passé. Second, connected to this, a ﬁfth category of Spanish histo-
riography has emerged which, to a large degree, has liberated itself both
from the ideological hegemony of Marxism on the one hand and the
rebarbative legacy of Francoism on the other. Much of this feat may be
attributed to Jordi Canal’s now standard work, El carlismo (2004). This
                                            Introduction, History and Sources   9


has revised the Marxist and neo-Carlist emphasis on structural causation
by showing the ‘multifaceted’ nature of its militants in different regions,
social contexts and times, distinguishing between Carlists as pragmatists
and Carlism as a creed. It has also rejected the neo-traditionalist ﬁxa-
tion with the ‘Marxist distortion’ of history: in claiming to be apolitical
and interested only in ‘objective’ facts, the Navarra school has failed to
acknowledge its own right-wing political mission (i.e. another politicisa-
tion of history).29 The freer intellectual environment since the Cold War
inspired new research questions, such as what quotidian Carlism meant
in practice.30 This has been answered by outstanding regional research
by such ﬁfth-category scholars as Pedro Rújula and José Ramón Urquijo,
who have shown in the Maestrazgo and the Basque country respectively
that support for Carlism was far more conditional than traditionalists,
neo-traditionalists and even Liberals believed.31
   The sum of Spanish historiography allows two relevant observations
to be made for this volume. First, it lays bare just how few histories of the
First Carlist War beyond travel and campaign narratives are available in
English. Although serious studies have been written, they have discussed
the First Carlist War only as part of either a wider narrative or a particu-
lar interest.32 The best English-language study is by John Coverdale, but
even this covers only one-third of the conﬂict’s span and, more impor-
tantly for this book’s rationale, explains the Carlist side much more than
the Cristino.33
   Second, twentieth-century research into the First Carlist War has
been more interested in the Carlist losers than the Cristino victors.
Nineteenth-century writers often referred to this conﬂict as the ‘seven
years’ war’, arguably a better title as it removes Carlist ownership of
the conﬂict and instead obliges scholars to understand the war’s impact
on the Cristino side. Carlism was both Europe’s most enduring legit-
imism and, paradoxically, a creed of losers, even when it was part of
the winning side in 1939. As a corollary to this, Spain’s Liberal revo-
lution, which was deﬁnitively victorious as a consequence of the First
Carlist War, has been seen as the normal trajectory of Spanish history.
This attitude was internalised by the school of Marxist social history
pioneered in Spain in the 1950s by Miguel Artola-Gallego, which was
morally driven by the need to historicise the Left’s defeat in the recent
Civil War, in other words, to explain the defeat of the ‘normal’ trajec-
tory of history. To this end, these social historians footnoted the military
history of the Cristino side to what they saw as the far more impor-
tant social and political transformation of the hallowed ‘bourgeois’ or
‘liberal’ revolution which Marxism could explain in structural changes
10   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


of the 1830s without having to resort to the contingencies of military
history.34 They found Spain’s 1830s liberal revolution not to have been
arrogant, as the neo-traditionalists held, but timid. The capitalist prop-
erty revolution actually left the old nobility’s socio-economic hegemony
unmolested and frustrated the ‘correct’ emergence of new forces as dur-
ing the French Revolution.35 As such, it is hard to categorise the Artola
school as part of the First Carlist War historiography, as these social his-
torians, with some important exceptions,36 saw the war as a symptom
rather than a cause of social conﬂict. As a result, the Cristino response to
the First Carlist War has not been studied with anything like the degree
of innovation as the Carlism side. Because of this, perhaps, the con-
ﬂict’s collective memory remains weighted towards the smaller Carlist
side, in their victory anthem ‘Oriamendi’ and the recording of colour-
ful village memory (‘Espartero hid in this farmhouse’).37 The relative
dearth of Liberal memory is especially stark when compared with the
1872–76 Second Carlist War, a conﬂict which forged a galaxy of Spanish
intellectuals across the political spectrum in a European context of class
conﬂict.38
   Yet the Cristino side deserves a study of its own in English: it was
both by far the larger side and ultimately victorious. As Cristino Spain
was the government rather than insurgent side, its hundreds of thou-
sands of conscripts had no a priori reason to be ideologically committed
(although interesting exceptions can be seen in the volunteer free corps
and militia) as they were recruited indiscriminately and via the estab-
lished arms of the state.39 Even during the most extreme episodes
(1835–37) of Carlist expeditions and Liberal revolutions, the vast major-
ity of Spanish territory and population remained outside of formal
Carlist control. Thus, the civil war, in all its diverse demands of mobili-
sation, violence, insecurity, militancy and deprivation, impacted on far
more inhabitants in Cristino than Carlist zones of control. Even cities
such as Málaga and Cádiz, hundreds of miles beyond formal front lines,
were affected by the civil war. Given that Cristino Spain’s war with
the Carlists profoundly shaped the 1830s liberal revolution, a narrative
study of the popular impact would seem particularly propitious. A study
of the militancies of combatants and non-combatants would offer schol-
ars a more diverse understanding of the conﬂict, going beyond the
historiographical pitfalls of elitist censure or apology and beyond struc-
tural analyses of the ancien régime. The civil war, after all, left no part
of Spain untouched and over seven years killed 5 per cent of the 1833
population. A new study therefore needs to show how the experience
of warfare shaped the politics and society of 1830s Spain, especially
                                          Introduction, History and Sources   11


the Cristino zone. My approach is no whim: the late Julio Aróstegui
observed that ‘the options for violence by individuals or groups ended
up creating cultures of their own’.40 In this spirit, this book tells an
untold story of how otherwise undistinguished Spaniards who hitherto
had been absent from political history underwent seven years of bru-
tal civil war and revolution and framed this military experience in a
politically meaningful and radical way.
   The state of affairs described above creates virgin territory for new
interpretations and justiﬁes the sources I use for this volume, most
of which logically come from Cristino rather than Carlist quarters.
I defend this stance in part in deference to the fact that Carlist archives
in the Basque country have already been mined by some outstand-
ing researchers,41 and in part because the Carlist sources I do use (the
Gaceta Oﬁcial and published memoirs) reinforce my general approach,
which is to show the impact of the war on Spain as a whole. This ambi-
tious task must inevitably fail to exhaust all sources, and experts will
doubtless be alarmed at omissions. Nonetheless, my combination of
secondary with primary sources must perforce appear selective within
the constraints of a medium-sized monograph. The primary sources
are in three categories, published memoirs, newspapers and unpub-
lished archives. The rationale for using memoirs, from both Carlist and
Cristino authors, is explained by the events described. Cristino news-
papers are particularly valuable, especially the advanced Liberal Eco del
Comercio, because their networks of provincial correspondents reported
on local military developments, and newspapers led the maturing Left
versus Right divide in Liberal politics. My unpublished sources come
from three major archives in Madrid (the Archivo Histórico Nacional,
Real Academia de Historia, Archivo General del Palacio), the munici-
pal archives of Barcelona, Málaga and San Sebastián, and the university
archive of Zaragoza. The unpublished campaign letters of the Cristino
‘Duke of Victory’, General Espartero, complete my Spanish archive base,
whilst some foreign sources (George Borrow, French and British press,
German campaign memoirs) help to internationalise the conﬂict.
   A note of caution is necessary with regard to my use of newspapers
as a primary source. News of atrocities was often embellished in the
telling. The war shattered normal systems of information gathering,
and terriﬁed correspondents often lent credence to the worst rumours of
Carlist atrocities, which subsequently sometimes appeared unsubstanti-
ated in the Cristino press. The press thus contributed to the perceptions
of such atrocities, and the degree of terror depended as much on per-
ception as concrete facts. The hold of ‘Carlist atrocities’ over Cristino
12   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


opinion is explained not only by the events to which they referred
but also by deeper mentalities and traditions through which they were
expressed. Neither the content nor the vocabulary was new, owing to
the trauma of Spain’s recent Peninsular and Royalist Wars. But the
greater duration and irregular quality of the First Carlist War created
a press-driven ‘myth-cycle’ which ﬂourished on patchy knowledge of
the march of events, and reduced strange experiences and inexplica-
ble events to a conspiracy of Carlist ﬁfth-columnists, poisonings, prison
breaks, arms storage in churches and so forth. Confused and poorly
led Cristino conscripts in unfamiliar territory sometimes ﬁred on each
other by mistake, but these shots were reported to have been ﬁred by
‘Carlist’ inhabitants: with dire results. As the war in 1835 expanded
beyond the Basque country, demographic factors increased the scope
both for actual and ‘reported’ atrocities to take place. Cristino atrocities
were often harsher in such irregular areas of Carlist control as north-
ern Castile and, especially, Aragón and Catalonia, precisely because,
in contrast to the Basque country, ‘suspect’ men of military age were
usually encountered inside hostile villages. (The Carlist Basque coun-
try, by contrast, had been thoroughly militarised by mid-war and thus
offered greater resistance to Cristino incursions, and those incursions
that did take place usually encountered few ‘suspect’ combatants as vir-
tually every available man was at the front.) Equally, Carlist raiders into
Cristino territory encountered a vulnerable population disproportion-
ately composed of women and children. Masculine anxieties in a culture
of honour must account for part of the rise in reports in the press of rape
and murder.
   Thus, even though real atrocities took place, it is in fact impossible to
know whether they can really be taken at face value. This uncertainty
can be mitigated in two ways, of a practical and conceptual nature. Prac-
tically, I have endeavoured to substantiate accounts of atrocities and
counter-atrocities, or at least tried to qualify their reporting. Readers
may still feel a humanitarian scepticism at the evidence offered through-
out these chapters, and such a response would ironically match that
of the Cristino Liberals whose politics were radicalised by the need to
combat ‘savage’ Carlists and the backward religiosity and royalism they
represented. Conceptually, then, what matters is not what happened but
what was believed to be happening. Much of the political radicalisation
of Cristino Spain was caused by ‘news’ as much as lived experience. This
news often came from anonymous sources, a routine cause of frustration
for historians interested in the social background of protagonists and
in corroborating facts. Yet anonymity also reveals two types of sources,
                                          Introduction, History and Sources   13


one motivated vindictively and the other ethically; that is to say, either
delation or whistle-blowing. These motives had an intimately violent
dimension that characterised the local experience of civil war. In sum,
the questionable veracity of sources is not the central issue. There was
enough truth in the sources as a whole to unleash violence and polarisa-
tion, plunging Spain into seven long years of civil war. The Carlists were
always the smaller side in this chaos, which means that there is greater
scope to study the impact of the war in Cristino areas of control. Yet the
frequently asymmetrical nature of expeditions, banditry, revolution and
conspiracy undermined Cristino control and gave the war an enduring
dynamic of insecurity. As most of this book studies the insecure Cristino
zone rather than the Carlist heartlands, it is helpful to set the context
by introducing the debates and dimensions of early Carlism.

Early Carlism

Carlism’s ﬁrst ‘safe zone’ was in the Basque country. The Basque coun-
try comprises the three Basque lordships – later provinces – proper,
Alava, Guipúzcoa and Vizcaya, and the ancient autonomous Kingdom
of Navarra. The early attempts of the Viceroy of Navarra to recruit vil-
lagers into Cristino ranks failed miserably, even when service wages were
offered of double, then treble, what the Carlist insurgents promised.42
Coverdale summarised four factors as to why this was so: (1) traditional
society was still economically viable for most of population, hence liber-
alism was seen a threat; (2) natural leadership strata (clergy, landowners)
lived cheek by jowl with peasants and supported the Carlist cause;
(3) the terrain was sufﬁciently abrupt and broken to prevent the use
of cavalry and to facilitate small bands to escape (to change their shirts
and ﬁght another day), whilst the landscape was densely populated
enough to allow regular food and supplies; and (4) the appearance of
the extremely gifted guerrilla leader, Tomás de Zumalacárregui. Other
parts of Spain may have had one, or some of these factors, but not
all four.43 (In the Aragonese Maestrazgo a comparable situation would
pertain only from 1836.44 ) The Basque provinces, like Catalonia, also
had a long-standing tradition of irregular military service (in the Basque
country recruits were called requetés and in Catalonia somatenes). The
Basque Carlist acceptance of war and armed struggle as a legitimate
means to seize power gave the cause its most enduring characteristic,
resurfacing 100 years later in preparation for the Spanish Civil War as
‘a quintessential part of the Carlist way of life’.45 Contemporary writers
noted the independent, agile and hard-drinking (by Spanish standards)
14   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Basque character, believing this to be the result of centuries of autonomy
and hardy geography.46 Recent victories against the French taught
the Basques to feel naturally superior in terms of military prowess.47
Writers of Basque culture have stressed the provinces’ historical pre-
eminence (the three lordships – señoríos – and the Kingdom of Navarra
matched other Spanish kingdoms in longevity). ‘False’ religions had
never penetrated the Basque country, and during the sixteenth cen-
tury the kingdom was rewarded accordingly with the grant of noble
status to all indigenous Basques, which meant that nobility became far
less signiﬁcant as a factor in social differentiation than in the rest of
Spain. This ancient history gave the Basques the conﬁdence to nego-
tiate their Spanishness on their own terms, via their fueros, language,
Catholic piety and the importance of the self-contained homestead – or
caserío (which was handed down in its entirety to a single male heir) –
in family and social relations.48
   The Basque fueros have played a major role in debates about early
Carlism. Marxist historians objectiﬁed the fueros and the local elites
they empowered as a doomed defence of pre-capitalist socio-economic
norms. The popular Catholicism which distinguished pre-capitalist
Basque society, and which was the cause and motive for popular
Carlism, was merged by beneﬁciaries of the fueros into the self-interested
war cry of ‘religion and fueros’, tying their debatable merits to the unim-
peachable merits of religion.49 But it would be superﬁcial to explain
Basque Carlism as a war in defence of the fueros. Whereas Carlist pro-
paganda defended the fueros, it did so in the defensive tone of the
‘ancient constitution’ violated by the pre-war police state (which in
the Basque country intensiﬁed its activities after 1830 whilst relenting
elsewhere in Spain), and by the martial law of Cristino ‘pashas’ once
the war had started.50 In fact early Basque Carlists did not place foral
interests foremost. The Carlists’ second-in-command claimed that the
Basque insurgents of 1833 saw the fueros as secondary to the legitimate
royal succession, and his subaltern, Wilhelm von Rahden, whose for-
eignness made him disinterested, agreed.51 Even though the October
1833 Carlist insurgency in Alava mentioned the fueros, its leader, Valen-
tín Verástegui, had a vested family interest. But the proclamations of
the insurgent king, Don Carlos, were slower to invoke them: not until
September 1834 did he make an explicitly foralist proclamation.52 The
Pretender’s foralism was not proactive but purely in reaction to the fact
that the rest of Spain (which had long been stripped of its fueros) had
failed to rally to his cause. He was forced to rely on the fueros because
no other wing of the Fernandine state, neither the army nor (mostly)
                                          Introduction, History and Sources   15


the Church, defected to the Carlist cause.53 In fact, the fueros grew in
importance only when military victory seemed impossible in the wake
of the failed Royal Expedition, and as Carlist peace feelers voiced a grow-
ing willingness to abandon Don Carlos who, since 1834, had been the
fueros’ champion.
   In sum, the fueros do not by themselves explain the prevalence of
Basque Carlism; yet Basque Carlism is also impossible to understand
without them, not least because their most outspoken defenders were
to be found in Carlist ranks and their most outspoken critics amongst
the Cristino Liberals. This dynamic was especially intense in the Basque
country itself, which entered the war bitterly divided. The port-cities
of Bilbao (Vizcaya) and especially San Sebastián (Guipúzcoa) welcomed
their Cristino garrisons. This was because the Cristino-Liberal policy of
moving the customs frontier from the River Ebro to the actual Spanish
frontier in the Pyrenees promised to achieve by war what frustrated com-
mercial elites had failed to achieve at the foral level for decades.54 Even
in the rural areas satisﬁed with their fueros, local juntas and deputa-
tions were split between supporters of Cristinos and Carlists in 1833.
Cristinos were sidelined and then expelled, but the powers of the fueros
themselves were increasingly curtailed by the Carlist Royal Government
in the name of the war effort. These actions made the government
unpopular, not least because they were frequently carried out by non-
Basque Carlists, ‘Castilian’ refugees from Cristino areas of control whom
Basques suspected of meaning to harm local interests.
   Such Basque versus Castilian friction within Carlism, combined with
the wider Cristino versus Carlist struggle, led some Basque nation-
alists to originate their cause in 1830s Carlism, stressing inchoate
wartime demands for Navarrese independence and the ancient fueros
as a blueprint for modern autonomy. The most vociferous nineteenth-
century example was the French Basque Augustin Chaho, for whom the
First Carlist War was a war between the Basque country and Castile.55
But such teleological nationalism deﬁes scrutiny. The late nineteenth-
century founder of Basque nationalism may have come from Carlist
ancestry, but Sabino Arana rejected Carlism because it sacriﬁced Basque
interests to Spanish dynastic politics.56 However, the link persisted in
the minds of several Basque nationalists. In 1935 Bernardo Estornés
Lasa claimed Carlism’s ﬁrst warlord ‘wanted to proclaim Basque inde-
pendence’, and in more recent years the vandalism inﬂicted upon
the ‘English cemetery’ at San Sebastián by the armed Basque sepa-
ratist organisation, Euskadi Ta Askatasuna (ETA), shows a link between
memory and contemporary militancy.57
16   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


   In fact Francoism manoeuvred the Carlists – who had already merged
with National and Traditionalist Catholics in 1931 to become the Tradi-
tionalist Communion – into a right-wing coalition of virulent Spanish
unitary nationalism and anti-separatism, the very antithesis of Basque
independence. Whereas Franco rewarded his allied provinces of Navarra
and Alava with limited autonomy, he stripped the Republican provinces
of Vizcaya and Guipúzcoa of theirs. Franco-era Traditionalists lauded
their 1830s Basque forefathers and their fueros as traditionalist models
not for Euzkadi, but for Spain as a whole. Although some complained at
how the ‘ungrateful’ Francoist regime was pushing the Basque language
and fueros into virtual extinction, most stressed their national region-
alism as Spaniards above all else, rejecting regional nationalism.58 That
said, even though Basque Carlists during the First Carlist War were not
ﬁghting for the independence of their homeland in the modern sense,
they certainly formed a caucus, not just against the Cristinos but also
against the non-Basque Carlists (the ‘Castilians’). This Basque–Carlist
cohesiveness was reinforced by priests who were always local as they
had to hear confessions in Euskera. Cloistered clergy, meanwhile, had
comparatively little presence in the Basque country, and those institu-
tions which did exist were disproportionately of the humbler Franciscan
orders; this explains the alliance of the vast majority of Basque religious
with Carlism.59 Thus, even though Carlism was not Basque proto-
nationalism, the Basque Carlists certainly mustered enough identity –
in particular, popular Catholic piety – to separate themselves culturally
and psychologically from the rest of Spain. Evidence of this is the persis-
tent xenophobic term guiri coined by Basque Carlists, an abbreviation
of the Euskera guiristino, or Spanish Cristino name of their enemy, a
term which survived the conﬂict to describe the centralising paramil-
itary police force, the Guardia Civil and, in the twentieth century, even
foreign tourists.
   But the Basque Carlists represented just one part (albeit the biggest
part) of the insurrection. Carlism in the 1830s was Janus-faced. Whereas
in the Basque country Carlism defended a still-viable socially conserva-
tive, stable rural society from the onslaught of liberalism, in the eastern
areas of rural Aragón, Valencia and Catalonia it was a protest vehicle
for peasants whose livelihoods were already being eroded by capital-
ism in nearby urban centres and by the Liberal property revolution.60
These socio-economic issues were the reasons why a second ‘safe zone’
for Carlism was forged in Lower Aragón and adjoining inland areas of
Valencia and the northern extremities of historic Murcia (an area known
as the Maestrazgo). The character of the war was different here. The
                                           Introduction, History and Sources   17


rural population was hostile to the cash economy, whilst recurrent Lib-
eral land reform in the Maestrazgo encroached upon the de facto rather
than de jure property rights in agriculture of ‘tenants’ who were really
common-law smallholders, or peasants. Nonetheless, these regions did
not enjoy the fueros of the Basques either, which meant that the popu-
lation here had perhaps the most to lose and the least to gain from the
war, as even Cristino urban centres in the region had to spend all their
economic gains defending themselves against the impoverished rural
population which joined the Carlist guerrillas. Thus, as Pedro Rújula,
Josep Fontana and Manuel Ardit have explained, this rural economic
stress fuelled such other factors as the local Carlist religious, and the lack
of sound administrative organisation and communication (caused by
adverse terrain), in turning the Maestrazgo Carlist.61 The Carlist insur-
gency gave political expression to recurring rural émeutes in Valencia
caused by a legacy of Spain’s harshest feudal regime.62 The Carlist effort
in the Maestrazgo was led from 1835 by Ramón Cabrera and went
through three incremental stages. During the ﬁrst stage, 1833–35, the
Carlist effort here was dispersed by the Cristinos and conﬁned to rural
guerrilla warfare. The second phase, 1836–37, saw the breakthrough as
Cabrera expanded the boundaries of the Carlist safe zone and turned it
into a military state. The third phase, 1838–39, in contrast to the stagna-
tion in the Carlist Basque country, saw the Maestrazgo reach its apogee
in terms of geography, military victories and recruitment. The August
1839 Peace Treaty in the north orphaned these achievements and sent
Cabrera into exile the following year, ending the First Carlist War.63
   A third Carlist safe zone operated in the Catalan interior adjacent to
the Pyrenees. Economic crisis again explains much of the rural sup-
port for Carlism here, but in contrast to the situation in the Basque
country, the nature of Catalan Carlism was not well understood by
Cristino Liberals at the time. For Espoz y Mina, Captain-General of the
Principality, his Catalan enemies were no social bandits but incorrigible
mountain folk, driven to violence from superstition and lack of civic
education.64 His fellow Liberal, Evaristo de San Miguel, held a vague but
kinder judgement: ‘agricultural improvements’ based on the true ful-
ﬁlment of the Constitution, so he thought, could win over the rural
masses from Carlism.65 It was not until the twentieth century that social
historians probed the economic complexities of Carlism in both the
Maestrazgo and Catalonia. Thus, the threat of Liberal property revolu-
tion in the Maestrazgo, and memories of how this, in the early 1820s,
had threatened common-law ‘ownership’, from 1833 pitted the country-
side against the towns.66 Studies of Catalonia, meanwhile, have shown
18   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


how economic reforms impoverished such declining economic centres
in the hinterland of Liberal Barcelona as Baix Camp and Berguedà, a sit-
uation which, in contrast to their counterparts in Castile and Andalucía,
persuaded many Catalan aristocrats to support Carlism.67 Thus, eco-
nomic factors explain some of the motivations for Carlism: a viable
rural economy in the Basque country, a traumatised economy in the
Maestrazgo and an impoverished rural economy in Catalonia. But the
economic factors cannot be simpliﬁed as a ‘rural–urban divide’, as even
in urban settings artisans threatened by recurrent Liberal abolition of the
guilds and redundant ofﬁceholders (cesantes) could be drawn to Carlism,
whilst, by contrast, villagers who had beneﬁted from the Liberal prop-
erty revolution would correspondingly turn Cristino;68 ﬂight either from
or to the countryside in many cases entrenched a rural (Carlist) ver-
sus urban (Cristino) divide, but as an effect rather than a cause of the
conﬂict. Moreover, as the 1832–33 palace revolution had driven Carlist
conspirators from the court and into the country, regional hierarchies
decided local popular political allegiance. The poorest areas of Spain,
which were in Extremadura and Andalucía, were lost causes for Don
Carlos. Carlist writers explained this by congratulating themselves on
how advanced, rather than backward, areas embraced the legitimate
cause.69 But the bald fact was that any nobles outside of the three heart-
lands pronouncing in favour of Don Carlos would have been banished
from Madrid and had their extensive lands and incomes in the south
seized. The northern nobility, by contrast, had either few or no lands
to lose.70 Economic factors thus explain the structural context, indeed
it would be strange if they did not, given that the Spanish state in 1833
collected in revenues barely more than half what it had at its imperial
peak in 1792.71 But they do not explain everything.
   Further explanation can be sought in the Carlists’ exploitation of
local military traditions, although recent research has shown this to be
more coercive in nature than voluntarist, as was previously assumed.
Both the Basque country and rural Catalonia had strong armed militia
traditions, the requetés originating in Navarra and the somatenes, respec-
tively. These militia, especially the Basque militia, which had employed
irregular resistance against the French during the periods 1793–95 and
1808–13 and against the Liberals during 1820–23, certainly possessed
a psychological ‘DNA’ of popular armed mobilisation. Carlist writers,
especially leftist neo-Carlists attracted to the ‘people’s war’ paradigm,
claimed these mobilisations were voluntary rather than coercive in
nature: ‘Don Carlos’s army was fundamentally popular, nourished by
volunteers . . . although ﬁnes were imposed on families who resisted
                                           Introduction, History and Sources   19


the calls to arms, these acted more as a tax than a ﬁne, and with-
out exception targeted families of Liberal sympathies.’72 But in fact the
Carlists suffered almost as much from draft-dodging and desertion as the
Cristinos, or rather popular Carlism was, at least in part, draft-dodging
and desertion. Even in the case of the Basque country, recent research
shows Carlist insurgent leaders at best tolerating rather than endorsing
the early phenomenon of ‘shirt-changing’ amongst their men.73 Yet for
a long time, shirt-changing, the practice of Carlist insurgents dispersing
in the face of superior Cristino forces, was romanticised as something
nobler than desertion, as a form of voluntarism winked at by other-
wise very authoritarian ofﬁcers. Certainly, geography allowed Carlist
combatants in the Basque country to return to their homesteads rela-
tively easily, concealing their weapons usually in the full knowledge of
civil authorities who not only tolerated but also protected them on the
understanding that they would return to ﬁght the Cristinos as soon as
they could do so with advantage, or at least that they would not cause
trouble with Carlist military authorities. But the consolidation of Carlist
military control in the Basque country diminished this practice, and
even though shirt-changing remained common on expeditions and in
patchy areas of Carlist control, the Basque safe zone, and to a lesser
extent, those in the Maestrazgo and Catalonia, produced predictable
and coercive patterns of conscription into increasingly regular armies.
This militarisation was answered by widespread draft evasion and deser-
tion, not on the scale of Cristino Spain, but signiﬁcant and deleterious
to the war effort nonetheless. Thus, even though the Carlists’ exploita-
tion of local military traditions was more successful, as we shall see, than
that of the Cristinos, it was distinctly qualiﬁed.
   The nature and extent of Carlism beyond the three safe zones diver-
siﬁed still further, and its adherents may be differentiated into Carlists
of conscience (‘civic Carlists’) and of violence (facciosos or ‘insurgents’).
Their geographical distribution was patchy and changing, although sup-
port for Carlism was generally strong in northern Castile, Asturias and
Galicia. Galicia was especially prone to Carlism as its clergy were lease
owners of tiny, subdivided peasant plots (minifundia) in a social geog-
raphy sometimes referred to as ‘Spain’s Ireland’. Several Galician clerics
self-interestedly mobilised their rural dependents against the enemy’s
property revolution.74 In general militant Carlism was dispersed into
the countryside and away from the larger towns. Rural pockets were
often held by ‘insurgents’, but many of these ‘Carlists’ were really ban-
dits looking for political cover or deserters from the Cristino army. Many
more Carlists were peaceful (‘civic Carlists’), and were found, above all,
20   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


amongst the religious but also in other walks of life. Even the largest
Cristino towns contained Carlist sympathisers (and Cristino support-
ers also remained in revolted provinces). Those ﬁnding themselves in
the wrong zone could be subjected to ﬁnancial penalties, imprisonment
and even death, in proportion to the approach of enemy forces, popu-
lar demands for reprisals and the radicalisation of politics produced by
the war.
   In sum, the diverse dimensions of early Carlism present scholars with
a striking paradox, namely that 1830s Carlism was militarily most devel-
oped at a time when its ideology was least cohesive. Early Carlism was
a mix of radical royalists, absolutists and theocrats, prisoners of Spain’s
multiple pasts, and rivals in court and military politics.75 Later civil wars
during the periods 1872–76 and 1936–39 never came close to threat-
ening a Carlist conquest of the state to the extent that 1833–40 did.
Yet Carlist ideology – its traditional culture, intense Catholicism and
espousal of a medievalist decentralised political structure – was more
developed then.76 Thus, events demand greater scrutiny than would
be the case in studies of the later Carlist conﬂicts; they justify this
book’s chronological history approach, liberal with the conﬂict’s ide-
ological background but rigorous with its physical manifestations. This
approach can be justiﬁed by the scale of the conﬂict. The First Carlist
War was the bloodiest civil war in nineteenth-century Europe – by some
measurements bloodier than the Spanish Civil War – with military casu-
alties alone amounting to as much as 2.5 per cent of Spain’s entire
population.77 This notwithstanding, it is the great conﬂict of which
most scholars and students outside of Spain are ignorant. If this vol-
ume makes even the slightest dent on this ignorance, this author will be
satisﬁed.
2
The First Carlist War: Origins




Ferdinand VII described himself as a cork in a bottle of beer: as soon
as that cork was removed, all the troubles of Spain would explode into
the open. He is routinely considered the worst of all Spanish monarchs.
Timid, mediocre and vindictive, his reign compounded such geopoliti-
cal disasters as the Peninsular War and the interconnected loss of Spain’s
mainland American empire. During the Peninsular War (virtually all
of which Ferdinand would sit out in the gilded captivity of France) a
Liberal clique had emerged to dominate the politics of the Spanish Patri-
ots who were ﬁghting against France in the name of the captive King.
This clique, led by such enlightened young nobles as Count Toreno,
Agustín de Argüelles and Martínez de la Rosa, was besieged in Spain’s
most bourgeois port-city, Cádiz, during 1810–12, crucial years in which
the Liberals pointed to the repeated defeats sustained by the Patriot reg-
ular armies in the rest of Spain as evidence of the need for a ‘Great Leap
Forward’ of political reform, the most iconic and advanced of which
was the Constitution of 1812, also known as the Constitution of Cádiz,
which would be adopted as a mascot by Spanish Radicals for much of
the nineteenth century.1 During the whole of the Peninsular War the
French army never conquered Cádiz, but bombarded it from afar on
occasion, and this gave the Patriot Liberals the illusion that they were
sharing the suffering of the rest of Spain.
   The Liberals, resentful of the eighteenth-century militarisation of
Spain’s Bourbon monarchy and resolute in their belief in the rights of
the individual against arbitrary power, cast a suspicious eye towards
Spain’s Bourbon military, even though most members of this had
sided with the Spanish Patriots in 1808 and subsequently took to the
ﬁeld against appalling odds, never abandoning the struggle against
Napoleon.2 The social composition of the ofﬁcer corps was already

                                   21
22   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


changing in response to the demands of the war. The revolution of
1808 saw ofﬁcers from undistinguished social backgrounds rise through
the Patriot ranks, whilst the Patriot provisional government (Junta
Central) embarked upon crash military reforms which were at least
beginning to improve the army’s ﬁghting potential. Yet the Spanish
Liberals divided opinion both inside and outside the military. Liberal
journalists, taking advantage of Cádiz’s ﬁrst (and ﬂeeting) free press,
frequently goaded Bourbon commanders to prove their patriotism in
battle against obvious French superiority on Spain’s high plateaus – with
appalling results. Just as the Carlist War would show, Spanish generals
did not like being second-guessed by civilians. Even though the Spanish
army did eventually prevail, it did so only as a humiliatingly junior
partner under British leadership, ﬁnanced and even clothed by British
subsidies.3 Factionalism, also a dominant feature in the Carlist War,
characterised the Liberals who, from the convocation of the Cortes in
Cádiz in 1810, increasingly dominated the war effort. On the one hand,
the Patriot Liberals championed several military mediocrities, such as
General Ballesteros (who had unsuccessfully challenged the Duke of
Wellington’s appointment in 1812 to the Supreme Command of Allied
Forces), mainly because they proved useful agents in domestic politics.4
On the other hand, the Liberals exaggerated out of all proportion the
effectiveness of the famous guerrilleros, or Patriot guerrilla bands, who
have given the Peninsular War its most enduring image. Viewed by the
Liberals as a useful counterweight to the ‘despotic’ army, the guerrillas
also neatly served the ideological purpose of the ‘people in arms’, literal
embodiments of national sovereignty – though the reality was, in fact,
far more complex.5 Meanwhile the Liberals, especially the more radical
ones, planned to check the Bourbon army with the National Militia, a
part-time citizens’ force organised at local level and in the hands of prop-
erty owners. The National Militia was written into the Constitution, yet
its existence during the Peninsular War was ephemeral: called into being
as late as April 1814, it survived only two weeks in the places where it
had even organised at all before the royalist coup of May rendered all
the reforms of the Liberals null and void. Yet this seed would germinate
during the later Liberal revolutions of 1820–23, and especially during
the Carlist War of 1833–40 when the militia would be dragged as much
into conﬂict in Liberal politics as against the Carlists.
   Given the anti-militarist trajectory of the Liberals, a faction grew
within the army that was hostile to the whole constitutional exper-
iment. Some historians have explained this disaffection in genera-
tional terms, with older ofﬁcers who had established themselves during
                                             The First Carlist War: Origins   23


Bourbon absolutism being more hostile than their younger counter-
parts, whose more progressive outlook had been forged in the war and
revolution of 1808–14.6 But whilst some ofﬁcers were certainly ideolog-
ically opposed to progressive politics, many more, by 1814, had learnt
to become anti-Liberal because of the army’s shabby treatment at the
hands of politicians: this attitude was common to both old and new
ofﬁcers. Army discontent was matched by a growing conservative group
in the Cortes whom the Liberals called the ‘servile ones’ (serviles) who
seconded the Church’s forlorn attempts to save the Inquisition (the
Holy Ofﬁce was abolished in January 1813). Whether the Liberals would
prevail in their reformist mission depended on the nature of the immi-
nent peace over which King Ferdinand – the legitimate monarch in
whose name even the street radicals of Cádiz agitated – would preside.
In December 1813, Napoleon, in a forlorn bid to split the Spanish Patri-
ots from their victorious British alliance, dispossessed his brother of his
now negligible Spanish realm and released Ferdinand back into Spain.
Whilst the Patriots of all political stripes rejected Napoleon’s gambit, the
arrival of Ferdinand on Spanish soil conﬁrmed the Liberals’ worst fears.
   The serviles of the Cortes issued a legitmist ‘Manifesto of the Persians’.
The title refers to the Persians of antiquity who suffered anarchy after
the death of their King; now the Spanish Persians appealed to the
King to end the anarchy visited on Spain by the Constitution of
1812. But this manifesto was not absolutist in tone but traditionalist.
It wanted Ferdinand to overthrow not just the Liberal Constitution
but also the Enlightenment-inspired reforms of the eighteenth cen-
tury from which liberalism had progressed. Such traditionalism meant
restoring the medieval rights of the king, Church and nobility, above
all the Church. Even though the Constitution had reserved a stri-
dently confessional role for the Spanish state, an anomaly in an other-
wise very progressive charter, the traditionalists countered that Spain’s
Catholicism came from God and history, not as a gift from the arrogant
young lawyers of Cádiz. With more reservations the absolutists agreed,
although the absolutist restoration of 1823 would be less clerical than
1814. During the crisis of 1813–14 these nuances on the Right were
shelved by the abolition of the Constitution. For this task, Ferdinand
needed a military coup, just as he had needed one in 1808 to oust his
father from the throne. Most of the army was happy to oblige and to
coalesce around the ‘reaction incarnate’, General Eguía, whose deﬁant
wearing of outmoded wigs seemed to symbolise a desire to return Spain
to the happier times of absolutism. On 4 May 1814, King Ferdinand,
having amassed enough military support to prevail, decreed all acts
24    Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


of the Cortes null and void. The Liberals were imprisoned, exiled or
banished from court, and were thus tarred with the same brush as the
afrancesados – the ‘Frenchiﬁed’ servants of the vanquished Bonaparte
regime. Thus ensued six years of what is known to Spanish history as
the Sexenio absolutista, Ferdinand’s absolute rather than traditionalist
(with the exception of reviving the Inquisition) monarchy. The White
atmosphere of May 1814 was described by a Zaragoza diarist:

     11 May: Around 300 men assembled in the centre of the city at
     midnight to smash the tablet (lápida) representing the Constitution,
     throwing its rubble into the Ebro, and leaving in its place vivas to
     Fernando VII.

     12 May: The ‘Great Day’. All householders have been ordered to illu-
     minate their windows for three days in honour of the king’s coup, on
     pain of being considered ‘traitors to the patria’.

     14 May: More than 4,000 copies of the king’s decree sold in one
     day, joy increasing all the more when lists were read out of all the
     imprisonments made in Madrid.7

Yet Ferdinand was unable to restore peace with stability. Instead of liv-
ing up to his wartime propaganda image of a hero of suffering Spain,
martyr and ﬁrst amongst Spaniards, Ferdinand treated his kingdom
as a personal patrimony rather than a sovereign nation, whilst plac-
ing himself in the hands of the Spanish Church far more than his
eighteenth-century predecessors did, thereby storing up false theocratic
expectations for the future.8 No peace dividend eased his lot. The post-
war peace established by the Congress of Vienna saw Spain formally
demoted to second-power status. The future peace of Europe would
be directed by the powerful states of Russia, Austria, Prussia and Great
Britain, and even defeated France would be welcomed back into the fold
in response to renewed turmoil in Spain. Even if Ferdinand had had any
vision of Spain’s place in the new era of peace (and all the evidence sug-
gests that he did not), it is difﬁcult to see how he might have achieved
it. The demographic and economic devastation caused by the Penin-
sular War was compounded by the post-1810 Spanish American revolt,
which ﬁrst reduced, and then curtailed altogether, the remittances of
silver and gold that had funded the eighteenth-century growth in state
power. The consolidation of Spanish American independence between
1810 and 1836 caused great economic loss to Basque exports. It also
disturbed traditional outlets for emigration at a time when the rate of
                                            The First Carlist War: Origins   25


Basque population growth was accelerating. This meant that Basque
communities which, in the eighteenth century, would have exported
its surplus manhood to the Americas now faced a bottleneck of impov-
erished and underemployed men of military age who had little lose by
joining Carlist insurrections.9 The ﬁnancial shortfall caused by the loss
of empire, meanwhile, had to be made up by attempts to modernise
methods of raising internal revenue. In 1817 a ﬁscal reform created the
derechos de puertas, which saddled municipalities with a greater burden
of taxation to fund state penury.10 Smugglers and bandits who managed
to dodge provincial revenue guards became as much popular heroes as
saboteurs of the state coffers. Meanwhile, the voided attempts of the
Liberals to substitute state jurisdiction in the countryside for that of
the manor, thereby creating a free market in landownership, were now
revived as a desperate means of raising revenue.11 Attempts to conﬁs-
cate and sell off ecclesiastical and military lands met with the successful
opposition of the Church and nobility. But a portion of lands owned
directly by the Crown, together with some common lands held by
municipalities, were sold off.12 Whilst this measure barely helped the
state’s ﬁnances, it obviously reduced the resources of the municipalities
and squeezed the amount of land available for rent.
   Underlying the economic crisis was what contemporaries identiﬁed
as a moral crisis. Parish priests anathematised the recent ‘atheism’ and
‘Jacobinism’ of the Liberals in equal proportion to what one royal
decree termed the ‘vices introduced during the war’, which ranged from
widespread prostitution to the new practice for women, even respectable
ones, to walk the streets unaccompanied by men.13 This practice would
be compounded during the Carlist War when women entered public
roles in the absence of male heads of family, a handful even becoming
militants and strikers. The rural masses, which during the war had seen
their hopes of owning nationalised land dashed by the Liberals, now
faced similar disappointment as the King kept the land in the hands
of its feudal owners.14 The rural population accordingly took varying
forms of direct action. Where legal appeals for redistribution (reparto)
failed, peasants tore noble insignia from churches, refused feudal dues
and turned ploughshares back into swords.15 The Fernandine regime
responded with the rigours of an absolutist police state, reviving for a
ﬁnal lease of life the Inquisition which, as one Liberal fugitive discov-
ered, kept ‘an active agent in every single village in the realm’.16 The
cities, especially the coastal ports receptive to liberalism, were divided
into police districts answerable to regional captains-general who would
crack down on unrest by closing taverns, establishing curfews and
26   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


monitoring the transit of outsiders.17 The army was thus key to the
Fernandine police state. But soldiers faced arrears in pay, casual bru-
tality at the hands of ofﬁcers and inadequate rations: hardly conditions
propitious to law and order. Meanwhile, despite the coup of 1814, the
army was no monochrome: a Liberal faction remained amongst its ofﬁ-
cers, often now being placed on half-pay or in remote garrisons by a
suspicious King.18 During the autumn and winter of 1819 several such
ofﬁcers were amassing forces around Cádiz as part of Ferdinand’s effort
to dispatch reinforcements for the cause of royalism in Spanish America.
Under the command of Rafael del Riego they would end up marching
not on Mexico, but on Madrid. And they would succeed where previous
Liberal pronunciamientos had failed.
   The ﬁrst of these failures would later end up as the sword-arm
of Spain’s Liberal revolutions during the Royalist and Carlist Wars.
Francisco Espoz y Mina had been the most successful Patriot guerrilla
commander, turning Navarra, a conservative and autonomous region
where socio-economic conditions had generated a uniquely popular
armed mobilisation against the French, into a virtual ﬁefdom (his
French enemies even dubbed him the ‘King of Navarra’). Despite his
later politics, Espoz had been violently at odds with the Liberals dur-
ing the Peninsular War, even allegedly executing by ﬁring squad a copy
of the Constitution of 1812. This anecdote remains apocryphal, but it
would have suited the Navarrese he commanded. Thus, when he hur-
ried to the newly restored court of Ferdinand VII, he expected a reward,
nothing less than the Viceroyalty of Navarra. But the King snubbed
his entreaty, and Espoz returned to his Pamplona garrison and raised
a revolt in the name of the Constitution he had so recently derided.19
When this failed, Espoz escaped retribution by escaping to France (his
idealistic nephew, Xavier Mina, even following through on his uncle’s
pretexts by ﬂeeing via France, the British Isles and the USA to Spain’s
restive Americas where he would die ﬁghting for Mexican indepen-
dence from Spanish absolutism). Ironically, when Espoz ﬁnally became
viceroy, it would be as a Liberal ﬁghting against the Carlist sons of veter-
ans he had once commanded, failing militarily in his advanced middle
age where in his youth he had triumphed. The year 1815 saw another
guerrilla leader, Juan Díaz Porlier, pay the ultimate price for rebellion,
and the following year saw an abortive Republican conspiracy called the
Triangle.20 In 1817 another popular war hero, Luis Roberto de Lacy, was
executed amid much controversy after having tried to raise Barcelona,
whilst in 1819 another commander, Vidal, would meet the same fate.
                                            The First Carlist War: Origins   27


These veterans had acted with not insubstantial civilian support, often
networked via powerful Masonic lodges which provided a natural con-
spiratorial environment for progressive elites who liked to exhaust all
options for keeping the people out of politics. Yet their conspiracies
ended in failure. What gave Riego success was Ferdinand’s resolve to
bring metropolitan rule back to Spanish America. The absolutist regime
set great store by pacifying America so that bullion could ﬂow again.
Yet quite apart from the unpromising diplomatic environment, this task
could only be achieved with the dispatch of sufﬁcient troops, itself a
task which was so costly as to be undertaken in circumstances which
virtually guaranteed military rebellion: namely, inadequate and irregu-
lar pay and troop concentrations around the Liberal port of Cádiz which
were slow to disperse due to insufﬁcient trans-Atlantic shipping being
available. Thus, when, late in 1819, the King ordered ten battalions to
the province of Cádiz to await transport on festering hulks to Spanish
America, the stage was set for disgruntled commanders to rally their
equally disgruntled men – whose rallying cry was ‘No America’ – to over-
throw the Restoration regime. Consequently, it was the army in 1820
which was breaking a regime, just as in 1814.
   For several weeks it seemed as though Riego’s revolution would meet
the same fate as that of his predecessors. Marching across Andalucía,
Riego’s army blended Liberal appeals aimed at the urban well-to-do with
populist promises aimed at the masses. The port-city of Algeciras, one of
the ﬁrst urban centres to be taken by Riego’s army, saw its import duties
on foreign goods abolished, the unpopular derechos de puerta tax system
mothballed ‘pending the design of a better system’ and the important
state tobacco monopoly broken up.21 Similar measures were enacted as
other cities fell to the constitutional rebellion in January, February and
March, whilst an open trade in grain imports was proclaimed as means
of staving off recurrent famines.22 Despite these measures, Riego’s ris-
ing soon petered out, as populations failed to rally, horses were hard
to ﬁnd, rains were torrential and soldiers deserted into banditry. Even
those men remaining under Riego’s command had no commissariat
and were thus so reduced by hunger as to make enemies of the rural
Andalucían communities in their path, demanding cash from the gen-
erally cashless villagers, and pillaging when none was forthcoming.23
Other soldiers, like the cash-strapped privates in General Freyre’s army,
feared becoming substitutes for the American expedition and so tried to
demonstrate their indispensability by pronouncing against Riego, com-
mitting atrocities (some 71 deaths and 171 wounded) in the genteel
28    Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


streets of Cádiz in the name of the absolute king.24 And if fellow ofﬁ-
cers saw Riego’s men as deserters, so did the civilian population. In a
January 1820 letter to a fellow revolutionary, Riego admitted the lack of
popular support: ‘What guarantees could we give to anyone who joined
us? . . . What help can we expect from the pueblos? Everyone runs for the
hills as soon as they hear the sound of our drums.’25 What saved the
situation were the calculations of commanders elsewhere in Spain who
thought that their careers would be better served by a constitutional
regime. Thus, on 4 March, Ocaña’s garrison pronounced for Riego, and
the Liberal hero of 1812, Ballesteros, followed suit in Madrid, thereby
sealing the fate of absolutism. Crowds sacked the gaols of the Inquisition
and invaded the palace, and the King’s nerve ﬁnally broke on 7 March
1820 when he swore allegiance to the Constitution of 1812.26 A provi-
sional junta staffed by what Ferdinand called his ‘gaolbird’ Liberals came
into being. This junta was soon replaced by the Cortes, whilst those Lib-
eral politicians who had been exiled or imprisoned even claimed six
years’ worth of back pay in a bid to re-establish the continuity with
1814.27
   Even though the army had restored to Spain its ‘liberty’, a crisis
ensued as the resurgent constitutional authorities tried to subordinate
it to civil supremacy. Liberal newspapers noted that, whereas in 1820
the army had been the nation’s liberator, as recently as 1814 it had been
its gaoler.28 As a safeguard, the ‘people-in-arms’ principle now dictated
the creation of the National Militia, a reform which, under the 1810–14
Cortes, had barely left the drawing board. For the time being the mili-
tia was little more than a society for the local well-to-do, enlistment
into its ranks being conditional upon both a literacy test and the ability
to afford the uniform.29 The militia ordinances of 1820 were similar to
