those of the later Carlist War, when the citizens’ force was reformed
more cautiously. Zaragoza, a provincial capital with a population of
36,000, was, in April 1820, ordered to create six companies of 144 men
each plus nine ofﬁcers:

     Uniform and turquoise pantaloons, white-metal buttons and black
     boots; ofﬁcers and sergeants longcoat with buttoned lapels, high-
     peaked cap marked with the sign of town and province. Duties to
     include patrols to maintain order, pursue and arrest deserters and
     wrongdoers, whilst militiamen will enjoy fuero militar when active
     in these duties and will thus be bound by army ordinances, and will
     be paid a daily wage of 5 reales for each soldier, 6 for each corporal
     and drummer, and 8 for each sergeant (without bread ration).30
                                            The First Carlist War: Origins   29


   Only under duress did the Liberals seriously open its ranks to ple-
beians, and even then on the understanding that they would do the
bidding of their social superiors. From the outset the militia vexed the
regular army, which was set on extracting a reward for having liber-
ated the nation. Militiamen enjoyed the same immunity from civil
jurisdiction (fuero militar) as the army, the only vague and practically
meaningless condition for this privilege being ‘when on active duty’.
Thus, as during the Carlist War, militiamen and ofﬁcers shared similar
facilities enabling them to compete on matters of entitlement and hon-
our. The army reserved the power to force the militia to disband under
a state of siege, but this ‘nuclear option’ only displaced inter-service
rivalry, to the streets, cafes and the Cortes where the Jacobin deputy,
Romero Alpuente, repeatedly called for the army to be replaced by the
militia altogether.31 Radicals like Romero Alpuente were representing
the Radical Patriotic Societies and local militias which increasingly exer-
cised an extra-parliamentary ‘double regime’ in Liberal Spain. Romero
Alpuente owed his radicalism in part to his antimilitarism – the Liberals
saw soldiers as brutish mercenaries lacking in civic nationalism – and
in part to his passion to cut the tax burden, the beneﬁts of which, to
his mind at least, should be used to improve living standards. Many
more civilian Liberals, by contrast, assumed that Spain’s depleted post-
imperial coffers should fund the expansion of state power provided for
by the Constitution. The dissident sevillano utilitarian, José María Blanco
White, criticised this careerism, noting that only Spain’s minority classes
of students, literati, merchants and ofﬁceholders could gain from con-
stitutional rule, and his words were borne out by the empleomanía, or
unrestrained competition for the government posts, unleashed by the
Triennium.32 Whilst many Liberals sincerely believed that expanded
government would eventually reverse Spain’s social crisis, empleomanía,
as in other Hispanic polities, should really be understood as the lad-
der undistinguished place-seekers climbed in order to achieve personal
honour.33
   The regime had to recruit constitutional administrators on a dimin-
ishing budget: the populist suspension of consumption taxes during
the Riego rising had robbed the government of 88 per cent of its pro-
jected revenue for the ﬁrst half of 1820.34 Forced to make economies, the
government ordered the disbandment of Riego’s revolutionary ‘Army
of Andalucía’.35 But Riego’s army resisted dissolution, and forged a
personality cult around its leader. Born in 1784 in Asturias into a typ-
ically minor noble background, Riego was the epitome of the Spanish
careerist ofﬁcer bent on mastering the vicissitudes of politics to advance
30   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


his career. As his career had been doubly marginalised in 1814 due
to both his suspected Liberal sympathies and his lengthy captivity in
France, Riego was now psychologically incapable of merely settling for
the promotion to general which he had gained for reasons of politi-
cal expediency during the Andalucían rising.36 In fact, in September he
entered Madrid amidst a wave of agitation stoked up by the Patriotic
Societies, whose members hoped that the liberator Riego might offer
a better ladder for gaining ofﬁce. Yet September 1820 was no Spanish
Brumaire. Riego was bought off with the Captaincy-General of Galicia
(followed early in 1821 by that of Zaragoza), and his men were sent
home to their villages. These men had hoped to gain from a September
1820 Cortes decree awarding parcels of land to revolutionary veterans,
but local authorities dragged their feet on this measure now that Riego
had been neutralised.37
   This particular wedge driven between the army elite and the rank and
ﬁle was masked by a humiliation of the throne and altar which appeased
Radical-Liberal opinion and added insult to the injury of Ferdinand’s
royal instincts. In the November 1820 ‘Carvajal affair’, the royal will
came second to the oratorical prowess of the most prominent Liberal in
the Cortes, the ‘divine’ Agustín de Argüelles, author of the Constitution
of Cádiz. Like all Liberals, Argüelles wanted to advance the regalism of
the eighteenth century by subordinating Spain’s traditionally powerful
Catholic Church to state power. Whilst parish priests would be left in
their posts and even paid stipends from the public purse, the cloistered
clergy should be defrocked, put to useful work, and their substantial
lands sold off for proﬁtable and productive uses. Thus, when el divino
threatened to raise the streets of the capital if the King did not sign a bill
to cut the cloistered clergy, Ferdinand ominously left the capital for the
sanctuary of El Escorial, where the clerical party of his younger brother,
Don Carlos, was known to hold sway. Having failed to rally support
there, however, the King had no option but to return to Madrid and
sign the bill into law. Now a virtual prisoner of the Constitution, things
took a turn for the worse for the King as some ‘hotheads’ (exaltados), or
‘men of 1820’ (so-called for their desire to make more radical reforms
than the ‘men of 1812’), triumphantly joined the government.38
   Yet this Liberal bipartisanship did not portend stability. The army lost
its revolutionary unity as Riego and his clique gradually dominated the
levers of power whilst the rank and ﬁle went unpaid and unshod. Just
as the Peninsular War had shown (and as the Carlist War would show)
army ofﬁcers tended to see politics in narrow career terms and their
men as pawns to this end; purely military considerations came second.
                                            The First Carlist War: Origins   31


As one historian put it with perspicacity, ‘Men who did not want to serve
were led by men who did not know how to serve.’39 And as the Riego
revolution had burdened the state with an excess of political ofﬁcers,
these potentially dangerous men had to be bought off with appoint-
ments which the Constitution had designed for civilians. Much of the
blame for this situation should be laid at the polarisation of Liberal pol-
itics. Certainly, Spanish liberalism was steeped in generational conﬂict,
young Radicals resenting the stranglehold of old conservatives. The men
of 1820 grew so embittered at the older Liberal generation’s stranglehold
on public ofﬁces that they forged pseudo-Republican Comunero clubs
which promoted increasingly violent attacks on the Church and monar-
chy in a bid to win power. The Left-Liberal challenge to the men of 1812
(moderados) in turn divided as the 1820 generation was caught between
the rock of moderado and court disapproval and the hard place of Radical
pressure.40 Anguished by the collapse in April 1821 of a sister revolution
in Italy, the Comuneros launched a press campaign to uncover absolutist
sympathisers and rebellious priests.41 Rightly fearing the growing hos-
tility of the Holy Alliance, the Comuneros also tried to safeguard the
Spanish revolution by internationalising it. They welcomed Italian émi-
grés (the carbonari) into their lodges and militia battalions, whilst one
Cortes deputy, Zorraquín, ‘the foremost revolutionary in Spain’, estab-
lished correspondence clubs in Gerona and Catalan frontier towns with
the aim of enlisting public support for Liberal Spain in the southern
départments of France.42 When, during September 1821, Riego compro-
mised himself in a plot forged by a group of Spanish and international
Republicans, the moderate-Liberal (moderado) government dismissed
him from the Captaincy-General of Aragón.43
   Meanwhile, government attempts to remedy state ﬁnances fell hard-
est on the poor. The unpopular consumption taxes (derechos de puertas)
were re-established. Even though the Argüelles law began evicting
monks and nuns and bringing their property to market, it was the
highest bidder, not the landless labourer, sharecropper, tenant farmer
or demobilised soldier who got possession of the nationalised property.
The rural masses might yet have been appeased by the early 1821 Cortes
decree that tithes for the parish clergy would henceforth be halved.
But the half-tithe was accompanied by other taxes payable in cash, a
massive burden for a rural population still largely unaccustomed to a
cash economy, which, most devastatingly, came at a time of slump in
prices for agricultural goods.44 In fact, at least some foreign observers
singled out the Liberals’ formalisation of cash taxation as the origin
of the collapse of the constitutional regime.45 Even though this policy
32   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


was accompanied by the renewal of the 1811 Cortes decree which abol-
ished feudal jurisdiction in the countryside, in theory obliging manorial
lords to provide legal proofs of landownership and the right to receive
rents from villagers, these crucial conditions were blocked by royal veto,
whilst only a minority of Cortes deputies like Romero Alpuente and
Alvaro Flórez Estrada pressed the matter, seeing reparto as a way of mak-
ing the countryside constitutionalist instead of royalist. When the royal
veto was ﬁnally overcome in May 1823, this was done merely as a for-
lorn attempt to win over the countryside from royalism. The Liberals’
failure to enlist much support beyond the urban clubs and militia does
not mean that the rural masses were apolitical. Attempts by the Liberal
bourgeoisie to advance capitalism were met by a recognisably modern
defence of traditional work practices. Alcoy was the textile capital of
the old Kingdom of Valencia, employing some 40,000 rural outwork-
ers. Thanks to the recent abolition of guilds, textile entrepreneurs were
able to introduce self-acting lathes (selfactinas) as means of employing
fewer workers more productively. The workers’ response was decisive:
some 1,200 of them overwhelmed the local militia, helped themselves to
arms and seized control of the town for four days whilst they painstak-
ingly dismantled all the selfactinas.46 Although the Argüellian Liberals
in the Cortes condemned these attacks on the sacred right of prop-
erty, brushing aside Romero Alpuente’s defence of the workers, similar
episodes happened in centres of Catalan proto-industry amidst the
French invasion of 1823.47
   As rural Spain moved from indifference to outright hostility to con-
stitutional rule, it was clear that it was royalism rather than liberalism
which enjoyed the initiative. This is a matter which divides historians.
Leftist historians hold that the 1820–23 revolution failed because it was
betrayed by its own leaders, the wealthy landowners and bourgeoisie
having hijacked the democratisation process inherent in the Constitu-
tion of Cádiz and steered the ship of state back towards absolutism.48
Rightist historians hold that if the masses remained royalist in 1820,
they were even more fervently so in 1823, as the King abandoned all
remaining pretences of accepting the revolution.49 Since the 1990s other
writers have tried to rescue Spanish history from its ideological parti-
sanship by offering regional, comparative and international studies of
the Triennium in a bid to Europeanise Spain.50 The exaltados certainly
went to great lengths in their attempts to embed the revolution in the
masses. Yet most Liberals, even radical ones, shared a superior disdain
for common Spaniards; and time and again they failed to comprehend
the degree to which they were hated in return. Romero Alpuente had
                                            The First Carlist War: Origins   33


co-founded the Radical Comuneros Society, and dominated the impor-
tant 1822 Landaburiana Patriotic Society. But even though both of these
clubs presided over sophisticated and virtually democratic networks
of participation and reward for the urban popular classes, they were
late in addressing the social crisis, and their leadership failed to rally
to match the counter-revolution of 1823.51 Royalist resistance to Lib-
eral rule in the countryside, by contrast, had been coterminous with
the Liberal revolution itself. There were 122 local revolts against Lib-
eral rule between 1820 and 1823.52 Royalist resistance was explained
by Spain’s geographical, social and economic diversity. As would be
seen during the Carlist War, the heartlands of anti-Liberal revolt were
to be found in the countryside rather than the towns, and, broadly,
in increasing proportion the further north and north-east the consti-
tutional authorities tried to impose their regime. The cradle of royalist
resistance was in upland Navarra and the villages and small towns of
the neighbouring Basque provinces, although resistance to the Liberals
also spread to inland Catalonia, Aragón, Galicia and Old Castile. By the
summer of 1823 even Andalucía, New Castile, Extremadura, Murcia
and the Valencian-Catalan coastline – all areas where commercial and
landowning oligarchies had vested interests in making liberalism work –
witnessed violent popular royalism following close on the heels of the
invading French army.
   By the summer of 1822 formal civil war raged in Spain between Lib-
erals and royalists. This ﬁrst modern civil war in Spanish history was
a geographical dress rehearsal for the Carlist War.53 Until the summer
of 1822 moderado-dominated governments had shown restraint towards
royalism in the countryside, releasing scarce monies to fund public
works and buy foodstuffs in the provinces, understanding that hard-
ship was driving peasants affected by necessary Liberal reforms into the
arms of rebellious priests and royalist guerrillas.54 Meanwhile since the
Carvajal affair the King had seemed safely under the Liberals’ thumb,
out of reach of the royalists acting in his name. King Ferdinand was an
inviolable prisoner of the Constitution, relegated like Louis XVI before
him to saying all the right things in public whilst conﬁning his real
thoughts to diplomatic correspondence. Don Carlos and his restive cler-
ical faction, meanwhile, would not move openly against his brother’s
ofﬁcial display of constitutional monarchy. But on 7 July 1822 a royalist
coup attempt in the heart of Madrid transformed the situation. The
Royal Guard rising (which had been ﬁnanced by the French embassy)
was quashed, the defeated royalists taking to the hills to wage guer-
rilla war on a government which, after July, was in the hands of the
34   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Radical Left, or ﬂeeing to France whence many would return the follow-
ing year under French arms.55 The judicial execution of General Elío,
Captain-General of Valencia, who had captained the absolutist coup in
May 1814 and hanged Vidal in 1819, united the new government in a
pact of blood whilst serving notice on those army ofﬁcers who lacked
ideological commitment to exaltismo that it was time to quit.56 Several
ofﬁcers, seeing that the national and international winds were turning
against the revolution, either followed the royalists into exile or defected
to the rebel regency which was proclaimed in Ferdinand’s name at La
Seo de Urgel in August 1822. In turn the Radical regime installed Riego’s
men in civilian governorships, and set about imposing a military dic-
tatorship which foreshadowed the most radical episodes of the Carlist
War a generation later. Although Romero Alpuente’s Comuneros were
far-sighted enough to use this crisis to parcel out some marginal lands
in the antediluvian south, most exaltados did not think beyond using
state terror against royalist villagers.57 The government of Riego’s right-
hand man, Evaristo San Miguel, imposed crippling cash ﬁnes on villages
which failed to resist royalist bands, or even simply failed to show ‘due
respect towards constitutional forces’.58 At the local level, priests and
absolutists were ﬁned, imprisoned, expelled, even killed. Espoz y Mina,
whom the revolution had drawn from his French exile, led a reign of ter-
ror against royalist villagers in the north, destroying the Catalan town
of Castellfullit.59
   If the constitutional army had managed, by the end of 1822, to
crush royalist bands, whether it would stand up to Congress Europe was
another matter. In October 1822, the Great Powers met at Verona to dis-
cuss intervention against Jacobinism in Spain. In the same month the
San Miguel administration formally militarised Liberal society by impos-
ing martial law on the National Militia and calling up some 67,000
men into the army.60 For a generation afterwards the men of 1812
remembered these actions as proof that the exaltados were the ‘real
absolutists’.61 But foreign diplomatic pressure only increased in response
to these measures, which emboldened the timid King to sabotage the
work of his Radical ministers. In February 1823 Ferdinand, seeing that
San Miguel had split the exaltados, exercised one of his remaining pre-
rogatives by dismissing him, surviving the inevitable mob invading the
palace shouting ‘Death to the king!’62 The Radicals retaliated by derogat-
ing the King’s remaining powers, pending the ‘recovery of his health’.
Meanwhile, the Congress of Verona, brushing aside Britain’s absten-
tion, ﬁnally invaded. Louis XVIII was eager both to lance a new boil
of Jacobinism and to revive French prestige in Europe. He sent some
                                            The First Carlist War: Origins   35


into Spain 56,000 French troops, accompanied by some 35,000 Spanish
royalists, with the mission to restore Ferdinand to full powers.63 The
invasion of the ‘One Hundred Thousand Sons of St. Louis’ was astound-
ingly successful: unlike Napoleon’s invasion in 1808, the French met
virtually no popular resistance. The networks of Spanish elites who had
mobilised the people in 1808 could not (or would not) do the same
in 1823. The French invasion even faced little sustained resistance by
either the army or the militia of the Liberal state. Had the army lead-
ership united against the invasion, such was the suffering of their men
that they would likely have turned on their ofﬁcers. The National Militia
either hid its guns, deserted or went openly royalist. Even the artillery
corps, a socially mobile and exclusive part of the army noted for its
liberalism, preferred to spike its guns rather than ﬁre on the French.64
The breach caused by factionalism, an unfair and political promotion
system, and the inability of the constitutional regime to pay or even
clothe its soldiers, had thus rendered the army unﬁt to face a profes-
sional invasion force in 1823 – all this despite the fact that some 88 per
cent of taxes received by the Treasury in February 1822 went to fund
the military.65 By the end of August Cádiz was once again under French
siege, and only some three weeks later this cradle of the Constitution
fell, the Liberals releasing Ferdinand from captivity in the vain hope
that the French might prevail upon him to issue an amnesty.
3
The First Carlist War: Context




The defeat of what historians call the Liberal Triennium strengthened
Spanish royalism, although not in the way that Don Carlos’s faction
demanded. In order to trace how the ‘Carlist’ movement campaigned
not just against the vanquished revolutionaries, but also against the
Ferdinand’s restored absolutism, we must ﬁrst explain how the social cri-
sis undid constitutionalism and bequeathed the conditions for renewed
civil war. The reasons for the constitutional collapse are threefold. The
regime had made itself unpopular with its repeated drive for taxes, con-
scription and the liberal property revolution, which, owing to both elite
disdain for the character and capabilities of the masses and the constant
economic crisis, had generally beneﬁted elite interests at the expense of
commoners. Thus, the Constitution was discredited, and the counter-
revolution vengefully triumphant. A royalist ofﬁcer in Córdoba pro-
claimed: ‘the whole nation has been subverted by secret societies which
have foisted upon us the foul Constitution of 1812, which, in the three
years of its existence, has produced evils which will take three cen-
turies to resolve’.1 This was good propaganda, but the terror royalists
unleashed against local Liberals was even more impressive.2 The other
reasons lay with the Spanish army, where divisions and political unrelia-
bility stretched from top to bottom. Whereas some ofﬁcers (like Manuel
Llauder) had remained royalist throughout the Triennium, many more
(like Vicente Quesada) opportunistically defected to royalism during
1822–23. As the Liberal poet, Manuel José Quintana, who had written
that ‘kings are for the people, not people for the kings’, would soon
note from the cruelty of his internal exile, these latter men vainly hoped
that by changing sides they might retain the careers and honours they
had obtained under the Constitution.3 Other ofﬁcers thought that more

                                   36
                                           The First Carlist War: Context 37


might be gained by defending the revolution. General Zorraquín, the
international revolutionary, died in a skirmish with French troops near
Vic on 26 May 1823.4 The tragically miscast hero of the revolution,
Rafael del Riego, was captured and executed, although not before he
became a left-wing martyr in the process. Corralled by the moderados,
Riego had returned to Madrid in August 1822 as exaltado President
of the Cortes. Having lacked the time and means to gain the respect
of his fellow ofﬁcers, Riego could not but divide army opinion, and
ended up being more admired by civilian Radicals than anyone else.
Other army Liberals played a more ruthless game. Espoz was involved
in skirmishes enough to save his honour, and then led his men into
French captivity, complaining without irony that although the Consti-
tution was great, it was detested by the Spanish people.5 A refugee in
Plymouth, Espoz became a Garibaldesque international celebrity avant
la lettre, local crowds ﬂocking to his public appearances and obliging
the jaded veteran to travel incognito to London; Riego, by contrast, was
publicly garrotted.6 During the decade of reaction that was to follow,
Espoz in exile would reinvent himself as a Radical ‘Great Dictator’, mix-
ing Benthamite with Bonapartist principles, and maintaining an iron
grip on the Spanish Liberals in ﬁrst Britain and then France.7
   But the constitutional collapse of 1823 did not produce the ascen-
dancy of the royalist faction surrounding Don Carlos. It had been
vanquished by Espoz, and remaining royalists who rallied in 1823
necessarily fought as auxiliaries to the French and thus lacked the
independent gravity to sway Ferdinand. What the ‘Carlists’ wanted
(the term ﬁrst appears in ofﬁcial correspondence in 1824) was not the
sort of modern absolute police state which Ferdinand favoured and
which had been common throughout Metternich’s Europe since 1815,
but a full-blooded reactionary policy that would restore the privileged
positions of the Church and nobility, decentralise legislative and juris-
dictional powers, and relegate the monarchy to a medieval coordinating
role. In other words, the Carlists wanted to revise not just the recent
Liberal revolution but the entire eighteenth-century legacy of enlight-
ened absolutism.8 These hopes were dashed during what was called the
‘Ominous Decade’ of 1823–33. The Carlist conception of society was
organic, and in the Basque provinces and Navarra in particular it spoke
out in defence of local traditions and privileges and against the cen-
tralising impulses of Madrid. The strongly Catholic, conservative and
economically stable countryside of upland Navarra made this the nat-
ural refuge for Carlist insurrection. Even so, Carlism during the 1830s
would spread to rural areas outside of its northern heartland. According
38    Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


to Charles Henningsen, a British volunteer in Don Carlos’s army who
later fought for the Confederacy, Spanish civil wars were essentially a
conﬂict between Liberal towns and royalist countryside, an observa-
tion shared a century later by the Hispanist, Gerald Brenan, for whom
‘Carlism (was) nothing else than the hostility of a sturdy race of moun-
taineers and farmers to industrial life.’9 In fact, as we shall see, the
urban versus rural divide was at least as much an effect as a cause of
the Carlist War (on account of the displacement of refugees and com-
batants). But certainly, historians have seen the land as the root of
popular fanaticism. Evaristo San Miguel, exiled in 1823 and returned
to Liberal politics during the 1830s civil war, thought that the spread
of Carlism to the eastern Spain could be arrested by equitable agricul-
tural reforms.10 Twentieth-century historians interpreted the endurance
of rural Carlism, not just during the 1830s war but also in a century
of subsequent conﬂagrations, as evidence that Spanish revolutionaries,
unlike their French counterparts, failed to win over popular support.11
   Some 24,000 Liberal families ﬂed Spain, and military tribunals pro-
cessed those who remained. By the time of their dissolution in August
1825, the tribunals had dealt with some 1,094 individuals, rather more
than half of these being charged with political crimes. Whilst around
three-quarters were condemned to punishments of varying severity,
including death (132), the rest were absolved.12 Former constitucional-
istas were condemned to banishment of a minimum of ﬁve leagues
from Madrid.13 The commercial elites of Málaga recorded the repression
unfolding around them:


     5 September 1823: Málaga has been occupied by French and absolutist
     troops who have decreed the establishment of the old system. The
     municipal staff have ﬂed, and the Constitution tablet has been
     smashed to pieces. Ofﬁcial celebrations have been proclaimed, and
     the pre-1820 municipal system reintroduced.
     27 October 1823: Orders have been issued to dismiss all teachers guilty
     of having taught the Constitution, whilst all National Militia arms
     have to be handed in within twenty-four hours.

     18 July 1824: 3 supporters of the Constitution have been executed for
     no other crime than having been found with several proclamations
     against the government.

     22 July 1824: General O’Lawlor has made important arrests of well-
     known malagueño Liberals.
                                            The First Carlist War: Context 39


   15 September 1824: 3 men have been sentenced to death and shot in
   Málaga: Lorenzo González (known as el Pato), Mateo de la Cruz and
   Antonio González; all for the ‘atrocious crime of having destroyed
   the royal tablet’ in the township of Benahavis.14

But even these draconian steps did not satisfy the Carlists. Despite
the execution of some high-proﬁle constitutionalists, and the venge-
ful imprisonment and internal exile of thousands who had not ﬂed,
political, ﬁnancial and diplomatic expediency persuaded Ferdinand to
relent. Although laws were passed making execution the penalty for
acts of ‘rebellion’ – laws which would be used by both sides in the
civil war after Ferdinand’s death – these could be implemented as much
against the Carlists as against the Liberals.15 Moreover, this crackdown
was to be enforced not by the Inquisition, as in 1814, but by Spain’s
ﬁrst centrally organised police force, which the Carlists condemned as
the ‘Frenchiﬁed’ takeover of Spain’s traditional character and an outra-
geous end for the Church’s temporal power, which was now reduced
to watered-down ‘faith councils’ (juntas de la fé).16 All these measures
conﬁrmed the worst fears of the royalists who were now referred to as
the ultras, apostólicos and Carlists. A token political amnesty as early
as 1824 provoked a royalist reaction in the abortive ‘Capapé affair’,
followed a year later by a more serious rising at Brihuega. When, in
1826, Ferdinand agreed to a more substantial amnesty aimed at former
moderados, royalist manifestoes denounced the King as a coward and a
disaster as ruler.17
   As with the Liberal ofﬁcers of 1814, part of this invective was
explained by frustrated careers. Reserve armies of royalist ‘heroes’ sought
ofﬁce and promotion but Ferdinand’s second Restoration found the state
coffers even emptier than in 1814. Once again, the state was virtually
bankrupt. Ferdinand’s default on the debts incurred under the Consti-
tution brought only temporary relief as this action barred Spain from
lucrative British debt markets. Ferdinand’s technocratic Finance Min-
ister, Luis López Ballesteros, managed to source new loans from Paris
and Amsterdam, but only under onerous conditions.18 Spanish America
remained free. Even though penny-packets of troops from Cuba and
San Juan de Ulúa continued to harass Mexico as late as 1829, any last
hopes for restoring metropolitan rule to the American mainland were
ended by the defeat of the last great imperial army at the Battle of
Ayacucho (Peru) in 1824. Moreover, even though Ferdinand ruthlessly
dispossessed Liberal ofﬁcers of their commissions, he did not replace
them with royalists. Rather, the regime maintained a French garrison
40   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


as a Varangian guard, and reduced the Spanish royalist army to a mere
skeleton. Even though the French carbonari had tried to sow discord
amongst the Duke of Angloulême’s troops before their 1823 invasion,
the French garrison – which did not withdraw until 1828 – proved a reli-
able tripwire against renewed revolution and could be counted upon to
stay out of Spanish domestic politics.19 Quite apart from the ire this pro-
voked amongst a population which, a generation earlier, had fought the
French, army elites bristled at the patronage diverted away from Spanish
pretendientes. In July 1824, General Quesada’s troops even skirmished
with the French garrison in Madrid.20 But during 1823, just as 1814, the
army had some grounds for optimism. Whereas constitutional regimes
had tried to subordinate the Spanish military to civilian authorities, only
tacitly accepting the abusive ‘fuero militar’ and grudgingly accepting
the rights of captains-general to declare states of siege, Ferdinand VII,
in 1824, opened up the chances for a military recolonisation of soci-
ety once more. The provincial assemblies and governorships instituted
under the Constitution were abolished, and the captains-general saw
their traditionally extensive police, judicial and administrative pow-
ers restored.21 But, in return, ofﬁcers’ commissions were squeezed and
a French garrison of occupation maintained from the public purse.
This meant that even during the Ominous Decade, around 50 per cent
of state expenditure still went on security, with Spanish forces com-
prising, by 1833, some 50,000 regular troops and 652 generals and
around 100,000 Royalist Volunteers (see below) according to an 1830
estimate.22
   And if army insiders had to endure Fernandine parsimony, things
were far worse for the thousands of ilimitados, cashiered ofﬁcers who
were forced to eke out whatever meagre living they could, sometimes as
‘professional subversives’.23 Several ilimitados, and many more deserters,
turned to brigandage. Brigandage has been asserted to be a ‘natural’ phe-
nomenon in geographically and economically marginal societies like
most of rural Spain.24 But during the Peninsular War it had become
conﬂated with patriotic resistance, and had thus been given political
legitimacy.25 Brigandage peaked in 1822 when unpopular conscrip-
tion and economic reforms drove thousands of men into the hills.
During 1822 two of the south’s most notorious bandits, ‘El Barbudo’
(the Bearded One) and ‘El Tempranillo’ tried to insure their careers by
asserting their royalism. It was a wise calculation, but it yielded very
different results. El Barbudo, a brigand at large in Alicante and Murcia
who had fought the French in the Peninsular War, was hunted down
and hanged.26 By contrast, El Tempranillo, a stronger presence in the
                                           The First Carlist War: Context 41


Andalucían Sierra Morena in command of a sophisticated extortion
network, was not only pardoned by Ferdinand but also given a for-
mal commission to punish other wrongdoers. El Tempranillo proudly
quipped ‘Ferdinand rules in Spain and I rule in the Sierra’; in 1833, at
the age of 27, he was killed by a Liberal bandit.27
   Wrongdoing was honoured by political ﬂags of convenience; during
the Carlist War banditry and Carlism would be, in many ways, indis-
tinguishable when operating behind the front lines. Spaniards since the
Peninsular War had been suffering a collective trauma which made them
materially and psychologically prepared to take to arms: this ‘reserve
army’ of demobilised soldiers, deserters, outraged priests and downtrod-
den peasants could become a potent weapon if they could be harnessed
to a political aim. At ﬁrst it seemed as though the restored absolutist
regime might channel this disaffection. As soon as the military tribunals
were wound up, the reactionary militia force of ‘Royalist Volunteers’
(Voluntarios Realistas), which had come together during the Royalist
War, was formalised by War Minister José de la Cruz, with a dedicated
budget independent of army funding, and no real ‘bottom limit’ on
recruitment. As Cruz remarked, this plebeian force would be instilled
with ‘elements of order and monarchical subordination’ in contrast to
the extinguished National Militia, which ‘seemed set on turning each
town into a state, and each town-hall into a government’.28 Rising in
number from 70,000 in 1825 to a peak of 284,000 in 1832, the Volun-
teers, however, proved to be no Sanfedisti. Like their National Militia
forbears the Volunteers enjoyed both immunity from civil prosecution
and cash remuneration when on service, and got to run all manner
of municipal rackets, whilst terrorising village Liberals. Thus, histori-
ans have labelled the Volunteers fanatical, violent and a ﬁnancial black
hole.29 Ferdinand hoped that they might please the royalists, thwart
the Liberals and check the ‘professional subversives’ in the diminished
regular army. Yet Ferdinand would not concede the Volunteers’ main
demand that they replace the army altogether. Nevertheless, the Volun-
teers blocked Liberal attempts to regain power after 1823. The Liberals’
attempts were organised by Espoz who, from his London exile, directed
doomed but often dramatic Liberal invasions. In 1826 the Bazán expe-
dition to Valencia, learning the lessons of the Royalist War, blanketed
villages with propaganda promising land.30 Liberalism was not just a
creed of desperate exiles. A few months after the Bazán expedition,
Don Carlos’s new bride, María Teresa, the ultra-conservative princess of
the miguelista side in the Portuguese civil war (1826–34), was publicly
insulted by a crowd shouting mueras whilst she toured Madrid.31
42   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


   Whilst Liberals attacked Fernandine absolutism from the Left, Carlists
did so from the Right. The rightists’ moment came in the so-called
‘War of the Aggrieved’ of 1826–27, a rising centred in rural Catalonia
(where the rebels were called Malcontents) which was Carlist in all but
name: quite literally, as the rising rallied to its ‘Manifesto of the Fed-
eration of Pure Royalists’ (Maniﬁesto de la Federación de Realistas Puros),
signed in Madrid in November 1826 by anonymous ‘Members of this
Catholic Federation, graced by Heaven and Eternal Blessing’ who explic-
itly called for the replacement of King Ferdinand by Don Carlos. The
authorship of this manifesto remains in doubt, but its spirit is clear. Its
demands for the re-establishment of the Inquisition, banishment of the
amnestied afrancesados, an end to technocratic reforms and the removal
of the constraints on the power of the Royalist Volunteers, all described
the demands of the most reactionary (apostólico, or theocratic) wing of
the Carlist insurrection of 1833.32 The Aggrieved were led by Agustín
Saperes and the smuggler, Josep Bosoms, both ‘heroes’ of 1822–23. Their
followers – whose numbers varied between 20,000 and 30,000 that
summer – comprised unemployed ofﬁcers and men, suborned Catalan
militia (known as somatenes) and Royalist Volunteers, villagers made
desperate by ongoing capitalist reforms in agriculture and several ide-
ologically committed ‘true believers’, most of whom were priests.33 The
Agraviados’ insurrection brieﬂy invaded the Basque provinces, the cradle
of the later Carlist revolt, seizing some Royalist Volunteers arms near
Vitoria.34
   Despite their manifesto, most of the Aggrieved claimed to be ‘lib-
erating’ Ferdinand VII from ‘evil counsellors’, the amnestied courtiers
of moderate-Liberal and afrancesado backgrounds, whilst at local level
manifestoes spoke against economic modernisation. Spain continued
the drive towards a single national market and a cash economy at a time
when crop prices were plummeting: from their peak level of 100 during
the famine year of 1812, agricultural prices plummeted to 63 in 1814,
49 in 1820, 45 in 1826 and to a low of 33 in 1830.35 Catalan parish
priests deﬁed the loyal Fernandine bishops in droves, drawing money
and even weapons from the restored monasteries.36 All bishops except
one stayed loyal for strategic reasons: the cloistered clergy had recovered
their numbers from a low during the Jacobinism of 1823 of 8,000 to a
ﬁgure which would climb to 61,272 by 1833.37 This ecclesiastical recov-
ery had been achieved by Ferdinand’s decision in 1823 to return to the
Church its lands which had been nationalised during the Triennium,
ruining local buyers who had bought into this Liberal revolution and
who got no compensation. (By contrast, notes issued during 1822–23
                                             The First Carlist War: Context 43


by royalist leaders for peasants’ supplies were paid. This made villagers
more willing to support royalist insurrection in 1827 and, especially, in
1833.38 ) But parish priests also justiﬁed their rebellion in spiritual rather
than strategic terms. Liberalism – or rather the progressive and episco-
palian Catholic current they called Jansenism which spawned Spain’s
ﬁrst Liberals – undermined those popular religious practices, such as the
idle saints’ days and the burial of dead inside churches, which deﬁned
cultural life at the local level.
   After a lacklustre start, the rebels made a capital out of Manresa,
whence a royalist newspaper brieﬂy appeared. The rebels took villages,
conveniently discovering amongst local notables ‘Liberals’ whom they
could ransom and rob.39 This conﬂict already showed signs of violent
actions against ‘spies’ and harbingers of ‘melancholic news’, which par-
ticularly damaged a society without railways and telegraphs (this feature
would also characterise the Carlist War).40 But the Agraviados’ domina-
tion of the Catalan countryside was short-lived. Ferdinand sent forth
an army which swept all before it, pacifying the whole region during
September 1827, executing dozens of rebels, and deporting to North
African garrison prisons around 300 combatants who refused the King’s
pardon. Saperes and Bosoms managed to ﬂee to France, although the
latter was lured back to the frontier in February 1828 and given up for
execution.41
   The rising had been launched in Ferdinand’s name, and Liberal histo-
rians have since been routinely perplexed by a legitimist rising against
the legitimate king. In 1823, after all, Ferdinand VII had been restored
to full powers, and wasted no time persecuting his Liberal tormentors
via his Justice Minister, the humble-born Francisco Calomarde – much
derided by contemporaries and historians alike as a base upstart – who
attacked not just the Liberals but also their values when he replaced
‘liberal’ science teaching in colleges and universities with Counter-
Enlightenment theology and bull-ﬁghting.42 But otherwise Ferdinand
would not share power. Rather, through a closed camarilla of favourites,
the King timidly pursued a policy of modernisation which gradually
adopted several of the more conservative, technocratic reforms of the
vanquished Liberals. Thus, all other noble, clerical and popular inter-
ests were to be subordinated to his will, Ferdinand seeing ﬁt, like any
eighteenth-century absolutist before him, to govern in the interests
of the people without having to be accountable to them in any way.
Ferdinand’s gambit reveals that there were not the ‘two Spains’ of pop-
ular legend, but at least three. Whereas the Liberals were certainly the
progressive Left, in that they wanted to advance the eighteenth-century
44   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


enlightened absolutist reforms into the modern realm of national
sovereignty, constitutional monarchy and individual (rather than cor-
porate) rights of property, the Right was divided in two ways. The
absolutist wing, personiﬁed in Ferdinand VII, understood ‘absolutism’
to rest on purely in the powers of the king. The traditionalist, or legit-
imist, wing of the Right, however, wanted to strip the monarch of all the
Bourbon reforms which, since the eighteenth century, had so enhanced
his power, passing onto the Church, nobility and other corporate bod-
ies the ‘absolute’ right to enjoy their traditional privileges.43 In the rural
Basque provinces and especially Navarra, where the fueros were resilient,
the Church popular and arms never more than a few yards away, this
Carlist current could raise an insurrection.44
   Luckily for Ferdinand’s regime, the failure of the Aggrieved meant
that the Carlists, for the time being, were a spent force. Don Carlos
remained loyal in deed if not in conscience.45 Portuguese miguelistas
tried to circulate proclamations aimed at reigniting a fraternal rising
in Spain, but postmasters in Badajoz intercepted these with apparent
ease.46 For the Liberals in exile, however, the failure of the Aggrieved
transformed their invasion schemes from spontaneous ‘heroic gestures’
into properly organised affairs.47 But the Fernandine regime also organ-
ised its repression, at both home and abroad. When in 1828 some
Burgos printers produced the inﬂammatory ‘Tears of Mother Spain’,
a thesis which condemned the entire history of Bourbon absolutism,
and demanded that the ‘deaf king’ reconvene the Cortes (‘for Spaniards
saved the nation on the 2 May 1808’), the government response was
ruthless. The writer and his printers were all gaoled, although not before
several copies of the thesis had circulated.48 Abroad, the Spanish gov-
ernment exploited exiles’ penury to inﬁltrate their networks. Romero
Alpuente was so short of cash that he wittingly maintained a spy for the
Spanish regime as his manservant and tried in vain to extract a subsidy
from Prime Minister Wellington, a man whom, in a previous incarna-
tion, he had excoriated.49 In July 1829, Spain persuaded the Wellington
administration to scrap the exiles’ subsidy.50 This was the nadir of Lib-
eral fortunes. But in 1830, the international situation changed in the
exiles’ favour and against the Carlists. In Britain a Whig ministry came
to power and boosted the long-standing links its Radical wing had cul-
tivated with Spanish Liberal exiles.51 More importantly, in France, the
Orleanists came to power in the Revolution of 1830.
   The centre of Spanish Liberal operations thus shifted to France,
where the revolution of July 1830 initially portended better prospects
of success. In October, Espoz sent one invasion across the Pyrenees in
                                           The First Carlist War: Context 45


coordination with a 400-strong invasion of assorted émigrés and adven-
turers led by the veteran of the lost American wars, General Jerónimo
Valdés, who had resisted coming under Espoz’s direct command. Espoz’s
propaganda appealed to Spaniards to ‘forget the past’, a shrewd choice
of words which might relate either to absolutism or to the disastrous
effects of Liberal rule during the Triennium. But they were defeated by
a 7,000-strong government force under General Llauder. Valdés’ force
was defeated at Vera, and only the swift intervention of Espoz extri-
cated the survivors and brought them back across the frontier.52 Despite
Espoz’s show of contrition, the civilian population did not rally to the
Liberal invasion, and it was small consolation that the Carlists failed
to answer with a rising of their own. But the Carlists were not demor-
alised. Don Carlos was still the heir to the throne and victorious General
Llauder accordingly ingratiated himself with the heir apparent.53 But
the changed international context belied Carlist conﬁdence. Although
Madrid got Paris to seal the border to Spanish émigrés, the Liberal French
regime received Spanish diplomatic recognition in return.54 Moreover,
the Whig administration in Britain, Belgian revolution, Polish rising
and the resilient pedrista side in the Portuguese civil war, combined
with the French Revolution, all had the hallmarks of an emerging
‘Liberal international’ which might sway Spanish affairs. In November
1831, an international force of idealists, led by the Spanish Liberals
Torrijos and Manzanares, invaded southern Spain in a Mazzinian-style
gesture of ‘spontaneous revolution’.55 This attempt also failed, but in
circumstances which made martyrs out of the invaders.
   Despite – or perhaps because of – the changed international scene,
Carlist conspiracies mounted during 1832–33. As Ferdinand’s health
deteriorated, a series of court manoeuvres contrived to put the crown
beyond Don Carlos’s reach. This scenario was caused by the ‘La Granja
affair’ of 1832, a palace revolution which conﬁrmed that María Cristina
would succeed King Ferdinand as regent until their daughter, Princess
Isabella, came of age. Thus, Don Carlos was excluded from what he saw
as his God-given duty to succeed. With the moribund King handing
over the running of the monarchy to his reformist wife, a crackdown on
Carlists ensued. The Royalist Volunteers were opened up to recruits of
non-royalist backgrounds and state power centralised over them. A tech-
nocratic Ministry of Development (an Interior Ministry by another
name) was created with wide-ranging powers against regional Carlists,
and this was reinforced by the 1833 territorial reorganisation of Spain
into 49 similarly sized provinces.56 Manuel Llauder, who so recently
curried favour with Carlists, now established a military dictatorship
46   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


against them. Other Carlist sympathisers who escaped the attention of
the Cristino military, were nonetheless driven to insurrection by eco-
nomic insecurity.57 Despite the government purge, it was clear that the
Carlist faction could not be brought to heel. Rather, the purge forced
the Carlists to contest the Cristino succession from the country rather
than the court. Staring into the abyss, the now uniformly Cristino court
faction tried for a ﬁnal time to persuade Don Carlos to renounce his
claim, a courtier asking him plainly: ‘Do you want a civil war?’58 Don
Carlos replied that it was the Cristino faction which wanted war by
illegitimately revoking his God-given duty to rule. Don Carlos’s hand
was then forced when one of the conspiracies (centred on the Bishop
of León) turned into an abortive rising launched in the prince’s name.
Don Carlos ﬂed to Portugal, where he raised his standard amidst friendly
miguelistas. Exasperated by the news that Carlos would not recognise
the Isabeline succession, the dying King ordered him exiled to Italy.
Carlos prevaricated and reopened the miguelista-agraviado conspiratorial
network which had been dormant since 1827, whilst at the same time
making sure he did not risk re-entering Spain until Fernando was dead
and enough armed Carlist support was forthcoming.59 For his part, the
bishop had his assets seized and was placed in solitary conﬁnement, but
escaped to join his King in Portugal after Ferdinand’s death.
   The Pretender’s ﬂight galvanised rather than stymied Carlist conspir-
acies. Arms were forged in convents and monasteries, illicit deliveries
took place between Portuguese miguelistas and Carlists in northern
Spain, whilst even a prospective ‘pan-legitimist’ uprising planned to
link Marseilles and southern France with northern Spain on the occa-
sion of Ferdinand’s death. Whilst Llauder purged Carlists from the army,
Prime Minister Cea Bermúdez, by contrast, pursued a juste milieu policy,
hoping, like any enlightened technocrat, that the ‘nonsense’ of Carlist
versus Liberal violence would cancel itself out.60 Whilst Llauder’s mil-
itary dictatorship was allowed to run its course, Cea hoped that his
crash reforms in the way the state was organised would suffocate the
warring factions ‘from above’. Cea’s civilian absolutism thus rivalled
the regional dictatorships of the Cristino generals. The February and
November 1833 municipal reforms were carried out by force, reforming
town halls to ensure that they would be under the sway neither of tax-
payers as during the Triennium, nor of local Royalist (Carlist) hierarchies
as during the Ominous Decade. Henceforth, town halls would represent
only ‘respectable’ local interests. (The latter were to be elected only on a
highly restrictive franchise: masters of guilds and members of other cor-
porations became local electors along with an equal number of those
                                           The First Carlist War: Context 47


who paid the highest rates of taxation.61 ) Cea’s moderate reformism
might have worked a decade earlier, but now it was largely irrelevant.
Carlists and Liberals were locked into a ﬁght to the death which was
not just played out on the streets but also at the highest levels of the
state. For Carlists, Cea was a servant of usurpation, and the thin end of
the wedge for godless revolution, and for Liberals, his reforms – and the
associated Royal Statute (see below) – did not go far enough. When Cea,
on 4 November 1833, proclaimed that ‘the principles of government
observed by the late Ferdinand VII will never be changed’, he showed
his fundamental misunderstanding of both sides.62 This popular ten-
sion was described in an 1833 literary journal by the young Romantic
and pessimist, Mariano José de Larra:

  In our vulgar language we are experiencing phrases which have come
  of age and are being spread throughout a whole nation. In political
  vocabulary especially it takes only one word to fall from the lips of
  an orator in his small circle, and a great number of people, anxious
  for words, pick it up, spread it by word of mouth, and with the speed
  of an electric bolt a larger number of living machines repeat and con-
  secrate it, most times without understanding it [Larra’s emphasis], and
  always failing to calculate that it sometimes only takes one word to
  raise a mob, inﬂame passions, and so cause a revolution.63

Meanwhile, Cea’s purging of political factionalism in the army reduced
this force by the time of Ferdinand’s death to the toothless size of
45,000 men. This was certainly too small for a military coup to seize
the state, which was his intention: but it was also too small to crush
the Carlist insurgency that grew in the north upon word of the King’s
death. By 1837, the Cristino army would grow to over 200,000 effec-
tives; but the early months of the civil war would prove the error in
Cea’s strategy.64
4
The Basque Phase, 1833–35




        I believe in the power of Queen Isabella II, creator of Spanish
        felicity, and in María Cristina her mother, our Queen-Regent,
        who gave birth to Ferdinand VII’s daughter in order to con-
        sole us and to condemn the friars. I believe that the mother
        and daughter suffered under the infernal government of
        Calomarde, that the mother was infamously oppressed and her
        honour outraged, that she descended to the dungeons to save
        faithful liberals . . . I believe in pardoning those who have been
        led astray, in the resurrection of our National Congress, in our
        political regeneration.1

This chapter narrates and explains the First Carlist War from the start
of the insurgency in October 1833 to the start of the siege of Bilbao in
May 1835. It shows how the Carlists mobilised the charismatic leader-
ship of Tomás de Zumalacárregui and propitious socio-political factors
in the Basque country to create a Carlist state which expanded in spite of
increasingly desperate Cristino military attempts to vanquish it. It also
introduces the signiﬁcance of Carlist insurgencies in the Maestrazgo and
Catalonia, although these are explored in greater depth in subsequent
chapters. It explains how the cumulative Cristino defeats on the bat-
tleﬁeld were both the cause and consequence of political revolution.
The popular experience of the Cristino war effort created a ‘commu-
nity of sacriﬁce’ amongst both soldiers and militiamen, who gave this
radical political expression through mutiny, defection, atrocities and a
new popular aesthetic of heroic martyrdom. Finally, this chapter also
introduces the fraught international context of this civil war.
   Ferdinand’s death in October 1833 transformed Carlist violence into
formal civil war. Technically the ﬁrst Carlist rising began not in the

                                   48
                                              The Basque Phase, 1833–35   49


Basque country but in Talavera de la Reina (New Castile), an isolated
and ill-fated attempt by a local postmaster in an episode celebrated
by Carlists as evidence that their cause was nationwide, voluntary
and popular.2 This case would be stronger if there were an even pat-
tern of civilian risings, or civilian support for Volunteers, across Spain
matching the activity in the Basque country. Yet this was not the case.
An attempted coup by the Royalist Volunteers at the seat of power
in Madrid failed on the ﬁrst day when the urban masses failed to
rally and the Cristino army seized control of the Volunteers’ artillery.3
Barcelona’s Carlist-ridden Royalist Volunteers were disarmed without
much violence.4 Oviedo’s Volunteers were disarmed during Mass by
troops who seized the weapons the Volunteers had piously left out-
side. Inchoate risings elsewhere did not prevail and failed to match the
conditions in the Basque country.
   But even here the rising had only mixed success. Navarra, where
Carlism was strongest, was actually the slowest to rise, ironically because
popular royalism had always been so dominant here that the local
Royalist Volunteers were barely organised to begin with. The Navarrese
veteran of the Peninsular and Royalist Wars, Santos Ladrón de Cegama,
had ﬁrst to raise Carlists in nearby Logroño before striking north. His
capture and execution on 11 October backﬁred on the Cristinos, as hun-
dreds of Navarrese peasants mobilised to avenge this popular royalist.
Meanwhile, the Deputation of Navarra was caught between the Carlist
sympathies of its members and countrymen and the reality of Cristino
military control.5 It prevaricated by refusing to swear allegiance to the
Cristino monarchy until María Cristina had ﬁrst sworn to observe the
fueros, as was indeed constitutionally proper, but which, more impor-
tantly, reﬂected Realpolitik.6 In the rest of the Basque country, the
greater preparedness of the Volunteers gave local Carlists little difﬁ-
culty in mobilising armed forces, most dramatically in Vizcaya, where
the Royalist Volunteers answered the calls of Carlist insurrectionists and
invaded Bilbao, levying punitive taxes on known Liberals. Six weeks’
‘curfew for the rich’ ensued until Cristino troops reoccupied the city on
25 November, after which Bilbao would become a symbol of the Cristino
and Liberal cause, resisting repeated sieges.7 But at the time, this future
was far from certain, and Bilbao’s civil authorities had accordingly tried
to maintain as ‘neutral’ a posture as possible during the Carlist occupa-
tion, keeping few and ‘antiseptic’ minutes of meetings, whilst prudently
funding the Volunteers’ wages at the same time and even arranging
for the chamber of commerce to ‘lend’ the Carlists a cheap loan of
1.8 million reales repayable at 3 per cent interest.8 When the Cristinos
50   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


liberated the city, they exposed this embarrassing accommodation, for
which the Madrid government made the commander of the Cristino
liberation force, General Sarsﬁeld, a boisterous Peninsular War veteran,
responsible, rather than the local authorities. Sarsﬁeld was removed by
the Cristino regime, which had had cause on other occasions to suspect
his loyalty.9 The early loss of Bilbao was a blow for the cause of Carlism,
as most of the Vizcayan volunteers deserted when Bilbao ceased funding
their pay, and would not return to ﬁght until Carlist territorial expan-
sion throughout 1834 subjected them to compulsory enlistment.10 The
burghers of Bilbao, for their part, stiffened their resolve against future
Carlist attempts.
   Risings elsewhere in the Basque country and beyond hardly com-
pensated for this loss. Royalist War veteran Valentín Verástegui raised
a Carlist insurrection in Alava, named himself commander-in-chief,
and invaded Alava’s capital of Vitoria. Here, foral authorities, as at
Bilbao, tried ‘neutrality’, deﬂecting Verástegui’s overtures. Verástegui
prevaricated and set up a rival junta in the name of Carlos V, an omi-
nous move which could only point to the eventual suppression of the
civil authorities.11 But the foral authorities persevered until Sarsﬁeld’s
Cristino division on its way to Bilbao defeated Verástegui’s insurgents
and liberated Vitoria. Unlike Santos Ladrón, Verástegui survived to ﬁght
another day. Looking beyond the Basque country, the internal threat
to Madrid had been quashed, but there remained the prospect of a
descent on the capital from without. The threat came from the aged
priest, Jerónimo Merino, famous guerrilla veteran of the Peninsular and
Royalist Wars and the obvious choice to command the forces of the
Carlist junta of Old Castile which convened in Merino’s old ‘ﬁefdom’,
the heavily churched province of Burgos. Merino’s conservatism posited
the Cristinos as dangerous revolutionaries: ‘Do you believe that the
declared enemies of Ferdinand VII, those who dragged him captive to
Cádiz, and who proscribed faithful royalists, can in any way be trusted
to defend our monarchical institutions and religion?’12 This proclama-
tion appealed to several thousand more in his path during his march
on Madrid, who remembered the disappointment of Liberal rule during
the Triennium and the opportunities offered by royalism in 1823. Some
even included disbanded royalists ﬂeeing from the Cristino capital. But
Merino was swamped with men he was unable to arm or provision,
and soon had to release the married volunteers from his service. Mean-
while, the Carlist junta near Burgos lost conﬁdence in his campaign and
ordered Merino to return to defend Carlism in La Rioja against Sarsﬁeld’s
counter-insurgency. Thus, the ﬁrst ‘March on Madrid’ in the conﬂict
                                              The Basque Phase, 1833–35   51


proved abortive. But its impact was felt inside the capital, where the new
Liberal press decried the subversive opinions and violence of disbanded
Royalist Volunteers, and demanded that the Liberals’ militia of 1820–23
be resurrected.13 Even though fears persisted of a Carlist ‘ﬁfth column’ in
the capital, the immediate danger to Madrid was over. General Sarsﬁeld
marched east from the Portuguese frontier, pursuing Merino’s retreat to
Salamanca and thence to Burgos, near which he clashed with Merino
in an inconclusive foggy battle on 13 November 1833.14 Merino rallied
in northern Castile, reinforced by Colonel Cuevillas, purged Military
Governor of Zaragoza who had raised a guerrilla force in La Rioja. But
Verástegui refused to send him any reinforcements from Alava, and a
Cristino force soon put Merino’s men to ﬂight, leaving the priest with
only Cuevillas and a dozen other followers, little better than fugitives.
Merino and Cuevillas now ﬂed west towards Portugal, avoiding Cristino
security patrols and escaping from a number of ambushes. At the end of
1833, Merino ﬁnally reached northern Portugal and the relative safety
of Don Carlos’s fugitive court and friendly miguelistas.15
   The Carlist insurgency obliged María Cristina to look for political
support amongst the exiled Spanish Left. On 23 October 1833, despite
Cea’s objections, a near-total amnesty was proclaimed for exiled Lib-
erals. Meanwhile, army ofﬁcers whose careers had survived the purges
were rewarded with powers to control, and disband where necessary,
the Royalist Volunteers. At ﬁrst these measures, along with initially suc-
cessful counter-insurgency operations and the onset of the northern
winter, promised an early peace. However, the Carlists had merely dis-
persed – ‘changed their shirts’ – and would return to ﬁght again.16 Given
that the number of rural insurgents was potentially limitless, and given
that the Cristino army was still too small, no end to the conﬂict was
in sight in 1833. A Cristino cordon sanitaire around the Basque coun-
try was spread too thinly and sapped reserves which might have dealt
a decisive counter-insurgency blow. Meanwhile, the process of purg-
ing the Carlist-ridden Volunteers was still not complete. At one end of
Spain, the weeks after the start of the war saw Liberal Cádiz reinforce
its Volunteers against any Carlist attempts, whilst at the other end the
Royalist Volunteers had either gone over to the insurgency or were held
in abeyance by Cristino captains-general reluctant to call them out in
a poisoned and uncertain political climate.17 Into this vacuum stepped
the Cristino army, but this was too small due to Cea’s fears of a coup,
and it had risen to only 60,000 effectives by the end of 1833. Also, a
large part of these were assigned to Extremadura and Portugal as the
‘Army of Observation’, tasked with ﬁghting miguelistas and arresting
52   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


suspect governors of Spanish border garrison towns.18 Admittedly, the
insurgents were also too few, as Don Carlos’s refusal to lead an insur-
rection until his brother died had limited their organisation. But the
regional balance in the Basque country would shift in favour of the
Carlists thanks to the emergence of a charismatic military genius as their
leader.
   Tomás de Zumalacárregui (1788–1835) is central to understand-
ing the Basque phase of the war. Born in Ormáiztegui (Guipúzcoa),
Zumalacárregui was a veteran of the Peninsular and Royalist Wars, as
a Patriot and Royalist respectively, and was purged for his Carlism
in 1833. Many of his biographies border on hagiography, detailing
such colourful episodes as his men accepting half-pay without protest,
and a Basque grandmother who offered her last surviving grandson
for service, and then a horse after Zumalacárregui tearfully rejected
her blood sacriﬁce.19 Such miracle stories reveal the imaginative and
ideational world of the Carlists. These stories were probably nonsense,
or grossly distorted, but useful, as in being deprived of the open sup-
port of Rome and almost all of Spain’s bishops, the Carlists rallied to
Zumalacárregui as their own Christian soldier, and even refashioned
their own organic religious iconography around him and subsequent
national models and symbols. Unsurprisingly, most modern histori-
ans have seen through this Romantic haze. His most recent biography
reverts to the Romantic legend, however.20 John Coverdale defended a
contingent interpretation of Zumalacárregui’s motivations: he was apo-
litical, the only books he was known to have read being either military
or religious in nature.21 Zumalacárregui’s importance explains the quan-
tity and diversity of his representations. Within weeks of the ﬁrst rising
in October 1833, Zumalacárregui made himself Carlist Commander-in-
Chief of Navarra, at the head of a growing army of local peasants which
used guerrilla tactics to turn Cristino garrisons and expeditions into a
pale imitation of the French a generation earlier.22 These guerrilla tac-
tics were overborne throughout 1834 by Zumalacárregui’s painstaking
formation of cavalry and artillery units, eroding Cristino superiority
in these arms (and indeed despite the lack of a cavalry tradition in
Navarra).23 Zumalacárregui’s military success earned him full admin-
istrative powers, conferred upon him by Don Carlos’s exiled court in
March 1834. Zumalacárregui thus enjoyed untrammelled power until
the King arrived in the Basque country in July.24
   The ﬁrst stage of Zumalacárregui’s strategy began between the winter
of 1833 and the following summer with the employment of his army
in a guerrilla war of surprise and ambush. During this ﬁrst stage his
                                             The Basque Phase, 1833–35   53


army lacked the strength to wrest Bilbao from its Cristino liberators.
His ﬁrst set-piece battle on 29 December 1833 in La Berrueza valley was
indecisive: Zumalacárregui simply wanted to blood his men against the
superior Cristino forces and disengaged in good time to prepare for his
next operation. This was a raid into the fertile Ribera region of south-
ern Navarra, populated by hostile Castilian-speaking landowners, whose
‘enemy’ produce would feed his guerrillas.25 Zumalacárregui’s second
stage ran from the summer of 1834 until the winter, and involved con-
trolling rural territory and communications. The ensuing third stage
involved taking Cristino forts and fortiﬁed towns.26
   Cristino Spain would never produce a commander to match
Zumalacárregui. Quesada, who had abandoned a lifelong career of royal-
ism in order to escape the Cristino purges, used court pressure to replace
Jerónimo Valdés as Viceroy of Navarra, an old ofﬁce which was inter-
changeable with the new command of the Army of the North. Valdés
was a veteran of the American Wars and Governor of Cartagena (Murcia)
in 1833, but his liberalism was ahead of court politics at the time.
Quesada, by contrast, had absolutist credentials which recommended
him to the Queen-Regent, and, as a ‘pasha’, in January 1834 had dis-
solved a defence junta in Santander which an advanced Liberal had
led against local Carlists.27 His perspicacious ‘State of Navarra’ letter
to María Cristina claimed detailed knowledge of the insurgency and
won him the viceroyalty. As Cristino Viceroy, Quesada acted on his
rightist political instincts by writing to Zumalacárregui, offering him
a post in Burgos in return for his recognition of the Cristino transi-
tion. But Zumalacárregui, in conference with his staff, publicly rejected
Quesada’s overture, thereby reinforcing his own authority and image of
indispensability for the Carlist cause.28 Writers from both sides agreed
that Quesada’s overture was a disastrous own-goal, for in reinforcing
Zumalacárregui’s legitimacy it weakened the Cristino claim they were
ﬁghting outlaws.29 Moreover, Quesada could not parry this blow by suc-
cessful campaigning. Although the new Urban Militia (see below) was
beginning to garrison towns, freeing up the army for mobile operations,
Quesada was deaf to its Liberal-patriotic pull and instead relied on his
army. But the latter waged war ineptly: launching frontal attacks against
mountain positions, losing baggage and communications, and being
outmanoeuvred and outfought by Zumalacárregui’s genius in active
defence.30 A key early Carlist victory occurred at Alsasua in April 1834
when Zumalacárregui routed Quesada’s convoy, cutting Pamplona off
from supplies from Vitoria, and enveloping the Navarrese capital in a
siege which lasted on and off throughout the war.
54   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


  The Liberal press blamed Quesada for his failure and in June 1834 he
was relieved of his command.31 Having conceded that the Carlists could
no longer be crushed by mobile counter-insurgency operations, Quesada
knew that fortiﬁed lines would be the best strategy but protested he
had too few troops to implement this.32 Even though this ‘blockhouse’
counter-insurgency strategy had been successfully pioneered by the
French during the Peninsular War, the Cristinos still lacked the resources
to make it effective, as one of his ofﬁcers complained in response to
the criticism of the Liberal press: ‘let us not fool ourselves . . . many
more troops are needed if we are to crush the hordes infesting these
provinces, just as our commander-in-chief explained when this com-
mand was conferred upon him’.33 Quesada’s strategy avoided expanding
the war effort to the people, in contrast to the doctrine of his leftist
colleagues, Espoz and Jaúregui. Gaspar de Jaúregui (alias ‘El Pastor’),
guerrilla veteran of the Peninsular and Royalist Wars and of Espoz’s
Pyrenean invasion, was amnestied and recalled to his native Guipúzcoa
to lead a local Cristino free corps. Jaúregui was ambitious to replace
Quesada, but Madrid thought him an uncouth parvenu and blocked his
promotion.34 His case was not helped when the French press reported
that he had promised to rid the Basque country of rebels within a
fortnight.35 Quesada’s replacement was actually the Galician Marquess
José Ramón Rodil, the only veteran of Spain’s lost American wars to have
emerged with any credit as a tactician, who, in April 1834, was disengag-
ing from the Quadruple Alliance invasion of Portugal. Unlike in Navarra,
Cristino operations here had been generally bloodless and effective.
Rodil’s force had helped force Dom Miguel renounce the Portuguese
throne (in the 26 May 1834 Treaty of Evora-Monte), even though the
Cristinos’ real prize, Don Carlos, escaped on a Royal Navy ship bound
for an exile in Britain.36 Despite this failure, Rodil’s 12,000 troops, fresh
from Allied victory in Portugal, were the best in the Cristino army.37
Rodil began his command of the Army of the North with speechifying
optimism, scorning the enemy as ‘weak, exhausted and agonised’. Yet
his results proved no better than Quesada’s. Zumalacárregui drew Rodil
into a fruitless summer campaign invading Navarra, managing to entice
the Cristinos away from strategic targets of any real signiﬁcance and
refusing open battle. After weeks suffering ambushes and empty cam-
paigning, which resulted in mass desertion from Cristino ranks, Rodil
had to retire to his fortiﬁed base at Mendavia near Logroño.38
  The only Cristino ofﬁcer to distinguish himself was Baldomero
Espartero, the son of a carter whom the Carlist War elevated from
mere divisional commander to Regent of Spain. Espartero led a clique
                                             The Basque Phase, 1833–35   55


of ‘ayacucho’ ofﬁcers, veterans of the lost American wars who, during
the Carlist War, forged a highly praetorian bond of leftist politics. The
campaigns of both Napoleon and Espartero show how men who fol-
low their leaders on distant campaigns grow very attached to them,
with destabilising consequences for civilian politics, as Espartero, the
‘Spanish Napoleon’ of 1840, would show. But whilst Espartero after the
war would divide Spanish politics, in 1834 his local victories had already
made him the symbol of Cristino hopes. Physically brave, Espartero was
wounded several times throughout the war and even more times had his
horse shot from under him. In February 1834, Espartero was promoted
to major-general, and later to Comandante General of Vizcaya. His hum-
ble background and bravery gave him good qualities for leadership, even
though these made him prone to immodesty. Espartero conﬁded to
his wife that ‘the troops love me like a father . . . they have an enthu-
siasm for me which borders on delirium and I fear that this could end
up harming me’.39 This dramatic irony was condemned as callow self-
aggrandisement by the British consul at Bilbao, who thought Espartero
was deluded in thinking that Zumalacárregui’s forays into Vizcaya were
anything more than a feint:

  (Zumalacárregui) seldom came into Biscay. Apparently convinced of
  the inefﬁcacy of Espartero’s operations, he let him amuse himself by
  running after the Biscay battalions, who were accustomed to lead
  him a dance all round the province; and when his men were totally
  fatigued with four and ﬁve days’ marches, they would make a stand
  upon some hillside, right in his road; then after disputing the ground
  for half an hour or an hour, and causing the loss of some 50 or 100
  men, they would run off, leaving the victorious general to write such
  bulletins as were never equalled . . . the ﬁeld of Burseña (for example)
  was cubierto de cadáveres (covered with corpses). There were plenty of
  spectators, the writer among the number; and yet nobody could ﬁnd
  above 18 dead bodies!40

The Carlist press matched the consul’s parody, punning Espartero’s
name, ‘General Embustero’ (General Fiction).41 But Espartero’s minor
victories at Portugalete, Guernica, Sollube and elsewhere, certainly won
him plaudits from the Liberal press. Unlike Espoz, future Commander
of the Army of the North who professed a particular understanding of
his native Navarra, Espartero claimed no insight into why the Carlists
were ﬁghting. His letters throughout 1834 spoke of the enemy as rabble
or canaille and his dealings with Carlists were as harsh as any Cristino
56   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


commander. Yet Espartero’s humble origins and political exploitation of
victories recommended him to Spain’s Romantic movement, and to left-
ist tributes to the ‘son of the people’, a Spanish George Washington.42
This rhetorical hyperbole was, of course, divorced from military reality,
from the martial law which Espartero, like other Cristino commanders,
imposed on Carlist territory. On 4 May, Espartero threatened ﬁnes for
families of insurgents, that houses locked upon the approach of Cristino
troops anywhere in Vizcaya would be treated as fair game, and also that
all 17 to 40-year-old men capable of bearing arms and who had had their
hair cut (a sign of military service) were to be sent to Bilbao to prove that
they were not Carlist guerrillas. Those who did not convince were to be
expelled from Vizcaya.43 Espartero’s repressive occupation was accom-
panied by the provisional suspension of the Basque fueros, a measure
which included the shifting of the customs frontier from the Ebro to
the Pyrenees, and replacing Navarra’s historic status as a kingdom with
that of a province conforming to the 1833 model. Henceforth, areas that
came under Cristino occupation would be doubly subjected by the state
of war and the emasculation of civil authority.44
   Zumalacárregui matched the Cristinos in ruthlessness as his reach
widened, demanding that local authorities throughout Navarra and
Upper Aragón swear allegiance to Carlos V or face the ultimate penalty.
This radicalisation placed the Deputation of Navarra in a dire posi-
tion. Early in 1834 Zumalacárregui ﬁnally moved against this ‘nest of
traitors’, pointing to its duplicitous care for the preservation of the
fueros on the one hand whilst speaking out against Don Carlos and peti-
tioning Madrid to reconvene the local Cortes on the other.45 By early
1834 binary Carlist and Cristino authorities confronted each other in
the Basque country. The Carlists had formed a ‘Superior Governmen-
tal Junta of Alava’, a ‘War Deputation of Guipúzcoa’, a ‘Governmental
Junta of Navarra’, along with their own Deputation. The Cristinos,
for their part, imposed their own authorities in the same provinces,
although their remit was shrinking beyond fortiﬁed urban centres on
account of Carlist military successes. As each side refused to recog-
nise the other, even a modest incursion imposed double jeopardy in
terms of taxation, conscription and reprisals on local communities
in the armies’ path. Particular stress was placed on the lowest lev-
els of local authority, aldermen and mayors forced to raise exorbitant
sums to fund both armies, and who faced brutal reprisals for hav-
ing been found to have helped the wrong side. Thus, local authorities
increasingly deserted their posts.46 As Zumalacárregui expanded territo-
rial control throughout 1834, local authorities logically became more
                                              The Basque Phase, 1833–35   57


prone to ﬂee before Cristino incursions, compounding Cristino weak-
ness in local intelligence.47 One of the most mobile Cristino tacticians,
General Marcelino Oráa, blamed his defeats at Zubiri and Urdaniz on
the enemy’s superior local intelligence.48 But Carlist domination by no
means ended the strain on local authorities. Zumalacárregui increas-
ingly overrode civil authorities whilst arrogantly rebufﬁng petitions for
alleviation by Carlist juntas and the Deputation. And little relief came
from the arrival of Don Carlos’s court: although Zumalacárregui’s per-
sonal authority was curtailed, the burdens on the civil authorities only
increased.
   But the military–political crisis of 1834 was far worse for the Cristino
side and quickly made the premiership of Cea untenable. Opposition
centred on the Isabelina Society, whose membership ranged from Lib-
erals of radical to moderate opinions and included several ‘pashas’ who
resented Cea’s civilian absolutism. Demands grew for a Liberal regime to
root out Carlists and attract intervention from Britain and France. Bow-
ing to this pressure, in January 1834 María Cristina appointed as Prime
Minister Martínez de la Rosa, an Andalucían patrician of Spain’s ﬁrst and
second Liberal experiments, who promised to restore Spain’s happiness
by marrying ‘order with liberty’.49 One of his ﬁrst moves was to create
the Urban Militia, to be organised at local level under the command
of the reformed town halls. The advanced Liberal newspaper, Eco del
Comercio, hailed this force as the ‘people in arms’ destined to vanquish
the Carlist insurgency.50 The reality in its ﬁrst year of existence was very
different, however. Property owners remembered the royalist proscrip-
tion of 1823 and had no wish to suffer the same now in the event of
a Carlist victory. Cristino captains-general in Andalucía and Valencia
despaired at the middle classes’ reluctance to lead the Militia, and exer-
cised a range of coercion against them as a consequence.51 The Radical
press complained how patriotic economic dependents substituted their
masters in dangerous roles, and how the notables’ listlessness meant
that disbanded Royalist Volunteers were being enrolled in the Liberal
force.52 Alborea (Albacete), for example, was dominated by the Meoro y
Sánchez family, who maintained their grip on local policing when the
