Volunteers’ commander, Anacleto, later Governor of the Bishopric of
Almería, handled the Cristino purge by simply abdicating his command
and preparing his nephew to take command of the ‘new’ militia.53
   The Cristino militia was despised by Carlists as a club for playboy
revolutionaries and was routinely the ﬁrst to be subjected to reprisals
whenever urban centres fell into their hands. It was also poorly regarded
by the Cristino army, which resented the militia’s parochial structures
58   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


(including, from 1835, elected ofﬁcers), comparatively good pay and
beneﬁts, and internal restrictions on where and when it might serve.
In return, the urbanos resented army ‘despotism’ and protested the Mili-
tia’s exclusion from an internationally brokered agreement in April 1835
which promised quarter to surrendering soldiers serving in the north.
Ultimately the Cristino army retained the upper hand in this inter-
service rivalry: it could impose a state of siege in response to revolution
or Carlist invasion, subjecting the Militia to martial law or even dis-
banding it altogether. But the Militia grew in power. The revolutionary
summer of 1835 made Militia service more attractive, and as its num-
bers expanded, its control over urban spaces and Liberal pageantry
claimed privileged ownership of Spanish patriotism: time and again this
was turned into a focus for radical political action, massacring clerics
and Carlist prisoners, and in September 1835 leading a Liberal revolu-
tion across Cristino Spain. A generation of historians saw this Militia
radicalism in Marxist class-conﬂict terms.54 But whilst class interests
were not absent, members of the Militia were radicalised more by the
experience of the civil war. Their sacriﬁces for the Cristino cause were
used to justify revolution and even the most extreme atrocities. Cities
like Barcelona were ﬁlling with refugees who had witnessed murders
and wanton destruction. Their arrival provoked demands for revenge
against Carlist sympathisers imprisoned in Cristino gaols, a demand that
became impossible to resist during the revolution of 1835.
   The inter-service rivalry about who was making the biggest sacri-
ﬁces raged as Zumalacárregui tightened his grip on the Basque country
and Carlist risings ﬂared up elsewhere. In 1834 the urbanos defend-
ing Cristino towns in the Basque country, Catalonia, the Maestrazgo
had no doubt that they were the defenders of liberty. During the win-
ter of 1834–35, besieged Pamplona ﬁlled with timorous army ofﬁcers
who avoided rejoining their units by exaggerating or inventing per-
sonal injuries, and often hiding behind the presence of their families.
Pamplona’s militiamen, by contrast, with a great deal more than hon-
our at stake, manned their city’s defence by day and night, often in
conditions of appalling hygiene and hunger.55 Forty miles south of
Pamplona, the besieged Militia garrison of Villafranca was heroically
martyred by defeat. Villafranca was the gateway to the Ribera and a
customs-post before the 1834 Cristino suspension of the fueros, and its
Militia garrison went to the utmost lengths to resist Zumalacárregui’s
siege. The Militia retreated into the defensive redoubt of the village
church. Zumalacárregui burnt down the adjoining buildings and burnt
sacks of chilli at the foot of the church in order to suffocate the defenders
                                               The Basque Phase, 1833–35   59


inside. A truce was brokered to allow the release of the militiamen’s
women and children, but shots were ﬁred during this delicate oper-
ation and Zumalacárregui reacted by beating and feather-tarring the
refugees. Before the surviving defenders choked to death, they stormed
out of the church and charged the Carlist ring. Those militiamen who
were not killed were captured and shot. Heroic Villafranca, and its
heroic refugee women, became Liberal cause célèbres. On 10 December
1834, after the Cristinos had retaken the town, the Cristino government
decided to rebuild the church, and compensate the widows and orphans
(the Queen-Regent, seeing the emerging popular meaning of the war,
prudently made a public donation).56 The martyrdom of Villafranca
was downplayed by Carlist historians who resented the muddying of
Zumalacárregui’s own heroic image.57
   The violent, sudden and unsolicited martyrdom which overtook
overwhelmed Militia detachments and pitiful prisoners of war pro-
voked demands for vengeance. Whilst poorly led Cristino armies were
defeated at every turn, these rural defeats were offset by urban defensive
victories – or by valiant defeats which made martyrs to liberty out of the
armed citizens who suffered them. Vergara (Guipúzcoa) controlled vital
communications with the French border, and its Militia garrison distin-
guished itself resisting a Carlist attack in August. In September, a Carlist
force which struck west into Old Castile was defeated by the outnum-
bered militia garrison of Villarcayo, which fought without outside help
and despite half the town being burnt by the attackers. Thus, whilst the
Cristino army elites failed on campaign, the ‘people’s army’ succeeded
in point defence.58 It would not be long until the militia cashed in these
military honours for a stake in politics. In the early stages this radicalism
amounted to vengeance, targeting Carlists captured on the battleﬁeld,
languishing in prisons, or civic Carlists cloistered in monasteries and
nunneries. But it also targeted Cristino elites who were either callous or
incompetent towards their men. And the militiamen were not the sole
agents of vengeance, as the result of Zumalacárregui’s great victory in
October 1834 at Alegría (Alava) showed. This triumph was prepared by
Zumalacárregui’s expert command of geography. Civilians in his path
were forced either to join his columns or to be corralled under guard
away from roadsides, out of sight of Cristino scouts. Then he ambushed
a superior Cristino force, dividing it into two and defeating both sections
in detail. Some 2,000 surviving Cristino soldiers (twice as many as he
had killed in action) defected to the Carlist side and made no protest
when he had their captured Cristino commander shot.59 As Cristino
revolutions and Carlist expeditions afﬂicted Spain from 1835, civilians,
60   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


too, followed the lead shown by militia and soldiers by making decisions
and acting on them. Episodes like these grew to spawn a Cristino mar-
tyrology that was distinctive in its subaltern and civic qualities, in being
no longer concerned with avenging heroic elites (like Riego in 1823 or
Torrijos in 1831), but about avenging popular grievances. In this regard,
1830s Spain contrasted with comparable ideological struggles in such
other Latin polities as Italy, where both nationalist and ultramontane
martyrologies were crafted to support elite rather than popular aims.60
   This civic martyrology had no equivalent on the Carlist side where
the motives of religion, dynasticism, impoverishment, adventure and
wrongdoing did not naturally lend themselves to progressive, civic
martyrdom, and still less beyond the Basque country, in areas where
wrongdoing and poverty played a larger role in Carlism. Throughout
1834, Cristino counter-insurgency forces in Catalonia had varied success
suppressing Carlist guerrilla bands raised from the traditional somatén.
The war here divided the staunchly Cristino coastal range, inhabited by
an inﬂuential bourgeoisie, from the front-line plateau and mountains of
the Catalan far west, where Cristinos fought Carlist guerrillas. The early
Carlist insurgency was led by the cleric and Royalist War veteran, Benito
Tristany. His opponent, Catalan Captain-General Manuel Llauder, com-
plained of the inextinguishable Carlist conspiratorial networks based
around parish priests and monasteries, but believed he had vanquished
the Carlist somatenes by the summer of 1834.61 But they never lost a
foothold in the mountainous north of the Principality, where a Carlist
rural network was ready to revive the disbanded somatenes for another
insurrection. Meanwhile, army detachments were drawn away from
Catalonia to reinforce the hard-pressed Army of the North, and Llauder
himself left for Madrid where, on 2 November 1834, he became Minis-
ter of War. Thus, the newly constituted Urban Militia was left to defend
itself against resurgent somatenes without much help from the regular
army.62 Outside the Basque provinces, Catalonia and the Maestrazgo,
Carlist risings had a symbiotic relationship with banditry, both feeding
off it and reverting to it when dispersed by Cristino counter-insurgency
actions. Carlist bandits were dangerous not only for operating outside
the norms of war, but also because of their example of ill-gotten gains,
which might encourage Cristino conscripts to desert. In October 1834,
one Cristino military commander in action against Carlist irregulars in
the Maestrazgo was acutely aware of this when he wrote to the War Min-
istry demanding that his troops get proper rations of bread, wine and
meat, ‘as it is most demoralising for them to discover that the facciosos
take and eat what they want’.63
                                             The Basque Phase, 1833–35   61


   Beyond these emerging Carlist safe zones, insurgents were moti-
vated, above all, by adventure and wrongdoing. The mundane case of
‘El Locho’ (Manuel Adame), a pig-rustling day labourer and veteran
of the Peninsular and Royalist Wars, and the main guerrilla plague
of La Mancha in 1834, was not untypical. Other bandits preyed on
Manchegan communities using Carlism as their cover. Their colourful
nomes de guerre (the Palillos brothers, Tercero, Rompe, Ventero, Barba,
Lobito) exempliﬁed the Spanish popular culture of using resonant nick-
names to hide mundane identities. Further south and west, virtually all
insurgents were bandits using Carlism as the ﬂimsiest of political covers.
Extremadura had a long border with restive Portuguese miguelistas, but
Cristino border garrisons prevented any organised Carlist insurgencies
from taking hold and security forces instead had only bandits to sup-
press. Andalucía’s aquiline bandits were already infamous, and attempts
to recruit them met with derision from several quarters. Nonethe-
less, Carlist emissaries were dispatched throughout the war to turn the
bandits, smugglers and deserters of the Serranía de Ronda into a guer-
rilla force.64 Thus, even though organised Carlism outside of its safe
zones could never prevail without outside help, the countryside was
strewn with bandit gangs whose varied motives (desertion, landless-
ness, crime) they often explained by allegiance to Carlism. In short,
Carlism was often a cover for contingent rather than ideological
motives.
   This is not to say that Carlism was nothing more than a ﬂag of
convenience. Civic Carlism in the Cristino zone, especially of priests,
often spilled over into incitement or violence in the face of leftist
provocation. Throughout 1834, Cristino authorities uncovered plots
involving Carlists who had got involved with the new Militia,65 sus-
picious arms caches66 and, especially, hostile clerics. Clerical hostility
included incitement: one cleric brought to trial proclaimed ‘Death to
all negros [pejorative for “Liberal”]! The Holy Spirit speaks through me!’
whilst a Valencian capuchin preached that the Church would resist the
onslaught of the ‘philosophers’ of the century and that the cholera
which appeared between 1832 and 1834 was divine punishment for
Spain’s wickedness. The cleric’s curse probably instilled the fear of God
into his parishioners. The Asiatic cholera epidemic had ravaged the pop-
ulation, with the province of Granada alone registering a grisly toll of
5,293 dead between January and August 1834, notwithstanding munic-
ipal quarantines uselessly designed to contain the ‘miasma’.67 In fact,
the cholera had an even more sinister legacy in the pretext it pro-
vided for Cristino elites to channel the anger of those elements of the
62   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Cristino population who saw the Church as the cause of the war. Inﬂu-
enced by similar phenomena in central Europe and Russia (and the
assaults on the Jesuits during the July 1830 revolution in Paris), Lib-
eral agents in July 1834 spread rumours in Madrid that the cholera
was caused by Jesuits poisoning the wells. The response was horriﬁc.
The militia rallied mobs in a violent assault on Madrid’s convents and
monasteries, massacring seventy-eight Jesuits, Dominicans, Franciscans
and Mercedarians. Whilst Liberal newspapers drew a veil over the whole
affair – ‘a few bad apples’ according to El Eco del Comercio68 – a police
report found the Militia to have been the chief culprits, whilst the addi-
tional participation of some Royal Guards conﬁrmed the suspicion that
the events of 17 July 1834 enjoyed both the foreknowledge and the tacit
backing of the government. Leniency was shown towards those token
culprits arrested, whilst for days afterwards groups of militiamen swag-
gered about the streets ‘boasting of their crimes’ and ‘showing more
pride and haughtiness than the French after Marengo’.69 A savage tra-
dition of anti-clericalism had been born, and generations of clerical
historians would remember 17 July as a day of infamy.
   Although religious personnel in the rest of Cristino Spain were spared
a massacre for the rest of 1834 and the ﬁrst three months of 1835, the
climate of hostility grew in the wake of Madrid. To some degree this
was remarkable, as even though relations between Madrid and the Holy
See deteriorated, culminating with the expulsion of the nuncio in 1835,
all but one of the Spanish bishops obeyed Rome’s orders not to recog-
nise the Carlist insurgency as legitimate. Thus, clerics who joined the
insurgency did so as individuals rather than as an institution, but given
the well-known religious claims of the Carlists, it was understandable
how episodes of anti-clerical violence spiralled at local levels in Cristino
Spain.70 Monks in Huéscar (Granada) were deported after they tried to
evict militiamen who played revolutionary music and adorned their
hats with the green ribbons of the Constitution. Fines were imposed
upon priests who refused to offer blessings to Isabel II during Mass.71
The enemy’s anti-clericalism gave Carlists hope of intervention in their
favour from abroad. One priest whipped up popular Carlism in the
Almanzora river area of Almería – hundreds of miles from the front line –
with the promise that Carlists from Genoa would make landfall.72 In fact
the expedition landed in Catalonia, and comprised mainly Catalan émi-
grés from the transition of 1833 rather than Italians. These went to
ground in the Barceloneta district of Barcelona, troubling the watch-
ful Cristino city authorities who feared a ﬁfth column.73 This was the
ﬁrst and last expedition organised from abroad before the British Navy
                                            The Basque Phase, 1833–35   63


closed off the Mediterranean to all but the most intrepid volunteers for
Carlism.74
   But from the landward side there was one volunteer who would not
be kept out of Spain. Don Carlos’s exodus through Portugal was remark-
able, even more his escape from exile in Britain. Reaching Spanish soil
via the Pyrenees at Urdax in July 1834 – around the same time as the
massacre of his beloved Jesuits in Madrid – Don Carlos ﬁnally gave the
insurrection its natural leader.75 This was a far cry from the months of
exile in Portugal. There the Pretender’s supply trains had been seized
by Rodil’s vanguard and his entourage reduced to scavenging potatoes.
Rodil had almost captured Don Carlos himself, a feat which earned him
Quesada’s incumbency. But this reward backﬁred when news shocked
both Rodil and his government – Don Carlos had reached Spain to pre-
side over the insurgency.76 Some of Madrid’s blindness in this affair may
be attributed to the Cristinos’ poor diplomatic connections resulting
from six years of miguelista control of Lisbon.77 Even though the pedris-
tas captured the Portuguese capital in 1833, the new Cristino legation
in the city had been powerless to inﬂuence Britain’s usual hegemony
in Portugal, in particular the latter’s resolve to undermine the Carlists
by exiling Don Carlos from Iberia, in the same way that the exiling of
Dom Miguel had ended the Portuguese civil war. Upon the Pretender’s
arrival in Britain, the Cristino ambassador in London at last managed
some intercession. Although Foreign Secretary Palmerston protested he
could not hold Don Carlos prisoner, he made the Pretender give his
word that he would not leave Britain, and in any case satisﬁed Cristino
foreign policy by signing the Quadruple Alliance. In the long term this
alliance augured well for Cristino Spain, as it united the constitutional
monarchies of Britain, France, Spain and Portugal in diplomatic sup-
port for Madrid, and was followed by agreements to raise volunteers in
these countries for service in Spain. As this alliance brought Europe’s
two foremost powers to the aid of the Cristinos, an unqualiﬁed Carlist
victory was now highly unlikely. At the heart of the alliance was the
Anglo-French ‘Liberal international’ of Whigs and Orleanists. Whereas
London used this to support a new era of Liberal interventionism in for-
eign policy, Paris used it to help fend off legitimism at home (Orleanist
fears had been evoked by an abortive rising in the Vendée in 1832). But
in the immediate term the alliance fell at the ﬁrst hurdle when Don
Carlos escaped from London, making for Brighton, then Dieppe, Paris
and Bordeaux and Bayonne, and ﬁnally Spain. Don Carlos was himself
amazed, whispering to his companion, Auguet Saint-Sylvain, ‘My good
cousin of the Orleans dynasty is very far from suspecting that I’m going
64   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


through his territories without his permission, to go and tear asunder
at the point of my sword his treaty of the Quadruple Alliance.’78 This
journey would have failed without the protection of Saint-Sylvain, the
French Carlist and private secretary to Don Carlos, who drew ﬁnance,
obtained passports and secured safe houses in France, and consequently
joined the Pretender’s court in Spain.79
   Once on Spanish soil, Sylvain helped the King to forge a central provi-
sional junta out of the several local Carlist juntas and set it to assessing
the economic and military potential of the newly created Carlist zone.80
The King’s mere appearance on Spanish soil galvanised the insurrection
and became the biggest propaganda weapon against both the Quadru-
ple Alliance and the Cristino war effort. In a revealing proclamation,
Don Carlos appealed to conscripts to the Cristino army to defect. Don
Carlos equated the sex of María Cristina with illegitimacy, weakness and
indiscretion, attributing these to the usurping nature of the entire Lib-
eral project.81 This was integral propaganda, but the fact that defectors
from Cristino ranks greatly outnumbered those from the Carlists owed
more to Madrid’s inability to succour its conscripts than to any dynas-
tic attraction. Cristino defection grew during 1834, and even though no
exact numbers exist – the Gaceta Oﬁcial printed the names and units
of defectors for propaganda purposes, but obvious fears of retribution
appear to have limited this practice82 – it is clear that their numbers
peaked during 1835–37, as expeditions tore across Spain and revolu-
tion derailed Cristino military strategy. Both defectors and non-Basque
recruits were called Castilians (castellanos) by the Basque Carlists – who
soon used the term to describe all Carlists who were not from the Basque
country – and by 1835 several battalions described as such had been
formed. The Castilian Carlists occupied an awkward position during the
war: loathed by the Cristinos, they were also treated with suspicion by
the Basque fueristas who saw them as a ‘foreign’ threat to Basque auton-
omy, as non-productive refugees consuming resources and as proxies for
the unpopular Castilian-dominated Carlist Royal Police.83 A diametri-
cally opposed suspicion pertained to the considerably fewer recruits and
defectors from the Basque country who served in Cristino ranks. Espoz
actively recruited deserters and villagers in his native Navarra, even from
French border villages. Their different language, uniform and pay struc-
ture, and, above all, their self-interested bravery in battle (they were
particularly vulnerable to Carlist reprisals), alienated conscripts from the
rest of Spain whose only thought was survival in a strange land.84
   Meanwhile, the arrival of the Pretender on Spanish soil threw Cristino
politics into turmoil. Prime Minister de la Rosa’s refrain that Don Carlos
                                              The Basque Phase, 1833–35   65


was merely ‘one more insurgent’ angered the Army of the North, which
by now thought Madrid was losing all grasp of reality.85 The union of
the Pretender with his commander-in-chief appeared the worst possible
outcome, and the Cristino army responded by waging war more strate-
gically. Serious consideration was given to conducting a scorched earth
policy.86 In a limited form this was carried out: raids routinely destroyed
war-related material which could help the insurgency, from ramparts to
entire arms factories. But this economic war was countered by the Carlist
soldiers’ readiness to use the arms of Cristino prisoners and defectors,
and even to loot their corpses. The ﬁrst and last year of the war when
the Cristino army was adequately supplied was 1833/34, which made it
a cash cow for the scratch Carlist forces. And the Carlists’ resourcefulness
countered the Cristino blockade. Zumalacárregui demanded parsimony
in his soldiers’ use of powder, ordering his men to take only ten
musket cartridges into battle and to ﬁre these only when the enemy
was in sight and range.87 Meanwhile Carlist materiel was improving.
An initial ‘powder scandal’ was gradually overcome when the quality
of powder smuggled from Aragón and France or made in overworked
forges was improved, and the conditions of Carlist armaments work-
ers militarised.88 The father of the Carlist artillery arm was Vicente
Reina, a Cristino defector who was killed in action in July 1835.89
By the end of 1834 the Carlists got to possess their ﬁrst artillery pieces:
three guns (including two captured from defeated Cristinos at Alegría).
Reina expanded this number by building two howitzers, improvising
shells from materials collected from villages. An ironmongers in a forest
near Labayen became the early centre of Carlist mountain-gun making.
An old iron gun with a wide calibre was pressed into service and chris-
tened ‘El Abuelo’ (grandfather) by its Cristino targets on account of its
age, and this name stuck with the Carlists, too.90 Now Zumalacárregui
had sufﬁcient siege and ﬁeld artillery, whilst the travails of ‘El Abuelo’
made the gun as much a propaganda as a military weapon, as it was
transported from burial in a Cantabrian beach and pulled by six-pair
of oxen until it reached Urbasa, where it was secretly reburied. A for-
eign observer noted ‘if the Carlists win, it will be lodged in the Madrid
museum as a memorial of the war’.91
   Carlist military expansion underpinned Zumalacárregui’s authority
in religious, political and economic matters. He embodied the ‘throne
and altar’ reaction of the Carlist movement, combining the role of
Christian soldier with exterminating angel, presiding over his troops’
daily attendance at Mass on the one hand, and endorsing the public
execution of some 70 Cristino captives in reprisal for Madrid’s Jesuits on
66   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


the other.92 Zumalacárregui combined austere charisma with discipline,
planning and resourcefulness. He responded to villagers’ complaints
about Cristino raiding parties by arming ﬂying parties tasked with their
protection. He also used draconian punishments when he judged it nec-
essary. In September 1834, he decimated a battalion of his own men
when their timidity spoilt his plan to trick his way into a key forti-
ﬁed village (Echarri-Aranaz) on the Pamplona–Vitoria road (pro-Carlist
sources conceded Zumalacárregui rare emotion when he halted the
executions halfway, tearfully realising the men were ‘his children’).93
Zumalacárregui’s military state was already in place when the King’s
court arrived. It had even established regular ﬁnance from internal
sources: the revenues of customs posts the Carlists were seizing along
the border with France, taxes levied on parish clergy, and ﬁnes imposed
on Cristino sympathisers and hostile settlements. The harvest of 1834,
meanwhile, was abundant without collapsing market prices, owing to
war depredations and Zumalacárregui’s supply needs.94 Expanding ter-
ritorial control allowed battle casualties to be transferred from homes
to Carlist military hospitals; patients in the latter stood better chances
of recovery than Cristino patients did in theirs. Cristino casualties, by
contrast, had to be transferred over exterior lines of communication,
which gave them greater exposure to the Asiatic cholera epidemic and
also absorbed far more manpower – some ten Cristino soldiers to litter
each patient to safety.95
   By the time of his King’s arrival, Zumalacárregui was thus already pre-
siding over an integrated military state. He even kept a platoon-strength
bodyguard who also relayed messages day or night.96 This insurgent
model provided a mystique for Carlist supporters which their Cristino
enemies completely lacked. Cristino commanders, by contrast, cut lum-
bering, conspicuous ﬁgures, which afforded local authorities time to
desert their posts en masse, so as to avoid reprisals from the stronger
Carlists.97 Thus, the Cristinos routinely lacked local intelligence and
liaison networks, and so manoeuvred their men into vulnerable group
formations and corralled them in valleys. Consequently, the Carlists had
little difﬁculty intercepting communications and, unlike their enemy,
faced no shortage of willing spies in the Basque country.98 The effect of
superior local intelligence was plain to see in Carlist victories through-
out 1834. In August, Zumalacárregui defeated a Cristino division at Las
Peñas de San Fausto (Navarra), inﬂicting some 250 fatalities for the
loss of barely more than a dozen of his own. Zumalacárregui seized
the secret codes with which Cristino commanders communicated with
one another yet, remarkably, the defeated general took no measures
                                              The Basque Phase, 1833–35   67


to change them. General Carondelet, who was called embarrassed or
incompetent, knew that Carlist spies were tracking his every move even
without the codes.99 Even worse, the numerical and material superior-
ity which the Cristinos had once enjoyed was now being eroded by
Carlist rearmament and recruitment of conscripts and defectors. The
Carlists formed a battalion of scouts called the Guías de Navarra, com-
prised entirely of Cristino defectors, and their artillery and even cavalry
arms were beginning to match their enemies’. On 4 September at Viana,
Carlist cavalry were decisive in victory for the ﬁrst time.100 For their
part, few Cristino commanders could match the Carlists in mountain
warfare, and ofﬁcers had to wait until late 1834 for a book on the
subject to appear.101 Much depended in the meantime on such resource-
fulness and initiative as the Cristino ﬁeld commanders could muster.
General Oráa, dubbed the ‘grey wolf’ by the Carlists on account of his
complexion and risk-taking with rapid marches,102 was one of the best
hopes the Cristinos had. A native of Beriáin (Pamplona), and Penin-
sular War veteran, Oráa knew local terrain.103 But he was nevertheless
outmanoeuvred and defeated by Zumalacárregui at Los Arcos.
   In November 1834, Cristino fortunes appeared to change with the
appointment to the Command of the Army of the North of the swor-
darm of Liberalism, Espoz y Mina, who replaced the exhausted Rodil.
Espoz’s advanced liberalism had made him one of the last exiles to be
amnestied, and his appointment to the most senior appointment in the
Cristino army divided military opinion far more than Jáuregui’s candi-
dacy. Espoz offered the surrounding Carlists an amnesty or even service
under his command, whilst imposing martial law in Pamplona, includ-
ing a shoot-on-sight curfew after nightfall. Despite Espoz’s recourse to
such illiberal measures, the Liberal press carved out an image of him as
the grand old man of liberty. A Zaragoza newspaper called him a ‘man
of his fatherland (patria)’, in contrast to Zumalacárregui who was merely
a ‘man of his mountains’.104 Espoz certainly had a great reputation,
but this did not bode well as Zumalacárregui had already spent a year
humbling Cristino reputations. A German Carlist compared Espoz to ‘a
well-practised gladiator forced to ﬁght his enemy with a shield instead of
a sword’.105 Yet it was nonetheless remarkable how the Liberal-patriotic
allure of Espoz persisted amongst the Cristino rank and ﬁle despite his
failures. After his death from stomach cancer in December 1836, Espoz’s
mortal remains were carried around Cristino Spain in a revolutionary
ritual of popular liberty. Evidence of the strength of feeling arose when
the Málaga militia rioted at their city’s refusal to pay public homage.106
On paper Espoz found the Army of the North still in a strong state, as it
68   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


included some 23,357 infantry and 1,089 cavalry, but barely more than
half of these were actually available for campaigning. Espoz knew that
they were tired, poorly equipped (they still only had summer uniforms)
and demoralised from previous encounters. However committed Espoz
was to remedying this, he continued the incompatible War Ministry
policy of garrisoning isolated small settlements, diluting his campaign
strength whilst failing even to defend in depth. Most settlements in
Navarra and the Basque provinces proper were unwalled and located in
militarily indefensible valleys, which left ridges and peaks free for Carlist
activities: indeed Cristino attempts at defence in depth had done noth-
ing to break the siege of Pamplona.107 Whilst the Carlists were amassing,
Espoz’s energies were absorbed safeguarding essential relief column sup-
plies for besieged Pamplona. Local Cristino victories at Mendaza and
then Puerta de Arquijas (12–15 December) ensured Pamplona received
supplies safely but barely altered the strategic situation.108 This remained
bleak, as Zumalacárregui’s victory the same month at Las Arquijas
attested.109 Then, inevitably, both sides yielded to the elements, with
a virtual truce over Christmas whilst Spain and the world took stock of
an alarming year of civil war.
   Zumalacárregui retired his army to his stronghold of the Améscoas
valley (Navarra) whilst Espoz had no option but to withdraw Cristino
forces from the countryside. Espoz’s background gave him an advanced
understanding of the war, but his illness usually conﬁned him to
Pamplona, and so he delegated generals to escort convoys into the
city. Espoz’s strategy was to box in Zumalacárregui by arming local
militia units as much as possible. This ran the risk that the inhabi-
tants, especially in the smaller settlements, would turn their arms over
to the Carlists, but Espoz hoped that their initial resistance would
‘soak up’ Carlist forces and free up the Cristino army for a decisive
counteroffensive. This close engagement strategy would turn on the
Cristinos’ clinging on to villages in the valley of Baztán (Navarra),
especially its local capital, Elizondo, as this region controlled access
to French customs revenues and contained valuable arms factories like
Orbaiceta.110 Espoz’s strategy was clearly informed by his ideology, a
form of Bonapartist Liberalism dictating that the people be armed for
authoritarian ends.111 He could claim a common touch (he wrote a
number of key proclamations in Euskera). Like any Bonapartist, Espoz
harmonised Cristino military with civilian administration and proved
tolerant towards civic Carlism, but also intervened ruthlessly when-
ever this turned to violence or reprisals. Thus, when Zumalacárregui
raided the Baztán he imprisoned the wives of absent Cristino soldiers
                                              The Basque Phase, 1833–35   69


and militiamen; Espoz responded in kind with the wife of a Carlist
mayor and several other prominent Carlist women, ransoming them
in a Pamplona prison.112
   Escalating reprisals provoked international interest in the conﬂict and
motivated foreign volunteers. Zumalacárregui assembled a squadron
entitled the Defensores de la Legitimidad, comprising Spanish and
foreign ofﬁcers who served as simple soldiers, and they increased in
number and distinction (research has revealed between 120 and 250
foreign Carlist volunteers, of whom more than a quarter boasted noble
titles).113 It became fashionable for European aristocrats hooked on
the Hispanophile Romanticism of writers like Chateaubriand, Schlegel
and Hugo to assert old-fashioned notions of honour in a continent
which had otherwise lapsed into the juste milieu. French volunteers were
the most numerous; reeling from their recent failure in the Vendée,
Spain’s conﬂict offered legitimists the chance to reignite the cause of
Henri d’Artois and restore honour. Aubert, a French ofﬁcer and fugi-
tive of 1832, ﬂed France under a death sentence to serve under Don
Carlos in Spain, but faced the same sentence once captured by Cristino
forces. Rejecting an invitation to defect and save his life, Aubert died
a Carlist: ‘In France I would have given my life for Henri V; here,
I owe it to Carlos V. Long live the king!’114 Prussians formed the sec-
ond largest contingent of volunteers; their Protestant piety in the service
of the Carlist Catholicism was explained by a pan-European, interde-
nominational defence of legitimacy.115 German conservatives of both
denominations united in pro-Carlism: the ultramontane Rheinlander
Josef Görres shared Protestants’ enthusiasm for Carlism as the organic
Spanish resistance to ‘foreign’ Liberalism.116 The prospect of adventure
lured other young legitimists. The young August Karl von Goeben, full
of a sense of Romantic adventure, followed his countrymen:117

  A royalist in the full sense of the word . . . I imagined those mountains,
  how the Carlists there were struggling against the overwhelming
  might of the revolutionary armies, bowing neither to sword, hunger
  nor death, and I resolved to offer my blood for the cause of legitimate
  monarch.

The Carlists claimed their cause was truly Spanish, unlike the ‘foreign-
ness’ of the Liberals. Yet his knowledge of European divisions inﬂected
Don Carlos’s proclamations against the Quadruple Alliance. The quar-
ter Zumalacárregui offered to Cristino prisoners after his victory at Los
Arcos was used by the King to counter the black legend his cause
70   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


attracted in the foreign press and capitals: ‘Europe does not know us and
yet pronounces without hearing us . . . and offers us nothing but unjust
hatred.’118 As we shall see in Chapter 5, far more foreign volunteers with
far more varied motives would soon join the Cristino side, with the
result that, by the summer of 1835, the expanded Spanish political and
military battleﬁelds were accompanied by an expanded international
civil war of ideologies and volunteers. Contemporaries certainly saw the
war as an international struggle between Liberty and Catholicism, not
unlike their great-grandchildren during the Spanish Civil War a century
later. According to three Aragonese Radicals, ‘the Spanish civil war was
not just a dynastic issue between Isabel II and her uncle, Don Carlos,
nor was it just a contest between despotism and liberty in Spain, but
also between Dom Miguel and María de la Gloria, and Henri V and
Louis-Philippe, between Liberal and Absolutist Europe’.119
   But, as with the Spanish Civil War a hundred years later, international
involvement had a ‘great distorting’ effect. The ﬁrst two years of Cristino
Spain, from the start of the war in October 1833 to the revolution of
the juntas in September 1835, were certainly marked by growing polar-
isation between Left (which tended towards republicanism in extreme
cases) and the Right (which in many cases leaned towards Carlism).
Leftist climates along the Mediterranean littoral (Barcelona, Málaga,
Seville) saw Cristino governors there champion reformist calls whilst
the inverse was true in such conservative cities in the north as Burgos
and Salamanca. But beneath this macropolitical trajectory, several thou-
sand local disputes were given a political dimension by the demands of
war. The Liberal postmaster of Valladolid had his house ransacked and
family abused by Merino’s royalist bands in 1822; in 1833 he faced the
same terror at Merino’s hands in 1833 when he refused to frank Carlist
mail.120 Women from the poor neighbourhoods in the south-west of
Madrid regularly invoked the cry of ‘Long Live Carlos V!’ in a primitive
protest against cholera – from which many of the wealthy classes had
ﬂed – and taxes.121 Journeymen resented master tradesmen who man-
aged to embellish their fortunes with Militia service, whilst unskilled
labourers (such as Madrid builders) jeered passing señoritos in uniform.122
Newspapers reported violence between militiamen and soldiers, which,
though sparked off by rivalry over women, in reality was caused by
resentment between those who had to go to the front and those who
could stay behind.123 The poor Triana neighbourhood of Seville saw riots
in protest against the cost of expanding local Militia forces and against
their insufferable behaviour strutting about in the name of liberty hun-
dreds of miles behind the front line, persecuting those without friends
                                              The Basque Phase, 1833–35   71


in authority. This tense situation was soon made worse by the execu-
tion of a cabal of conspirators led by a purged army ofﬁcer. They failed
to raise Seville for Don Carlos, but not before renewed riots and a pru-
dent decision by the Cristino authorities to execute the conspirators in
secret.124
   Tensions grew between mobilised militiamen and civilian popula-
tions in either front-line or bandit-infested areas. By the summer of
1834, Cuevillas had returned from Portugal and was invading several
rural communities in the province of Logroño, and Cristino mobile
Militia was sent out on a punitive expedition. Unable to catch up
with Cuevillas, the militia nonetheless ﬁned villages which ‘failed to
report’ the transit of Carlists, whilst militiamen were also entitled to
claim ‘expenses’ incurred whilst away on campaign.125 Militiamen sent
on fruitless campaigns to exterminate Carlist bands often vented their
frustrations on civilians. Irregular risings to the south of Madrid that
summer caused a tense atmosphere in Ciempozuelos, where the Urban
Militia rampaged in reprisal for the killing of one of their number in a
tavern. This arrogant paramilitarism was ﬂawed because it tied the exer-
cise of Liberal patriotism on terms favourable to the Militia to the use of
force. To some extent violence against non-combatants was understand-
able in a civil war which was both highly localised and asymmetrical.
But Cristino heavy-handedness often recoiled on the authorities. When
a June 1834 tax riot in Corella (Aragón) was brutally suppressed by
militiamen, the population responded with cries of ‘¡Viva Carlos V!’126
In Novelda (Alicante), local people proclaimed the Pretender in protest
at the continued imprisonment of hundreds of men who, in December
1833, had attempted a Carlist rising. In this case, the Militia likewise
brutally cleared the streets, banishing ﬁve ringleaders, and forbidding
meetings in the open.127 The terrible – and sometimes liberating –
choices that civilian communities were forced to make in this war would
be starkest during the years of ‘deep war’ between 1836 and 1837. In the
meantime, the civilian experience of the war, at the hands of both
Carlist and Cristino forces, created its own community of sacriﬁce which
had a distinctive impact on popular politics. Fundamentally, civilians
under Cristino control resented conscription. The ‘blood tax’ by the end
of 1834 was set at 8,000 reales, a sum beyond the means of all but a
select few, and draftees could not easily escape, as communities had to
provide replacements for any of their number who failed to enlist or
soldiers who subsequently deserted.128
   The blood sacriﬁces demanded by the Cristino regime poisoned the
popular reception of its Liberal project whilst also providing Radicals
72   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


with political oxygen. Popular disaffection was heightened because
Carlist violence obliged Liberals to explain often elite-driven reforms
in the strident language of liberty. In order to rally Spain to reject
Don Carlos, it was necessary to make the cause of the infant Isabella
that of liberty against despotism, constitutional government against
absolutism and civilisation against barbarism. Thus, the government
decision in February 1834 to create the Urban Militia on a very lim-
ited basis was quickly overturned by a press campaign, which saw this
force’s expansion as a tripwire against despotism. By the end of the
year the Militia contained about 200,000 effectives, one-third of whom
were unarmed.129 Political newspapers, which were permitted for the
ﬁrst time since the Triennium, exercised unprecedented importance
in radicalising the politics of the Cristino zone. Strict censorship laws
passed in January and June 1834 would be swept away in the revolu-
tion of 1836.130 The Eco del Comercio was mouthpiece to the Left-Liberals
(known as the exaltados – ‘hotheads’ – and, after 1836, progresistas),
whilst more radical, Republican journals emerged besides. The Right-
Liberals (moderados) were represented by a succession of titles (La Abeja,
El Español, El Castellano, El Correo Nacional, El Piloto). The civil war thus
ushered in a recognisably bipartisan political system. The Carlist press,
by contrast, constrained by territory and prior censorship throughout
the conﬂict, failed to evolve. Its ofﬁcial gazette, of which Metternich
despaired for its inquisitorial, sermon-like quality and blindness to
positive propaganda opportunities, was the Gaceta Oﬁcial.131 Just like
the Liberal press with its correspondence, the Carlist Gaceta ﬂourished
on the correspondence of fellow travellers, especially civic Carlists in
Cristino territory. This led Cristino authorities to destroy as many edi-
tions of the Gaceta as came into their hands.132 Liberal newspapers, by
contrast, due to their sheer quantity, inﬁltrated the Carlist zone and
permeated Cristino territory where they became a vehicle for political
parties and revolution.
   Newspaper opinion was central to pressurising María Cristina into
yielding to progressive reform. In April 1834, the Royal Statute (Estatuto
Real) was ‘granted’ by the Crown to the nation. But the bicameral Cortes
it established was even more restrictive with regards to suffrage than
the French Charte of 1814, and only 27 representatives of the 188-
strong lower house had been deputies during the Triennium, and fewer
still exaltados.133 Much of its conservatism was overruled by liberalising
decrees in 1835 before the Statute itself was abrogated the following
year, but this liberalism had to be wrested from a Queen-Regent who
remained almost absolutist in her political instincts. The war caused
                                              The Basque Phase, 1833–35   73


extra-parliamentary radicalism to re-emerge, the ﬁrst manifestation of
which was the Radical Isabelina Society. Government listlessness in
response to the anti-Jesuit massacres encouraged the Radical Isabelina
Society to act. Meeting in secret on 20 July whilst the cinders of Madrid’s
monasteries were still warm, the society’s leader, the now elderly Romero
Alpuente mobilised a petition of prominent leftists to the Queen-Regent,
saying that the Royal Statute was too restrictive and asking for it to be
replaced by a Constituent Assembly tasked with introducing a version
of the Belgian Constitution. The petitioners threatened street barricades
if these demands were not met. But whereas the government was pre-
pared to overlook anti-clerical violence, the threat to the monarchy
posed by advanced Liberal elites was another matter. The conspiracy
was quickly penetrated, but despite the arrest of Romero Alpuente and
such other Radical elites as the leader of the dissolved Santander junta,
Flórez Estrada, the network was so wide and well connected that only
Eugenio de Aviraneta e Ibargoyen, an experienced Liberal conspirator
and veteran of Spain’s last attempt to reconquer Mexico, was actually
convicted and imprisoned. Aviraneta was the easiest Isabelina leader-
ship scapegoat for the government. But Madrid’s Liberals did not forget
him. Aviraneta was allowed to keep abreast of politics from his prison
cell, a concession which, so Liberal opinion fumed, had to be wrenched
from the Apostolic ‘Junta of Selva’, a group of Carlist political prison-
ers who informally determined which privileges and punishments their
fellow captives might receive.134 The notion of Carlist prisoners running
a prison in the Cristino capital is almost as striking as the subsequent
story of Aviraneta, whom the 1835 revolution would free from captivity
and elevate to the leading counter-espionage role in Liberal politics.
   The Carlist state would also become politically divided, between
theocratic and enlightened absolutist factions, jostling in a notori-
ously Byzantine court. Indeed, Don Carlos’s civilian court faced several
problems improving Zumalacárregui’s military state. Lacking formal
diplomatic recognition, even from sympathetic absolutist powers, the
Carlists lacked predictable foreign ﬁnancial support. The Carlist com-
mander in Catalonia, the ill-fated Count Romagosa, may have managed
to secure a substantial donation from Turin, but British markets were
the most lucrative, and this foremost ally of Cristino Spain blocked
most private Carlist efforts to tap London loan markets. Privately con-
tracted arms shipments frequently failed to reach the Carlists either
because cargoes were impounded in French and British ports or because
they were intercepted by Quadruple Alliance seapower before they could
reach the safety of the Carlist-held coastline.135 Therefore, populations
74   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


under Carlist control had to shoulder the burden, which meant Navarra,
Alava, Vizcaya and Guipúzcoa (in that descending order of magnitude).
A junta collected income from customs posts which the Carlists had
seized along the French frontier, monies were raised from Basque and
Navarrese clergy, especially during Lent (‘a crisis time for the cause of
Isabel II’136 ), titles and ofﬁces were sold, and other income was found
in ﬁnes, both in an ad hoc (i.e. against local Cristino sympathisers) and
organised basis, such as the 20,000 duros ﬁne imposed on Cristino com-
munities that came under Carlist occupation in the Baztán. Don Carlos’s
arrival had brought some new money from international sympathisers,
but also court sinecures who frittered away the budget.137 Nominally, the
Carlist war economy was dwarfed by the economic and demographic
potential of the Cristino zone, even though revolution there meant that
this was not optimised. Carlist geostrategic inferiority could only be
overcome by formal and substantial diplomatic support. And this, in
turn, could only be won by decisive victories on the battleﬁeld to make
foreign powers view the Carlists as a regime in waiting instead of a mere
rural insurgency.
   The year 1835 seemed to promise the Carlists such an outcome.
Bloody three-day actions opened on New Year’s Day at Celandieta and
Ormáiztegui were defensive victories for Zumalacárregui (they were
falsely reported as Cristino victories in San Sebastián where the Mili-
tia had to rescind victory celebrations).138 Zumalacárregui intensiﬁed
pressure on besieged Cristino towns by employing guerrilla forces. The
Cristino army could win few victories, and those it did win were
ineluctably skewed by political and careerist considerations. General
Córdova, whose later leadership of the Army of the North I address
in Chapter 5, felt his role in the victory had been downplayed and
offered Madrid his resignation in protest; Madrid appeased him with
the comandancia of the Basque provinces, replacing the American wars
veteran, Canterac, who was made Captain-General of Madrid. Political
pressure, buoyed by a radicalised press, exhorted Cristino commanders
to win victories no matter what the cost. Zumalacárregui was dislodged
by overwhelming enemy numbers at Orbiso on 17 January 1835, and
this was subsequently celebrated as a Cristino victory, even though
the Cristinos then abandoned their useless territorial gain, allowing
Zumalacárregui to claim the ﬁeld.139 Two weeks later Zumalacárregui
repulsed a Cristino attempt to seize the bridge at Arquijas, in cir-
cumstances which summed up the Cristino quandary: Córdova and
Oráa saw their communications paralysed by Carlist interception, and
                                             The Basque Phase, 1833–35   75


Oráa ended up advancing onto the wrong enemy ﬂank, allowing
Zumalacárregui to defeat both armies in detail.140
   Whilst the Cristino army was bogged down, the Carlists were suc-
cessful in a series of localised offensives to expand their hold on the
Basque country. In the front line was the Baztán, whose hard-pressed
Militia felt abandoned by the Cristino army. Elizondo was terrorised by
artillery bombardment: such was the panic that the town’s women were
allowed to leave to take their chances with the surrounding Carlists.
The town made desperate appeals for reinforcements from Pamplona
but Espoz, conscious of Pamplona’s precarious situation, at ﬁrst rejected
these. Espoz himself begged Madrid for more reinforcements, but those
that arrived were of insufﬁcient quantity and quality.141 War Minister
Llauder, himself so recently a royalist and accustomed to seeing Espoz
as a revolutionary, was reluctant to send him reinforcements, and did
not believe his remark that ‘it did not matter whether Juan or Pedro
are War Minister as I know how to obey’.142 Rather, Llauder was one of
the ﬁrst moderados to utter a preference for an international rather than
national solution to the conﬂict, precisely so as to avoid Espoz’s revo-
lutionism. Espoz would soon have his revenge by publicly condemning
the leftist Cardero rising of January 1835, thereby placing the blame
for its success entirely on Llauder’s shoulders.143 Meanwhile, as besieged
Elizondo began to starve, pressure mounted on Espoz to send a relief
force, which he scraped together by mobilising Pamplona’s militia cav-
alry. But this suffered a reverse at Larráinzar, where Espoz himself was
almost captured by the Carlists after a series of errors which histori-
ans have explained along partisan lines.144 Espoz eventually relieved
Elizondo and made a brutal example of the nearby village of Lekaroz,
which had been a Carlist thorn in the Cristino side of the Baztán cap-
ital, executing three male residents and relenting only when villagers
marked for execution disclosed the whereabouts of three buried Carlist
cannon. Carlists condemned Espoz for repeating his terrorism during
the Triennium at Castellfullit. But to the Carlist cry to avenge Lekaroz,
the Cristinos answered with Villafranca. Espoz defended his ruthless-
ness as stiffening the resolve of front-line Cristino communities and
intimidating other Carlist villages.145
   But a pattern had emerged whereby a Cristino local victory in one area
would be cancelled out by two defeats elsewhere. Whilst Espoz relieved
Elizondo, Zumalacárregui took Los Arcos after a bloody siege involving
artillery which the Cristino garrison – accustomed to seeing the enemy
as insurgents rather than a regular army – assumed the Carlists could not
76   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


have. Unlike at Villafranca, the Cristinos yielded the town, evacuating
safely under cover of night and rainfall. Whilst the Cristino rearguard
fought off Zumalacárregui’s cavalry pursuit, the vanguard faced the
wrath of Espoz, who arrested the ‘cowardly’ garrison commander and
his ofﬁcers.146 Espoz knew that the Carlist ring was tightening on the
sensitive Baztán valley, but his exhortations for a maximum effort
sounded hollow as defeatism swept his ranks. When Zumalacárregui,
on 19 March, made a second attempt at Echarri-Aranaz on the key
Pamplona–Vitoria road, the town yielded after a short bombardment
from El Abuelo, the Cristino ofﬁcers accepting terms to keep their swords
and withdraw to Pamplona, whilst the subaltern ranks (including the
artillery), who were also free to go, chose to serve under the Carlists
rather than their own ofﬁcers. These defectors were amazed to discover
the still shoestring nature of the attack: El Abuelo’s bore had worn out
just as the garrison accepted terms (Carlist soldiers joked that it had gone
quiet due to it being ‘promoted’ to lieutenant-colonel), whilst the par-
simonious Zumalacárregui uncharacteristically bribed the inhabitants
with scarce money in lieu of sustaining a full garrison of occupation.
He had now consolidated his communications between Guipúzcoa and
Navarra; Espoz, by contrast, had to abandon and destroy the nearby
fort at Olazagutía, which had now been rendered indefensible.147 Espoz
thus lost hope in destroying the Carlist army in open battle, and instead
focused on holding fortiﬁed towns, and absorbing virtually all his ener-
gies escorting supply convoys. And when fresh troops became available
for a major offensive in April (see below), they failed to wrest the initia-
tive from the Carlists. For the Carlists, meanwhile, only one prize now
mattered.
   Carlist legend says that Zumalacárregui opposed the decision to
besiege Bilbao. Rather, Vitoria, so Zumalacárregui thought, was an eas-
ier target, whose fall could easily be followed by a general offensive
towards Madrid. As he would be killed during this siege, it was com-
forting for the Carlists to read infallibility – and martyrdom – into their
fallen leader. Certainly, the overruling of Zumalacárregui on the Bilbao
question gave the court a pretext for bringing its commander-in-chief
to heel. But early events also strengthened the court’s case. Bilbao was
blockaded from the land as early as March 1835, and illness and exhaus-
tion forced Espoz to resign his command only weeks later. This deprived
the Cristino cause of an experienced counter-insurgent strategist and
Basque speaker. Also, the Bilbao operation was led by Zumalacárregui’s
second-in-command, the very able Francisco Benito de Eraso, who had
fought Espoz during 1822–23 and again in 1830, and who probed the
                                             The Basque Phase, 1833–35   77


weaknesses in Espoz’s strategy by dispatching guerrillas to harass multi-
ple Cristino fortiﬁed centres simultaneously, always reserving his main
effort for Bilbao. Eraso was aided both by increasing defections from
Cristino ranks (during the ﬁrst ten days of March alone some 76 men
and an ofﬁcer defected) and by crisis within Bilbao. The Basque capital
was in the grip of an economic crisis caused by the inﬂux of refugees,
mainly from the exposed coastal towns of Portugalete, Bermeo and
Lequeitio, and by the paralysis caused by rural Carlism to local eco-
nomic activity.148 The world anticipated some sort of decision at the
Vizacayan capital; Carlists indeed hoped that their capture of this port
would trigger decisive aid from Metternich’s Europe. International inter-
est in Bilbao also magniﬁed reports of atrocities. On one occasion some
37 Cristino soldiers captured by the besiegers outside the city walls were
shot in reprisal for the killing of three wounded Carlists. The Carlists
castrated some of their victims, as if playing out the emasculation trope
of dynastic propaganda.149
   Mounting atrocities like these alarmed European opinion, and caused
the British expert on Spanish affairs, Lord Wellington, to dispatch a
humanitarian mission under the politician and diplomat, Lord Eliot,
and his adjunct, the Peninsular War veteran, Colonel John Gurwood.
The ‘Eliot Treaty’ which resulted from this mission gave quarter to
regular Carlist and Cristino troops who surrendered in the Basque
country, allocating them mutually recognised prisoner-of-war camps, or
‘depots’, from which they were to be exchanged at regular intervals.
Both Zumalacárregui and Cristino War Minister Valdés agreed that the
treaty could be ‘extended if the war reaches other places’, even though
the Cristinos were horriﬁed by this prospect.150 In fact, this humanitar-
ian mission by their Quadruple Alliance ally poisoned Cristino politics,
especially later in the war when pressure grew from some quarters
to extend the treaty to the irregular war raging in Catalonia and the
Maestrazgo. Both Left and Right Liberals protested that the treaty treated
the Carlists as equals, and even worked in their favour as their forces
were numerically inferior. Left Liberals protested that the treaty allowed
the Carlists to exclude militiamen, foreigners and Cristino free corps
from quarter, licensing them in particular to massacre any militiamen
they captured. As one Bilbao Radical complained whilst his city was
under Carlist siege: ‘here is a treaty which carefully excludes the mili-
tia, the very pith of the popular cause . . . The Spanish Generals ought
to have spurned the mere idea of bargaining for their own safety at the
cost of the families who fed, clothed, and paid them.’151 Other Liber-
als in safe urban centres shared the same indignation in the press.152
78   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


The treaty touched a raw nerve in terms of inter-service rivalry, and
the behaviour of Spanish ofﬁcers increased leftist grievances. Episodes
abounded of Cristino ofﬁcers’ callousness towards the Liberals’ Mili-
tia. When the Cristino commander of Ochandiano surrendered his
town to Zumalacárregui, he negotiated the safe handover of his horses
but neglected to negotiate anything for the town’s Militia garrison.153
Episodes likes these fed a popular martyrdom complex and primed the
‘people-in-arms’ to start the revolution of the juntas in 1835. In the
meantime, the Eliot Treaty could not have come at a worse time for
Cristino fortunes, for Valdés signed it only days after having left his
Ministry to replace Espoz in the command of the Army of the North in
order to mount what turned out to be a disastrous offensive.
   On 18 April 1835, the Army of the North mounted the biggest cam-
paign of the war so far, involving some 21,000 Cristino soldiers, which
led on 22 April to the Battle of Améscoas. Valdés’s offensive aimed to
destroy the Carlist army in its ‘den’ of the valley of Améscoas in Navarra,
which Zumalacárregui had developed as a redoubt in the early days of
the insurgency. The army invaded as pincers, one arm led by Valdés
and the other by Córdova from his base at Vitoria. The Cristinos’ 34
battalions vastly outnumbered the 11 which Zumalacárregui had com-
mitted for defence (his main effort was massing against Bilbao) but
the Carlists’ signature tactics of ambush and superior local intelligence
hampered the invaders’ progress. Early success by Córdova’s pincer at
destroying Carlist depots proved a false dawn when his men lost disci-
pline and gorged on the supplies Carlist civilian evacuees had stashed
in oak boughs. Both Cristino armies lost around half their effectives to
desertion during the two-week campaign, and these losses were com-
pounded by suffering as the Cristinos’ ﬂimsy footwear broke on the
notoriously tough limestone terrain and their supplies broke down.
The hunger of Valdés’s men drove most of their effectives away from
the planned route and towards the Carlist capital of Estella instead.
Even though they occupied Estella, their ignorance of local geogra-
phy and a complete breakdown of communications caused the Cristino
columns to ﬁre on each other the whole night before the occupation.
Meanwhile, Zumalacárregui’s rearguard wore out Córdova’s force with
repeated ambushes, especially at night, which meant the Cristino force
which reached Artaza, the gateway to the Améscoas valley, on 22 April,
was hungry, sleep-deprived and diminished. Zumalacárregui issued a
halt order, and repulsed the Cristino attack despite his three-to-one
inferiority, inﬂicting over 700 fatalities. The Cristino line broke and
the demoralised soldiers ﬂed for their lives, vanishing into the hills,
                                              The Basque Phase, 1833–35   79


some ending up being lynched by enraged Basque villagers, and oth-
ers reaching the temporary safety of Estella. The Army of the North
was spent, and even worse it had not even drawn any Carlist battal-
ions away from their offensive against Bilbao.154 As the English Carlist
veteran Henningsen noted:

   The high expectations entertained in both London and Madrid, of
   what Valdés was to do against the faction, were crushed, particularly
   when it was known that Zumalacárregui had overthrown him with
   eleven battalions, having in the provinces twenty-eight under his
   command, if he had chosen to bring them up; but that while he
   effected this, every fortiﬁed place was as strictly blockaded as ever.155

The defeat at the Améscoas valley handed the initiative to the Carlists,
who now embarked upon a general counteroffensive. Espoz’s recent
strategy of mobile warfare to retain as much territory as possible was
now impossible to sustain, and by the end of May Cristino forces were
driven from all of the Basque country outside the fortiﬁed capitals of
Pamplona, Vitoria, San Sebastián and Bilbao. The greatest loss was the
Baztán valley and the huge stretch of sensitive French border it com-
manded. The evacuation of the Baztán was followed by that of all
Cristino fortiﬁed centres in Navarra outside of Pamplona; the Carlists
occupied them and sometimes even did not even bother to harass
the retreating Cristinos. Liberals, especially those enrolled in Elizondo’s
Militia, felt abandoned and fearful of Carlist reprisals: Oráa called an
extra-ordinary session of the Baztán town hall in order to allay their fears
of the army’s retreat. It was to no avail. This episode crystallised an ide-
ological divide between the army and the militia which had, of course,
long distinguished Spanish liberalism but which now, in the context
of brutal civil war, became an anguished question of survival. Unlike
the Militia, which answered to local government, the army followed
a chain of command deaf to local interests. The militia, by contrast,
was a localised force that in the Carlist north made more enemies than
friends. The army’s retreat from the Baztán turned into a rout in wet
weather, with swollen streams hampering the retreat of often barefoot
soldiers. And the situation for the Cristinos elsewhere in the Basque
country was equally grim. In late April, General Iriarte was defeated at
Guernica, losing almost 500 in action and countless others when sol-
diers careered off a bridge into a fast-ﬂowing river. Captured ofﬁcers were
shot in reprisal for a recent Cristino atrocity elsewhere.156 The only ofﬁ-
cer who stemmed the offensive was Espartero, who, at Rentería, halted
80   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Iriarte’s ﬂight and reoccupied Guernica. A grateful government pro-
moted Espartero to Comandante General of the Basque provinces.157 But
the cupboard was bare when compared to the prizes on the Carlist side.
Zumalacárregui took Treviño (an exclave of Castile inside the Basque
country) after an almost bloodless siege. Its demoralised garrison of both
soldiers and Militia was happy to surrender on the Carlists’ word that the
new Eliot Treaty would be binding on both forces. Zumalacárregui now
had truly contiguous territory under his command with all its economic,
demographic and military potential, and he justiﬁed his overlordship
using the language of a Christian soldier.158 He implored his prisoners
to renounce revolution and embrace Catholicism and King Carlos V.
Learning of his commander’s ﬂourish, the pious Don Carlos offered
Zumalacárregui a noble title: Duke of Améscoas, but Zumalacárregui
coyly replied that he would ‘accept this honour once the Carlists are
marching into Cádiz’.159 But despite Zumalacárregui’s plans for Vitoria,
the court had resolved that the path to Spain’s Babylon lay through
Bilbao. Only Bilbao now stood between the status of Carlism as a rural
insurgency and Carlism as a regime-in-waiting.
5
The War Radicalises Cristino Spain,
1835–36




This chapter charts the Carlist recovery from their failure to take Bilbao
(ﬁrst siege) and the death of Zumalacárregui, including Carlist territo-
rial expansion in the Basque country, the Maestrazgo and Catalonia.
It explains how Carlist military success was both a cause and a con-
sequence of the Radical-Liberal revolutions in the Cristino zone of
September 1835. It shows how this leftist shift in politics was fed
by a popular anti-militarism and anti-clericalism, which, with varied
results, forced the hand of the Radical Prime Minister, Mendizábal.
Whilst his anti-clerical reforms solidiﬁed Cristino support at the expense
of provoking support for Carlism, his 100,000-man levy provoked an
expanded pattern of desertion, particularly because of the peculiar sup-
ply problems the Cristino army faced. It shows how the war radicalised
international opinion and led to volunteers enlisting for both sides,
especially the Cristino auxiliaries who helped defeat Bilbao’s second
siege, and explains their impact on Spanish politics and society.
   The Cristino army now abandoned all of the Basque country apart
from San Sebastián, Bilbao and Pamplona, the front line being estab-
lished at Miranda del Ebro. The Carlists thus gained population, money
and other war-ready resources. Eraso already had Bilbao under siege:
now the Carlist cry went up of ‘To Bilbao!’ Eight artillery pieces were
trained on the capital of Vizcaya, and shortages, if not outright hunger,
began to envelop the city. Carlist women followed the besieging army
in the hope of entering Bilbao for plunder.1 Count Mirasol, Cristino mil-
itary governor of the province of Vizcaya, came from second-generation
military aristocracy and was no natural friend of the popular cause. Yet,
on 18 June 1835, he made a stirring speech to the Urban Militia of
Bilbao, telling them they had ‘nothing to learn from the army’. The

                                   81
82   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


walls were breached on 14 June, but the Militia managed to secure the
gap. Even though the Carlists, as always, managed to work miracles with
their limited artillery, two of their guns were so worn that their bores
split. The Carlists were too exhausted to continue the assault on the
night of 14 June, and the Cristino batteries opened up a counter-ﬁre.
Mirasol had scoured the northern front for spare artillery, as far aﬁeld
as San Sebastián. British and French gunboats were also active in the
River Nervión, and the allies accepted Mirasol’s request to strip their
vessels of some of their guns in order to bolster the defences of the city.
Mirasol’s resourcefulness was matched by a chance catastrophe in the
enemy camp. On 18 June, Zumalacárregui was wounded outside the
walls and died a week later.2 Zumalacárregui’s death turned him into
a Carlist legend, at the right hand of such patriotic ﬁgures as Pelayo
and El Cid.3 Carlism was deprived of its ﬁrst and last undisputed leader,
and no sooner had Zumalacárregui died that rumours abounded that he
had been poisoned by Don Carlos’s court.4 It was therefore strange that
early Cristino responses included panic as well as triumphalism. The
consulate at Bayonne repeated ill-digested rumours that the Quadru-
ple Alliance was abandoning plans to reinforce the Cristinos with some
3,000 Polish émigré infantry who had been awaiting embarkation from
Southampton; news of Zumalacárregui’s death, it seemed, convinced
Paris and London that the war would now be short and that interven-
tion was both unnecessary and dangerous in view of renewed legitimist
plots in France.5 In fact, Liberal Polonophilia in the wake of the recent
Russian repression of Poland had exaggerated the real ﬁgure of 500
infantry, and these Poles would still reach Spain as part of the French
Foreign Legion (and they would be joined the following year by three
Polish cavalry squadrons).6 Rumours and half-truths shaped the Carlist
War like no other.
   Cristno spirits were raised when a relief force led by General Manuel
Latre, a veteran of the Riego revolution, forced a gap in the Carlist
siege line. On 26 June, Don Carlos appeared in person before Bilbao,
but this emboldened rather intimidated the defenders. When a particu-
larly savage bombardment on the morning of the 27th opened a breach
in the walls, into it sprang soldiers and the artillerymen of the Urban
Militia, even British Royal Marines, the shock troops of the Quadruple
Alliance, all of whom displayed doggedly high morale, shouting vivas
to Isabel II and liberty.7 Seeing this resolve, on 27 June Eraso made a
plea for the besieged to surrender, promising even to show mercy to
the Militia as the Carlists recently had at Villafranca and Vergara. But
the resolve of the Militia, Quadruple Alliance and even Bilbao women
                                  War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   83


who answered the sound of shellﬁre with piano music and dancing,
emboldened Mirasol to reject capitulation.8
   The Carlist siege was formally broken by the arrival, on 30 June, of
a Cristino relief force led by Córdova, acting head of the Army of the
North. The Carlists had ﬁred a total of 1,580 projectiles into Bilbao,
and, in all, more than 200 soldiers and militiamen had been killed or
wounded (no exact ﬁgures exist for civilians).9 On the one hand, the
Carlists had been defeated by the Cristino defence: Bilbao’s Militia had
excelled in urban point defence, giving time for the relief force, and
the propaganda value of the Liberal ‘people in arms’ surged in the wake
of the victory.10 On the other hand, the Carlists also defeated them-
selves via internal dissent. The Carlist court was riven with intrigue and
animosity. Maroto and Eraso disliked each other whilst Villemur, the
French émigré of the 1790s and now Carlist prime minister (‘Universal
Minister’) disliked Zumalacárregui. These court divisions in part resulted
in the Cristino relief force from Portugalete relieving Bilbao. Sturdier
siegeworks and more intense bombardment might have taken the city,
so the Carlists reckoned.
   Meanwhile, the death of Zumalacárregui and the victory at Bilbao
gave the Cristino side the initiative for the ﬁrst time since the Améscoas
offensive. Strategically, the situation showed promise: on 17 March
1835, Madrid had decreed the creation of a Reserve Army comprising
both regular troops and, controversially for the Cristino Left, mobile
militia, to be based in Castile to combat both Carlist incursions from
Navarra and guerrilla activity behind the front line. Valdés’s failure to
lead the lifting of the siege of Bilbao had cost him his job, and on
2 July 1835, he was replaced as commander-in-chief by Luis Fernández
de Córdova.11 A royalist during the Triennium and diplomat during the
Ominous Decade, Córdova’s career was tarnished by a notorious alterca-
tion in 1831 with Prime Minister Calomarde. But Córdova returned to
favour after the transition and became an intimate ally of the Queen-
Regent.12 For the ﬁrst time since the appointment of Espoz the previous
November, the Cristinos had a commander with real vision, albeit a very
different one (Córdova planned a static war of attrition). The Carlists,
by contrast, never found a replacement equal to Zumalacárregui. The
hero’s successor, Vicente González Moreno, was 58 years old – almost
20 years older than Córdova. Moreno’s Ominous Decade had honoured
the Liberal black legend: as Captain-General of Granada he had presided
over the execution of Torrijos. The ‘Hangman of Málaga’ also alienated
his own side: in 1827, he had supported Calomarde’s anti-Carlism and
had timorously ﬂed possible retribution from vengeful Liberals after
84   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


being purged of his captaincy-general in October 1832. Moreno thus
needed some military success to restore Carlist fortunes and silence his
detractors.13
   Moreno advanced the Carlist army to Mendigorría (Navarra), rashly
joining battle with the Cristinos in a tactically weak position, as the
River Arga lay behind his troops with only one bridge allowing for
the contingency of retreat. Thus battle opened on 16 July 1835, and
Córdova won his ﬁrst victory as commander-in-chief, even though his
cavalry failed to exploit this in pursuit. For the ﬁrst time, Carlist casual-
ties (some 2,000 dead) signiﬁcantly exceeded those of the Cristinos, who
lost about half this toll.14 Moreno’s hopes of silencing his critics were
thus dashed. It was suggested that Moreno hoped his reckless deploy-
ment would intimidate an enemy which had been showing every sign
of nerves, and yield the possibility of a general invasion of Castile.15
Don Carlos, who had never forgotten Moreno’s betrayal during the
Agraviados, replaced him with the ambitious Nazario Eguía, who was
of a similar age but was a career ofﬁcer and a better tactician.16
   Even though Córdova was feted in Pamplona as a hero, the strate-
gic situation was not signiﬁcantly changed. Despite Cristino defensive
victories at Bilbao and Mendigorría, the Army of the North remained
chronically undersupplied, only limited help came from outside Spain
and poor conditions made an ideal environment for Radical agitators
to subvert the army. Córdova repeatedly offered his resignation in dis-
gust but Madrid neither called his bluff nor managed to attend to his
needs. The ﬁrst problem was that the granaries and other foodstores
established in northern forts were either abandoned or empty. The sec-
ond problem was the corrupt and chaotic system of private contractors.
The government regularly defaulted on its payments to the contractors,
or asentistas, and these in turn did not supply the army, or at best, supply
would be partial, erratic and unpredictable. The largest contractor of this
chaotic scheme was Agustín Alinary, and as little as 30 per cent of his
contracted supplies actually reached the army. The third problem was
that it proved difﬁcult to surmount this shortfall by living off the land.
Cristino invaders in Navarra seldom found booty as Carlist defenders
were supplied at short range and the Royal Government issued decrees
against the hoarding of foodstuffs (Carlists invading Castile, by con-
trast, could get booty). Córdova thus had to show an administrative
and political acumen beyond his relatively young years. On the one
hand, he coerced Cristino civilian populations in his zone to shoulder
the burden of supply. This won him the loyalty of his soldiers but not of
civilians. For operations either on the actual front or in hostile territory,
                                  War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   85


Córdova contracted 1,000 muleteers, of whom some 700 presented for
work (not enough, but a valuable addition nonetheless). But as these
men routinely went unpaid, or were paid only in arrears, most ended
up deserting. Córdova blamed the political leadership for these failings,
and owing to the revolution of the juntas in September 1835, his sup-
ply situation deteriorated still further as Radical juntas exercised their
‘sovereignty’.17
   The everyday life of the Cristino soldier and the ofﬁcer on campaign
was thus precarious in the extreme. Letters to ofﬁcers from loved ones
at best arrived twice per week when soldiers were in garrison, and not at
all when they were on campaign. Relatives might send mail to fortresses
and towns which approximated the soldiers’ whereabouts, but there was
no guarantee that recipients would pass through.18 Billets were dreary
and unwelcoming: Pamplona, Vitoria and even San Sebastián lay under
close blockade. Only Bilbao, with its busy port and entertainments, was
a popular posting, at least for ofﬁcers. But as for the ranks, foreign
observers of both Cristino and Carlist sympathies agreed upon their dire
state. Pro-Carlist Honan remarked: ‘(Cristino conscripts) were chieﬂy
boys from sixteen to twenty years; many of them in tatters, and present-
ing most ungainly specimens. They took all opportunities of deserting,
and some old soldiers escorted them from town to town to prevent it.’19
A British Legion ofﬁcer in San Sebastián, for his part, saw wonders in his
allies’ predicament:20

  The Queen’s recruits are mainly short, poorly clothed and worse fed.
  It is a mystery to me how any man can survive on their fare: meatless
  rations of rice some days, boiled vegetables with an ounce of bacon
  on others, coarse dark bread, no wine, and the meanest serving of
  rough spirits on Sundays.

In fact, this diet compared favourably with that of the civilian popula-
tion: it has been estimated that in 1834 only some 13 per cent of Spain’s
population, some 1,550,000 out of 12,000,000, ate meat as part of their
diet.21 But food supplies were erratic, especially at times of revolution,
which fed a spiral of popular radicalisation, and the war for supplies was
a constant Achilles heel for the Cristinos.
   Meanwhile, politics radicalised in Cristino Spain. A pre-emptive
rightist coup was plotted by Manuel Llauder: the ministerial La Abeja
newspaper, on 18 January 1835, called for a military dictator to come
to power and end the war. Llauder, meanwhile, ordered a levy of 25,000
men into the army and that martial law be imposed on the Urban Militia
86   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


to replace troops in fortiﬁed areas.22 This threat spurred a leftist coup
forged by the Isabelina and led by a junior rather than senior ofﬁcer, Lt.
Cayetano Cardero, a man with unlimited reserves of ambition. Such vet-
erans of the 1834 Isabelina as Quiroga and Palarea were to be assigned
to captaincy-generalships, whilst the cry that went up in the centre of
Madrid on 18 January was ‘Down with Llauder!’ Llauder, occupying the
all-powerful War Ministry, had got wind of the plot, including its exact
details, through his ﬂourishing network of spies and informers. But,
refusing to stop a ball being held in honour of the Queen-Regent, he
self-assuredly predicted that the plot would fail. But the Madrid rising of
18 January went far beyond Llauder’s expectations. The Captain-General
of Madrid, Canterac, was drawn into negotiations as a stand-off emerged
outside the Post Ofﬁce on the Puerta del Sol, the centre of the revolt.
Seeing that the militia had gone over to the rebellion, Canterac voiced
an appeal for the rebels to unite behind the Royal Statute, but their
response was ‘Long live liberty!’ Canterac then made an appeal to the
loyalty of a palace regiment, but this was misheard as a Carlist cry.23
A hail of bullets was the response from the rebels, who took little con-
