vincing to suspect ofﬁcial collusion with the enemy, and Canterac died
instantly.24 Seeing that they had crossed the Rubicon, Cardero’s men
returned to fortify themselves in the Post Ofﬁce whilst pro-government
troops descended on the centre and opened ﬁre. Several bystanders,
especially the ubiquitous aguadores, or water-sellers, were caught in the
crossﬁre. Madrid, which had already suffered cholera and the anti-
Jesuit massacres, had now become an open battleground between the
ministerial and radical wings of Cristino liberalism.
   Eventually a ‘no-winners-no-losers’ truce was negotiated, and Cardero
was even allowed to send a petition to the nearby Cortes (the former
Radical, Argüelles, contrived to ruin the document by spilling ink over
it). Cardero and his men marched out of Madrid towards Navarra, draw-
ing the cheers of a large part of the population. At Burgos Cardero
was taken aside from his detachment and dispatched to ‘safety’ in
the Balearic Islands. But real damage had been done to the Martínez
de la Rosa government. Whereas Liberal writers explained Cardero’s
actions generously in idealistic terms, conservatives condemned the
moral defeat the rising imposed on lawfulness and respectability, and
the political cover it gave to urban criminality which had been rising
since the anti-clerical massacres of July 1834.25 In any case, such was the
powerlessness of the government that all those soldiers involved were
pardoned, Cardero even being promoted, after which, like Riego before
him, he entered the Cortes; by contrast, his soldiers were sent to the
                                  War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   87


front.26 Riding the tiger of army grievances, Cardero went on to become
a darling of the Left, and the nemesis of conservative army elites.
   In the wake of this rising a memorable debate ensued in the Cortes
in which Palarea, a mooted beneﬁciary of the rising, bizarrely attributed
the 18 January events to a Carlist plot, whilst Alcalá Galiano and others
condemned it as showing how honest, liberty-loving soldiers and militi-
amen had been led astray. The rightist Liberal, Fernández de Córdova, by
contrast, was scathing both of Cardero’s careerism and of Llauder’s weak-
ness in allowing the insurgents to march out unpunished. All pointed
the ﬁnger at Manuel Llauder, who had combined Bonapartist vainglory
with shocking timing and incompetence. Llauder was dismissed from
the War Ministry and dispatched with a token decoration to captain the
generals in Catalonia. His replacement was Jerónimo Valdés, a man of
the Left, whose reputation had grown as Captain-General of Valencia,
in which role he had virtually wiped out the insurgency, and who had
acted as temporary Commander-in-Chief of the Army of the North in
April 1835.27 When the speaker of the Cortes on 21 January made a
widely reported speech condemning the heavy-handed treatment of
Liberals across Spain, including their imprisonment in Cartagena for
singing the Riego Hymn, it was clear that the Cardero rising had shifted
Cristino politics to the Left.28
   Madrid remained in a state of heightened tension for months. A riot
on 11 May 1835 provoked by the Eliot Treaty led to rioters surround-
ing the Cortes in protest against Martínez de la Rosa, who narrowly
avoided being lynched by indignant Urban Militia when he alighted
from his coach. The capital was becoming ever more radicalised, as
remaining royalists found that they could not walk the streets in safety.29
Popular royalism had been greatly diminished by the Bourbon feud
which saw Don Carlos rebelling in the north and the emigration of
other family members, leaving María Cristina and her two daughters
alone in the capital to negotiate the leftist shift in Cristino politics.30
Thus, a leftist junior ofﬁcer and his network of Liberal conspirators had
caused the death of Madrid’s captain-general, the humiliation of the War
Minister, an embarrassed debate in the Cortes and a terminally weak-
ened moderado administration that was unable to survive any further
radicalisation.31 For a while it even seemed as though the controversial
question of foreign mediation, in the form of the Eliot Treaty, could be
such a spark. Despite the Francophile de la Rosa’s constant reluctance
to see British-led foreign intervention, the government now sought it,
citing relevant clauses in the Quadruple Alliance which had been signed
the previous April. It was desperately hoped that even the symbolic
88   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


showing of the Royal Navy ﬂag off the northern coast would cow the
rebels, and also that the French would send in an army.32 But Britain
would not move. Westminster knew that greater intervention in Spain
would need another Act of Parliament and that this would be rejected
by a resurgent Tory opposition, in both chambers, which was either neu-
tral or Carlist in sympathy. If the reactionary Northern Powers tried to
put Charles X on the French throne, then Britain would act, but for the
time being Britain thought that the Spanish Liberals could manage by
themselves. The only olive branch was an undertaking not to oppose
a stronger intervention from the French side. But France would only
intervene after a British security guarantee against the Northern Powers,
which was not forthcoming. The absence of an interventionist French
army, which Interior Minister Thiers desired for purely expansionist rea-
sons, fatally weakened the de la Rosa government and strengthened the
hand of the Radicals.33
   Meanwhile, the Cristino home front beyond Madrid underwent radi-
calisation. In March 1835, Málaga saw riots provoked by the supposedly
lenient treatment of a few Carlists behind bars and the militarisation
of the citizens’ force for service in the Serranía de Ronda. The mili-
tary governor and de la Rosa’s relative, Nicolás de Isidro purged the
citizens’ force of troublemakers, only to ﬁnd a trenchant rival in the
civil governor, who publicly sided with the militia. The clash between
the heavy-handed military governor and the more conciliatory civil
governor resulted in a power vacuum which the militia ﬁlled. Fearing
that what had happened in Madrid could follow in Málaga, Isidro tried
to disperse the crowd, only to be thwarted by a local agitator, Miguel
Deomarco (a tinker by trade and progenitor to a Radical dynasty in
Málaga), whose cry of ‘¡Viva la Constitución!’ rallied the militia to defy
martial law. The following day saw a funeral cortège of a dead mili-
tiaman pass by Isidro’s residence, playing the Riego Hymn. An order
the following day prohibiting subversive music and establishing a cur-
few went unheeded, and the situation spiralled quickly out of control.34
When, on 22 March, the militiamen were summoned to hear a War Min-
istry circular about discipline, they broke ranks and marched into the
town square shouting vivas to the Constitution. Three days of stand-
off followed, as rival factions patrolled the streets: the civil power led
by the militia who besieged Isidro’s residence, and the military power,
which had recruited a posse of some wealthy individuals who had ‘a
decided interest in re-establishing order’. Yet on 24 March, it was the
militia which triumphed, as Isidro suffered a negative pronunciamiento in
his ranks and was forced to resign and ﬂee to Granada. The replacement
                                   War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   89


governor did the Radicals’ bidding, presiding over a tablet-laying cer-
emony honouring the Constitution of 1812 and collecting signatures
for a petition to the Queen-Regent protesting the states of siege. The
moderado government was losing control of its rearguard.35
   Málaga, one of the most Liberal cities in the kingdom, had been rad-
icalised by a civil war whose front line lay hundreds of miles away,
and both central and regional authorities were heeding its example.
Ofﬁcers needed special leave to enter Madrid and a mass purge of the
Urban Militia was decreed. Captains-general were empowered to assem-
ble military committees against anyone disturbing the peace and a
crackdown was ordered on La Isabelina and other secret societies. But
central government decrees were countered by a Radical double regime
at local levels. Cristino militiamen cleansed town halls of symbols of
absolutism, especially the ﬂags of the disbanded Royalist Volunteers,
and organised revolutionary symbols in their stead, such as a marble
statue of Riego in Seville and a cenotaph to intimidate priests and pro-
Carlists.36 Civil governors publicly burnt police ﬁles from the Ominous
Decade, channelling revolutionary tension and also removing evidence
of their own collusion with the police state.37 It took little to turn this
atmosphere into violence against both real and imagined Carlist ﬁfth
columnists. In Zaragoza, a Cristino priest led a mob against the pseudo-
Carlist archbishop. The violence intensiﬁed as part of the militia went
over to the assailants, and several clerics were killed. Three clerics were
also killed in Murcia on 6 April 1835.38 More serious violence ﬂared up
again in Zaragoza on the night of 5 July 1835 when some 11 clerics were
killed, and similar killings followed in Barcelona. In both places, the
militia stood by as the mobs burned convents and monasteries, a few
hours later, most actually went over to the side of the assailants.39 The
militia stepped in to halt the riots only at the point when the crowd,
having satiated itself on the Church property, moved on to rob nearby
shops.40 Assaulting the Church was one thing, but property was sacred,
and the ‘safety valve’ of popular anti-clericalism could not be allowed to
recoil on Liberal interests. Drawing on this lesson, the military governor
of Huesca forestalled similar riots in his provincial capital by temporarily
evacuating the nuns from their convents and placing them in asylums
which were protected by militiamen he could trust.41 The Cristino mil-
itary was seeing its supremacy challenged by the rising radicalism of its
Militia rivals. Whereas Zaragoza’s captain-general conﬁded his fears to
paper, noting how ‘popular heroes were being made out of the militia
who committed the most atrocious crimes’,42 violence in other Cristino
cities soon forced the military to more drastic reactions.
90   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


   The worst anti-clerical violence struck Catalonia, where mountain
Carlists threatened Cristino garrisons protecting the Catalan coastal
plain. Carlists were blockading the proto-industrial town of Reus and
ambushing its garrison’s supplies and patrols. An ‘exterminating angel’
monk led an ambush in July which left, amongst seven Cristino dead,
a mutilated militiaman who was father of eight children. In response
Reus’s convents went up in ﬂames, whilst Llauder had few troops to
spare outside of Barcelona even to ﬁght the Carlists let alone pacify
the militia on his own side. Even in Barcelona the military authorities
were losing control.43 Monasteries were torched and religious person-
nel killed, often at the hands of what contemporaries called ‘obscene’
women. The defection to Carlism of the Catalan nobility had made
the Principality’s clerics second only to those in the Basque country in
their pro-Carlism, and they were confronted by Cristino Spain’s reddest
city in 1835.44 Barcelona’s new anti-clerical tradition was forged by its
war refugees, nascent proletarian militancy, and even its inauguration
in 1834 of crowd-pulling bullﬁghting in the working-class Barceloneta
district.45 Part of this revolutionism was being channelled in support
of the federal Republican agenda of Xaudaró y Fábregas, editor of El
Catalán and a militia commander known for recruiting citizen-soldiers
who failed to meet supposedly stringent property qualiﬁcations.46 The
ministerial press, by contrast, was relegated to impotent commentary,
complaining how ‘the worst symptom of our situation is the simultane-
ity of the killing of the clerics on the one hand and the, if not universal,
then common indifference with which they were witnessed, heard, and
spoken of on the other’.47 Ambivalence describes attitudes better than
indifference, as Catholics were cowed by the collusion of the author-
ities with the massacre, or, as a young girl later remembered: ‘I recall
the upheaval at home and my family’s despair. As if in a dream I recall
the reddish glow illuminating a high wall overlooking our roof-terrace,
and of the horror etched in my mother’s face as two friars ran for their
lives past our house.’48 An out-of-town lawyer who tried to stop a textile
worker beating a captive priest was told to ‘fuck off!’49 Captain-General
Llauder’s humiliation during the Cardero crisis had weakened the once-
strong relationship he had enjoyed with Barcelona’s dominant business
community and the militia it patronised. His military tribunals failed to
arrest any serious anti-clerical instigators, and his inability to protect the
Church matched the distant Carlists’ inability to rescue it. Barcelona’s
realistic civic authorities conﬁscated the contents of the religious insti-
tutions, boarded their doors and windows, and then abandoned them
to their fate.50 Llauder had not only estranged the bourgeoisie but also
                                 War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   91


enraged popular radicalism with his policy of militarising some 40,000
Militia for want of regular troops.51 Whereas members of the Militia
did not object to being sent out of their homes on active patrol, they
did object to garrisoning distant villages and forts surrounded by rural
Carlism, where torture and summary execution often awaited those
citizen-soldiers unlucky enough to be captured alive in ambush.52
   The patricians’ ‘negative pronunciamiento’ against Llauder served
notice on him to leave Barcelona. Leaving for Mataró, Llauder left
power in the hands of his fellow Catalan who became Captain-General,
Pedro Nolasco Bassa y Gerona, and civil governor, Pedro María Pastors.
But hopes of re-establishing normal control were rendered impossible,
because the assault on the convents had unleashed the people not only
against Carlism, but also against the economic liberalism of the Cristino
elites. The eve of the war was also the eve of the modern industrial age
for Barcelona, as José Bonaplata built the country’s ﬁrst steam-powered
cotton mill, known simply as the ‘Steam’ (El Vapor) or the Bonaplata
works. The machines required fewer workers than had been custom-
ary, and even those workers the employers retained were made to do
unpopular piecework, with the measure of cloth per worker being con-
stantly widened for each wage. Meanwhile, the dislocation caused to
agriculture by the war had increased food prices more quickly than
commensurate wage rises.53 But the Bonaplata was well protected: its
owner, José Bonaplata, commanded a militia battalion which had led
the attacks on the Church, and there were middle-class vigilante juntas
besides. The propertied elites also had Captain-General Bassa on their
side, whose ‘restraint’ (moderantismo) was unequivocal. Yet the revo-
lutionaries had not dispersed since their 25 July assault. Whereas the
civil authorities dared not turn the militia against them, Bassa decided
to follow the letter of martial law by marching 1,500 troops down the
Ramblas, rounding up and imprisoning revolutionaries. The revolution-
aries tried to win over Bassa, and when this overture fell on deaf ears,
the civil authorities, on 5 August, implored him to follow Llauder into
ﬂight. But Bassa was adamant: ‘within two hours either I or the people
will have succumbed’.54 The revolutionaries held nothing but contempt
for Bassa, an armchair general who had ‘never even seen a faccioso’, and
the riot that broke out was inevitable. Bassa ﬂed to the near-deserted
Ciudadela, the eighteenth-century fortress overlooking the city, seek-
ing reinforcements in vain, but the mob broke in, cornering Bassa,
shooting him dead and defenestrating his corpse. They then moved on
to the Bonaplata. The militia battalion defending the factory failed to
pacify the revolutionaries with invectives against Don Carlos and the
92   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Church. The anti-clerical massacres may have bound the executioners
together and intimidated the restrained Liberals, but they had failed as
a social prophylaxis against working-class radicalism, for the revolution-
aries killed more than 15 militiamen who were defending the works
whilst they also burned the Bonaplata to the ground. Meanwhile Bassa’s
body was dragged around the city and burnt to ashes in a bonﬁre in La
Rambla.55
   The following day, the civil governor, Pastors, who had survived the
violence, made a show of humility which won over both the anti-clerical
and working-class factions of the Left and halted further fratricidal
killing. Two prisoners were executed in a pathetic resurrection of law-
ful authority, a Carlist cabecilla (Garri) and the Radical instigator of the
Bonaplata attack (Pardinas). Llauder, his nerves wrecked, crossed into
France and exile.56 The militia was expanded from six battalions to 13 in
order to accommodate hungry workers, entrenching popular radicalism
in Barcelona.57 But matters did not rest there. Barcelona’s example was
followed by other Cristino cities threatened by Carlist encroachments.
Tarragona and Valencia, whose hinterlands were invaded by the Carlist
forces of Quílez and Serrador, both saw their prisons invaded by mobs
and several inmates lynched for ‘conspiracy’, whilst the authorities
saved more than a hundred others by hurriedly exiling them to garrison
prisons in Ceuta, North Africa. Equally, Murcia and even Zaragoza, the
latter already an exaltado stronghold saw similar violence. Seven Carlist
prisoners were executed in kangaroo courts, including Don Carlos’s con-
fessor, Blas de Hostalaza.58 The growing violence propelled attempts
ﬁrst by the toothless parliamentary opposition, and then by radicalised
militia in the provinces, to replace the moderado government with a
proactively Liberal and anti-Carlist regime.59
   The Cristino government soon discovered that it was powerless to
arrest this radicalism. A cosmetic cabinet reshufﬂe in July replaced one
restrained (moderado) prime minister with another, Martínez de la Rosa
with Count Toreno, both former Radicals who now shared a grim assess-
ment that popular opinion in the country as a whole had swayed in
favour of Carlism. They may have been right, but this guardedness
was read as defeatism, even as collusion with the enemy, in Radical
circles. On 15 August, Radicals raised the capital’s militia in rebellion,
demanding the imposition of the Constitution of 1812 and the release
from prison of the leader of the Isabelina, Eugenio de Aviraneta e
Ibargoyen.60 But this insurrection was met by a Carlist rising in some
of the poor neighbourhoods of Madrid where artisans and women sus-
tained a low-level urban guerrilla against Militia patrols. Aviraneta had
                                   War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   93


not been the only prisoner running street politics from his prison cell:
the Carlist Junta of Selva had been doing the same. Seizing the chance to
re-establish order, the moderado government mobilised army units and
conservative elements in the Militia to disband three of the capital’s
most radical Militia battalions. The War Ministry then reformed them,
ﬁltering out the Radicals.61 Powerless to resist this purge, the Radicals
replied with a pamphleting campaign. Whereas the Militia was praised
for its sacriﬁces resisting Carlism, the army was scorned as the parasitical
tool of despotism: ‘You are not the people! Nor are you Spaniards! You
don’t belong to or care about our soil and laws at all! It’s a shame you
forget that you clothe and feed yourselves on our blood and sweat!’62
   The government’s success in Madrid encouraged the Interior Min-
istry to issue a triumphalist communiqué the following day (17th),
but this sangfroid proved short-lived. Revolution was sweeping across
all provincial capitals. And gone were the days of popular riots: now
the Radicals were linking the struggle against Carlism to a positive
programme of Liberal renewal. Insurgent juntas frequently restored to
buyers the nationalised property which royalists in 1823 had conﬁs-
cated. They also tried to win over the unpropertied by abolishing the
hated consumption taxes called the derechos de puertas.63 Revolutionar-
ies, meanwhile, attacked not just Carlists but also moderado elites who
attempted resistance. In Tarragona, the governor was killed trying to
escape the revolutionaries by boat.64
   The alarmed Toreno administration reshufﬂed the cabinet again,
but no serious candidates could be made to resist the revolutionary
tide, posts being ﬁlled with the senile and politically innocent. The
ministerial Gaceta de Madrid condemned the revolutionaries as dis-
honourable cowards. But the revolutionaries were no longer leaderless.
Juntas were being formed by exaltado public ﬁgures, including members
(procuradores) of the parliamentary opposition. Even though the gov-
ernment decreed the ‘abolition’ of these juntas, this was vain rhetoric.
The government may have hoped for survival due to the 9 September
1835 proclamation from the Commander of the Army of the North,
Fernández de Córdova, that he would defend the central government.
But Córdova’s attention was absorbed containing a Carlist breakout and
suppressing mutinies in his own ranks, and he could spare only piece-
meal forces under General Latre to shore up the central government.65
At ﬁrst, Latre’s forces were successful pacifying such provincial capitals
as Burgos and Palencia, which were instinctively conservative anyway
and whose militia revolutionaries were not dispersed in the countryside.
Unlike the Carlists, the Radicals centred their militancy in towns, which
94   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


meant that government troops and artillery could easily be concen-
trated against them. But the further south Latre pushed the greater the
degree of revolutionary resistance he encountered. Juntas in Andalucía,
Valencia and Catalonia had won over local regular forces in addition to
the Militia, and some had launched populist manifestoes which went
beyond a mere tax amnesty. The Andalucían revolutionaries convened
their junta at Andújar under the leadership of Count Donadío and
throughout September waged a physical struggle against Madrid, which
they justiﬁed, in part, by sloganeering social justice. Whilst Donadío
let his 4,000-strong revolutionary army behave like ‘anarchists’, cut-
ting all communications between Andalucía and Madrid,66 his future
father-in-law, the procurador Count Navas, rallied Andalucían labourers
with the cry of íViva la República!67 When Latre’s column passed the
Andalucían frontier at Depeñaperros on 17 September, his men defected
to the revolutionaries; only the elite cavalry and artillery stayed loyal to
the government.68
   The juntas have been heralded by leftist writers as evidence of nascent
socialism and federalism. Twentieth-century Andalucían regionalists
saw the Andújar revolution as a vehicle for combating the notorious
abuses of the agricultural proletariat, and a model of regional solidarity
against centralising diktats of Madrid.69 Equally, members from revo-
lutionary juntas in Valencia, Aragón and Catalonia discussed federating
themselves along the historical lines of pre-absolutist Hapsburg model.70
In fact, these later representations overlooked the actual behaviour of
the juntas and how their revolutionary decisions were driven by the
contingencies of the war rather than ideology. The republicanism of
Navas and Donadío was mere rhetoric. When Navas was returned to the
revolutionary Cortes he lost interest in land reform and never bothered
to intervene in debates about widening suffrage.71 Donadío’s radicalism,
meanwhile, was bought off with his marriage into the Navas family and
the military governorship of Málaga.72 The federalism plan was dropped
as soon as the juntas realised that the Toreno government could no
longer use military force against them. Its real rationale, in any, case
was telling. The Captain-General of Valencia brieﬂy captained the con-
federation idea for military rather than political reasons, in protest at the
Cristino government policy of denuding the east of regular troops: ‘The
only sufﬁcient force under my command is the militia.’ Unless excep-
tional rulings applied, the militia answered to local civil authorities, and
the captain-general therefore sensibly subscribed to ‘a type of confed-
eration between the three kingdoms that used to form the Crown of
Aragón’.73
                                  War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   95


   Moreover, the devolution of power to juntas did not equate to a
democratisation process in the way that rareﬁed studies of the 1835
juntas have argued.74 For one thing, Spanish liberalism never diverged
from an intolerant inability to admit pluralism – or ‘loyal opposition’ –
which in practice meant the exercise of close clientelist networks of
patronage and punishment and rigged elections.75 Mendizábal grew nos-
talgic for his 12 years in Britain where he had discovered that political
adversaries could yet remain friends because of fair play in politics.76
The revolution of the juntas often impaired the war effort by releas-
ing hitherto repressed networks of corruption and cronyism into public
ofﬁce. The Andújar junta soon found itself ﬁghting, in Málaga, a civil
war inside a civil war. The notorious local extortionist, Cuban-born
Vicente Abello – whose coup during the Peninsular War in 1810 had
ruined Málaga’s defence against the French – mobilised a mob to seize
the local Captaincy-Generalship of Granada. Abello justiﬁed this act by
proclaiming the Constitution of 1812, but this did not convince sub-
ordinate juntas at Jaén and Málaga, which led a tax-strike against him.
Andújar promptly deposed him, but Abello’s mob assaulted a meeting of
Andújar revolutionaries held at Granada’s Chancellery, forcing them to
reinstate him. Abello now emerged as a statesman, having successfully
deﬁed the Liberal ‘Right’ of Andújar whilst also violently suppressing his
own Liberal ‘Left’ once he could safely dispense with it. Half-a-dozen
rioters were exiled to the penal colony at Ceuta and a leading mobster,
the ‘notorious Olmo’, was executed.77
   Even the power struggles of the revolutionary juntas paled in com-
parison to the subaltern ranks of the Cristino army who used the
revolutionary climate to vote with their feet. Ofﬁcial bulletins during
1835 complained about Cristino draftees’ illegal emigration to Portugal
and France, and this exodus peaked during the breakdown in authority
in September.78 Popular pressure at local level often forced revolutionary
authorities in Barcelona to suspend the hated draft, and several other
Cristino authorities followed suit, most completely in the Andalucían
town of Antequera.79 Historians of revolutions in politically undevel-
oped societies have noted how communities ‘import’ slogans devised
by elites (‘republic’, ‘Constitution’ and ‘liberty’) and invest them with
their own meaning.80 The meaning the Cristino rank and ﬁle invested
in the revolution was freedom from abusive military service. Sergeants
occupied a key role in the social history of the Cristino army as they
disciplined, championed or subverted their men. In September 1835,
a squadron of off-duty cavalry led by Sergeant Manuel Vilches staged
a pronuciamiento in Burgos in the name of the Constitution of 1812,
96   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40

actually a pretext for the entire squadron to desert.81 A similar attempt
weeks later by infantry sergeants in Soria met with little following,
Comandante General Valdés conﬁding to Espartero that ‘the ambition
of some sergeants created much hot air but fortunately no response’.82
Even the militia, which had expanded to 394,717 men and 16,149 cav-
alry by the end of 1835, shared many of the grievances of the Cristino
army as the war made conditions of service deteriorate (Table 5.1).83
The government divided its membership between privileged volunteers
and a less privileged ‘legal’ category (legales) whose conditions of service
approximated those of the army as they could be ‘legally’ commanded
to campaign against Carlists outside of their home province. Sacedón
(Guadalajara) in October 1835, witnessed legales refusing campaign
orders and rioting against the privileges of the volunteers.84 In fact,
the ‘revolutionary’ juntas of 1835 occupied a middle ground between
the moderado central government and a radicalised Militia and soldiery,
channelling the grievances of the latter in order to get the exaltados into
power.
   For the moderado central government all depended on whether the
Cristino Army of the North would switch its allegiance to the juntas.
The government sensibly did not want a repeat of Latre’s failure and
ordered Córdova to hold the front in Navarra. For their part, those
juntas threatened by Carlist invasions, such as Zaragoza and Barcelona,
lacked conﬁdence in the military ability of their own radicalised Mili-
tia and urged Córdova to send regular reinforcements. Other juntas,
                                  War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   97


especially those deeper in the Cristino zone, made explicitly political
pleas. Zaragoza urged the army to disobey Madrid which was ‘the real
enemy’, Granada declared the Queen-Regent a prisoner, Badajoz con-
demned the government as ‘the real anarchists’, issuing it with a 12-day
deadline to resign, whilst Cádiz declared Toreno a traitor.85 Terriﬁed,
Toreno appealed for French help, but the French foreign minister, the
Duc de Broglie, refused. The only concession France could make had
already made. On 28 June 1835, agreement was reached to dispatch
to Spain the 4,100-strong Legión d’Algérie (the early Foreign Legion)
to Catalonia under the command of Colonel Bernelle. Prime Minister
Thiers had wanted to expand this force by some 8,000, increasing French
inﬂuence in Liberal Europe without provoking Metternich (whose pro-
Carlism, Thiers knew, had practical limits) to intervene militarily on
the Carlist side. But the Liberal interventionism of the French king was
restricted in Spanish matters: Louis-Philippe, much to the chagrin of
Liberal Europe, privately held Don Carlos’s claim to the throne to be
stronger than Isabella’s and in any case did not want Spain to become for
France what Italy was for Austria. French foreign policy never managed
to square this circle.86 Thus, by late 1835, the Legion’s Spanish pres-
ence had not expanded beyond six battalions. Nearly all its ofﬁcers were
French, but its rank and ﬁle included many Germans, Italians and Poles,
this last nationality replacing the Spanish battalion which, in Febru-
ary 1834, was disbanded for service in the Cristino army.87 The French
government, which was worried about Spanish revolution infecting its
soldiers, dispatched its Legion to combat positions only, away from the
‘contagion’ of the exaltados.88 But, despite the hopes of several moderados
for formal French assistance against the exaltados, this force would not
leave its quarantine to be drawn into Cristino domestic politics. His last
hope gone, and revolutionary death threats mounting, Toreno resigned
on 14 September and ﬂed into the safety of a French exile.89 His suc-
cessor, exaltado Juan Álvarez Mendizábal, would channel the radicalism
caused by the war. Mendizábal was the favoured candidate of Britain, the
second Quadruple Alliance great power whose auxiliary British Legion
had come to the aid of Cristino Spain in June 1835, around the same
time as the French. The arrival of organised foreign volunteers coincided
with the radicalisation of Cristino politics and had the effect of the pro-
jecting the Spanish Civil War into a world war between Absolutism and
Liberty.
   Exaltados praised the altruism of the foreign auxiliaries but criticised
their governments’ refusal to make open war on the Carlists.90 Even
though the French had a kind of cultural entrée to the Cristinos which
98   Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


the culturally more distant British did not at ﬁrst enjoy, exaltados gener-
ally identiﬁed with British liberalism, even when its free-trade mantra
alienated Spain’s ﬂedgling industrialisation. Most moderados, by con-
trast, were drawn to the authoritarian, centralised liberalism of the
July Monarchy, and many also to the prospect of formal French mili-
tary intervention. The Carlists, for their part, ridiculed the auxiliaries as
proof of the Cristinos’ need to use foreign mercenaries to impose liberal-
ism on an unwilling population, and traditionalist historians a century
later excoriated the auxiliaries as the ‘ﬁrst International Brigades’.91
Carlists regularly betrayed xenophobia in their cause. Ramón Cabrera
expressed ‘Spanish pride’ at defeating a succession of Cristino generals
with cosmopolitan surnames (Borso di Carminati, Oráa, Van-Halen and
O’Donnell), taunting the indigenous generals who remained at María
Cristina’s court.92
   Unlike the brigadistas of the Spanish Civil War, the 1830s auxiliaries
failed to disabuse the enemy of this impression of their mediocrity. The
best auxiliaries were Peninsular War veterans like the Irishman Lieu-
tenant Edward Costello, a brilliant recruiter whose return in 1835 to
Vitoria, scene of a bloody battle a generation earlier, evoked poignant
memories bordering on post-traumatic stress for him and his fellow
handful of veterans.93 But most British auxiliaries were new to ﬁght-
ing. The French and Portuguese auxiliaries, by contrast, were more
seasoned, but political divisions in Paris and Lisbon impeded their full
deployment. The British auxiliaries, which also suffered from equivocal
political support, were slow to acclimatise to northern Spain and spent
the ﬁrst months constructing fortiﬁcations.94 They were decimated in
body by typhus (‘Vitoria fever’) during the 1835–36 winter and spring,
and in soul by the virulent attacks of the Tory press, and their tragic
neglect upon their return to Britain after 1837.95 Chronic arrears of pay
persisted even after the war, when British Legion veterans protested
outside the Spanish legation in London, in a series of stand-offs last-
ing until 1843.96 Even when most arrears were ﬁnally settled, several
Spanish veterans lapsed into pauperdom which caught the eye of social
reformers. Henry Mayhew, in his seminal London Labour and the London
Poor, labelled the ‘Spanish Legion men’ as ‘those who will not work’ and
categorised them under ‘Professional Beggars’.97 Ironically, Mayhew’s
sententiousness was anticipated by the Carlists, who reserved particu-
lar scorn for the British auxiliaries as ‘pirates’ and Protestants, recruited
from the taverns and pie-shops of London.98
   This distasteful image has overshadowed the history of the for-
eign auxiliaries. Certainly, many men joined for the basest motives
                                  War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   99


of adventure or desperation. As so few auxiliary rank and ﬁle left
memoirs, we must privilege those that have been discovered, such as
that of William Turner, a poverty-stricken volunteer who, after two
years’ service in Spain, returned to England wounded and penniless,
and was later imprisoned for committing acts of fraud.99 Most other
humble biographies are extremely fragmented and yet suggest that
many of the authors’ motives and outcomes were similar.100 Yet two
qualiﬁcations must be made to their mercenary image. Recruits came
mainly from expanding British and Irish cities, and, in the case of
the Glasgow, a particularly strong cohort were weavers proletarianised
by industrialisation.101 It was no small irony that Scottish handloom
weavers and other victims of industrialisation entered Spain alongside
record British cotton imports. The economic grievances of recruits were
championed by their Radical ofﬁcers such as Alexander Somerville, an
Anti-Corn-Law League member who defended his men from Tory-Carlist
insults. Thus, the British auxiliaries were no more scum than the British
army proper which, under Wellington during the Peninsular War, had
defended the old order of ‘physical and intellectual slavery’ whereas
volunteers for Liberal Spain upheld the ‘good of mankind’, an opin-
ion shared by the great utilitarian and pioneering advocate of foreign
intervention in Spain’s civil war, John Stuart Mill.102 This Liberal inter-
nationalism was also held by other prominent ofﬁcers, such as the
journalist, Charles Southwell, who preferred the ‘pseudo-liberalism of
Queen Isabella to the monkish absolutism of Don Carlos’.103 Above all,
the Commander-in-Chief of the ﬁrst British Legion and Radical Mem-
ber of Parliament for the Westminster constituency, General George de
Lacy Evans, linked the cause of British and Spanish radicalism. Evans
was accused by both his Tory opposition at home and the Carlist
enemy abroad of vainglory, greed, and of possessing a self-publicising
Bonaparte complex.104
   In short, European Liberals saw the struggle against Carlism in inter-
national rather than merely Spanish terms. The most inﬂuential auxil-
iary in this regard was Joseph Tánski, a Polish refugee from the Russian
invasion of his homeland in 1831 and an ofﬁcer in the French For-
eign Legion. He left a remarkable account of the political and military
state of the Cristino war effort and a nuanced critique of the Con-
stitution of 1812 beloved of his fellow Spanish Radicals.105 The most
seasoned Liberal internationalists were in the Belgian Auxiliary Legion,
which comprised veterans of the Belgian Revolution and Portuguese
civil war. Portugese civil war veterans used their battle honours to call
themselves the ‘Cazadores de Oporto’ (Hunters of Oporto), making up
100 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


one of the two Belgian formations, even though their ranks contained
many Portuguese and Italian Mazzinian exiles, who were the most rad-
ical Liberal internationalists. The other Belgian formation (which also
contained Portuguese and assorted nationalities) was commanded by
the Genoese Mazzinian who had fought in revolutionary wars in Italy,
Spain and Portugal, Gaetano Borso di Carminati, a Romantic conspirator
who would be executed for his part in the October 1841 rising against
Espartero’s regime.106
   The greatest manpower contribution in proportion to national popu-
lation was made by the Portuguese who, during the winter of 1835–36,
were billeted in Zamora.107 Their legion was distinct as it was for-
mally on loan from the regular Portuguese army, and included a large
minority of pardoned miguelista veterans from the Portuguese civil war.
The Portuguese auxiliaries were thus of the best quality.108 But their
questionable loyalties were complicated by the cinders of civil war in
Portugal, which included resilient miguelismo in northern Portugal on
the one hand, and Radical-Liberal revolution on the other (Radicals
under General Sá da Bandeira seized power in September 1836 but were
defeated by the moderate-Liberal ‘Revolt of the Marshals’ under General
Saldanha the following July). Such domestic uncertainty meant that
the Portuguese were reluctant to deploy their legion too far beyond
their own frontier.109 Thus, the lengthy billeting of Portuguese troops
in Zamora, far behind the nominal front line, was typical of this pol-
icy. General Córdova complained ‘I may have 2,500 Portuguese mouths
to feed but I don’t have 2,500 Portuguese arms.’110 The Portuguese faced
similar supply and remuneration problems as the other auxiliaries. Their
currency had been accepted at an agreed exchange rate, but that winter
the cash-strapped Cristino authorities devalued it in the Spanish favour.
The response was two nights of rioting as the Allies smashed up mar-
ket stalls and Spaniards retaliated with the assassination of a Portuguese
spendthrift. Finding the body of their comrade, the Portuguese ram-
paged through the streets, injuring civilians and looting and destroying
shops. The Cristino commander did not ﬁre on his allies, but restored
calm by circulating news that the Portuguese soldier had been murdered
by a ‘Carlist’.111 But trouble returned in January when Portuguese sol-
diers and Spanish market traders exchanged ﬁre over the price of olives.
Joint Luso-Cristino patrols restored calm, and the Portuguese martial
band was tactfully invited to play patriotic music at a ceremony to
restore a monument to liberty vandalised by royalists in 1823.112
   The foreign auxiliaries were not protected by the Eliot Treaty, and
could expect execution when captured. The Liberal Eco del Comercio
                                 War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   101


detailed such outrages as part of a moral campaign to enlist interna-
tional support. Thus, three British artillerymen who, on 13 July 1835,
fell for a Basque ‘honeytrap’ were ambushed and executed.113 The
following winter the bodies of several of their executed compatriots
were left to decompose on the walls of Guevara castle.114 In January
1836, ﬁve British soldiers were captured near Vitoria, and forcibly bap-
tised into Catholicism before being executed humanely and with the
sacraments.115 Whilst auxiliaries who were captured could expect no
mercy, those who defected were welcomed, especially Protestants who
voluntarily converted to Catholicism. In August 1836, the Carlist press
reported that six British defectors were publicly baptised in the presence
of the King at Azpeitia cathedral, and contrasted this serenity with the
atheistic chaos of the revolution in the Cristino zone.116
   This ‘atheistic chaos’ was presided over, since the fall of Toreno, by
Prime Minister Juan Álvarez Mendizábal. His comparatively humble ori-
gins (and Jewish ancestry) are the exception that proves the rule of
Spanish Liberal elites having long-established privileged backgrounds.117
Mendizábal’s cosmopolitan experience in ﬁnance had allowed him to
source vital funds for Espoz’s invasion of 1830 and the cause of Dom
Pedro in the Portuguese civil war.118 His premiership of 1835–36 marked
the peak of British inﬂuence in Cristino Spain, via Ambassador Villiers,
who was so powerful that at times he virtually chaired cabinet meet-
ings. Mendizábal’s background was targeted by the Carlist press which
condemned the usurping Prime Minister as a Wandering Jew merchant
bent on looting the Spanish Church as collateral for international Jewish
ﬁnanciers and their British guardians.119 Mendizábal’s representative
government was attacked by the Carlists, as a ‘repugnant Protestant
innovation’ that distorted 14 centuries of Spanish Catholic monar-
chy, and later, when his rule became more dictatorial, as Robespierre
reborn.120
   Yet Mendizábal’s immediate task was neither to democratise nor
demonise Spain, but to resurrect a working central government which
could both revive the deteriorating war effort and subject the revolution-
ary juntas. This daunting task he approached with the mantra of ‘Liberal
union’, the rallying of all anti-Carlist forces, which he later summarised
as ‘forgetting, respecting, revising, repairing and reforming’.121 Whilst
rejecting, for now, demands to reinstitute the Constitution of 1812,
Mendizábal prorogued the Royal Statute and incorporated the juntas
into provincial deputations along the 1812 model but with different
powers. They were charged with establishing and managing committees
of armament and defence to defend their respective territory against
102 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Carlists.122 The Zaragoza commission tried to suppress Carlist guerrilla
activity by compulsorily purchasing all horses not owned by the army
or militia, banishing gypsies (whose mobile spatial relations, outsider
status and domination of horse-trading made them suspect), and giving
political expression to the army’s martial law.123 This demotion to an
administrative role was resisted by the Andújar junta, which held on to
its ‘sovereignty’ until the Cortes were recalled. In response, the govern-
ment maintained martial law in Madrid and other provincial capitals
and suppressed communications with the revolutionaries. The repeal
of martial law and calling of the Cortes on 28 October made even the
Andalucían Radicals conform to Mendizábal’s suzerainty. His premier-
ship now secure, Mendizábal set out to channel the anti-clericalism of
the revolutionaries to politically useful ends. Already, on 7 July, the
Toreno administration had expelled the Jesuit order for the second
time in Spanish history, conﬁscating its assets to reduce the balloon-
ing National Debt. This legislative anti-clericalism opened two years of
government onslaught on corporate Catholicism which would be scaled
back, but not halted, only when the moderados returned to power in
1837. Mendizábal expanded this anti-clericalism by suppressing all reli-
gious houses containing fewer than 12 inmates and auctioning their
assets. Citizens who had bought Church property during the Triennium,
only to see it conﬁscated in the 1823 counter-revolution, were reunited
with their purchases courtesy of a July 1837 decree.124 The tithe, along
with an extra clerical tax levied in Galicia called the Voto de Santiago,
had long been unpopular, but their abolition became politically impossi-
ble to resist as Carlism spread during 1835.125 These were both abolished
and replaced from 1837 by a smaller tax (culto y clero) collected by the
constitutional municipalities in order to pension off monks and nuns
evicted by the disentailment.126
   Mendizábal did not expect formal French or British intervention and
so pledged to use nationalised property to fund a decisive campaign in
the Basque country.127 The degree to which he channelled revolutionary
anti-Carlism was remarkable. His Liberal union saw an initial ‘patriotic’
ﬂurry of donations to the Cristino war effort: ministers volunteered cuts
to their salaries, ladies’ charity circles made clothing for the front and
Liberal students answered the call to enlist.128 Despite the paternalis-
tic opposition of the moderados, enlistment to the militia was expanded
by removing the requirement that unmarried recruits obtain permis-
sion from their heads of family.129 A freer intellectual environment grew
in proportion to the mendizabalista assault on corporate Catholicism.
Barcelona’s medical academy ruled that suicide victims died ‘insane’ and
                                     War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36    103


thus qualiﬁed for Christian burial. The British Protestant missionary and
know-it-all, George Borrow, set up a Bible Society in the same city.130 The
ﬁrst athenaeum came to Madrid and the Radical government histori-
cised its claim to rule by starting to build a pantheon to the fallen masses
of the Second of May.131 Some Liberals even proposed that conﬁscated
Church property should be leased to the landless, thereby inoculating
the rural masses against Carlism. Flórez Estrada, leader of the dissolved
anti-Carlist junta of Santander in 1834, serialised his ‘social liberal’ trea-
tise, La cuestión social, o sea, origen, latitud y efectos del derecho de propiedad,
which culminated in February 1836, with his proposal to grant landless
labourers smallholdings under 50-year renewable leases.132 But the Lib-
eral union waxed sectarian over the winter of 1835–36, as the exaltado
Militia exercised a double regime targeting moderados as well as Carlists.
Tellingly, in February 1836 the leftist Eco congratulated the Militia for
increasing and popularising its enlistment as a ‘dyke against the machi-
nations of the perverse’.133 By the summer, the march of events would
create a revolutionary dictatorship in the Cristino zone.
   Mendizábal’s most important decree was to conscript 100,000 men
for Córdova’s Army of the North. Three separate rounds of conscrip-
tion, each for 25,000 men, had been enacted since the start of the war,
but Mendizábal’s measure augured the mass militarisation of society.
The 100,000 quinta made all males aged between 18 and 40 liable to
serve, the only free exemptions being disability, unigeniture, ordina-
tion and veteran status. Alternatively, draftees could pay 4,000 reales,
or 1,000 reales and a viable horse, in order to avoid service.134 The net
‘blood tax’ collected by the government was some 25 million reales and
700 horses; this income was in the spirit of Mendizábal’s demand for
‘abundant men and money to end the war soon’.135 But the sums col-
lected made little impression on an annual state deﬁcit of 30 million
reales, which was made acute both by the lapsed customs revenues dur-
ing the revolution (some £500,000 worth of British imports had entered
Spain that summer without suffering any duties) and the expanding
army budget, which had reached some 600 million reales annually.136
Only Mendizábal’s long-standing contacts with the Paris and London
bourses, combined with the structural reforms of the desamortización,
kept the Cristino state liquid, ensuring terms of credit which no other
Spanish prime minister could have achieved.137
   Only about half of Mendizábal’s 100,000 actually made it to the
front, despite draconian regulations making relatives and then town
halls responsible for deserters. In front-line areas many conscripts
defected to the Carlists, especially in La Rioja, Aragón, Castellón de la
104 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Plana, Cuenca, Toledo, and Ciudad Real. Several deserters from Galicia,
Salamanca and Zamora ﬂed to Portugal. Even then, the Commander-
in-Chief of the Army of the North, Córdova, complained bitterly of
the incomprehension of the Madrid regime and of how the radicali-
sation of Liberal politics was making his task harder. Civilian Radicals
turned into armchair generals, criticising Córdova’s inaction in light
of these reinforcements. Yet Córdova complained that the revolution
had sent him conscripts who were worse than useless. They had not
passed any height or health tests, had had little training, and invariably
ended up burdening the hospital system. Municipalities across Cristino
Spain had clearly sent their most destitute and desperate sons. Córdova
was obliged to establish laborious training camps south of the Ebro,
where more conscripts deserted and ended up straining food supplies
(whereas before they had been producers). In any case, Córdova esti-
mated that only some 17,000 actually made it into service after all these
losses.138 In other words, Mendizábal had scored a great political vic-
tory in mobilising his 100,000; military victory, however, patently still
eluded him.
   The march of events at local levels escaped Mendizábal’s attempts to
manage anti-clericalism from the centre. Part of the anti-clerical atmo-
sphere in Cristino Spain may be attributed to episodes of anti-Carlist
hysteria returning with a renewed outbreak of the cholera in July 1835.
The rest was caused by the very real attempts of individual clerics to
ﬁght the Liberal revolution.139 The Cristino authorities sent suspect
monks and priests, especially in the Maestrazgo, into internal exile in
Andalucía, and from November 1835 all new entrants into religious
orders required a certiﬁcate of loyalty to Isabel II signed by the civil
governor.140 The need for deportations was clear after the 10 August
1835 Carlist rising of evicted Dominican friars in Manacor (Mallorca)
who, the Radical press alleged, ‘burnt alive’ militiamen they captured,
and whose ‘lenient’ sentences provoked a revolutionary rising by some
1,400 Militia in Palma demanding ‘a sound example be made of the
guilty to guard against future risings’.141 Other religious were terrorised
by the massacres into ﬂeeing into Carlist zones, where nuns nursed
war casualties, or even leaving into Spain altogether: several were wel-
comed to Buenos Aires where they occupied vacancies in River Plate
monasteries.142 Clerics who remained under Cristino control faced a
bleak future of eviction from disentailed monasteries and subjection to
laic authorities in the meantime, either constitutional or ‘double regime’
in nature. In the former case, the Civil Governor of Pontevedra forced
local nuns to observe night curfews and provide charity relief to war
widows, remarking impudently that the sisters should be content to do
                                 War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   105


God’s true work.143 Elsewhere in Galicia (Ribadeo) the double regime
targeted Carlist clerics as militiamen forced civil authorities to close
local monasteries and expel their inmates. Their vigilantism was sup-
ported in the Radical press, which published their complaints of how
the clerical guerrilla captains they had captured had faced no greater
punishment than deportation to their home parishes, or imprisonment
in Vigo castle from which, in one instance, a cleric escaped with ofﬁ-
cial collusion and rejoined the facción.144 The economic power wielded
by the Church in Galicia over rural leaseholders was shrinking the
Cristino safe zone into the port-cities and fortiﬁed centres. Illicit arms
production for the Carlists was widespread, and was concealed from
passing militia patrols.145 When Latre was dispatched to La Coruña to
replace Morillo as Captain-General of Galicia, both men undertook their
journeys under false names and even physical disguises, Latre being con-
vinced that his execution would follow being identiﬁed by any priest in
his path.146
  The growing Carlism of clerics prevented the Cristino authorities
from relying on them as natural guardians of the social order, which
was always the aim of the Liberals, who wanted only to strip the
Church of its political rather than moral power. But the refusal of
many clerics to accept Cristino political legitimacy drove the double
regime to attack their moral authority. In March 1835, the Mayor of
Vera (Almería) prohibited any contact between women and monks of
the nearby monastery at Beruela, citing the scandals the monks had
been causing amongst local women.147 Radicalised Cristino soldiers and
militiamen targeted Church property and symbols. In April 1835, a
Cristino patrol sent out from Pamplona robbed and vandalised a church
at nearby Yanci.148 Cristino army elites worried about the violent and
socially subversive radicalism that led people to invade abandoned and
conﬁscated Church property.149 In a nationalised Dominican convent
in Barcelona several nuns’ tombs were opened and the remains disre-
spectfully scattered.150 During July 1835, mobile militia near Belchite
rebelled against the government when a popular lieutenant (a certain
Blas Pober) was arrested and a Radical demagogue (a certain Urbina) was
found dead in suspicious circumstances. In response the militia refused
to march out against rural Carlists, and sacked a monastery in the name
of the Constitution of 1812.151 In October 1835, mobile militia used
Church icons along the Imperial Canal linking Aragón with Navarra for
target practice, assaulting the chapel on the Marquess Lazán’s estate,
ﬁring their muskets at the altar of the Virgin of Pilar inside, despite the
fact that the Virgin during the Peninsular War had been ‘nationalised’ as
the celestial Captain-General of Patriot Spain.152 In Madrid, authorities
106 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


grew concerned at the growing number of women visiting newly con-
ﬁscated monasteries, hitherto a closed and sexless male world which
was now the haunt of rowdy National Militia musters, and, it was
feared, copulation.153 In Barcelona, the captain-general was forced to
deal with the local authorities’ quandary about the former convent of
San Francisco del Asís. Its premises had ﬁrst been used for cholera vic-
tims, and then as a ‘lock hospital’ for prostitutes whose numbers had
been increasing since the start of the war. But, much to the annoyance
of the captain-general, both male and female prostitutes were still sell-
ing themselves there, undermining the physical and moral condition of
his conscripts154 :

   It has come to my attention that the inmates there, far from being
   cured of the vice of prostitution, have been propagating it amongst
   themselves, drawing to them individuals who have no other business
   being there. They have been engaging in homosexual acts (amance-
   bamientos de un mismo sexo), corrupting those of a youthful or gentle
   disposition.

Centrifugal radicalism impaired Mendizábal’s relations with General
Córdova. By early 1836, the moderados were openly calling Mendizábal
a dictator, forcing a parliamentary vote of no conﬁdence, blocking
attempts to expand the electoral franchise and politicising the tensions
between the Army of the North and Madrid. All this tension caused
Mendizábal many sleepless nights. Aides recounted how he would spend
days without proper sleep, collapsing onto his papers at his desk for
snoozes, or stealing carriage time for sleep during his trips to the
Queen-Regent at El Pardo.155 The British missionary, George Borrow,
who secured a brief interview with Mendizábal, remembered the prime
minister’s remarkable ability to maintain an English-language conversa-
tion without once looking up from a pile of documents at his desk.156
But even Mendizábal’s energies were exhausted by summer 1836, as his
erstwhile exaltado allies refused to serve under the ‘dictator’, the Cortes
was dissolved, and the Carlists expanded their domain over the north
and east of Spain.157 Again, both the cause and effect of this political
disunity was the failure of the Cristino army to arrest the spread of
Carlism. Mendigorría was the last signiﬁcant victory in a long time, as
the Carlists regrouped, tightened their blockade of Cristino towns in the
Basque country and dispatched expeditions deep into Cristino territory.
   In August 1835, the Carlist court launched the ﬁrst major expedition
of the war in a bid to link the Basque country with Carlists in Catalonia.
                                War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   107


Juan Antonio Guergué, a purged colonel of 1833 and prominent ‘brute’
in the theocratic wing of Carlism, on 8 August 1835 led a legion
of just under 3,000 men past Córdova’s covering force and towards
Catalonia. Since the Cristinos had executed Count Romagosa, Catalan
Carlism had been in almost as much disarray as under Llauder’s early
counter-insurgency. Guerrilla bands led by Porredón, Borges, Sobrevías,
Galcerán, Vall, Masgoret, Zorrilla and, above all, Tristany, had kept the
insurgency alive, but Guergué’s expedition aimed to unite the striking
power of these bands and carve out contiguous territory.158 Taking small
towns along the way in Aragón, he intercepted diplomatic correspon-
dence from the Cristino legation in Paris which conﬁrmed Carlist hopes
that the revolution was debilitating the Cristino war effort. Guergué was
welcomed into Huesca on 16 August by local authorities who had tired
of revolution and who implemented Carlist orders to demobilise the
Militia, seize its arms and draft men of military age. Guergué enjoyed
similar success occupying an abandoned Barbastro, where he resurrected
the Royalist Volunteers of 1833 and used them to repulse an attempt by
mobile Militia to recapture the town. Crossing into Catalonia, Guergué
was joined near Tremp on 25 August by Antonio Borges’s force of 500
irregulars and then by the 3,500 Carlists of Jacinto Orteu, landowner
and ex-colonel of the Royalist Volunteers. The next day a Cristino reg-
iment at nearby Orgaña was subverted by conscripts who disarmed the
local militia and defected to Guergué’s Carlists. In two weeks Guergué’s
expedition had traversed Aragón, subverting Cristino populations and
defences at every turn. Major difﬁculties began only as he entered
Catalonia, caused both by his own Navarrese, who complained of inad-
equate food and footwear, and by a stiffening Cristino defence, owed in
part to the unpardonable excesses the revolutionaries had recently car-
ried out. Guergué sent home his troublesome Navarrese, who by now
formed a minority of his force, and concentrated on regrouping the
Catalan guerrillas: by the end of the year these numbered some 22,363
infantry and 395 cavalry.159
   Since the revolutionaries had ousted Captain-General Llauder, neither
Bassa nor Pastors had matched his counter-insurgency zeal. The revo-
lutionary crisis had ended any hopes of regular army reinforcements
creating a mobile reserve. Thus, virtually the entire Carlist offensive
was met by the Militia, ensuring hatred and atrocities on both sides.
When Carlists attacked the village of Camarasa, its 50-strong garrison
of mobile militia fortiﬁed itself in the church, but were soon smoked
out by the outnumbering Carlists and forced to surrender. All were then
executed, most by gruesome beheadings, their corpses being dumped
108 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


into the River Segre weighed down by stones. Residents at nearby set-
tlements for months afterwards avoided the river-water for drinking
and ﬁshing. Such brutality was counterproductive for Carlist campaign-
ing, as news of such outrages only served to embolden the Cristino
defence elsewhere. After Torá, on 8 August 1835, came under Carlist
siege, it resisted against appalling odds, including an all-enveloping
inferno of outlying buildings, set by the Carlists. Concepción Preciado,
wife of a Cristino captain, stepped into the breach brandishing a sabre
and goading the men on. A total of 200 Cristinos had been wounded
and some 70 killed before a relief column ﬁnally arrived to save Torá.
Pastors’s counteroffensive halted the Carlist incursion on his left, retak-
ing Guimerá and establishing a front line from there to the university
town of Cervera and Torá. This protected Barcelona by ensuring Cristino
control of southern, and a large part of central, Catalonia, but it relin-
quished much of the north and all of the far west to the Carlists, who
made a regional capital out of Berga and blockaded remaining Cristino
fortiﬁed centres (Olot’s garrison defected to the Carlists on 28 August).160
Even at his base in Cervera Pastors’s control did not extend far beyond
the town. Cervera, the base of the Aggrieved of 1827–28, had a violent
history of conﬂict between its reactionary ‘town’ and Liberal ‘gown’.161
On 12 October 1835, the Liberal university – whose students, travel-
ling to and from Barcelona, had repeatedly been robbed and attacked by
Guergué’s men – was closed.162 After Pastors repulsed a Carlist breakout
towards San Quintín on 23 October 1835, executing prisoners he had
captured, the Catalan front reached stalemate between a Carlist west
and a Cristino coastal hinterland.163
   Elsewhere the Army of the North came under political pres-
sure to match Mendizábal’s rhetoric with action. The two opposing
commanders-in-chief, Córdova and Eguía, fought a bloody stalemate at
the Carlist capital, Estella. Both sides claimed victory, Córdova because
he managed brieﬂy to occupy the capital on 16 November and Eguía
because he quickly liberated the town. Some residents who had bro-
ken cover by welcoming the Cristino occupation were imprisoned and
ransomed.164 With Cristino forces monetarily spent, Eguía chose to
strike not southwards but northwards, to seize control of the French
frontier and coastal towns garrisoned by Cristinos. The latter objec-
tive, he hoped, would mitigate Quadruple Alliance naval supremacy
by ending the growing Anglo-Cristino naval cooperation in matters of
supplying and fortifying bridgeheads. At the onset of the civil war the
Cristino navy had been small and decrepit. But Madrid acquired for-
eign steamships to sustain the blockade of the Carlist coast, and these
                                 War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   109


cooperated with the French and British by transporting men and sup-
plies along the Basque coast; now the Cristino navy would be at full
stretch supplying and defending coastal towns.165 A key target in this
Carlist campaign was therefore San Sebastián, on account of its wealth,
command of the French frontier and its port crammed with Quadru-
ple Alliance vessels. The close blockade had already driven hundreds
of rural refugees into the city and caused a severe shortage of fuel and
food which the shipping bottlenecks had been unable to solve; this was
compounded by the billeting of Cristino troops in private homes. San
Sebastián’s town hall had to counter-license some resident merchants to
whom Carlists had given permits to trade with their zone. These mas-
ters of no-man’s-land brought imported goods to the Carlists in return
for food and fuel for the hungry Cristinos.166
   Carlists began their assault in a swift and devastating conquest of the
fort of Arambarri, adjacent to San Sebastián, wiping out its Asturian
and Andalucían garrison. The Queen-Regent wrote to the city author-
ities, anguished that Arambarri might presage the loss of the city itself,
boosting popular radicalism: ‘it is not just that the faction is embold-
ened insufferably, but also that men may think that my government
has not undertaken precautions in the city’s defence’.167 The loss of
the fort impaired San Sebastián’s deployment of defensive artillery, and
maximised the Carlists’ effectiveness of theirs (only three – but well-
positioned – siege cannon). With the city closely blockaded, the Carlists
turned to securing the French border. In response, French General Arispe
warned the Carlists that if they assaulted the remaining Cristino border
posts, the French army would invade. The French side of the border
had been ﬁlling with refugees, including, ignominiously, the entire
Cristino Provincial Deputation of Guipúzcoa, which timidly feared
Carlist reprisals in the event of San Sebastián’s fall. The French were
afraid the war might spill over the frontier, and whilst they pursued a
hard line with the Carlists, they also ordered all Cristino refugees to
move either deeper into France or back into Spain.168 Sensibly back-
ing down to the threat of formal French intervention, Carlist General
Montenegro decided to assault San Sebastián instead. His close-range,
overnight bombardment of the city began on 6 December. In response,
all remaining 18 to 50-year-old male residents who had not already
enlisted were called into the militia.169 Montenegro hoped that the ﬂight
of city Liberals and the effect of his terror bombardment on refugees
might yield him San Sebastián through a coup from within. But the city
held fast, and the Carlists turned their ﬁre from the city to the forti-
ﬁed ex-convent of San Bartolomé. Concentrated ﬁre forced its Cristino
110 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


garrison to withdraw into the city proper. As the pursuing Carlists occu-
pied the ruins, they burned the wooden bridge linking the remains of
the convent to the rest of the city. Therefore, San Sebastián was closely
blockaded from the landward side, but the offensive lacked the strength
to storm the city. Carlist cannon-bores had become so overworked that
Montenegro was delighted to pose as a humanitarian by agreeing to
a French-brokered ceaseﬁre to evacuate foreign civilian casualties: this
gave him time to repair his guns.170
   Meanwhile, Eguía’s next effort came on the Carlist Left, with the
assault on the fortiﬁed port of Guetaria. At ﬁrst General Córdova, as if
remembering Napoleon’s famous dictum of not interrupting an enemy
making a mistake, indulged Eguía’s strategy. The Carlist drive towards
the coast diverted attention away from the demoralised centre of his
army at Miranda del Ebro. That said, Guetaria commanded a harbour
and a promontory which could shut off most Guipúzcoan cabotage
when emplaced with coastal artillery. The Cristino garrison knew this:
when Carlist guns, on 19 December, opened ﬁre on the town, the
defenders answered with some 13 guns of their own.171 The Carlists
stormed the town, but not before virtually every building in Guetaria
had been destroyed or damaged. They did not remain long in posses-
sion, as Carlists were caught between the pincers of Espartero’s landward
march and amphibious reinforcements from Bilbao.172 Espartero’s troops
included a free corps of Cristino Basque scouts, the chapelgorris, who
were noted for their brutality and who had looted and destroyed ‘Carlist’
churches during their march.173 When they looted a ruined church
in Guetaria, however, Espartero ordered harsh reprisals. He could not
afford to alienate war-ravaged guetarense civilians and accordingly con-
demned ten chapelgorris to death, actually carrying out the sentence on
nine.174 Espartero’s disciplining of anti-Carlist Basque volunteers made
every possible wrong impression with Cristino Radicals who were still
indignant after a publicised exchange of gifts between the Cristino and
Carlist Commanders-in-Chief, and there was even talk of the need for
revolutionary justice against overbearing ofﬁcers. Espartero thought this
interference by civilians the utmost impudence, conﬁding to his wife
that he would: ‘break the heads of those who have asked for mine’.175
   Elsewhere Catalan Carlists were encroaching on the frontier with
France, causing consternation amongst Cristinos and French alike.
Unlike General Arispe in the Basque country, the governor of the border
fortress of Bellagarde appeased the Carlists by promising to permit no
more armed Cristinos into French territory. This overture recoiled on the
French: not only did it recognise the Carlists as legitimate combatants,
                                  War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   111


but it also failed to prevent their border violations.176 These were starkest
in the Cerdanya/Cerdagne region of Catalonia, where Cristino loyalties
in the border town of Puigcerdá and the exclave of Llívia invited hostile
Carlist attention. Llívia was separated from Spain proper by two kilo-
metres of French territory through which a ‘neutral road’ ran to the
Franco-Spanish border at Bourg-Madame/Puigcerdá. The Guergué expe-
dition made refugees out of Puigcerdá’s public ofﬁcials.177 Their ﬂight to
France was followed by an attempt by townsfolk who were stopped, dis-
armed and returned by Carlist irregulars operating in French territory.178
The Carlist violation of French territory was repeated on 6 February
1837 when some 200 of them crossed the border in order to loot Llívia.
On the way back along the neutral road French troops arrested the
Carlists and conﬁscated their pillaged goods.179 On occasion Carlist sol-
diers were found 45 miles inside France, and Carlists issued passports
to aid smuggling.180 Border contingencies like these involved France in
local interventions which moulded debates about the extent of French
intervention in the civil war. This section of the border would not sta-
bilise until Puigcerdá decisively defeated the Carlist siege of November
1837.181
  Mendizábal desperately summoned Espoz from his French convales-
cence to captain the generals in Catalonia and arrest the spread of
Carlism. Espoz was a leftist hero, a sop to junta opinion, and he was on
good terms with the French who gladly transferred to him impounded
Carlist arms. On 29 November 1835, he imposed a state of siege on the
Principality, with measures including collective punishment on commu-
nities invaded by Carlists, ranging from summary executions of heads
of households harbouring enemy agents to the conﬁscation of property.
These conditions were so draconian that even his Liberal allies in gov-
ernment resiled from them. Espoz had always seen the Catalan Carlists
as criminals deserving of no better treatment. But the effect of these
measures was to promote reprisals and to terrorise civilians caught in
the war zone.182
  Espoz had used collective punishment in the Royalist War, and it was
more than matched by what Cabrera was doing in the cruellest front,
the Maestrazgo. Cabrera’s eastern Carlists combined ruthlessness with
bravery but without the magnanimity that had characterised many of
Zumalacárregui’s operations. Cabrera established a pattern of attack-
ing small towns which were garrisoned only by a handful of local or
mobile militiamen. During these localised but ferocious sieges, Cabrera’s
Christian soldiers ironically became more prodigious at church-burning
than the mobs of Barcelona. This was because the church steeple was
112 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


usually the ultimate refuge for Cristino militiamen resolved to ﬁght to
the last, and was targeted by Cabrera’s artillery and arson. Cabrera’s
rural terrorism was answered by Cristino municipalities organising fund-
raising not just for the Militia but also local fortiﬁcations and defences,
all in addition to the usual taxes and conscripts levied by the central
government.183 Carlist atrocities provoked reprisals against mountain
villages where, as the Prussian Carlist, von Goeben, recalled, ‘troops
used terror; all inhabitants, it was said, were Carlists and had to be
destroyed, plundered, violated, and their homes burnt down. Several
hundred were forced by the ensuing misery to join the Carlists.’184 The
Cristino counter-insurgency had the effect of merely reshaping rather
than eliminating the nature of the Carlist threat. General Nogueras
mobilised both troops and mobile militia but his expeditions caused
the Carlists to regroup, reducing their ability to terrorise but increasing
their striking power. Thus, the columns of Miralles and Torner linked
with Quílez in Aragón, whilst Cabrera reorganized his and Forcadell’s
men at Beceite, their fortiﬁed headquarters and refuge.185
   The revolution obliged Nogueras to retire to his starting point, and
Cabrera and Forcadell exploited this in an assault on Rubielos (Teruel),
in the nineteenth century a populous town commanding vital transport
links. Its garrison was outnumbered by the Carlists, whose attack on
11 September 1835 resonates both for its representative military details
and its brutal aftermath. Cabrera fought through the town and obliged
the Cristino garrison to withdraw to the highly defensible convent in
the centre. Cabrera tried to force entry using ﬁre and testudo forma-
tions from carts which were armoured and used as battering-rams. He
then seized an adjoining house in an attempt to break into the con-
vent from the side. Desperate, the Cristinos set ﬁre to the house. But the
wind blew the ﬂames into the convent, so the Cristinos, now thirsty and
suffocating in the fumes, hoisted a white ﬂag on Cabrera’s stated guar-
antee of quarter. But when the pitiful survivors descended the convent’s
tower, many were killed outright whilst the rest of the captives were
herded together and marched to a ﬁeld near Nogueruelas where they
were stripped naked (Cabrera needed the uniforms) and forced to run for
their lives. All the men were hunted and killed by the Carlist cavalry, one
corpse registering no fewer than 26 lance wounds.186 The martyrdom
of Nogueruelas outshone Villafranca’s in 1834, and, soon after the war
ended, the public disinterment and reburial of the martyred militiamen
allowed Radicals to capture public memory of the conﬂict.187
   Cabrera’s initiative turned the remote regions of Aragón and Valencia,
centred on the Maestrazgo, into a hybrid of guerrilla insurgency and
                                 War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   113


counter-insurgency punctuated by increasing episodes of intense vio-
lence. Whilst there were indeed economic origins for rural Carlism
and urban liberalism, the war distorted these into geographical loy-
alties as civilians sensibly obeyed whichever side was strongest in
their area. Cabrera’s successful military strategy earned him the sobri-
quet ‘Tiger of the Maestrazgo’, whose threat was answered by signal
sacriﬁces from Cristino populations deprived of adequate garrisons.
Requena (Valencia), for example, resisted Cabrera’s siege because its
women and children dug trenches and constructed barricades, max-
imising the defensive power of its outnumbered garrison until a relief
column arrived.188 The revolution of the juntas undermined Cristino
counter-insurgency efforts as militiamen prioritised parochial interests
and soldiers deserted. Nogueras’s minor victory at Orta on 24 September
1835 was important only because it helped to stem the desertion
affecting his ranks. Insufﬁcient Cristino forces encouraged the spiral of
atrocities and reprisals. There was no Eliot Treaty in the east, and prag-
matism overrode any residual compassion. Cristinos were reluctant to
take Carlists prisoner as they had too few forces to guard them sufﬁ-
ciently (on one occasion, some 600 captured Carlists escaped because
the Cristinos had too few cavalry to round them up).189 By the same
token, any acts of mercy which did result from military action usually
had pragmatic motives. On 17 October 1835, Cabrera assaulted Alcanar,
only four miles from the Mediterranean Sea on the Catalan–Valencian
border. There, the tragic routine of an outnumbered Cristino garrison
retiring to the biggest church, defending itself to the last, even throw-
ing beehives at the attackers, repeated itself. Cabrera had wiped out a
scratch Cristino force rushed from the nearby port of Vinaroz which
had tried to relieve Alcanar. The desperation of the besieged women
and children compelled the militiamen to surrender and the town was
sacked.190 Alcanar cut off land communications between revolutionaries
in Barcelona and Valencia. But Cabrera’s dash for the sea was short-lived,
as Anglo-Cristino gunboats repulsed his attempt to seize a nearby port,
and overreach eventually obliged him to evacuate Alcanar.
   Cabrera’s ascendancy was formalised with the 11 November 1835
royal decree making him Commander-in-Chief of Lower Aragón.
Cabrera was still theoretically answerable to the junta at Berga, but in
practice his forces – which at the end of 1835 numbered some 3,416
infantry and 218 cavalry – imposed military rule in territory he com-
manded. Cobblers were forced to produce footwear, Cristino columns –
such as that of Terrer (Zaragoza) on 13 December – were wiped out
and raided for weapons and uniforms, and a factory was constructed to
114 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


turn saltpetre, lead and sulphur into arms.191 In response, the Cristino
counter-insurgency imposed its own state of siege, impounded deliver-
ies of war material to areas where Carlists were known to be operating,
and exploited occasions in which urban and rural geography favoured
the Cristino order of battle. After Terrer Cabrera invaded Castile, but at
Molina de Aragón terrain and cavalry superiority favoured the Cristinos,
and General Palarea won a victory which dispersed the invaders and
silenced his revolutionary critics. Cristino victories were rare that winter,
so Prime Minister Mendizábal tried to consolidate this success by offer-
ing an amnesty to Cabrera’s men. Many accepted but the vast majority
who did used it to ‘change shirts’ and regroup in Aragón.192
   The brutality of the war in both Aragón and Catalonia radicalised
Cristino politics, most violently in Barcelona. The Ciudadela fortress
contained several Carlist prisoners, including the high-proﬁle Colonel
Juan O’Donnell. Civil Governor Pastors knew that O’Donnell was
the target of vengeful radicalism and desperately tried to get him
transported elsewhere, even interceding with the British consul about
