possibilities for the evacuation of prisoners by Royal Navy ships.193
Matters were made worse by newspapers’ exaggeration of atrocities com-
mitted by the Carlist defenders of the besieged hill fortress of Hort
(‘news’ reached Barcelona that the Carlists were answering each Cristino
artillery shot by throwing live militia prisoners out of the fortress
windows to their deaths below).194 Cristino spymaster, Eugenio de
Aviraneta, whom Mendizábal had dispatched to Barcelona to work for
the ailing Espoz, was anonymously tipped off that the Carlist prisoners
of the Ciudadela would be massacred.195 On 4 January 1836, Barcelona
revolutionaries allied with sympathetic militiamen and marched on the
lightly defended Ciudadela, proclaiming Liberty and the Constitution
of 1812. Pastors showed physical bravery once again by mediating,
but whilst he spared the Ciudadela’s powder stores from a potentially
catastrophic explosion, he could do nothing for the inmates, who were
all massacred. Carlists turned O’Donnell into a martyr, recording his
last words as ‘Give me a sword! Don’t let me die at the hands of vile
assassins!’196 The massacre was worse than Carlist outrages at Hort.
Although seven Cristino prisoners had indeed been barbarically defen-
estrated, many more victims were Carlists themselves who, after a failed
breakout on 23 January, had desperately tried to escape the siege by low-
ering themselves onto the rocks below. When Hort was ﬁnally taken,
some 104 unmolested Cristino casualties were found whom the Carlists
had abandoned rather than massacred.197
                                 War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   115


   Local authorities thought it expedient to excuse the prison mas-
sacre with reference to ‘the inhumane ferocity and cruelty with which
the defenders of the usurpation sacriﬁced at Hort and other parts of
Catalonia a large number of our prisoners’.198 But behind the scenes,
army reinforcements were quietly entering the city, Pastors was deposed
for his temporising and Captain-General Espoz dared to impose a state
of siege. Proclamations of the Constitution of 1812 were forbidden
and the most egregious Radicals in the militia arrested and exiled to
the Canaries. To guard against sedition, prisoners were dispatched, for
the ﬁrst leg of the journey, on Royal Navy rather than Cristino ships.
Amongst the deported was Aviraneta, who was accused of inciting the
massacre with alarmist claims of a Sardinian-led international Carlist
invasion bound for Catalonia.199
   But there were international plots, and Don Carlos was playing to the
international gallery of public opinion when, on 20 February, his man-
ifesto condemned the Liberals as anarchists and foreign revolutionaries
whose barbaric outrages at Barcelona, Zaragoza and elsewhere made
a mockery of the humanitarian intervention of ‘respectable powers’.
True Spaniards would not stomach such ‘democracy’, which amounted
to barbarism.200 His manifesto unwittingly coincided with news of the
execution of Ramón Cabrera’s mother, who had been held hostage by
Cristino forces for almost two years, and this apparently senseless act
of barbarity ricocheted around Europe in support of Don Carlos’s mani-
festo. María Griñó’s iconic end reveals a story of the intimate madness of
revenge, for she was shot in reprisal for Cabrera’s execution of the may-
ors of two villages in contested areas of Teruel province (Valdealgorfa
and Torrecilla), who had given Cristino mobile militiamen intelligence
about local Carlists.201 The mayors’ deaths prompted the Cristino arrest
of Cabrera’s three sisters, two of whom, ironically, were married to mili-
tiamen who appear to have connived at their escape. Tragically, as soon
as Cabrera got news of his sisters’ escape, he avenged his mother by
executing four Cristino hostages of his own, all wives or daughters of
serving Cristino ofﬁcers.202 Such high-proﬁle Cristino victims obscured
countless others known only to their families and, sometimes, the Radi-
cal press. Thus, the ﬁve-year Carlist occupation of the hill settlement of
Alcalá de la Selva (Teruel) began with the dragging out of his home and
public execution of the ‘only liberal in the village’.203
   Such barbarism certainly complicated the task of the Cristino
Commander-in-Chief of the Army of the North, as troops had to be
diverted to the cities and captured Cristinos were even more likely to
116 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


face reprisals from outraged Carlists. Two weeks after the Barcelona mas-
sacre, Córdova began an offensive at the mountain pass of Arlabán
dividing Guipúzcoa from Alava, employing ‘foreign revolutionaries’ –
French and British auxiliaries – in large numbers for the ﬁrst time.
But the Carlists turned the geography, snow and fog to their defensive
advantage, repelling the Cristinos at the cost of only half the enemy’s
600 casualties. Captured British and French auxiliaries were shot, and
retreating French committed reprisals of their own against Carlist pris-
oners whom the Cristinos had handed over to them.204 Arlabán had
been one of the strongest parts of the Carlist defensive line, but
Córdova lacked the intelligence networks needed to have known this
in advance.205 Consequently, he was thrown back onto the defensive,
resorting to the static strategic warfare of the blockhouse system.
   In different conditions Córdova’s static strategy might have been
sounder than Espoz’s war of movement. But the Carlists turned the
enemy’s revolutionary crisis and the porous French border to their
advantage. The Royal Army, with its 16,000 infantry, 1,000 cavalry and
13 artillery pieces, was vastly outnumbered by the Army of the North.206
But superior local intelligence, internal lines of communication and the
distraction provided by the expanding Carlist forces in Catalonia and
Aragón, all allowed the Carlists to wrest the initiative after Arlabán in
a series of victories. On 8 February 1836, the Carlists overwhelmed the
1,300-strong Cristino outpost of Valmaseda, capturing ﬁve swivel-guns,
360 riﬂes, 60,000 bullets and 65 quintals of cod, 800 bread rations, 53
boxes of biscuit and other stores besides. The quarter shown to the garri-
son encouraged the capitulation of nearby Mercadillo three days later.207
Things got worse still for Córdova. Plencia, a coastal town on the west-
ern extremities of the Basque provinces, was taken after a short siege
on 25 February 1836, giving a morale boost to the Carlists, who had
now captured a seaport (albeit a very modest one); another seaport,
Lequeitio, would follow on 12 April. A total of 800 prisoners were taken
at Plencia, including National Guardsmen, the revolutionary rebrand-
ing of the Urban Militia, who were conscripted into Carlist ranks whilst
their ofﬁcers went to depots to await exchange. Some 13 artillery pieces
and other effects were taken as prizes, and a 3,000-real ﬁne (the Cristino
‘blood tax’) was imposed on those refusing to defect to Carlist ranks.208
Apart from a minor cavalry victory at Orduña by General Esparero
(whom Córdova had placed under Lacy Evans’s command in a bid for
more British support), followed by a bloody stalemate at Unza two weeks
later on 19 March, the winter and spring of 1836 had seen the Carlists
steadily undermining the Cristino blockade.209
                                 War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   117


   The greatest strategic blow to the Army of the North originated not
at Don Carlos’s court in Durango but in Paris. Since July 1834, the
French had ofﬁcially closed their south-western border to all supplies
destined for the Carlists. But smuggling was a way of life in the Pyrenees,
and it was in part due to this that Zumalacárregui, throughout 1834,
had been able to assemble his army under the noses of the Cristino
forts. That said, the Spanish Liberals knew from bitter experience that
the French police force was the best in Europe, and French protesta-
tions to the contrary cut little ice with Prime Minister Mendizábal, who
scoffed, ‘let them treat the smugglers as they treated me and other Patri-
ots when we tried to cross the Pyrenees in 1830’.210 But worse was to
come. In February 1836, the Broglie government fell and was replaced by
a new administration led by Thiers, which tried to appease Metternich’s
alarm at the internationalisation of the Spanish revolution. Hence, on
26 March 1836, the French government revoked its 3 July 1835 decree,
which had intensiﬁed the embargo on any trade with Carlists on the
Spanish side of the border. French legitimists rejoiced when they heard
that their allies in Spain would not be starved into surrender. Madrid’s
indignation was only partly appeased by French permission for the
transit of Cristino troops and supplies across their territory. This mea-
sure may have allowed Córdova to outﬂank some Carlist positions, but
its advantage paled in comparison to the abandonment of the French
blockade.211
   Córdova was caught in the vice of Radical pressure and Carlist rear-
mament, and whereas the Carlist threat could be evaluated, that of
the revolutionaries could not. In April 1836, as two regiments rose in
a pay revolt in Bilbao, Córdova conﬁded to General Narváez: ‘I fear that
agents of different extreme parties are working my men with sinister
aims, singling out the sergeants and troops who are most vulnerable
to seduction and intrigue.’212 In May 1836, two companies of Mili-
tia rampaged through the village of Arévalo (Soria), some 50 miles
behind the northern front, smashing the windows of ‘Carlist’ inhabi-
tants. Cristino civil authorities were helpless and the militiamen had to
be disarmed at gunpoint by loyal troops.213 Only victory could silence
Córdova’s critics and stem rebellion in the ranks. British auxiliaries
allied with Royal Marines (a new departure for Quadruple Alliance ‘non-
intervention’) and Cristino militiamen in a bloody attempt on 5 May to
relieve besieged San Sebastián. The withering bombardment by Anglo-
Cristino gunboats and the death of the charismatic Carlist commander,
Sagastibelza, sealed the Allied victory, and the siege of San Sebastián
was lifted.214 At the same time, Córdova renewed his offensive against
118 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


the heights of Arlabán, even though the same geography, and even
the same snow on the peaks, favoured the defenders once more. The
result was a bloody stalemate, compounded by Cristino casualties from
exposure as men could not light ﬁres lest they reveal their positions,
and instead slept beside each other and mules for warmth. Córdova’s
renewed assault in the same sector of the front was greeted with relief
by the Carlist Royal Government and consternation in Madrid. Eguía
knew that there were more vulnerable sectors of the Carlist front line
(the Baztán, Irurzun, Lecumberri), and he thanked the enemy’s rev-
olutionary crisis for having made Córdova so blind and desperate.
Prime Minister Mendizábal, by contrast, had lost patience with his
commander-in-chief. Córdova’s protestations that the second battle of
Arlabán had actually strengthened the Cristino line around the Basque
country made no impression on the Radicals, who demanded swift
victories.215
  The second battle of Arlabán came in the wake of an irate exchange
of letters between Córdova and Mendizábal, in which the military will
clashed with the political. Both men implied that the other was neglect-
ing to put the interests of the fatherland (patria) ﬁrst, emotive language
in the Liberal revolution, and its use was all the shriller from Mendizábal
because of his cosmopolitan background. He assured his commander-
in-chief that Madrid was doing all it could, decreeing mobilisations,
advancing the revolution and even being aided by a fortuitous cabi-
net change in Portugal – which promised to bring 10,000 auxiliaries to
Córdova’s side – and that all this could come to sound effect if only
the commander-in-chief would show some initiative and achieve vic-
tories. Córdova called Mendizábal’s decrees empty, castles in the air,
and his quest for victories as mistaking cause for effect, of wanting to
reap the harvest of victory without sowing the seeds. Córdova claimed
a detailed understanding of the fundamentally defensive nature of the
war which made no impression on Mendizábal. No Cristino comman-
der could force the Carlists to engage if they did not want to. Finally,
Córdova tendered his resignation, trying to remove the potential polit-
ical threat this posed by reassuring the prime minister of his Liberal
credentials in a manner which nonetheless revealed the powers of his
ofﬁce. Thus Córdova boasted about how many convents he had closed
on his own authority, and stated he was in agreement with the legal
reforms and the sale of nationalised property. Córdova, who claimed to
be non-political, obviously could become political at any moment. The
choice facing the Queen-Regent was obvious: either her prime minis-
ter or commander-in-chief had to go.216 On 4 May 1836, Córdova was
                                  War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   119


summoned to Madrid and a council of war; where his fate and that of
Mendizábal would be decided.217
   The Queen-Regent, who was hardly even liberal, let alone progresista,
in sympathies, seized upon Córdova’s presence in the capital to dis-
miss her Radical prime minster and to call new elections. Córdova had
wrongfooted the Radicals by leaving Espartero in temporary command
in the north, dashing their hopes of animosity between the two men.
Once in Madrid, Córdova petitioned for four things, all of which the
government approved. Firstly he sued for reinforcement. He asked for
whatever troops could be spared from Madrid plus that France be asked
to increase its legion by 4,000 to 6,000 men, the same request to be
made to the British that they might thus reinforce Evans’s strategi-
cally vital position. Secondly, he wanted a Reserve Army to be created
and centred on Burgos. This force should be comprised almost entirely
of National Militia that could be subjected to army command when-
ever necessary.218 Clearly, a more divisive issue for Liberal politics could
scarcely be imagined. Thirdly, he begged for sufﬁcient supplies so that
his army would not starve, beg and riot. And fourthly, Córdova made a
partisan demand which he disguised as a concern for the morale of the
Cristino ‘home front’, namely that the government should refrain from
making a formal request for direct French intervention unless it was
clear that Paris would reply in the afﬁrmative.219 A likely French refusal
would leave Cristino Spain diplomatically stranded and make the posi-
tion of the French Legion untenable. In fact, Córdova was worried that
a refusal would boomerang against the moderados in domestic politics
as it became clear that they did not have the French army as a counter-
revolutionary card to play as a last resort against the exaltados (as the
absolutists had done in 1823). Silence on this matter, by contrast, might
maintain the illusion of the French army’s moderantismo.220
   But the victory of the Queen-Regent and her general was short-lived.
The Cristino army continued to collapse as a cause and consequence
of Carlist expansion, and ousted mendizabalistas began to manipulate
Radicals in the army in order to win back power. The Radical revolu-
tion of August 1836 would be made by junior ofﬁcers and sergeants
against their own leaders, and the Constitution of 1812 they revived
was designed to ensure both a greater degree of civil authority over the
suspect army leadership, and an emasculation of the powers of the trou-
blesome Queen-Regent.221 In the meantime, the removal of Mendizábal
encouraged ofﬁcers to take revenge on the Liberal press for ‘abusing’
its freedom of expression to criticise the army.222 Córdova’s brother, a
colonel based in the capital during the summer of 1836, invaded the
120 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


home of a Radical editor, assaulting him and insulting his wife. A simi-
lar episode almost unfolded at the headquarters of the all-powerful Eco
del Comercio, but a National Militia detachment was on its premises to
face down the indignant ofﬁcers.223 But Córdova’s ascendancy could not
last long. His return to the northern front did nothing to arrest the dete-
rioration of the war effort. On 11 July 1836, Carlists repulsed a British
attempt to evict them from the French frontier at Fuenterrabia, and on
17 July 1836 a Franco-Cristino force of six battalions failed to force the
Carlist-held bridge at Muniáin on the River Ega. In frustration Bernelle’s
legionaries burnt the harvest of local Carlist villagers, which the Carlists
publicised as part of their reactionary Black Legend against the French
Left and with a threat to execute Cristino prisoners if such outrages were
repeated.224
   The Quadruple Alliance had undertaken a greater military burden
because desertion and draft evasion had rendered the Cristino Army
of North unﬁt for further offensive operations. Even defensive troops
increasingly surrendered at the ﬁrst opportunity, many of these defect-
ing to the Carlists. On 10 June, Carlist Commander-in-Chief Eguía
publicised his government’s offer to ‘true Spaniards’ who wished to
defect, including a sliding scale of rewards depending on rank and chat-
tels, and promotions for ofﬁcers who brought numerous defectors with
them. In response the Cristinos imposed drastic measures, including
cash incentives and even promotions to informers betraying plots to
defect, and verbal councils of war imposing the death penalty against
offenders.225 Yet even if such measures stemmed some defection on the
northern front, they could do nothing to reverse the draft-dodging and
desertion that plagued the Cristino safe zone behind the formal front
line. Virtual ‘reserve armies’ of deserters roamed across the country-
side, sometimes securing remote territory against punitive missions of
mobile militia and preying on civilian communities and travellers. The
revolution of 1835–36 may have ‘democratised’ conscription by abolish-
ing exemptions which the nobility traditionally enjoyed (by abolishing
the nobility as a separate legal estate). Yet in their place it formalised
the system of cash payments (‘blood tax’) for exemption.226 And the
vast majority of draftees had neither money nor protection to escape
conscription. As a British pro-Carlist remarked:

   I had no idea of the desolating effects of the repeated quintas, or
   conscriptions, until I had traversed Spain in so many directions, but
   I was not so forcibly struck with them anywhere as in Estremadura,
   where I saw only old men, women or children; the youth having
                                War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   121


  been carried off four times within the last two years, and the land
  being tilled only in the immediate neighbourhood of the villages.227

It was small wonder that conscripts increasingly either absconded or
deserted once they had been pressed into service. Fugitives and desert-
ers, of course, resented state power in principle. But given the tendency
of the Radical-Liberals towards populism, and given that their platform
of autonomous town halls offered certain networks to shield men from
conscription (Militia membership, employment, fraud), Radical-Liberals
were often successful at mobilising a popular hatred of conscription
against the moderados, especially in regions like Andalucía that were
far from the Basque country. Earmarked conscripts without friends in
authority sometimes committed the desperate act of leaving town to
impersonate militiamen in settlements where their anonymity could
make them look convincing, complete with embellished uniforms and
fake moustaches. Local authorities often chose to ignore this tragic cha-
rade, except when this phenomena evolved into further wrongdoing,
such as in Seville, where complaints reached the national press of fake
militia street-rowdies, and in Granada, where a spate of burglaries were
committed by ‘criminals masquerading as militiamen’.228 In 1837, the
Ofﬁce of National Sub-inspector of Militia was set up in order to stamp
out this fraud. In Málaga during the May 1836 elections mendizabal-
ista Liberals conveniently described wrongdoers in nearby Vélez-Málaga
as ‘enemy insurgents’ rather than the deserters and draft-dodgers the
moderado newspaper magnate, Andrés Borrego, believed them to be.
Borrego, whose surname means ‘lamb’ in Spanish, was lampooned in
a charivari involving the symbolic slaughter and skinning of a lamb,
which was then turned into the mascot at the helm of a wagon carrying
copies of Borrego’s offending newspaper that was publicly burnt. Radi-
cal Málaga did not like being reminded that so much of its power rested
on indulging the popular classes’ hatred of military service.229 When the
government, in October 1835, complained of its slowness in drawing up
lists for conscription, Málaga responded by vehemently professing its
patriotism whilst ‘disappearing’ draftees from the lists all the same.230
   Other Andalucían communities also shielded their menfolk as best
they could. Antequera, for example, exaggerated the very modest con-
cessions to anti-militarism imposed by exaltados in 1836 (men’s right
to object to their name being included on a list of potential draftees at
the very moment that the list was compiled) into a vehicle for shielding
virtually all of its menfolk from conscription.231 Vélez-Málaga’s munici-
pality continued to connive in draft-dodging and was ﬁned accordingly
122 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


in early 1838.232 Radical liberalism was more implacably opposed to
Carlism than moderantismo ever was. But leftist mayors maintained a
parochial relationship with the war, especially in parts of Cristino Spain
far from the front line, defending the expansion of their local power-
bases, the militia, but opposing conscription. In some instances, this
anti-militarism bordered on terrorism. In early 1840, the prefect of
Granada toured his restive province and was depressed to discover a
history of wrongdoing which local authorities could not – or would
not – prosecute. In August 1836, the Mayor of Baza had sent militia-
men to seek and murder an unpopular customs guard and war veteran,
Antonio Honorati, whose body was dragged back into Baza bearing both
his assailants’ wounds and older ones inﬂicted by Carlists in Navarra.
The following year, another Cristino soldier was murdered in public
view and his killers went unpunished. Mayors who tried to punish sub-
sequent killings were themselves terrorised and even banished. At the
root of this lawlessness, the prefect held, were the decrepit militia forces
which, in this region, ‘contained simple day-labourers and vicious fel-
lows with no stake in society’, including brigands who (before 1834)
‘belonged to the frenzied royalists who committed outrages in the name
of God and who were now doing the same in the name of Liberty’.233
   Episodes of popular anti-militarism were repeated across dozens of
locations throughout Cristino Spain. By the summer of 1836, these
had been exploited by Radicals to expel the commander-in-chief and
restrain the powers of the Queen-Regent with the Constitution of 1812
during the revolution at La Granja (see Chapter 6). Córdova had just
suppressed a military conspiracy at his base in Logroño in favour of the
Constitution of 1812 (a plot which appeared to be political cover for
mass desertion as the city’s artillery was to be spiked and troops were
to retire to Aragón). Soon after his success, however, news reached him
of the La Granja rising, which had made that Constitution the law of
the land. Córdova saw this as ﬁnal notice to quit. The exit of Córdova
meant the exit of the natural leader of the moderados, in the same way
that the death of Espoz four months later would deprive the progresis-
tas of their hero. The government asked Córdova to recommend his
successor, which he did willingly, recommending Espartero, despite the
latter’s rumoured progresismo. Córdova made his way to France with his
personal escort, refusing to swear allegiance to the Constitution of 1812
whilst not directly disparaging it either. Crossing into France unscathed,
on 25 August 1836 he met the Spanish consul at Bayonne, instructing
him that he should obey the new government, whilst also conferencing
                                 War Radicalises Cristino Spain, 1835–36   123


with some exiled Carlists, including Maroto, with whom he would even
strike up a friendship. Córdova’s parleying with the enemy appeared to
conﬁrm Radical accusations that the moderados were a ﬁfth column for
Carlism. In all likelihood, however, Córdova was motivated by humani-
tarian rather than treacherous concerns.234 But whatever the truth of this
matter, the march of events had eclipsed Córdova, his military strategy
and his moderado allies.
6
Deep War Feeds Revolution,
1836–37




This chapter charts ‘deep war’, when the extent of violence, suffering
and civilian casualties reached their peak. It is the geographically most
extensive chapter, detailing the Gómez Expedition of 1836. It explains
how the collapse of the Cristino war effort – pace the Carlists’ third
failed siege of Bilbao – radicalised Liberal politics and disrupted social
and economic relations across Spain, and forged new patterns in their
place which became a focus for Radical political action. It shows how
this radicalisation was caused by suspicion of moderado collusion with
Carlism, by actual or threatened Carlist invasion, and by the interlinked
phenomena of mass desertion and gloriﬁed banditry that this provoked.
It explains the diplomatic crisis into which Cristino Spain was plunged
by the August 1836 revolution at La Granja.
   The cycle of Carlist atrocities and Cristino counter-atrocities mounted
throughout 1836 and provides the crucial context for the La Granja rev-
olution. The Basque theatre had the Eliot Treaty and increasingly formal
(though still porous) front lines, but Catalonia and Aragón witnessed
an increase in military activity without any of the humanitarian pro-
visions of the north. By April 1836 Carlist forces in Catalonia alone
had grown to 13,367 infantry and 218 cavalry, reinforced by troops
from Navarra, and this greater military presence led to a correspond-
ing war for resources. On 10 May, Tristany sacked the town of Caldes,
25 miles north of Barcelona, after it had failed to supply his forces,
destroying all but a handful of its buildings. Mobile Militia chased the
invaders northwards, but not before Tristany’s men had abducted the
wives of several militiamen and pillaged villages in their path. Terroristic
episodes like these were met with harsh countermeasures, targeting not
just the Carlists, but also demoralised Cristino militia garrisons which
were ordered to ﬁght Carlist invaders whenever the latter’s numbers did

                                    124
                                    Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   125


not exceed one-half of local available defence forces. Crippling ﬁnes
were to be imposed on local authorities which failed to enforce this
order, whilst a deforestation programme was even ordered in an attempt
to deprive Carlist bands of natural cover.1 Months earlier, the ﬁrst siege
of Gandesa was underway, a town whose siege became legendary in
nineteenth-century Spain on account of its heroic women. On 5 March
1836, Cabrera, Torner, Quílez and El Organista attacked the town. Not
only the local militia but also the civilian population, including women,
rallied to its defence. Luisa Bará honoured the example of Zaragoza’s
Agustina de Aragón by delivering arms and food to the defenders whilst
under ﬁre. Turning to terror to subdue the defenders, Torner seized from
adjoining villages the wives and daughters of militiamen and impris-
oned them. The Cristinos replied by imprisoning powerful men and
women in Gandesa who were related to the besieging Carlists. Gandesa’s
heroism was thus overshadowed by a mundane cycle of reprisals.2
   The war in the east was Cabrera’s war: on 11 April Eguía promoted him
to brigadier, and on 15 August 1836 to major-general.3 On 29 March
1836 Cabrera took Liria, just four hours from Valencia and a wealthy
town in its own right. Some 20 militiamen were killed in the ﬁelds and
streets. Twenty-seven more were captured and led to Chiva where they
were slowly murdered by a gruesome ritual, which included being shot
in the kneecaps at point-blank range before having their genitals sliced
off.4 Castration had been a common ritual in the French Wars of Reli-
gion for emasculating the enemy, feminising bodies in death which in
life had not been ‘truly masculine’.5 Carlists saw their enemy as emascu-
lated by the double usurpation of an illegitimate and female candidate
on the throne. The wives and daughters of the Chiva victims, for their
part, did not submit to the domestic grief traditionally prescribed to
their sex. Rather, they joined militiamen on campaign seeking to exact
vengeance on the perpetrators. Palarea overwhelmed Cabrera’s rear-
guard near Chiva and committed a revenge massacre against captured
Carlists.6 But Torner soon counter-attacked, dispersing Palarea’s men.
Women avenged their men not only by killing Carlists, but also incom-
petent or cowardly ofﬁcers on the Cristino side. When some militiamen
were killed in September 1836 in a skirmish near Alcaraz (Albacete) with
the Carlist irregular, Orejita, their widows went to the Militia captain’s
house and, ﬁnding him absent, burnt it down, killing his wife who was
inside.7
   Catalan communities were thus put in an impossible position. Both
Cristinos and Carlists punished communities severely for helping the
other side. Even though the Cristinos lifted the provincial state of
126 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


siege on 5 November 1836, they also applied the law of 17 April
1821, which exempted charges of ‘conspiracy’ and ‘anti-constitutional
activities’ (activities described as infracción de Constitución) from civil
jurisdiction. Further north in Catalonia, villages were reduced to penury,
something which in itself encouraged villagers to ﬂock to the Carlist
ranks so that they might at least become perpetrators rather than vic-
tims. An economic war ensued. In order to prevent hardship swelling
the ranks of the Cristino Militia, Carlist commanders frequently forced
local landowners under their sway to hire hands in order to starve the
Cristinos of recruits.8 Invasions of Cristino territory were marked by
Carlists’ sacking and pillaging civilian property they did not expect to
hold. Quílez’s guerrillas, who, on 29 June 1836, were repulsed when
they attacked a fortiﬁed church in Alcorisa (Teruel), burnt down 173
neighbouring houses before retreating towards Montalbán. Quílez then
stormed Montalbán after its Cristino militia had murdered two of his
emissaries. Quílez performed a vengeful ruse by burning Montalbán, but
not before granting its inhabitants an hour to ﬂee with as many posses-
sions as they could carry. After burning the town, Quílez’s vanguard
hunted the refugees and robbed them all the same.9 Sometimes even
such acts of total war could appear comparatively merciful. By the onset
of the Maestrazgo winter the Carlists had extended their terror against
remaining Cristino settlements in Teruel province. On 20 October 1836,
some 100 conscripts from Extremadura surrendered the church and
village of Albentosa (Teruel) after brief resistance against the outnumber-
ing guerrilla forces El Royo and Peinado. Evacuation terms were being
discussed when an exterminating angel priest, a certain José Lorente,
who had been attached to a different band, arrived to insist on the
Cristinos’ deaths. Some 73 captives were stripped naked and killed in
the road, including an 11-year-old who had followed his father from
Extremadura, despite desperate pleas for mercy, even offers of ransom,
having been voiced by the local municipality, including priests. Lorente
rejected the ransom, but offered a sadistic compromise: that the people
might ﬁnd replacements for the soldiers’ fate. After the massacre was
over, Lorente robbed the inhabitants of the money they had offered for
ransom all the same.10
   Patrician Liberals unhelpfully condemned the enemy’s barbarity as
the product of an ignorant plebeian rural culture, vulnerable to back-
ward clerics and deprived of productive labour. The rural, asymmetrical
nature of the Carlist insurgency complicated the Cristino counter-
insurgency. The fortiﬁed Carlist hill town of Cantavieja, Cabrera’s
strategically most valuable possession, was thus a rare target for a
                                    Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   127


conventional assault. It was known to hold Cristino prisoners as well
as foodstores which could augment meagre Cristino rations at the onset
of the dire regional winter. Evaristo San Miguel, Radical prime minis-
ter during the Triennium and Cristino Captain-General of Aragón since
the La Granja revolution, conquered Cantavieja at the end of October
1836, and the momentum of the offensive persuaded the Carlists to
burn rather than try to defend Beceite, their other fortiﬁed town in the
Maestrazgo. Cantavieja was a boost for revolutionary morale, even if
the foodstores were depleted by corrupt commissaries in the vanguard,
who ended up selling on such delicacies as cinnamon and cocoa at
inﬂated prices.11 Yet San Miguel qualiﬁed his victory address with a grim
prediction for Cristino communities:12

  Cantavieja, hitherto a nest of bandits and false defenders of the
  altar and throne, is now under national occupation . . . Yet, people of
  Aragón, defend yourselves. Dedicate yourselves to this sacred task the
  moment you fall under enemy attack. Defend your homes, which are
  yours after all, along with the fruit of your sweat and toil. Who else
  will care as much about your security and who else is willing to match
  your sacriﬁces?

Civilians were thus on their own, and were exhorted to manage their
own affairs as best they could. Army units were too dispersed, and
even militia garrisons could be overwhelmed in point-defence, often
in circumstances which recoiled on civilians they abandoned. Soneja
(Valencia) during the summer of 1836, for example, saw its militia garri-
son ﬂee the superior forces of El Serrador, but not before it had poisoned
foodstores which it knew the Carlists would seize. El Serrador avenged
the agonies of his men not on the absent militia, but on the inhabitants,
who were ﬁred upon as they ﬂed their burning houses.13 Patrician Lib-
erals who were unable to gain victory over the enemy instead proposed
prophylactic measures to divert listless men from Carlist bands. National
elites, like San Miguel, and the deputy for Alicante, Joaquín Abarques,
shared the vague opinion that ‘agricultural improvements’ could turn
Carlist swords back into ploughshares.14 Abarques became a self-parody
of patrician arrogance when he proposed ‘enlightening’ the insurgent
provinces by sending them copies of the Constitution of 1837.15 Local
elites, like Barcelona’s Council of Armament and Defence, worried about
the inﬂux of refugees who might be blandished into Carlist ﬁfth-column
activities, and prescribed practical measures like putting the idle to
work in nationalised monasteries and convents.16 Such measures were
128 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


either unhelpfully long term in nature or limited to reinforcing control
of urban centres already inside the Cristino zone. But what mattered
most during the middle years of the war was the expansion of the con-
ﬂict, and not just in Catalonia and Aragón, but also across Castile and
even Andalucía, where banditry grew under the banner of Carlism and
expeditions tore across the countryside. Civilians who were neither sol-
diers nor active revolutionary citizens (militiamen) had to forge their
own responses to violence, occupations and counter-occupations in an
insecure environment.
   Popular responses to this security crisis were part and parcel of the
leftist revolution in the Cristino zone. Jacobin politics had overtaken
Madrid in August 1836, and this was soon reﬂected in the ﬁgure of
Antonio Quiroga, who enjoyed even better Radical-Liberal credentials
than San Miguel, taking over the Captaincy-Generalship of Aragón.
The victorious Army of the Centre was stripped of men to help con-
front a Carlist breakout from the northern front (Gómez expedition),
allowing Carlists to regain lost territory. The Carlist breakout tested the
revolutionary Spain of La Granja beyond breaking point.
   Meanwhile, the Queen-Regent’s closure of the Cortes led to insurrec-
tions in Málaga, Granada and Cartagena, the last one being a bloody
exercise in settling scores. When Cartagena’s governor, O’Daly, deported
the miscreants to Ceuta, the prison ships were recalled so the cul-
prits could stand trial and O’Daly was replaced by Count Mirasol, the
hero of Bilbao. The Málaga revolution had heavy republican under-
tones, despite its protestations of loyalty to the Queen-Regent.17 In July
1836, Donadío, now military governor, was assassinated after some
800 troops under his command refused his order to disarm the rad-
icalised Militia and to come to the rescue of the civil governor, José
San Just, who was hiding from enraged militiamen. When San Just
ﬂed to a nearby building, he was shot dead by Carlos Barritaro, a
militiaman who in 1838 became a correspondent in a press campaign
defending the widows of two murdered Radicals against persecution
by moderado authorities. Donadío, meanwhile, disguised himself as a
National Guardsman and tried to ﬂee, only to be executed in a simi-
lar fashion.18 The following day, a quickly assembled junta arranged for
the Constitution of 1812 to be proclaimed in Málaga, and the revolu-
tion spread until the Queen-Regent was physically forced by rebellious
sergeants to sign the Constitution on 13 August 1836. Some 2,000
malagueño militiamen were dispatched to Catalonia to ﬁght Carlists
in the name of the Constitution.19 Málaga’s radicalism expressed one
extreme of the 1836 revolution; the other extreme was the gentlemanly
                                    Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   129


stand-off between the pro-Constitution militia and the town hall of
Cáceres (Extremadura), which went the militia’s way without the need
for any violence let alone killing.20
   As the Radical insurrection spread, the patriarch of exaltismo, Evaristo
San Miguel, deﬁed the Istúriz government’s orders to resign his com-
mand of the Army of the Centre and rallied the Zaragoza Militia
to the cause of the Constitution of 1812, keeping loyal government
forces at bay. Santander’s garrison joined the revolution because of pay
and supply grievances.21 Málaga, the cradle of revolution, exercised its
sovereignty by ‘declaring war’ on the pro-moderado French after one of
the latter’s vessels refused to acknowledge salutes from the revolution-
ary authorities.22 For the time being, meanwhile, Madrid stayed loyal.
Cardero had been mooted as leader of a revolutionary pronunciamiento
in the capital, but Quesada showed some timely ruthlessness for which
he would shortly pay with his life. He proclaimed martial law in the
capital, threatening any political activists who disturbed the peace with
the death penalty. But Madrid’s temporary quiescence was overtaken
by the expanding revolution elsewhere, including Cristino Spain’s sec-
ond city, where Captain-General Espoz, though responding admittedly
less quickly than San Miguel had done in Zaragoza, used his revolu-
tionary credentials to win over Barcelona’s powerful popular Radical
movement to his leadership and to the proclamation of the Constitu-
tion of 1812, thereby forestalling the sort of political violence to which
Catalonia’s capital had become accustomed. The welcome bloodless-
ness of Barcelona’s revolution was Espoz’s last service before he ﬁnally
succumbed to his agonising stomach cancer on 24 December 1836.23
   The revolution culminated at La Granja, at the hands of sergeants
posted to the Spanish monarchy’s summer residence at the town of San
Ildefonso, some 80 kilometres north of the capital. Nineteenth-century
writers, for whom the French Revolution was the dominant example
of royal humiliation, were fascinated by María Cristina’s submission
at La Granja, seeing this as the product of conspiracy funded by Rad-
icals, unlike the popular revolution of the juntas the previous year.24
Certainly, structural pay arrears made even the royal bodyguard vulner-
able to agitators, and it was logical for ofﬁcers and sergeants who could
not pay their men to search for political solutions instead. Twentieth-
century writers gave the La Granja revolution secondary importance to
that of the juntas the previous year. Whilst the social signiﬁcance of
lowly sergeants dictating to royalty was noted, writers saw La Granja
as the work of a mendizabalista clique bent on holding on to power
in the face of newer, and often more radical, chaos generated by the
130 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Málaga revolution and the penetration by the Gómez Expedition of the
Cristino ‘home front’.25 During the ﬁrst days of the sergeants’ rising, the
moderado prime minister who had replaced Mendizábal, Istúriz, tried to
win over the rebels, and when this attempt failed, tensions rose further
as the Madrid government declared the Queen-Regent a captive of the
revolutionaries. London and Paris grew alarmed. But whereas the British
backed the revolution to the hilt (an attitude which raised suspicion that
the whole affair was Ambassador Villiers’ doing), France disapproved of
this violent shift to progresismo. But both Quadruple Alliance powers
advised that the Queen-Regent captain the revolution as an act of free
will on her part: otherwise abdication (or worse) could be the alterna-
tive. Villiers was shocked by the apparent leaderlessness of this army
revolution, and the precedent it might set to Europe.26 Franco-British
diplomacy was proving highly prescient. The militiamen who rose in
1835 and the soldiers in 1836 both did so in the name of the con-
stitutional monarchy, but so central had the army’s claims to Spain’s
political future become that it would not hesitate to dispense with any
given monarch in order to recraft the state in its own image, as the
revolution of 1840 would show. The Queen-Regent was defenceless,
and the revolutionaries sent out fraternal calls to other units exhort-
ing them to proclaim the Constitution of 1812 elsewhere in Cristino
Spain. The army and militia in besieged Pamplona united in replac-
ing the city’s elderly governor with a younger leftist.27 Eventually, María
Cristina agreed to reinstate the Constitution of 1812, and the capital’s
revolutionary militia were rearmed and recalled, unpopular ministers
being sacked whilst other diehard moderados fared worse.28
   The revolutionary repression of political adversaries distinguished
1836 from 1835 as it was done by central government rather than
regional juntas. Mob violence certainly featured strongly, perhaps more
strongly, during 1836, too. Captain-General Quesada was lynched by
a Madrid mob led by revolutionary militiamen, his body mutilated
and paraded around the streets. The former moderado Prime Minister,
Istúriz, escaped a similar fate by ﬂeeing in disguise.29 For weeks after-
wards his son went into hiding whilst his wife was forced into house
arrest by demonstrations of rough music.30 In the provinces moderado
governors were often lynched, such as in Figueres or Cartagena, where
the governor was chased upstairs in his house by militiamen and
defenestrated.31
   Gone were the days of ‘Liberal union’. The capital was placed under
a revolutionary state of siege which invaded the homes of its political
opponents. The war had made the regime desperate for money, but it
                                     Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   131


was shunned by non-British lending markets. The government decreed
the famous round of nationalisation of ecclesiastical properties (known
in Spanish as desamortización), accompanying this with the abolition
of all remaining noble and clerical distinctions in law by the following
summer. A levy of 50,000 men was ordered, the blood tax for exemption
being lowered to 2,000 per draftee, or 1,500 with a viable horse. This
cheaper tax helped the lower middle-class constituency of progresismo.
Wealthier Liberals, by contrast, tended to support the moderados, and
were collectively subjected to a 200,000,000-reales forced loan (termed a
‘patriotic subscription’), captive subscribers being promised reimburse-
ment in instalments yielding 5 per cent interest. Wealthy individuals
who tried to ﬂee the country without permission were subject to the
decree of 16 September 1836, which conﬁscated their property, this
being made retroactive to emigrants since the victory of the La Granja
revolution on 15 August. Conﬁscation of property had so far happened
only to the Carlist enemy, so sectarian theft was a new departure. But
such was the economic chaos that even Finance Minister Mendizábal
could not prevent Cristino Spain defaulting in November 1836, for the
ﬁrst time, on some of its interest repayments.
   Other solutions conveniently conformed to stated progresista policy,
including the revival of the 1823 municipal legislation, which devolved
wide-ranging local autonomy (and ﬁnancial responsibilities), under-
pinned by voting rights for male householders.32 But this reform was
inoperable for two reasons. First, states of siege grew to combat grow-
ing Carlism, and military law overrode all civil jurisdiction, even in
reformed town halls. Second, the rural insecurity was compounded by
the revolutionary Cortes decree that wealthy notables should quit their
homes for safe urban centres whenever they were threatened by Carlists.
This decree was designed to drain the lake of economic Carlism by
depriving the enemy of loot or hostages for ransom. But in fact it proved
disastrous. It recoiled on poorer citizens who were robbed instead of
their absent social betters. Such citizens were even more likely to take
their chances joining Carlist bands (despite the fact that the families
they left behind faced the full rigour of revolutionary reprisals).33 Third,
the ﬂight of wealthy individuals severely undermined both the prac-
tice of respectable politics and the organisation of the militia. The tragic
Mayor of Urda (Toledo), for example, should have been a Cristino suc-
cess story. He had spent his family fortune to become mayor during the
transition of 1833, but the civil war saw nearby insurgents rally under
the Carlist ﬂag, ravaging local agriculture and forcing the mayor to take
on debts. Failing to negotiate a middle ground between Cristino and
132 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Carlist forces, the mayor’s administration was preyed on by both sides,
as local elections of councillors were dominated by corruption and vio-
lence and the whole administration, including the Militia, collapsed
into decrepitude. In May 1836, a 600-strong force of Carlists sacked
Urda, burnt the militia commander’s home to the ground and raped all
the women they could ﬁnd.34 Urda’s remaining wealthy families were
relieved to follow the revolutionary Cortes’ decree to ﬂee the town for
safer urban centres. The mayor tried to follow them but the Cristino
forces, who had declared a state of siege, ordered him to stay in his post,
also refusing his resignation (and even ﬁning him when he made this
desperate plea). At his wits’ end, the mayor took his own life.35 Whatever
else it was, this era of ‘local democracy’ was no ‘golden age’.36
   The La Granja revolution had a greater impact internationally than
the intervention of foreign auxiliaries discussed in Chapter 5. The
year 1836 was a crisis one for Spanish foreign policy for two reasons:
the La Granja revolution and Madrid’s recognition of the indepen-
dence of the Spanish American republics. Existing studies have stressed
political, cultural and international law factors surrounding this recog-
nition. Politically, the Quadruple Alliance has been viewed as the
subordination of Cristino Spain’s foreign policy to Paris and London,
especially with regard to British pressure for free markets and inde-
pendence recognition.37 Culturally, Madrid’s tardy recognition has been
invoked as Spain’s failure to reinvent itself as a post-imperial state.38
This certainly applied to the Carlists who impotently condemned this
recognition as the bitter harvest of 1820, and of one illegitimate revo-
lution begetting another, to the ruin of Peninsular Spain.39 Finally, the
law of the peoples was being accepted as the natural law base for inter-
national law across the Americas, and Cristino Spain’s honouring of this
integrated it back into the European concert.40
   Whereas links between the Carlists’ war effort and their foreign policy
have been well studied,41 comparatively little has been investigated with
regard to the Cristino Liberals. Yet the very ﬁrst motion passed by the
1836 revolutionary Cortes was to proclaim that Spain would remain a
monarchy. The revolutionaries were right to be worried about European
diplomatic reaction. In September 1836, Tsar Nicholas I, smarting from
a Quadruple Alliance snub regarding his proposed international peace
conference, urged Austria and Prussia to join him in recognising Don
Carlos and withdrawing ambassadors from Paris; Metternich, by con-
trast, urged caution, but agreed this course of action the moment the
Carlists got control of Madrid, whilst in the meantime increasing his
covert help to the Carlists.42 The absolutist powers, Austria and the
                                      Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   133


Italian states in particular, had memories of 1820 which still rankled: for
them the proclamation of the ‘Republican’ Constitution of 1812 proved
little short of a casus belli.43 Russia feared revolutionary Spain would
give strategic depth, and even a battle-hardened army, to a revolution-
ary France bent on tearing up the Vienna settlement.44 The Kingdom
of the Two Sicilies withdrew its skeleton legation from Madrid, whilst a
Spanish merchant at their court who had been acting as a semi-ofﬁcial
ambassador was withdrawn before he could be expelled. A group of 35
Carlists who had seized control of a Cristino ship in the Mediterranean
and sailed it to Naples were welcomed into Neapolitan service. Unof-
ﬁcial Cristino representatives were expelled without regard to rank or
ceremony (Naples, after all, had never recognised the Pragmatic Sanc-
tion of 1830 nor the subsequent accession of María Cristina in the
name of Isabel II), whilst the ministerial Giornale del regno delle due
Sicilie, mouthpiece of the absolutist regime, mounted a sustained cam-
paign of viliﬁcation against Liberal Spain and against the British legation
which was seen as its staunchest defender.45 The Calatrava adminis-
tration responded by expelling such remaining Neapolitan agents as it
could ﬁnd. Yet this was barely more than symbolic: as diplomatic rela-
tions had been broken anyway since 1833, the only Neapolitan agents
who mattered were undercover and protected by powerful friends or
identities. Marquess Grúa, ofﬁcial Neapolitan representative until 1833,
had continued in Madrid ostensibly in the role of court archivist. The
privileged access this commission gave him spared him from revolu-
tionary witch-hunts and equipped him to play a key role in unofﬁcial
diplomatic manoeuvres in 1837 to reach a compromise peace.46
   Meanwhile, serious cracks were appearing in the Quadruple Alliance
itself. Portugal withdrew its legion, citing domestic threats of its own.
France, resentful of Britain’s forward role in the revolution, impressed by
the resignation of the Cristino ambassador to Paris (a move which the
ambassador to Brussels followed)47 and, above all, paralysed by domes-
tic political divisions on Spanish affairs, gave as little support as possible
whilst remaining within the alliance. The French legitimists (dubbed
‘Carlists’, even in France) wanted to support Don Carlos, the govern-
ing party of ‘resistance’ (Guizot, Broglie, Molé) wanted strict neutrality,
and the party of ‘movement’ comprising Republicans and centre-Left
Liberals wanted formal intervention on the side of Spanish liberty.48
Meanwhile, even London’s support could not be taken for granted as
power swayed between the pro-interventionist Palmerston and cautious
Peel. The revolutionary regime thus undertook radical diplomatic mea-
sures. Prime Minister Calatrava concluded a policy, begun in 1835, of
134 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Cristino recognition of the Latin American republics on the American
mainland. Mexico, whose independence had been the bitterest pill for
Spain to swallow and the biggest threat to her remaining possessions
in the Caribbean and Paciﬁc, was recognised in a Treaty of Peace and
Friendship on 28 December 1836, which was ratiﬁed in turn by Mexico
in May 1837, and soon led to an exchange of ministers and commercial
relations.49 Moreover, both the Jackson and Van Buren administrations
of the USA, the rising hemisphere power, supported the Cristino side
for ideological reasons and as a guarantee of continued good diplo-
matic and commercial relations, despite the often pessimistic reports
of US missions to Madrid regarding anarchy and militarisation.50 Yet
this diplomatic sea change in its favour was not immediately appar-
ent to Cristino Spain. Madrid was rightly tormented by the threat that
such remaining Spanish possessions as Cuba might be invaded (Mexico’s
General Santa Anna and President Victoria had recently aborted an inva-
sion of this island), compromising the war effort, or even given up to
Carlist insurrection with outside help. Thus, the peace treaty stipulated
that Mexico would prohibit expeditions being launched from its ports
against Spanish territories and refrain from encouraging agitation for
the liberation of the same.51
   Cristino Spain in 1836 was more worried about Carlist than Bolivarian
subversion of its colonial and ex-colonial relations. During 1836, Don
Carlos’s agents in Rome focused on trying to frustrate the Vatican’s
impending recognition of the former New Spain and putative jewel
in the Pretender’s empire.52 But various schemes also ﬂourished in the
extant colonies, including a bid to raise Cuba for Don Carlos once
Gómez Expedition had been launched in Spain, on another occasion
when Bilbao was under its second siege, and on a third occasion when
the Royal Expedition crossed the Ebro. Conditions were ripe for sub-
version in this colony. Captain-General Tacón, a sexagenarian veteran
of the Spanish American Independence wars and high-handed architect
of administrative and police reforms, countermanded orders from the
revolutionary Cortes in 1836 to hold elections on the island under the
Constitution of 1812. But whilst Tacón’s order held sway in Havana,
Cuba’s second city, Santiago, inspired both by the possibilities of the
Constitution of 1812 and by resentment at the capital’s economic domi-
nance, staged a ‘Liberal’ revolt, which saw the underdeveloped east hold
out for three months against Tacón’s military rule. Tacón’s paciﬁcation
brought him no respite, as news reached him of a mysterious ‘English
agent’ who was en route to the island in a bid to persuade its oligarchs
to become neutral in the civil war raging in the motherland (given
                                     Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   135


Cuba’s tax revenues – which accounted for some 15 per cent of Madrid’s
exchequer – ‘neutrality’ objectively meant Carlist53 ). Cuba’s fervent reli-
giosity, so Carlists hoped, could be exploited, especially in the person of
Santiago’s Archbishop, Cirilo Alameda y Brea.54 But Tacón expelled the
archbishop from Cuba. Alameda y Brea made, on 2 January, for Jamaica,
then, early in July, to London.55 After a brief exile the archbishop found
his place at Don Carlos’s side as a spiritual leader of the anti-apostólico
faction.56 Deﬂecting the Carlist threat, the Madrid government lost no
time in seeing off the danger that liberalism would lead to indepen-
dence on the island; a special law was quickly passed by the Cortes
exempting Cuba from constitutional rule and allowing for ‘special laws’
in the colony. This came in spite of a spirited campaign by Radical
progresistas to demand political rights for these colonies and even, on
the margins, to abolish slavery (a proposal which was quickly blocked
due to fears of creating ‘another Haiti’ and, most crucially, depriving
the hard-pressed Spanish exchequer of the tobacco plantations’ vital
revenue).57 The effect of these laws was soon felt. In 1838, the ﬁnancially
starved Madrid government ordered the sale of a round of ecclesiastical
property on Cuba, whilst imposing on both Cuba and Puerto Rico an
extra-ordinary tax of 60 million reales.58
   Given Cristino preoccupations with Cuba, it is surprising that the
most impressive Caribbean impact of Spanish Carlism was not conspir-
atorial in nature, but Romantic, in the inspiration it gave to the cause of
Santo Domingan independence from Haitian rule. The Santo Domingan
‘father of the nation’, Juan Pablo Duarte, had spent the Carlist War
in Catalonia, but in 1838 he left for his native Dominican Republic,
inspired to agitate for his nation’s independence against Haitian rule. He
adapted the Catalan Carlist slogan, Rey, Patria y Fueros to Patria, Fueros y
Libertad. This inspired his Trinitaria secret society, which he founded in
1838 in Santo Domingo, and led to the 1844 declaration of Dominican
independence under a Carlist-inspired slogan.59 The symbol of eman-
cipated, black Haitians exercising sovereignty over whites had terriﬁed
colonial societies, and Duarte was doubtless inﬂuenced by the constant
Carlist allusions to the ‘usurping’ Spanish Liberals as ‘blacks’ (negros).
   The international impact of the civil war may have mattered to the
Jacobin politics of Madrid, but was marginal to the daily experience of
war. The Spanish Left’s greatest ‘export’ since 1812 had been the Con-
stitution of Cádiz, but its domestic reimposition after the La Granja
revolution had rendered it otiose as the army overruled local politicians.
Senior ofﬁcers who, unlike San Miguel, Quiroga and Cardero, were not
part of the victorious revolutionary party had even more reason to use
136 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


high-handed military jurisdiction against troublesome civilian authori-
ties. The revived civil supremacy principles of the Constitution of 1812
had rendered the daily management of military operations difﬁcult.
Troops had to go miles out of their way in desperate pursuit of insurgents
just so they did not ‘invade’ the Captaincy-General of Madrid. Mean-
while, contending civilian authorities, both well-established ones like
intendants, and new constitutional varieties of provincial governors,
prefects, elected local mayors and civil governors, may have been pro-
gressive as far as Liberal politics was concerned but proved detrimental
to strategic operations. This autonomy impeded the commissariat’s task
even more than the inefﬁcient system of private contractors, and pro-
voked sharp inequalities. In Valencia province, for example, Chiva and
Buñol were overburdened with demands for supplies whereas nearby
Utiel and Requena were not, even though all four municipalities were
threatened by Cabrera.60
   The insecurity caused by rural terrorism places a virtual embargo on
travel. Even the royal escort was robbed on one occasion. A govern-
ment report in 1837 blamed the human geography of Andalucía for
the rural insecurity of the south, as labourers traipsing miles between
pueblo, cortijo and ﬁeld were particularly vulnerable targets for bandits.61
But matters were even worse in the pro-Carlist north, where the Protes-
tant missionary, George Borrow, reported ‘almost every road is teeming
with banditti, who under the name of Carlists plunder friend and foe,
and to robbery join cruelty so atrociously horrible that indignation at
the crime is frequently lost in wonder’.62 Muleteers and labourers suf-
fered particularly, as they were often the easiest targets for opportunistic
raiders. Their fates made for grim government reports and press cover-
age. Accounts abound of civilians being preyed upon by Carlists acting
as bandits (or the other way round). A raid near Vendrell (Tarragona) was
typical: ﬁve farm labourers ridden down and a ten-year-old boy mor-
tally wounded.63 A Cristino supply column trapped by Carlist invasion
in the village of Ruidera (New Castile) refused to surrender to the local
Carlist bandits led by the Pallilos brothers but agreed to surrender to
a Carlist expeditionary force of outsiders.64 Agriculture, especially valu-
able and vulnerable livestock husbandry, was badly hit. Daimiel in New
Castile was so terrorised by marauding Carlist bands during the autumn
of 1837 that its farmers left their animals exposed in the ﬁelds to die,
the harvest going uncollected.65 The absence of village menfolk femi-
nised the practice of agriculture, devolving food production to the close
environs of dwellings. The war accounted for much of the general sub-
sistence crisis of 1837, just as the Peninsular War had done with the
                                     Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   137


more severe famine of 1811–12.66 It was worsened when the moderado
government of 1837 imposed an extraordinary compulsory purchase of
5,000 horses. This had originally been mooted by the revolutionaries in
February 1837 for the Militia; now the moderados did it for the army,
impairing agriculture whilst also removing the social mobility offered
by Militia membership to peasants with horses.67
   This security crisis meant that villages and small towns in both
Castiles which had no Cristino garrison could be regarded as ‘Carlist’,
either because they were occupied or invaded by bands or because
these bands operated close enough in the vicinity to induce willing or
unwilling ‘geographical loyalty’. At least 24 Carlist bands were known
by Cristino security forces to be plaguing both Castiles in 1836. Their
members came from one or more of the following four categories:
deserters/draft-dodgers, men conscripted by the Carlist bands, crimi-
nals/adventurers and agricultural workers displaced by war-ravaged har-
vests and the Liberal property revolution. The presence of so many rural
poor in Carlist bands, and the fact that these were usually led or men-
tored by reactionary priests, led generations of historians, especially the
Marxists and neo-Carlists of the second half of the twentieth century, to
understand the Carlist war as a conﬂict between a liberal, urban, mod-
ernising bourgeoisie and a backward, traditional, Catholic rural society,
the latter comprising a broad spectrum of threatened livelihoods united
by their false consciousness of throne and altar.68 Yet the twentieth-
century preoccupation with class conﬂict was too self-contained and
hermetic. Castile showed that ‘Carlism’ covered many more categories
than declassés, and in any case the same economic grievance could be a
recruiting sergeant for Cristinism, too. In New Castile, for example, the
naturalised Spaniard and Irish-born veteran of the British Army, Colonel
Jorge Dawson Flinter, beat the Carlists at their own game. Inﬂuenced by
his ﬁrst-hand knowledge of the Spanish American Independence wars,
Flinter created a Cristino counter-guerrilla force of desperate men called
peseteros (they earned one peseta per day) who were armed with nothing
more than shotguns and who fought the Carlists and their support-
ers in a private, asymmetrical fashion.69 Later in the war, the Cristinos
recruited a mounted volunteer guerrilla force in Logroño under Martín
Zurbano Baras (alias Martín Varea), which plundered and terrorised
Carlist villages in the Basque country for pecuniary gain.70
   In effect, the deep war privatised politics, and not just for combatants,
as local communities used the aims of each side in order to protect or
advance their interests. The ﬂight to Valladolid of local village author-
ities drove villagers to seek the protection of rural insurgents, selling
138 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


them arms, sacking the homes of the absent wealthy and proclaiming
Carlos V. When a Cristino counter-insurgency operation at the end of
1837 re-established control, the captain-general showed no compassion
for the villagers’ plight, and imposed crippling ﬁnes on families whose
menfolk had joined the insurgents. The intimate details of ‘wrongdo-
ing’ possessed by the captain-general implies the widespread use of local
informants.71 Sometimes exasperated municipalities protected them-
selves by deﬂecting military oppression back onto the commanders. The
commissariat of a Cristino division quartered in Sarrión (Teruel) refused
to give the municipality a receipt for 3,000 rations commissioner (habiti-
lado de comisario) Castillo demanded as only 2,800 had been produced.
Even worse, the same division returned to Sarrión a few days later. The
Mayor of Sarrión therefore acted fast. He humoured Castillo and man-
aged to get him to sign off the receipts, and not only these but also
some receipts from Cabrera who had plagued the area shortly before
with similar demands. Castillo did not bother to read the small print.
For this mistake a court martial started against Castillo, but before he
could be sentenced, he defected to the Carlists out of embarrassment.72
   The ideologically committed minority despaired at the apolitical prag-
matism of communities at war. A solitary Liberal in Sonseca (Toledo)
complained at how ‘the insurgents are the only people in charge around
here’, conﬁscating the cattle of local Cristinos and abducting the local
mayor for ransom.73 In Méntrida (Toledo), meanwhile, local authority
had passed into the hands of ‘men of no character’ who, so an inves-
tigating judge was appalled to ﬁnd, paid off Carlist irregulars in the
surrounding hills, thereby preserving a modus vivendi.74 Communities in
Toledo which failed to make such accommodations could expect to fare
worse. In March 1837, some 26 irregulars descended on Malpica del Tajo
(Toledo) and might have kept it in their possession, as the few militia-
men who reported for action merely barricaded themselves in the local
castle. Yet the irregulars contented themselves with sacking the house of
the wealthiest Liberal and burning the mayor’s residence to the ground
before moving on.75 The Protestant missionary, George Borrow, was self-
evidently prone to attract hostile Carlist attention. But his account of
León in the summer of 1837 is nonetheless indicative of how nominally
Cristino civilian authorities were powerless in the face of Carlism:76

   I had scarcely been at Leon three days when I . . . had published my
   advertisements as usual, though without very fervent hope of success,
   as Leon is a place where the inhabitants, with very few exceptions, are
   furious Carlists and ignorant and blinded followers of the old Papal
                                    Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   139


  Church. It is, moreover, a Bishop’s see, which was once enjoyed by
  the prime councillor of Don Carlos, whose ﬁerce and bigoted spirit
  still seems to pervade the place . . . I had scarcely risen from the bed
  where the fever had cast me, when I found that my servant had
  become alarmed; he informed me that he had seen several soldiers
  in the uniform of Don Carlos knocking at the door of the inn, and
  that they had been making enquiries concerning me. It was indeed a
  singular fact connected with Leon that upwards of ﬁfty of these fel-
  lows, who had on various accounts left the ranks of the pretender,
  were walking about the streets dressed in his livery, and with all the
  conﬁdence which the certainty of the protection of the local authori-
  ties could afford them, should any one be disposed to interrupt them.
  He moreover informed me that the person in whose house we were
  living was a notorious spy to the robbers in the neighbourhood, and
  that unless we took our departure speedily and unexpectedly, we
  should to a certainty be plundered on the road.

The further south and south-west the Carlist bands roamed the more
they encountered Cristino resistance. Don Carlos knew that the north-
west, Asturias and Galicia, offered the best prospects for a new Carlist
safe zone, if they could be reached by an expedition to match the
achievements of Guergué in Catalonia the previous year. The ‘Batanero
raid’ into Castile of some 220 infantry and 52 cavalry during January
and February 1836 had shown promise of what a force more than ten
times that number might achieve. The summer of 1836 posited the
enemy in an even greater revolutionary crisis than during Guergué’s
expedition. The last weeks of Córdova’s command, in July, had been
marked by a serious mutiny of a battalion in Gerona and a general
breakdown in deference as soldiers disobeyed their ofﬁcers, deserted and
defected in increasing numbers. An irate and desperate Córdova pro-
claimed from his headquarters at Pamplona on 6 July 1836 that the
revolutionaries who were rising in the name of liberty and the Constitu-
tion of 1812 were actually treacherous ﬁfth columnists for the Carlists.77
Subjectively, this was arrant nonsense, and enraged Cristino soldiers at
Lerín (Navarra) almost lynched Córdova for this calumny as he ﬂed the
revolution the following month.78 But objectively the ‘Gómez Expe-
dition’ that summer did indeed show a strong military link between
Carlist expansion and revolutionary militancy.
   Miguel Gómez, born in Jaén province in 1796, veteran of the Penin-
sular and Royalist Wars, and purged colonel of 1833, was like a minor
character in a play whose actions reveal the bigger plot. His expedition
140 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


westwards from the Basque country into Old Castile and Asturias began
with not much more than 3,000 men, but, crucially, he picked up more
on the way. High secrecy surrounded the expedition before it began at
ﬁrst light on 26 June 1836. Twenty-ﬁve hours’ march later in blister-
ing sunshine got the expedition to Colina, some nine leagues from the
starting point. A crucial early defensive victory was secured for Gómez
at nearby Baranda when General Tello y López’s Cristino division lost
vital hours discovering the nature of the breakout. By the time Tello
had closed on Gómez on 27 June, the Carlists had had time to choose
their ground. Both sides suffered in the baking heat, and several of Das
Antas’s Portuguese literally died of thirst when pursuing this breakout.
But the Carlists intercepted part of the Cristino supply wagons, which
meant that it was the Cristinos who ran out of water ﬁrst. Tello’s defeat
allowed Gómez to continue the expedition, even though Espartero pur-
sued with forced marches, knowing that he had to deny the Carlists
exploitation of territory because it was marked by ‘weak and abandoned
Cristino garrisons’.79
   Gómez’s remarkable invasion has been analysed in exhaustive detail
elsewhere.80 Its salient feature is a six-month campaign across north-
western, western, southern and eastern Spain, which achieved both
remarkable victories (such as the capture of Córdova) and survived
important defeats (such as Majaceite). The expedition was supported
by a more limited westward raid by Pablo Sanz y Baeza, and another
south by Basilio García, and it was joined across New Castile and
Extremadura by Cabrera’s seasoned forces. Oviedo was twice occupied
by Carlists, ﬁrst by Gómez in July who seized its artillery and small arms,
and hundreds of recruits, before Espartero’s vanguard on 8 July obliged
him to evacuate, and second by Sanz in October who was repulsed by
subaltern revolutionaries who shamed the more cautious army and Mili-
tia ofﬁcers and their declaration of an open city.81 As Espartero drove
Gómez from Oviedo, the Carlists continued westwards into Galicia,
following their King’s instructions. Gómez was aided not just by the
Carlism of clerics, but also by the vicious (or virtuous) cycle of military
paralysis and revolution afﬂicting the Cristino zone. Galicia’s Captain-
General Latre, cowed by his failure against the Andújar revolutionaries
the previous summer, thought the streets of Lugo too dangerous to
leave unguarded and therefore refused to intercept Gómez, conﬁning
himself to ﬁring artillery when the Carlists came into range of the city
walls.82 A political crisis in Portugal withdrew Das Antas’s badly mauled
auxiliaries across the frontier. The provincial committees of armament
and defence came under dictatorial pressure from the army to increase
                                    Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   141


their contributions; Córdova increasingly had to intervene personally.
On 9 July, Córdova found time between his endless threats of resigna-
tion to warn Madrid that he had only enough food to supply the Army
of the North until the next day.83 On 17 July, General Espartero, the
only ofﬁcer yet to show any élan against the expedition, wrote to the
government complaining of the state of his men. Two weeks later the
response he received was evasive and rhetorical.84 Meanwhile, Gómez
seized Espartero’s pay-wagon carrying 8,000 reales and attracted volun-
teers from across the generally pro-Carlist north. On 18 July, Gómez
reached the cathedral city of Santiago, and was welcomed with a Mass.
Gómez ordered a mobilisation in Santiago, to be enforced by the usual
draconian penalties, but Espartero’s pursuit evicted him the following
evening, although not before the Carlists had stripped the city of 100
oxen, 350 sacks of powder, 450 of bullets, 2,000 muskets, 3,000 bayonets
and some 350 cartridges.85
   Gómez’s subsequent evacuation of Galicia and invasion of Old Castile
was remarkable, not least because his expedition survived a defeat by
Espartero at the Asturian village of Escaro on 8 August and the loss
of some 500 prisoners. Yet even more remarkable was the fact that a
rural expedition alternating around 6,000 men (some of these being
priests and other itinerants of little military value) seemed not only
unstoppable but also an external factor in Cristino Spain’s revolu-
tionary crisis. Gómez spent the ﬁrst week of August occupying León,
before ﬂeeing before Espartero’s vanguard once again. Soon afterwards,
Espartero himself fell ill from exhaustion, and the counter-insurgency
forces were led, from 23 August, by Alaix.86 As the expedition drew
closer to the Cristino capital, revolutionary opinion turned on War
Minister Rodil. Rodil had presided over an embarrassing defeat of the
Royal Guard, which, despite its better training and artillery, had suffered
demoralisation since the La Granja revolution, symbolised by the revo-
lutionaries’ insistence that the Guard’s standard be emblazoned with
the green ribbons of the Constitution. Local authorities at Guadalajara
ﬂed to Madrid in panic.87 Guadalajara province saw its communities
caught between Carlist violence and ‘revolutionary justice’. Cristino
forces ﬁned municipalities which did not display tablets dedicated to
the Constitution of 1812, and Carlists sacked those that did. A consti-
tutional judge of Riaza (Segovia) on the border of Guadalajara cut an
undigniﬁed ﬁgure by ﬂeeing to La Granja soon after exhorting others
to ﬁght to the end.88 The isolated Guadalajara village of Maranchón
was surrounded by opposing forces but had no particular afﬁliation
of its own. Its villagers decided that the Carlists were stronger, and a
142 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


delegation gave them intelligence about a nearby Cristino unit which
was then subjected to an attack that yielded dozens of prisoners, several
of whom chose to serve under the Carlists. Other communities were
divided between themselves. Almoguera’s (Guadalajara) divisions had
been dormant, but news of both revolution and Carlist invasion radi-
calised local politics. When its Liberal mayor held a banquet to celebrate
the proclamation of the Constitution of 1812, his address was cut short
by one of the musicians in attendance, who lunged forward with a dag-
ger, killing the mayor’s wife instantly and mortally wounding the mayor
(the local surgeon, a civic Carlist, refused to treat him), before snufﬁng
out the candles to assist his escape. The assassin was later caught by
militiamen and executed, but not before he had declared himself ‘glad
to have killed a negro’.89
   Gómez could not seriously consider assaulting Madrid, but it would
have helped Rodil’s career if he had. War Minister Rodil decreed a
strategy which would soon be his undoing. Adopting his tactic of ‘cen-
tralisation’ sustained by parallel lines of deployments and patrols on
the approaches to the capital – ‘defence in depth’ – Rodil certainly
saved the capital, but in a manner of strategic overkill more suited to
his dogged defence of Callao in Peru than against a guerrilla band. His
strategy ceded the initiative to Gómez, deﬂecting him into the surround-
ing areas of Extremadura, New Castile and, eventually, Andalucía, where
he enjoyed a series of embarrassing conquests. Alaix’s campaign troops,
meanwhile, were impeded by the attitude of the local constitutional
authorities, which wanted to stabilise their power by refusing army
demands for supplies and, especially, the mobilisation of local militi-
amen, out of fear that the revolutionary process could be reopened with
unpredictable results. Cristino troops, tired of having to deal with pre-
sumptuous civilians, reverted to imposing states of siege, suspending
constitutional guarantees in the hope that force rather than negotia-
tion would yield more support. This equated the Cristino campaign to
that of Gómez, who was under no such constraints and could offer
civilians either threats or rewards.90 The threat of martial law sup-
plied Alaix’s troops in Cuenca on 11 September with sufﬁcient shoes.91
Cobblers, like telegraphists in later conﬂicts, were artisans whose skill
was so essential that they were targeted by military law. Consequently,
cobblers in revolutionary Málaga faced forced mobilisation on pain of
imprisonment.92
   The disarray of Cristino forces offered the Carlists surprisingly easy
conquests. News reached Gómez that Córdova was lightly garrisoned
(actually, the Cristino garrison would prove larger than Gómez was led
                                     Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   143


to believe, containing some 2,000 militiamen, including several who
had evacuated local villages, and some 200 cavalry). Gómez regrouped
with the forces of Cabrera, and Brigadier Santiago Villalobos managed to
breach the city walls, in part thanks to the city’s restive poor neighbour-
hoods of Santa María and San Lorenzo, whose inhabitants welcomed
the Carlist invasion as an emeute. After days of skirmishing, the Militia
retreated and blockaded itself into the last redoubt, the former head-
quarters of the Inquisition, where it surrendered to terms on 1 October,
only for a misunderstanding to break this truce, which was followed
by a deﬁnitive surrender. Moderados attacked the Radicals’ mismanage-
ment of the war effort. Exaggerating the strength of the Cristino garrison
