and minimising the Carlist losses (but not by much in either case), El
Español reported that the city’s 3,000-strong Militia surrendered to a
Carlist force less than three times in number, and which had to suffer a
mere four fatal casualties (including Villalobos).93 Gómez had performed
a rare feat: the capture of a Cristino provincial capital, and his insurgent
lifestyle could, for the moment, take pause. Gómez pointedly remained
in Córdova for a week: hasty departure would be seen by all as a sign of
insurgency, and not the actions of a regime-in-waiting. Hence, Gómez
concocted a deliberately elaborate Carlist regime in the city, led by the
Dean of the Cathedral and effectively run by the subaltern crowds,
who now helped themselves to Militia arms, joined Carlist ranks, and
victimised and sometimes killed local Liberals. Several prominent mili-
tiamen were executed to popular applause, along with their commander
and the prefect. Meanwhile, the city’s poor joined the Carlists in their
plunder of local Liberals’ property.94
   It was a week before Alaix’s force could threaten Córdova. Gómez quit
the city, reoccupied it for a night on 11–12 October, but then left it
deﬁnitively in response to the coordinated movements of Alaix and
Rodil. The War Minister had come under intense pressure to abandon
his cautious strategy, but Rodil dared not leave the capital under-
garrisoned. Although Córdova was retaken, Gómez (and the city’s recent
Carlist junta) remained at large in Andalucía. It was small comfort that
Gómez was burdened by Cabrera’s restive contingents who wanted to
withdraw to Aragón, by a growing sick-train and some 300 Cristino pris-
oners from a Málaga regiment defeated at Alcaudete. Gómez parleyed
with Alaix to propose an exchange of prisoners. But Alaix knew that
the sick and wounded prisoners in the enemy’s entourage were the only
burdens obstructing the Carlist general’s progress, and wished to avoid
offending revolutionary opinion by subjecting virgin Andalucía to an
Eliot Treaty-lite.95 Gómez outmanoeuvred Rodil and escaped through
144 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


the Sierra Morena towards another valuable target, Almadén, whose
mercury mines had been leased for a Rothschild loan in 1835.96 The
barren sierra treated Gómez’s forces harshly. His 8,000 infantry were
hungry and badly shod, only 400 of his 1,200 cavalry were battle-worthy
and the lower-ranking Cristino prisoners had to be ‘freed’ to fend for
themselves in the wastes.97 But he was still able to take Almadén, a
town of 8,000 inhabitants stretched along an east–west line which
made it hard to defend against a determined attack. In fact, the town’s
modest 50-strong Militia garrison, earlier in the war, had proved hard-
pressed to keep at bay small guerrilla bands. Continued pressure from
Carlist guerrillas and news of the Gómez expedition led to a reinforce-
ment of the militia and the establishment of a serious, though still
modest, army garrison. Despite these measures, Gómez needed only a
few days’ bombardment carried out by two well-positioned mountain
guns to capture the town on 24 October 1836; the Carlists charac-
teristically employing night attacks to secure parts of the town. The
Military Governor of Almadén, Aranguren, surrendered the town after
its general destruction had served as morbid battle honours. Aranguren,
displaying a non-committal rhetorical ability common to Cristino elites,
made a parting plea to Gómez not to destroy the valuable mercury
mines as these would be vital for national prosperity ‘whoever wins
this war’.98
   The occupation of Almadén was the ﬁnal straw for Rodil’s position.
An image appeared in the press lampooning him: showing him wearing
an eyeglass to survey the campaign horizon, whilst Gómez’s expedition
passed between his extended legs.99 Laughter damaged reputations in
cultures of honour and Rodil was sacked both from the Royal Guards
and the War Ministry. Gómez, for his part, evaded capture yet again, and
on 31 October marched into Cáceres (Extremadura), beginning a three-
day occupation of this provincial capital which had been abandoned to
its fate by its civil authorities and Militia.
   Rodil’s shame was the ﬁrst order of business of the unicameral revo-
lutionary Cortes which convened on 24 October 1836. Cristino Spain
been seized by Jacobin politics. Whereas Espoz and Meer, and other
captains-general, used their military authority to impose forced loans,
a civilian police state gripped Madrid. Salustiano de Olózaga, an ambi-
tious lawyer with excellent radical credentials, was made Civil Governor
of Madrid. He used constitutional defence legislation to open corre-
spondence relating to the forthcoming elections and imprison over
500 civic Carlists.100 Quiroga, Captain-General of Granada, wrote to
Mendizábal informing him of his political opponents. This crackdown
                                   Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   145


was followed in other provinces like Badajoz, where some 160 civic
Carlists were imprisoned.101 Priests who refused blessings to the Queen
or who even expressed sorrow for the plight of the religious evicted by
the disentailment laws were arrested by the National Militia.102 Andan,
Civil Governor of Zaragoza, imprisoned priests without due process,
whilst Latre, Captain-General of Galicia, threatened mayors with prison
if they refused to cooperate with his military orders.103 Civic Carlists
faced unprecedented government persecution.104 The non-ministerial
Left also ﬂourished, as several Radical conspiracies and secret societies
were now liberated, to an extent, from police pressure. Secret soci-
eties old and new ﬂourished such as Joven Italia, Leñadores escoceses,
Templarios sublimes, Asociación de Derechos del Hombre and others
besides.105 The government tried to keep control of the revolution via
such symbolic actions as auctioning church bells and treasures and
rejecting or extorting those wealthy rightists in the capital and else-
where who applied for passports for exile in France.106 Yet the years
1835–37 still saw the ministerial Left outﬂanked by republican secret
societies, which regarded Carlism as a mortal threat because of its roy-
alism. The Federation (La Federación) convened a clandestine network
of Radicals in Andalucían provincial capitals under the leadership of a
‘Supreme Federated Regulator’ (Supremo Regulador Federado), who resided
out of harm’s way in Paris.107 By the end of 1837, government spies
had penetrated much of its activities, recording the oaths taken by new
members:

 1. I swear never to respect any class of government, power or authority
    which does not originate in the people.
 2. I swear obedience and submission to the Supreme Federated Reg-
    ulator: likewise to those who conduct work and operations in his
    name, whether from the Central or Departmental sections.
 3. I swear to obey no-one other than those purely of the Federation
    residing in my area.
 4. I swear to observe the greatest discretion in everything I obey, see
    and understand, of the Venerated Federation, and to disclose nei-
    ther the names of fellow federados, nor the location of the Assembly,
    nor the hours it meets.
 5. I swear to offer all manner of services demanded of me, sacriﬁcing
    my life, and all my belongings, so that the regenerating principles
    of Universal Federation may prosper and spread.
 6. I swear to recognise tyrants and all those obeying them as enemies
    of man.
146 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


 7. I swear to consider as a usurpation of the People the power and
    Authority known as Royal.
 8. I swear to work to give liberty to the world and take it out of the
    slavery of being subject to just one person, even if that person’s
    power is restricted.
 9. I swear to obey Federated law, and any amendments and modiﬁca-
    tions agreed by the Assembly.
10. I swear to submit to suffer the penalty which article 84 prescribes
    for perjurers.
       I make a special oath before the Supreme Legislator that I have
    freely and spontaneously sought admission to this sacred place
    without having been prompted or coerced by anyone.108

Outﬂanked on the left, the progresista government tried to capture pub-
lic disaffection by scapegoating Rodil. The Cortes narrowly rejected a
motion to send deputies on permanent mission to the headquarters of
every ﬁeld army (a version of the French Revolutionary représentantes
en mission), but it did decide to send a deputy to interrogate Rodil.109
The Cortes nominated none other than the upstart Cardero, the darling
of the Radicals, who, since his January 1835 rising, had become Cortes
deputy and aide-de-camp to General Espoz, to lead an investigation into
Rodil.
   Rodil was incandescent with rage receiving Cardero, the man whose
entire career had been attributed to his killing of Rodil’s friend and
fellow ayacucho, Canterac. Rodil feared that what had happened to
Canterac, and later to Bassa and Quesada, could happen to him.
On 11 November 1836 Cardero arrived at Rodil’s headquarters at Monte
Rubio, but pointedly did not go to interview him until nightfall, and
only then in secret. This precaution was sensible, for Cristino troops
across Spain were revolting against Rodil, such as at Miajadas (Cáceres),
where troops refused to march against Gómez and instead shouted
‘Death to Rodil!’ On 29 November 1836 a battalion in the Royal Guard
of Infantry launched a revolt which was suppressed when three of
its leaders were executed.110 During the meeting between Rodil and
Cardero, the former was beside himself listening to the tactful preach-
ings of this upstart; he threatened to silence Cardero and to execute him,
before ﬁnally ordering him to leave his presence. With Cardero turned
out, Rodil now had to act fast. He had received the envelope contain-
ing the order for him to resign his command, so before he opened it,
he assembled his staff, got their support, and only then opened the let-
ter in their presence. All being suitably indignant, Rodil nominated an
                                     Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   147


acting commander who would do his bidding whilst he left for Madrid
with only a personal guard, ready to defend his honour at the capital.
Reaching Almaráz (Extremadura), a further order came into his hands
ordering him to march directly to Toledo, not Madrid. Rodil, terriﬁed of
being made a sacriﬁce to Radicals, wrote to the Queen-Regent and got
her personal guarantee for his safety and modiﬁed terms. He could now
choose internal exile in Ciudad Rodrigo, Zamora or Segovia. He chose
Ciudad Rodrigo for its proximity to the Portuguese frontier. After cross-
ing into exile, he issued an indignant manifesto protesting the political
interference in army affairs, the underhand behaviour of Cardero, the
Radical government’s failure to supply his army, particularly with shoes,
and its second-guessing of his strategy.111
   Rodil’s exile was a sideshow to the government’s inability to control
its territory. One of the most powerful men in Cristino Spain was the
editor of the Radical Eco del Comercio, Fermín Caballero, now president
of the revolutionary junta of his native Cuenca. Gómez’s interception of
his correspondence revealed intelligence about the identities of Carlists
and Liberals in La Mancha.112 Córdoba was reoccupied, but the Carlist
junta had stripped the city of military supplies, including some 20,000
sorely needed pairs of shoes. Cobblers were forced to make as many
shoes as possible, and ofﬁcers ordered to buy soles and to set their men
to work making sandals. Poor supplies and unreliable communications
complicated the Cristino pursuit of Gómez.113 Such impediment as came
to the Carlists came from within rather than without.
   At Cáceres Gómez ordered Cabrera to retire to Aragón, overriding his
bitter protest at this breaking of an earlier agreement, and at the manner
that the order was delivered (Gómez amassed armed men around him in
the rituals of a pronunciamiento). The King, Gómez said, needed Cabrera
in the east in the wake of the fall of Cantavieja, but he provided his rival
with nothing more than a personal guard of cavalry, and a suggested
route which would suspiciously take him close to Cristino garrisons.
In the end, Cabrera accepted his assumed identity (Llorens, a Valencia
guerrilla subordinate of Quílez), but set out on his own path through
Cristino territory. Despite Gómez’s parsimony, ‘Llorens’ fought a serious
of successful rearguard actions, zigzagged in order to confound pur-
suers, set off a minor panic in the capital between 21 and 25 November
by invading the Tarancón (Cuenca) area and, most impressively, as he
neared the Basque country on 24 November, captured the Bishop of
Pamplona, Andriani, who had played a double game with both sides and
whom the Cristinos had been holding in internal exile. Cabrera escaped
back across the Ebro after losing a battle to the Cristino covering force
148 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


at Rincón de Soto, abandoning stragglers and wounded, but keeping
the bishop. He had ignored Gómez’s instructions to go directly east and
would instead protest before his King at his treatment.114
   Meanwhile, the Cristinos dispatched three divisions towards Andal-
ucía under General Ramón María Narváez. Narváez is a ﬁgure who dom-
inated Spain’s mid-nineteenth century as the cornerstone of moderado
praetorian politics. Of a long-standing, moderately wealthy, Andalucían
military-aristocratic family – highly representative of the military caste –
Narváez’s experience in the Carlist War taught him to deride all civilian
politicians as ‘lawyers’ and to conserve a paternalistic disdain for the
capacities of ordinary people. Leftist historians have seen in Narváez
everything that was wrong with Spain’s praetorian politics, whereas
rightist historians, such as Córdova’s brother in the late nineteenth
century and his hagiographer, Andrés Révész, in the mid-twentieth cen-
tury saw him as a Romantic man of action and a political innocent.115
An innovative recent study by Guy Thomson, by contrast, casts Narváez
as a reﬂexive, proactively conservative strongman of post-Carlist War
moderado politics.116 Perhaps Narváez’s legend will shift, from the man
who, on his deathbed in 1868 was asked to forgive his enemies, only to
reply ‘I have no enemies to forgive for I have had them all shot’, to the
man who, for decades, was the next-door neighbour of Andalucía’s most
proliﬁc Democrat. The Narváez of the Carlist War has been less studied
than the Narváez of mid-century liberalism. The Ominous Decade had
not been kind to Narváez’s career as he refused to compromise his lib-
eralism, but the Carlist War saw him re-enter the army as a captain,
after which his bravery on the battleﬁeld (especially at Mendigorría)
saw him promoted to colonel by the end of 1835.117 In November 1836
he became Commander-in-Chief of the Army Reserve, making him the
moderado rival to the progresista Espartero, whose Army of the North was
stretched containing shorter-range Carlist offensives.118
   Gómez’s march from Cáceres to San Roque, Algeciras and other parts
of the Cádiz province was dictated by the presence of the only serious
Carlist enclave in Andalucía, the Serranía de Ronda, where brigandage
in rugged terrain had ﬂourished under the banner of Carlism. This cor-
ner of Andalucía, an acute triangle between Málaga, the Ronda and
Gibraltar, had long been characterised by conspiracy and wrongdoing.119
The Cristino police state had been watchful of subversive activities:
the secretive travels in the region of a Tangier Jew claiming to seek
Christian baptism, and the incendiary activities of a French agent col-
lecting money for Don Carlos once the war had begun.120 More than any
other location, the Ronda conformed to the wild, Irvingesque image of
                                    Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   149


Spain, placing costumbrista outlaws into an international conspiratorial
network. Even before the arrival of the Gómez Expedition reached the
region, the Carlist Royal Government had appointed guerrilla leaders
there. But Navarra was far away and Don Carlos discovered that Carlist
commanders in the Andalucía had a short life expectancy.
   In July 1835 the former royalist colonel, Vicente Mateos, was caught
near Gibraltar with documents incriminating him as Don Carlos’s man
and was imprisoned pending court martial.121 Mateos was executed
amidst the radicalism of the 1835 juntas in retrospective ‘revolution-
ary justice’ for his role in the execution of the Liberal hero, Manzanares,
in the Torrijos expedition of 1831.122 Brigadier Salvador Malavilla was
named Mateos’s successor as Carlist commander in Andalucía. Malavilla
had presided over a plan to raise a loan in Gibraltar to fund the cre-
ation of a proper army. (Seeing the threat of Gibraltar and suspecting
British motives, Narváez, on 22 November 1836, reported the Carlist
deployment of three battalions on the border in the Campo de Gibraltar
as proof of their intent to launder their loot in the British colony.123 )
But when he was arrested and his correspondence uncovered, Malavilla
was executed in Seville (after which the dangerous rank of Carlist
commander in Andalucía passed to Pedro Fermín de Iriberri).124 The
Gibraltar–Serranía de Ronda conspiratorial network proved a potent way
to induce Carlism in a part of Spain which would otherwise have been
entirely lost to the cause of legitimism. May 1836, for example, saw
Seville rocked by a conspiracy led by a group of priests and redun-
dant monks who had hired the allegiance of some tanners, bakers and
sergeants, to spring Carlist prisoners from gaol and seize the city for Don
Carlos. Luckily for the authorities, government spies were active in the
cafés, and arrests were made before the plot was enacted.125 Gibraltar
provided a springboard for cloak-and-dagger smugglers, especially the
mochileros (‘backpackers’) who made the seldom-trodden paths of the
Serranía de Ronda their own, and who, in the context of civil war, could
be recruited to act for Carlist conspiracies.126 The revolutionary authori-
ties knew that Gómez’s invasion could act as a magnet for insurgents in
the Serranía de Ronda and even ﬁfth columnists inside Cristino towns
and cities. In October 1836 the Seville Armament and Defence Commit-
tee warned that gatherings of more than ﬁve people within a league of
known enemy positions would be broken up by bullets, any correspon-
dence with the Carlists pronounced espionage and Seville closed off to
all comers without passports.127
   Narváez’s famous skill for rapid marches inconvenienced Gómez who
wanted to spend more than a few days regrouping in the Serranía. His
150 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


subordinate, General Rivero, detailed the terror Gómez inﬂicted during
his Andalucían campaign. Liberals imprisoned in Córdova were dragged
on a forced march, tied up in villages in the Carlists’ path whilst the
villages themselves were pillaged. Prisoners who could not keep pace
were bayonetted by the wayside. This terror made Andalucía even more
hostile to Carlism than it already was. Estepona’s militiamen circulated
arms to civilians in a successful repulse of 200–300 ‘Carlist scum’ who
had demanded 30,000 reales. The invaders later returned demanding
half this sum, only to be repulsed again. Several British military and
civilians were spectators of an unlikely invasion from the north of San
Roque, on the actual borderline with Gibraltar, on 22 November. With
British help, the Cristinos had reinforced the border with artillery which
harassed the Carlist occupation of San Roque, provoking Gómez into
reprisals including ‘sacking homes, burning others, and smashing the
town’s vats containing wines, olives, oils and spirits, so that the streets
resembled a noxious ﬂood’.128 This sacking of Liberal San Roque con-
trasted with the Carlists’ behaviour in the Serranía where ‘they took
more care of the inhabitants, as they committed nothing like the same
outrages, nor any signiﬁcant inconvenience’.128 But there was no way
back into the friendly Serranía, and whilst Gómez left half his combat-
ants on the border to negotiate their internment (and that of Córdoba’s
fugitive Carlist junta), the other half invaded the nearest Cristino port
of Algeciras. Here the Carlists’ lack of artillery told once more as they
were repulsed by the concentrated ﬁre of a Portuguese frigate, a British
corvette and Cristino coastguard vessels, suffering considerable losses.
The Carlists invaded the Mediterranean on horseback up to bridle-
height in a vain attempt to get near enough to the Quadruple Alliance
naval supremacy to ﬁre their muskets. However, the pursuit of Cristino
forces obliged Gómez to ﬂee Algeciras, but not before he had burnt the
town to the ground.128
   While Gómez was put to ﬂight, the Carlists and their junta of Córdoba
on the Gibraltar frontier were allowed internment in the colony. The
French consul arrived to agree terms, but the internees’ voyage out
of the colony on 23 November was intercepted by the Cristino coast-
guard, the passengers being taken prisoner and sent to Seville for trial.
There they were defended by the local lawyer, moderado provincial
deputy and National Guard battalion commander, Manuel Cortina, and
despite – or because of – his efforts (Cortina had been targeted by local
revolutionaries during riots in July) the Carlists were all deported to
the Philippines.129 The harsh treatment of Córdoba Carlists allowed
Seville’s Radicals to save some face in the context of the moderado rally.
                                    Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   151


Cortina had risked provoking anti-militarism by mobilising the militia
for service under Narváez. His decision paid off when Narváez expelled
Gómez from Andalucía as a consequence of the Battle of Majaceite on
23 November 1836.130 The Carlist presence in Andalucía had had the
effect of rallying communities to the less worse option of liberalism and
its defence of the municipal autonomy resurrected under the Consti-
tution of 1812. The militia’s battle honours led the citizenry-in-arms
to assert their social and political demands. When, in early December,
some of Loja’s militia were ordered to mobilise, they refused to do unless
the whole battalion was so ordered, as campaign rations and higher pay
were sorely needed for the men’s families, whose livelihoods had been
disrupted by the war.131
   The revolutionary Cortes, desperate for victories against Gómez,
played two generals against each other by ordering Narváez to relieve
the more sclerotic but senior Alaix at the ‘most opportune moment’.
Yet Alaix’s forced marches had made his worn-out men virtually muti-
nous, and they were in no mood to accept command from a more
ruthless general.132 One lieutenant pronounced ‘now is the time to
march on Madrid!’ and demanded the death of ‘counter-revolutionary’
Narváez. However, Narváez used anti-Carlist rhetoric to regain the ini-
tiative before ordering the execution of 11 of the mutineers.133 But these
measures only bought Narváez time, and he was soon embarrassingly
forced to reinstate Alaix (who was an ally of Espartero and had pro-
nounced against his dismissal). Narváez, meanwhile, was summoned
to Madrid to explain his conduct. Luckily for him, the Cortes was far
more divided about what his fate should be than it had been for Rodil;
Narváez asked to be escorted to Cuenca to answer charges, and the
matter passed.134 The Cabra mutiny was a parable of revolutionary mis-
management of the war. A revolutionary Cortes had not allowed its
ignorance of the march of events to override its deliberations and the
revolt of the lieutenants and sergeants had lost Alaix’s division several
vital hours in its pursuit of Gómez. Even though the Carlist expedition
was defeated at Alcaudete (Jaén) on 29 November, Gómez extricated the
bulk of his force once again and disengaged from Andalucía.135 Over
fewer than four weeks, until 19 December 1836 (when Gómez arrived
at the Basque city of Orduña), the expedition executed a remarkable
withdrawal, traversing Bailén and several smaller places on the route
northwards, ﬁghting battles and skirmishes, and bivouacking across sev-
eral rivers, along the way. Gómez halted in friendly mountains near
Soria to take a counsel of his staff: should they continue the retreat or
rally to launch another invasion from this mountain base? Deciding
152 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


to continue the retreat, the expedition fought its way past a Cristino
covering force in the Ribera, and arrived back in Carlist Spain and
safety. Gómez returned with twice as many men as he had set out with
(his recruits included 1,643 defectors from Cristino ranks).136 But Don
Carlos, in a move which confounded his international sympathisers,
placed Gómez on trial for having disobeyed his orders to concentrate on
Galicia, and for having ‘deceived’ Cabrera and deprived him of forces
which might have stemmed San Miguel’s offensive in the east.137 The
King was blind to the fact the expedition had destabilised Cristino Spain,
deﬂected the enemy’s war effort away from the Basque country, caused
the panic-selling of Cristino bonds in European bourses,138 and embold-
ened the Carlists into thinking that victory was possible.139 International
opinion was dazzled by the Carlist effort, as George Borrow wrote:140

   Gómez has proved himself to be a most extraordinary man, and with
   his small army of Aragonese and Basques has within the last four
   months made the tour of Spain; he has very frequently been hemmed
   in with forces three times the number of his own, in places whence
   escape seemed impossible, but he has always bafﬂed his enemies.

Given Gómez’s impact, it was remarkable that the King sided with
Cabrera and appointed Marshal José Mazarrasa, a hard-bitten apostólico
ﬁercely loyal to the King, to lead an inquiry into Gómez’s conduct and
that of other senior ofﬁcers (Eguía, Villarreal, Elío, Guergué) accused of
being too lenient with Cristino prisoners and ‘ignoring the interests of
the Basque country’.141 Gómez sent the King an indignant letter attack-
ing the whole management of a war which had boxed the Carlists into
the Basque country. But Gómez passed into obscurity; besides, the court
had decided to break the strategic blockade by making a second attempt
at taking Bilbao.142 The court were labouring under the old impression
that the capture of this port would lead to Northern Powers’ recog-
nition, and as such had been blind to the possibilities that Gómez’s
much smaller force had shown. Conditions for a conquest were per-
haps worse in the autumn of 1836 than they had been in the spring
of 1835. Espartero, recovered from his illness brought on by pursuing
Gómez, was named Commander-in-Chief of the Army of the North on
16 September, and his bravery was now matched by greater military
skill born out of experience. The Cristinos had shown themselves to
be effective in point-defence whenever the will to ﬁght existed, and
the location of Bilbao played to Quadruple Alliance naval supremacy
                                    Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   153


and all its morale-boosting possibilities for reinforcement. British Royal
Marine and Legion commanders, Lord John Hay and Lacy Evans respec-
tively, quickly deployed 6,000 of their men to seal the French frontier
to Carlists between Irún and San Sebastián, and Evans sent 150 artillery-
men to bolster Bilbao’s defences. The Córdova legacy of strong defensive
lines around the Carlist Basque country, including ﬂash telegraphs and
mutually defensible forts, had improved Cristino capabilities for active
defence.143
   Bilbao was certainly not a unanimous decision from the Carlist junta
of generals which met to decide the matter at Durango. La Torre urged
a bold breakout invasion south towards Madrid as the best way to
win European recognition and aid and so win the war. But Erro was
resolved, and Lt-General Vicente González Moreno’s even-handed let-
ter on 15 October 1836 swayed the decision. González explained to his
King that Bilbao was the less worse option (‘better’ options, he insisted,
did not exist in civil wars). A strike south, by contrast, would expose
the Basque country to the mercies of the Cristino defensive ring and
so destabilise civilian morale. Moreover, this time the Carlists had more
artillery to use against Bilbao, including some abandoned pieces from
the Peninsular War which had been salvaged and even those 13-inch
shells ﬁred by the British from Pasajes and San Sebastián that had failed
to explode on impact and had been turned in by local peasants.144 Also,
a dispute between Bilbao’s civil and military authorities over the fund-
ing of fortiﬁcations had simmered since the revolution of the juntas,
slowing progress improving the city’s defences.145 The Carlist hold on
Bilbao’s outlying towns and countryside, meanwhile, had strengthened
since 1835 and had emboldened morale against the blockaded Cristinos.
One local soldier made good was ‘generally on duty blockading Bilbao,
and as soon as the cowardly Cristinos withdraw their advanced guard
within the walls for the night, he and half-a-dozen wags will follow
and write some moral or political abuse of the Queen on the town
gates’.146 But Carlist morale was matched by the Cristinos who had for-
tiﬁed the heights and nationalised convents surrounding Bilbao, and
could rely on some 4,300 soldiers and militiamen garrisoning the city
with as many as 86 cannon. When the Carlists launched their assault
on 22 October, the Cristinos were ready for them.147
   The Carlist bombardment was at its most intense on the 24 and
28 October. Women, children and the elderly took to cellars and store
rooms in order to stay out of harm’s way, even though some ten women,
seven children and three elderly were amongst the 79 fatal casualties in
154 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Bilbao that week, whilst dozens more were amongst the total of 307
Cristino wounded. The Carlist projectiles included incendiary ﬁreballs
which had been invented by the French Carlist engineer, Colonel
Lizoire, better known for his braggadocio as ‘Tout-terraser’, whose pro-
jectiles expelled noxious ‘Greek ﬁre’ upon impact.148 Lizoire was joined
by several other European Carlists in the assault on Bilbao, and in being
opposed by European Liberals the struggle for Bilbao became a cru-
cible of the international struggle between Absolutism and Liberalism.149
At one point the Carlists brieﬂy breached the walls, only to be ﬂushed
out by militiamen supported by Bilbao workers. Meanwhile, the morale
of the civilian population was sustained by the erroneous belief that
Gómez had been utterly destroyed somewhere in southern Spain. The
Carlist assault ﬂagged from 29 October and the bitter divisions in the
Carlist court reappeared. Eguía, still rankling from his replacement by
Erro, seized his chance by convincing Don Carlos’s courtiers that he
could take the city within 24 hours, a gambit that won him command
of the besieging troops and the relegation (and subsequent resignation)
of Villarreal.
   Eguía’s conference on 5 November with his artillery generals and engi-
neers resulted in a plan to break the deadlock by assaulting Bilbao’s
adjacent fortiﬁed points and convents which protected its access to
the sea. An unlikely Carlist spy inside Bilbao (an elderly woman) had
disclosed the weakness of the Cristino fort which had been put up at
Mallona on the city’s outskirts. This was taken after a short artillery
bombardment, and was followed up by the capture of a fortiﬁed con-
vent whose surrender gave the Carlists a stranglehold on the city’s river
links to the sea. French residents being evacuated were ﬁred upon by the
Carlists, and, despite the French consul’s protests, Eguía refused them
safe passage. Meanwhile, the Royal Navy commander of the Ringdove
complained to the defending Cristinos of the speed with which the
defenders had surrendered the outlying forts. Eguía in November was
showing promise where his predecessor had failed, as well as a com-
mon touch as he disguised himself as a simple soldier and shared the
violence of the front with his men.150 Time appeared to be on his side:
Carlist espionage revealed to the besiegers that the city was going hun-
gry as food stocks dwindled and the prices of bread, eggs and especially
meat, rose exponentially. A chance shipload of cod had been made to
last and bequeathed Bilbao its signature dish,151 one of two enduring
gastronomic legacies of the First Carlist War.151
   The further Eguía advanced, the stiffer the resistance he encoun-
tered. The Cristino detachment at Luchana withdrew only after it had
                                     Deep War Feeds Revolution, 1836–37   155


exhausted all its ammunition, and even then it redeployed to the
stronger position garrisoned by an Anglo-Cristino force at a place known
as El Desierto, which was strengthened by the gunﬁre of the Royal Navy
brigantines Ringdove and Saracen.152 But Erro was conﬁdent enough with
Eguía’s progress to outline to his King on 17 November elaborate details
of the peace the Carlists would impose on Bilbao. Thus, foreign consuls
in the city representing powers opposed to Don Carlos would be treated
as nothing more than private aliens, a ruinous 12,000,000 reales tax
would be imposed on known Liberal families opposed to Don Carlos,
all nationalised convents and monasteries in the city would be revived
and even put to work ‘imprisoning and correcting those decent women
who had fallen into corrupt and unruly ways and so scandalised the
people’.153 Clearly, the inference between revolution and breakdown of
family morality, which began in the Carlist imagination with the female
usurpation of the throne, was plain to see. Whereas Carlists had the
unimpeachable Virgin Mary as their icon, the Cristinos had the secu-
lar Isabella as theirs. Isabella’s ‘innocence’ and her mother’s worldliness
provoked a veritable avalanche of books, essays and pamphlets debat-
ing the proper role of woman in Liberal society, the vast majority of
which predictably reverted to the traditional feminine values of religion,
morality, self-abnegation and self-control.154
   But in planning the correction of Bilbao’s corrupted women, the Royal
Government was counting its proverbial chickens. Bilbao’s counterbat-
tery was now outgunning the Carlist attackers. The Carlists managed
to take the fortiﬁed ex-convent of San Agustín before being halted by
a Cristino rally in front of Sendeja, where the defenders intimidated
some of the weary Carlist attackers by draping a barricade in front of
the ex-convent with a slogan saying ‘Transit to Death’. Meanwhile,
Carlist logistics were also ﬂagging. Carlist civilians shirked work building
wooden siege-trains with the result that soldiers had to be redeployed
for this task. One ﬁnal Carlist effort to take the fortiﬁed ex-convent of
La Concepción, which was repulsed on 29 November, ﬁnally pushed
the besiegers into exhaustion. The Cristino garrison was now returning
artillery ﬁre at rates twice and then three times that of the Carlists, who
were under orders to conserve their dwindling ammunition. Even worse,
intelligence arrived of Cristino relief forces. After Evans’s British force
was sent by sea from San Sebastián to Portugalete on Bilbao’s extreme
right, Espartero resolved to relieve the city by striking through the area
of the bridge at Luchana, a key location where the Asúa River ﬂowed into
the Nervión. Espartero’s victory here relieved Bilbao on Christmas Day,
restoring Cristino Spain’s fortunes in the wake of the Gómez debacle,
156 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


and turning both himself (ennobled as Count Luchana) and the city
into heroes of Liberal resistance. Cristino casualties during November–
December 1836 exceeded those that of the Carlists: 240 dead and 887
wounded militiamen, 3,700 army dead and wounded (including some
1,000 dead at Luchana), and incalculable civilian casualties. Wide areas
of Bilbao lay in ruins, some 26,000,000-reales worth of damage having
been caused.155
7
Carlist Failure, 1837–39




This chapter explains how Carlist Spain was transformed by the defeat
of the Royal Expedition. The Carlist Basque country was debilitated by
civilian war weariness and army revolts. Popular hatred of ojalateros
(‘would-to-God-ers’, or shirkers) grew on the backs of Basque peas-
antry overburdened by taxes and conscription. War weariness created
factionalism both from within between an ‘enlightened absolutist’
(Marotista) and theocratic absolutist (Apostólico) camp, and even
from without (Muñagorri’s ‘Peace and fueros’ insurgency sponsored by
Madrid). Meanwhile, Cabrera’s military state in the east maintained
its cohesion, defying the Cristino ascendancy elsewhere (as, to a lesser
extent, did the Catalan Carlists). But the strategic situation reverted to
an unsustainable stalemate, renewed minor Carlist expeditions notwith-
standing. The Carlist ‘home front’ in mid-war differed greatly from
that of its enemy. Carlist Spain matched a stultifying social and polit-
ical culture with a generally effective military strategy and order of
battle: the very reverse of the Cristinos. Unlike Cristino Spain, its ter-
ritory was small, restricted to a Basque–Navarrese statelet, and much
more makeshift rural areas of Catalonia, Aragón and Valencia besides.
Although Carlist guerrillas roamed in other areas of Spain, even domi-
nating signiﬁcant geographical spaces, their itinerant nature prevented
them from enforcing anything more than the most rudimentary mil-
itary administration. Thus, the Carlist ‘home front’ entails the Basque
country, and, to a lesser degree, the east. Its study involves three broad
categories, the cultural, the political–economic and the military.
   Culturally, Carlist Spain was characterised by an ofﬁcial puritanism
based on the union of throne and altar. The Vatican never recog-
nised Carlist Spain. Even though most of the cardinals supported such
a policy, Pope Gregory XVI in 1832 had condemned all rebellion

                                   157
158 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


against constituted authority, which meant that Papal recognition of
the Carlists was impossible without either a dynastic reconciliation or
the capture of Madrid. In this respect, the very conservative Pope resem-
bled Chancellor Metternich.1 Yet the lack of formal Papal recognition
did not impede the Carlist mobilisation of Catholicism in its war effort.
On 1 August 1835, the Virgin Mary was proclaimed Generalísima of
Carlist forces, repeating similar anointments by Peninsular War Patriots
and anticipating Franco 100 years later.2 In fact, beyond ofﬁcial puri-
tanism, Carlists exploited the range of popular Catholicism to inspire
Christian soldiery, exculpate exterminating angels or even justify acts
of protest. Faced with the intensely irrational and violent environment
of war, many Carlists relied on the miraculous powers of icons. Hence,
Carlist guerrillas in Aragón turned against their leader, an exterminat-
ing angel priest called Escoriguela, after he discarded a holy cross when
wading across the River Ebro. The weight of the cross almost made him
drown, but this counted as nought to his men who had always kissed the
icon before battle and almost executed the priest for his act of sacrilege.3
  The demands made by the war distorted the public and private
exercise of Catholicism. In April 1836, Don Carlos revived and trans-
formed the Sancti Spiritus University at Oñati (Guipúzcoa), purging
those scholars suspected of the merest whiff of liberalism, replacing
them with theologians of often inferior qualiﬁcations, and forcing staff
to take an oath of allegiance to the King and to uphold the mystery of
the Marian Immaculate Conception. Yet this traditionalist drive failed
as the University had to be used to house armaments, all areas but
the chapel being used for arms storage.4 Ofﬁcial concerns mounted
that total war was eroding popular religiosity and encouraging licence
and blasphemy, and these were answered by punishments targeting
swearing and ‘scandal’.5 Carlist Spain’s greatest female poet, Vicenta
Maturana de Gutiérrez, suffered from the cultural crackdown when,
on 1 May 1838, her ironic lyric poem, Himno a la luna, was banned
from Carlist territory.6 Of greater concern for its pervasiveness was the
growth of extramarital liaisons and the children these produced, bely-
ing the regime’s ofﬁcial piety as expressed in the Gaceta Oﬁcial, and
the cult of the Virgin. A decree was therefore passed on 25 December
1836 allowing soldiers and ofﬁcers to marry, even though these married
men would have to forgo their rights to military pensions. Beyond dog-
matic anti-liberalism, Carlist religion was always a reﬂection of changing
social relationships. Yet both leftist and traditionalist historians have
tended to view Carlist War Catholicism as a constant rather than diverse
and changing, even though the orphaning of the Pretender’s cause by
                                                Carlist Failure, 1837–39   159


the Vatican in fact liberated religious commitment away from political
strategy and enabled it to mean different things to different people.7
   The ofﬁcial culture of Christian soldiering was undermined by a
counter-culture of shirking, which shared some of the features, but not
the extent, of the Cristino zone. The ojalatero phenomenon (‘would-
to-God-er’) showed how shirking military duty was more controversial
for Carlists than for Cristinos.8 Ojalatero became a humorous and offen-
sive term to describe all men of ﬁghting ability in the Carlist camp
who, for whatever reason, were not ﬁghting; after the Royal Expedi-
tion it was used by diehard Carlists to describe the disproportionately
non-Basque peace faction known as the transaccionistas. Often, Basque
women unkindly called wounded men in the rearguard ojalateros. The
moral belligerence of Basque women was matched by Cristino women
only in the embittered front-line areas of the east, as was evident in the
enemy camp by the Eco del Comercio’s attempt to shame men into ﬁght-
ing by sensationalising the story of a young heroine. Eighteen-year-old
Luisa Casiñol disguised herself as a man and enlisted in the Cristino
army in Catalonia, proving herself on the battleﬁeld and becoming
something of a mascot (dubbed ‘Luis’) in the eyes of her comrades,
even after her true sex had been revealed.9 Catalan Cristinos had no
problem with temporal rather than supernatural female icons: their
very battle cry ¡Viva la petita! referred to Princess Isabella.10 As the
ojalatero phenomenon had the potential to damage the war effort, it
was answered by repression from the Carlist Royal Police. Measures
included a ban on all non-combatant males from bearing arms or wear-
ing martial moustaches, dishonouring them in a chauvinistic society.11
Towards the end of the war, the Cristino Basque spymaster, Aviraneta,
turned the ojalatero phenomenon into a propaganda offensive to desta-
bilise the Carlist war effort from within. His ‘Letter from a Basque farmer
to a tinker’ was a pun on the shirkers of the Carlist home front (tinker,
or ‘hojalatero’, is a homophone of ‘ojalatero’). Its content brilliantly
set the Carlist Basques against the Carlist ‘Castilians’.12 Even though
Aviraneta’s impact has been overstated by neo-Carlists keen to stress
the autonomism of nineteenth-century Carlism, it remains the most
effective propaganda campaign of the war.13
   The repressive arm of the Carlist state was the Royal Police. Created
in April 1836, initially with the modest aim of being a royal bodyguard,
it quickly assumed classically nineteenth-century police-state functions
in the face of growing discontent and war-weariness, controlling inter-
nal passports, combatting desertion, supervising local foral elections
and, controversially, superimposing its law-and-order jurisdiction over
160 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


that of the fueros. Above all, it hunted deserters. Soon after its creation
the Royal Police enforced punitive measures enacted by the juntas of
each Basque province and Navarra during the spring and summer of
1836, ﬁning and imprisoning the families of deserters, and its repres-
sion reached a peak during the ﬁrst half of 1837.14 The harshness of
the law of hostages was moderated in March 1837 by the personal
intervention of Don Carlos (only families which had sheltered desert-
ers remained in prison).15 Such was the Royal Police’s unpopularity that
it was disbanded in June 1837 by Prime Minister José de Uranga, a
middle-aged royalist veteran of 1821–23 and 1831 and erstwhile ally of
Zumalacárregui. The King was on expedition, hence technically there
was no longer the need for a royal bodyguard in the Basque coun-
try, but Uranga’s real reasons were telling. If legend is to be believed,
Uranga slept protected by a personal bodyguard of two men inside his
room and another two outside. The Byzantine world of Carlist politics
often saw ministers attend meetings armed, and this friction was largely
caused between the disproportionately Basque apostólico (and fuerista)
and the disproportionately non-Basque enlightened absolutist wings of
Carlism. Since the death of Zumalacárregui Don Carlos had appointed
prime ministers whose centralising measures had alienated apostólicos
and fueristas. One such, Carlos Cruz Mayor, a very capable enlightened
absolutist, was already controversial for having led the ‘Bilbao faction’
blamed for Zumalacárregui’s death. But in April 1836 he alienated the
apostólico faction further by speaking out against peace plans to extend
the fueros beyond the Basque country, and Don Carlos yielded to pres-
sure to replace him with the more tactful Juan Bautista Erro y Azpíroz.16
But Erro, a 62-year-old academic and fugitive from Cristino captivity,
soon alienated the same elements with his zeal to centralise revenue col-
lection, his creation of the Royal Police and the ‘Armamento General’,
the May 1836 decree forbidding private ownership of arms. The deci-
sion to attempt a second siege of Bilbao was largely Erro’s, and its failure
provided Don Carlos with a pretext to sack him.17 Thus Uranga, whose
cabinet included both apostólicos and moderates, trod carefully with the
former. His continuation of the centralisation of his predecessors was
made palatable both by his abolition of the Royal Police – who were
sent to the front to assuage ojalaterismo – and his campaign of lim-
ited offensives against Cristino forts in the Basque country to distract
Cristino forces from the pursuit of the Royal Expedition. In August 1837,
Uranga silenced his critics by taking the important Cristino fortress at
Peñacerrada.18
                                                Carlist Failure, 1837–39   161


   The war forced the Royal Government to impose unprecedented reg-
ulation and taxation upon the Basque population. Money was lacking
and it was only a matter of time before the crushing ﬁnes imposed
on local Liberals, combined with forced loans levied on Carlists (even
on nuns), would run dry, not least because discontent was growing
even within the ranks at the one-third pay given to each soldier and
ofﬁcer. Administrative reforms proved more effective at ﬁnding new
resources: Juntas Generales, it was decreed, should meet every two years
at Guernica, the Carlist Deputation of Vizcaya decreed new levies on
imports, whilst the Guernica junta on 14 July 1835 hit the civil popu-
lation yet again with a new forced loan of 2 million reales to be paid
back at 5 per cent annual interest, and to be guaranteed against all
national assets across Spain. Don Carlos, was less successful, however,
when it came to enlisting international ﬁnancial support for Carlist
Spain. The mooted 125,000,000-franc loan arranged by ﬁnanciers Haber,
Jange and Gower never materialised, nor did other deals, largely because
Don Carlos displayed the rare ability always to pick the wrong argument
at the wrong time. He promised that a future Carlist government would
categorically refuse to honour any foreign creditor who had outstand-
ing debts to the ‘usurping government’. This debacle took the wind out
of the sails of overseas Carlist representatives.19 In the event no real
ﬁnancial help arrived apart from the erratic and varying donations of
European legitimists, despite the varying pledges of ﬁnancial support
made by Prussian, Dutch, Austrian and Italian rulers, so Carlist agents
were forced to tap the vagaries of private lending markets.
   The militarisation of Carlist society proceeded more thoroughly than
in Cristino Spain. The Carlists developed a comparatively more efﬁ-
cient hierarchical organisation of their military. Comandante generales
retained default control of all forces in their province unless a divi-
sion general asked for them.20 Beyond this, the demands of the war
regulated food and production far more than in Cristino Spain. From
28 January 1836 food was rationed: two daily rations of bread and meat
and wine for serving soldiers and workers in arms forges and factories,
and half this quantity for all ‘passive’ classes (such as retired, and other
civilians) except heads of family. The wine ration was harder to pro-
vide in Guipúzcoa and parts of Durango where vines were traditionally
not cultivated, but such was the demand of soldiers for wine, especially
Basque soldiers whose relative lack of sobriety was not satiated by the
abundant but unsatisfying local cider, that the Carlists passed a special
decree reserving all wine in these areas exclusively for soldiers and arms
162 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


workers. Arms workers were given special consideration because of the
vital need for increased output. Output did increase markedly, largely
because of territorial expansion in 1835, but there was an accompanying
rise in explosives-related accidents.21 Otherwise, the Carlists had only
unreliable and intermittent imports from Castile. Alava banned the stor-
ing of grain in order to prioritise military needs; in return a 14 March
1836 decree from Durango treated all food shipments throughout the
Basque country as if they were traversing within a single province (hence
an anti-foral single market), and maximum prices were imposed on
wheat, corn and barley. Thus, the general European grain depression
of the 1830s was distorted in the Basque country into a boom as prices
stayed high.22
   The regulation of the domestic economy was made harsher by the
growing burden of conscription. A decree on 29 January 1836 ordered
all 17-year-olds into the Carlist ranks, imposing a 200 ducados ﬁne on
communities who failed to give up these youngsters within three days.23
Clearly, the early image of hardy Basque volunteers, rising up against
the usurping government in Madrid in defence of throne and altar,
was overborne by the grim demands of total war. This conscription
decree had been preceded by draconian measures targeting desertion.
During November and December 1835, three decrees imposed penal-
ties escalating to the death penalty against deserters. The Carlist state
was thus relying on depressingly modern machinery in order to sustain
the war effort, which contrasted with the timeless religiosity motivat-
ing Carlist villages and combatants. The fact that the Carlist rearguard
still offered a more peaceable and united aspect than the chaos reign-
ing beyond the Cristino lines owed a great deal to the fact that virtually
every available man was at the front, and that Cristino occupation could
realistically threaten any village in the statelet. The taboo of desertion
and draft-dodging thus explained popular anger at ojalateros and further
decrees passed in 1836 tightened up conscription still further, exempt-
ing only the most desperate families (for example, unigeniture with
parents over 60, widowers with dependants under 16).24 The regulation
of the war economy was more consistently applied than in the far larger
Cristino zone. In light of this fact it is remarkable how pre-modern val-
ues of religion and personalities continued to shape Carlist propaganda.
Zumalacárregui was dead but the Royal Government persisted in deploy-
ing the Great Man’s popular resistance motif: the unaltered address of
‘volunteers’ to conscripts, a press publication of an alleged conversation
between a foreign traveller and a belligerent Carlist mother in the Baztán
and the ennoblement of the commoner, Juan Bautista Esaín, dubbed
                                                 Carlist Failure, 1837–39   163


‘Don Carlos’s donkey’, in recognition of his close-run rescue of the King
from Rodil’s forces in September 1834.25
   In the Carlist east, the intangible power of personalities and religion
continued to play a central role as the Carlist state and its civil powers of
regulation were far less developed. Cabrera lacked the Zumalacárregui’s
magnanimity yet his austere charisma commanded the loyalty of his
men. Cabrera was deﬁant: ‘I know the King is being told that I’m not
very pious; this may be true, because I am no saint, and yet I per-
form miracles.’26 As the Carlist north was deteriorating in 1839, Cabrera
inspired his men into victory in battle on his saint’s day, St Raymond’s
Day, the medieval Catalan saint beloved of his men.27 In other words,
the itinerant nature of warfare in the east fostered charismatic rather
than bureaucratic leadership, mobilising religiosity with audacity, just as
during the ﬁrst year of Zumalacárregui’s command in the north. Much
of this was Romantic haze, but this should not discount the impact
of charismatic leadership on Carlist military successes – elements of
nineteenth-century ‘Great Man theory’, rather than twentieth-century
sociology and Marxist social history.
   Meanwhile, both sides recovered, over the winter of 1836–37, from
their respective setbacks of Gómez and Bilbao. A winter ﬂu epidemic
added to the Cristinos’ usual burdens of supply and desertion.28 Statis-
tically, the northern front was as militarised as it would ever be: 30,000
Carlist infantry supported by 1,500 cavalry and 40 artillery pieces and
opposing 59,094 Cristino campaign infantry supported by 2,491 cav-
alry, and a further 23,225 Cristino infantry supported by 293 cavalry on
garrison duty. The Carlists were thus outnumbered, but their traditional
advantage of interior lines of communication was augmented by a ﬂour-
ishing spy network at Bayonne, which had given them advance warning
of Cristino operations during early 1837.29 A major Cristino offensive in
March was turned into a defensive victory at Oriamendi, a mountain
near San Sebastián that gave the Carlist movement its enduring hymn.
   The Carlist victory at Oriamendi on 16 March 1837 was a text-
book example of its superiority in active defence and local initiative in
defence of the Basque homeland. In brief, the pincer action promised
by Sarsﬁeld was thrown into retreat when a forced march by the Carlist
Prince Sebastián threatened his ﬂank, thereby allowing a Carlist counter-
thrust between the forces of Espartero and Evans which defeated both
Cristino commanders in detail. The Cristinos’ losses were more than
400 dead, 900 wounded, and 137 prisoners and several small arms and
artillery pieces captured, whereas the Carlists had 88 dead, 669 wounded
and only a handful of prisoners. This defeat was even harder to bear for
164 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


the Cristinos as their offensive had started well, so the Carlist counter-
attack subsequently led to many decorated Cristino ofﬁcers theatrically
forsaking their awards until they regained victory. Evans’s auxiliaries,
for whom Oriamendi was their greatest setback, were singled out for
brutality at the hands of the pursuing Carlists, no quarter being given
to stragglers who failed to keep up with the retreat.30 Only the brief
positioning of a Royal Marines battalion on the road from Oriamendi
prevented a wholesale rout from taking place. Lord John Hay, com-
mander of the British naval squadron stationed at Pasajes which had
disembarked the Marines, had felt obliged to withdraw his men and,
crucially, their artillery, at the ﬁrst available opportunity. He knew that
Tory attacks in the House of Commons and press were mounting against
the Whigs’ forward policy in Spain, and the deployment of regular
British forces some seven miles inland from the coast could hardly be
seen as anything other than formal military intervention.31 The British
Legion’s underperformance at the Battle of Oriamendi provoked an
intense debate in the British parliament.32 The early ground won by the
Cristino offensive, meanwhile, had caused such widespread destruction
in such nearby Carlist settlements as Hernani, much of it malicious,
that even heads of family who had been exempted from conscription
into the Carlist ranks thereafter formed volunteer anti-Cristino guerrilla
units in the area. The retreat of the Cristinos towards Bilbao ensued,
ﬁghting a bloody rearguard action on the way.33
   The Carlist victory did not halt court intrigues. The word ‘transac-
tionist’ (or transaccionista) surfaced as a description of those Carlist
elites who sought some sort of the compromise peace with the Lib-
eral regime in Madrid. In early 1837, this attitude was associated with
Count Casa-Eguía, who, so rumours ran, was conspiring with the French
king, who was exasperated by the cost of fortifying his southern border.
But the transactionist phenomenon still had more than two years to
run before it culminated in the compromise peace accord of Vergara,
and even then the Basque country was divided broadly between pro-
transaccionista Vizcaya and Guipúzcoa and diehard Alava and Navarra.34
These court intrigues impelled Don Carlos to undertake a bold strategic
offensive during the summer of 1837, known as the ‘Royal Expedition’.
   The Carlist capture of the Cristino town of Lerín, lynchpin of the
Ribera, in May 1837, at ﬁrst appeared as a minor victory like any other:
400 Cristino prisoners were taken prisoner and a local judge taken
hostage.35 But this attack was actually the opening feint of the Royal
Expedition. This expedition would last 160 days, traversing Navarra,
Aragón, Catalonia, Valencia, both Castiles, La Mancha and Alava, in
                                                    Carlist Failure, 1837–39   165


total over 2,000 kilometres, four cities, 152 small towns and some 353
villages.36 The summer of 1837 witnessed three interconnected develop-
ments which would seal both the outcome of the war and its legacy.
Cristino Spain moderated its revolutionary Constitution, the Carlists
launched their Expedition as a ‘liberation’ and Cristino Spain was turned
into a praetorian state. We deal with the Constitution of 1837 elsewhere,
but the other two developments bear discussion here. The elevation
by the Cortes of the Commander-in-Chief of the Army of the North,
Baldomero Espartero, to Generalísimo, had both short-term and long-
term consequences. In the short term, Espartero’s overlordship rankled
with his colleagues, in particular the Commander of the Army of the
Centre, Marcelino Oráa. Espartero’s gratuitous failure to acknowledge
Oráa’s victory at Chiva led, in July 1837, to a breakdown of coordina-
tion between the two armies, allowing the Royal Expedition to recover
its fortunes and, indirectly, to inﬂict a serious defeat on the Cristinos at
Herrera.37 It remains moot whether the Cristinos would have handled
the Carlist expedition better had their generals been answerable to the
War Ministry rather than Espartero (Table 7.1).
   The long-term consequences of Espartero’s powers are more poignant,
for they completed the transition to praetorianism, which, apart from a
hiatus during the last quarter of the nineteenth century, would remain
the dominant feature of Spanish politics until the era of Franco. This
transition was not fully understood at ﬁrst for what it was. Espartero,
like Espoz before him, was a man of the Left whose anodyne mantra of
‘Let the national will be fulﬁlled!’ rallied most of both army and Militia
opinion in the ‘peace dividend’ revolution of 1840; only the more rad-
ical wing of the Left noted that the ‘people’s choice’ had in fact turned
into the ‘Spanish Napoleon’. The dominant, pseudo-dictatorial role of
Espartero now became evident when he complained of the publication,

Table 7.1 Eco del Comercio, 18 January 1839: State of regular Cristino and Carlist
