166 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


in El Patriota newspaper, of an article criticising Valmaseda’s evacuation.
Espartero called the author an imposter and agent of Don Carlos and
demanded he be punished. The government could not risk displeasing
its hero and two press censors who had let the article through were
replaced.38 Espartero’s power forced newspaper editors to tread carefully.
The wife of the editor of a new leftist satirical journal (La Injusticia)
fawningly asked for the Generalísimo’s endorsement of its mission to
use satire for the service of ‘the happiness of the people and in defence
of their rights’.39 The structural shift towards a praetorianism caused by
the Carlist War was plain for all to see when, in 1843, moderado counter-
revolutionaries violently evicted Espartero and his remaining supporters
from ofﬁce. A parallel praetorianism dominated Carlist politics as a
result of the defeat of the Royal Expedition and its apostólico advocates.
But both Maroto’s military dictatorship in the north and Cabrera’s in
the east were truncated by the Carlist defeats of 1839 and 1840.
   None of this, of course, was the intention of the fateful Carlist
decision to march on Madrid in 1837. Carlist calculations were com-
pelling for four reasons. The ﬁrst two reasons were interconnected:
diplomatic and dynastic. Despite Cristino Spain’s improved diplomatic
links with Britain and the Americas following the La Granja revolu-
tion, the European response had favoured the Carlists. France’s policy
had been equivocal, whereas several absolutist powers had emboldened
their support for Don Carlos to the verge of virtual recognition. Only
Metternich’s caution – and British naval supremacy – prevented the
Italian kingdoms from launching a Sardinian–Neapolitan naval attack
on Valencia in 1837.40 Although short of direct intervention, the ﬁnan-
cial help and illicit arms deliveries to Carlist Spain had grown since La
Granja, and even though the King’s emissaries overseas still felt let down
by the European capitals of the counter-revolution, they had every rea-
son to believe Metternich’s recognition would follow their capture of
Madrid.41 The second reason was dynastic in nature. María Cristina was
more wounded by her humiliation at La Granja the previous August
than most nineteenth-century writers realised. The very real prospect of
violence against her person, and her regal captivity under Calatrava’s
revolutionary dictatorship, had driven the Queen-Regent to correspond
with her brother, Absolute King of Naples, seeking a private release from
Spain via her abdication and exile, and a dynastic resolution to the civil
war via the future marriage of her daughter Isabella with the son of Don
Carlos. Thus, her captaining of Liberal resistance in Madrid in September
1837 was a double game. Ironically, María Cristina’s eventual capitu-
lation at Valencia during the post-war ‘peace dividend’ revolution of
                                               Carlist Failure, 1837–39   167


October 1840 ended a process of her personal fear of Spanish liberalism
that began with the sergeants’ rising at La Granja on 13 August 1836.42
   The third reason was military. The Carlists had proved secure in their
northern heartland, and the Gómez expedition had shown their abil-
ity to destabilise the enemy’s rearguard. Thus, a three-pronged strategy
gave them the initiative: whereas the actual Royal Expedition marched
south-east, a smaller expedition struck south-west, and the bulk of the
Royal Army remained in the Basque country to harass Cristino garrisons
in a series of local offensives. Moreover, the Royal Government had
demanded the removal of as many Carlist forces as possible from the
Basque country, as the local economy was on the point of collapsing
under the ‘total war’ burdens of men, food and munitions.43 March 1837
had seen Navarrese battalions in Estella openly insubordinate for the
ﬁrst time, shouting ‘Death to the ojalateros and the junta! Long live the
people (paisanos)!’, as their pay arrears had made them desperate. They
disbelieved the King’s promise of redress.44 It remains unclear whether
the Expedition’s champions were exaggerating in their warning that the
Basque country could support only 15 more days of operations.45 But
rising interest from some Carlists in quack military solutions to their
strategic blockade (including a British adventurer’s offer to arm cor-
sairs with a prototype torpedo from the Woolwich arsenal) is highly
suggestive.46
   The fourth reason was moralistic in nature. The ‘re-evangelisation’
of revolutionary Spain was Don Carlos’s main strategy, even though,
in measuring his success in the number of Masses held in liberated vil-
lages, he invited criticism from contemporaries and historians.47 Church
ordinations and attendance had collapsed in the wake of two years
of Cristino anti-clericalism. The Carlist press trailed stories of aban-
doned parishes in the wake of the schism with Rome, the powerlessness
of the bishops, the expulsion or imprisonment of priests by Cristino
soldiers and militia, and, above all, the mendizabalista disentailment;
indeed, some 400 parishes closed due to lack of ministers during the
ﬁrst half of 1836 alone.48 Linked to this moralistic reason was the
real hope that Madrid would bloodlessly pronounce in favour of Don
Carlos as soon as his army reached the city walls. A foreign journalist
who had visited Madrid the previous year reckoned that some ‘three-
fourths of the respectable portion of its population are in favour of
Don Carlos’.49
   But the Carlists were not complacently hoping for a spontaneous ﬁfth
column to rise. A clandestine Carlist junta operated from a safe house in
the Calle de Alcalá where a pro-Carlist noble met agents of the absolutist
168 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


powers to advance a dynastic solution to the conﬂict, involving an
honourable exile for the ‘widow of Ferdinand VII’ and a Carlist takeover
humanised by an agreement neither to resurrect the Inquisition nor to
oppose ‘sensible reforms’. These terms were agreed in a private letter
from the Queen-Regent to her brother (Ferdinand II of Naples), which
the unofﬁcial Neapolitan representative carried with him when the rev-
olutionaries deported him.50 All that was needed, it seemed, was the
appearance of the Carlist Royal Army and its welcome into the capital
by a grateful Queen-Regent. Carlist peace terms had certainly improved
since the previous year, when Don Carlos turned away a Cristino delega-
tion by demanding the dismantling of the Quadruple Alliance, refusing
to recognise the constitutional regime in Portugal and tactlessly suggest-
ing that all Spain offer Masses for the Virgin as Generalísima of the Carlist
forces. Delegates Roncali and Zambrano were aristocrats and right-wing
moderados, enemies of the Calatrava revolution, but Don Carlos saw
in these men not two potential counter-revolutionaries (as they would
indeed become in 1844), but two Liberals.51 But even though pro-Carlist
historians sympathised with Don Carlos’s integrity in 1836, they also
criticised the dynastic peace option of 1837 for making the entire Expe-
dition a hostage to the whims of the Queen-Regent, and for distorting
the campaign into an eschatological carnival of religious piety rather
than a ruthless assault on the capital.52
   In retrospect, Don Carlos’s campaign of re-evangelisation is not as
supine as it may seem. Both the Catholic resistance against revolution-
aries during the Vendée during the 1790s and during the twentieth-
century Mexican Cristiada showed a link between religious fervour
and popular resistance, and Don Carlos had been encouraged by the
Church’s policy of excommunicating expropriators and purchasers of
disentailed ecclesiastical property. But the King’s thinking created two
ﬂaws, one of strategy and another of perception. As the expedition was
supposed to be a government-in-waiting, its mobility was burdened by
excessive numbers of non-combatants and shoestring logistics, which
impeded the Carlists’ ability to transport and deploy sufﬁcient artillery
and alienated ‘liberated’ communities more than necessary by forcing
the ‘liberators’ to live off the land.53 The second ﬂaw was far more
profound. Don Carlos believed that the enemy’s will to ﬁght was still
coerced by the ‘usurping’ elites of 1833, and that the war remained
a dynastic dispute. In fact, such had been the wave of revolutionary
change since the start of the civil war that the Bourbon family troubles
had become irrelevant, or at the very least their solution could no longer
be relied upon to create peace. Ironically, the will to exercise absolute
                                               Carlist Failure, 1837–39   169


powers for peace by both Don Carlos and María Cristina proved not only
hollow, but also the last serious attempt by any Spanish monarch to rule
on so important an issue as war and peace; after the Royal Expedition,
the generals, not the monarch, would have the last word. Moreover, reli-
gious fervour outside of the Carlist safe zones was a notoriously useless
ally. Most purchasers of disentailed Church property had, in any case,
been using intermediaries to avoid excommunication, and could thus
claim innocence if they were unlucky enough to come under Carlist
occupation.
   Don Carlos’s pilgrimage of grace was not immediately exposed as
militarily ﬂawed, in large part because of the usual pattern of Cristino
disintegration. The invasion broke through the Cristino covering force
near Estella on 15 May 1837. Commander-in-Chief of the Army of the
North dispatched a pursuing force under General Iribarren, who was
reinforced by 12,000 militiamen mobilised from Huesca and Zaragoza.
But the ﬁrst battle at Huesca on 24 May 1837 was a Carlist victory, as
some 1,000 Cristino soldiers and militiamen were either killed or cap-
tured as the Carlist invaders were skilled at pinning Iribarren amid the
sticky mud along the banks of the swollen River Cinca, forcing him
to retreat. Virtually all the Cristino authorities ﬂed the city, but the
bishop remained to welcome Don Carlos, who stayed for three days
before moving on to take Barbastro, which surrendered without a shot
being ﬁred. Don Carlos heard Mass in this ancient city’s cathedral whilst
the Cristinos tried to regroup. The next Cristino counteroffensive came
from Oráa’s 12,400 infantry, 1,400 cavalry and artillery of the Army of
the Centre. Yet the Carlists had fortiﬁed Barbastro in time for the bat-
tle on 2 June 1837. The Carlists were massively outmatched in artillery,
but they managed to defeat the Cristinos, by again using the geograph-
ical contours of the river bank to their advantage. Barbastro resonated
for being a civil war within a civil war. The thousand-strong Carlist for-
eign legion (comprised of French Foreign Legion defectors and pardoned
miguelistas defecting from the Oporto Hunters54 ) fought its former com-
rades, and the German Carlist, Prince Lichnowsky,55 was saddened to
hear that they killed each other shouting in French and German.56
One of the fratricidal casualties was the French Legion’s commander,
Brigadier Conrad, an Alsatian Napoleonic Wars veteran known for his
common touch and his humanitarian treatment of civilians, whose
death was all the more galling as it came in a forlorn bid to rally the
terriﬁed ﬂight of men who had recently distinguished themselves at
Huesca.57 The Carlist foreign legion, in the thick of the ﬁghting during
this victory, was almost wiped out and was dissolved.58
170 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


   Despite these Aragonese victories, the Carlists’ poor logistics, com-
pounded by the large number of non-combatants accompanying the
invasion, made Don Carlos’s progress appear sluggish when compared
with Gómez a year earlier. Some 300 expeditionaries were drowned in
an ill-fated mass crossing of the swollen Cinca.59 The poor agriculture
of Huesca province could not support invasion, and the Carlists made
enemies of the villages in their path. Angüés had tried to ‘welcome’ the
expedition but had been sacked and pillaged by its hungry contingents
all the same. Estopiñan’s villagers, on 6 June, tried to hide its food-
stuffs, but the telltale eggshells in a pantry drove the Carlists to break in
the chicken pens and slaughter the poultry before moving on.60 Pillage
blunted the expeditionaries’ use of Catholic revivalism in order to win
over civilians. But pillage was inevitable for an expedition advancing
on a nutritional shoestring. Poor nutrition also made it hard to recover
from defeats. It was remarkable that the expeditionaries managed to
extricate themselves from a serious defeat by the Captain-General of
Catalonia, Baron Meer, on 12 June 1837; so remarkable that Cristino
Radicals suspected treachery on Meer’s part.61 Ever since riots had swept
Zaragoza and Barcelona in January 1837 Meer had appeared to be more
concerned about subduing radicalism than ﬁghting Carlism.62
   Radicals were exasperated because the expedition had invaded
Catalonia, a province which, beyond its traditionalist far west, had
become virtually diabolical in Carlist eyes. Santpedor, only 45 miles
from Barcelona, saw its outskirts burnt and far worse threatened after
its militia garrison refused to capitulate.63 The expedition traversed
Catalonia, crossing the Ebro at Cherta on 29 June, a busy day marked
by the defeat of a pursuing Portuguese division, a solemn Mass and Te
Deum in the town’s patron saint church of St Martin (and a pillage of its
surroundings), and a personal meeting between the King and Cabrera,
whose forces were now linked with the expedition.64 Reinforced by
Cabrera, other victories followed. Castellón, a Valencian provincial cap-
ital only a few miles from the Mediterranean, had been abandoned
by revolutionary soldiers and militiamen the previous summer, and
subsequently sacked by Cabrera. The Militia had re-formed since and
those citizens who had not ﬂed were resolved not to face the same fate
again. But Castellón fell a second time all the same, and the expedition
got closer to the safe zone of the Maestrazgo.65 The Cristino Army of
the Centre used superior topography and cavalry to defeat the expe-
dition at the Battle of Chiva (Valencia) on 15 July 1837. But Oráa’s
lack of food and water stopped him routing the expedition, which
escaped into Aragón.66 The march into Aragón was punishing for both
                                                Carlist Failure, 1837–39   171


expeditionaries and pursuers, and doubly so for the civilian commu-
nities in the armies’ path, which saw their entire wealth pillaged by
one army, only to end up being occupied by the following army. After a
retreat of some 150 miles, the Carlists, on 24 August 1837, defeated Oráa
at Villar de los Navarros (called the Battle of Herrera), inﬂicting over
2,000 casualties and capturing sorely needed weapons and supplies.67
   No tragedy matched that of the Cristino prisoners taken after this
battle.68 More than half would die over the course of an itinerant captiv-
ity which would stretch over six months before the surviving prisoners
were exchanged. Cabrera was bent on keeping conditions so dire as to
force the prisoners to defect to his ranks. That the Cristino prisoners
refused to do so turned them into patriotic-Liberal heroes of the Cristino
effort. But it also condemned them to the direst excesses of cold, hunger,
disease and mortality. No trial was spared the prisoners: cannibalism,
scavenging, murder, exposure, typhus, forced marches, random execu-
tions. By the time that the survivors had been exchanged in March 1838
they had become martyrs to liberty. The nineteenth-century historian,
Antonio Pirala, published the diary of a captive ofﬁcer (Sub-Lieutenant
Juan Manuel Martín), which resonates for being a rare literary account
of common suffering in Spain’s last great pre-literary conﬂict.69

  25 August 1837 Passing through the village of Blesa the inhabitants
    take pity on us poor prisoners, giving us big servings of bread,
    chocolate, and some shoes.
  1 September 1837 We return from Cantavieja to Villarluengo, we ofﬁ-
    cers staying in the same building as three days before, and the
    men in a convent. For nine days we remain here, sleeping on hard
    ﬂoors and being given nothing more than a hundredweight of bread
    every day.
  10 September 1837 We return to Cantavieja and are housed in the local
    castle for a fortnight, after which we are incarcerated in the local
    prison.
  27 September 1837 The Liberal prefect of Teruel organises a donation
    which sees us receive some shirts, linen and bandages, and later
    some blankets, too. The Carlists abuse us for this.
  15 October 1837 Word spreads that General Oráa is about to lay siege to
    Cantavieja, so the facciosos spirit us off towards Beceite, after which
    we march by day and sleep at different locations each night.
  20 October 1837 We leave Peñaroya for Valderrobles, some ofﬁcers
    remaining behind stricken with typhus. Reaching Valderrobles we
    realise that this disease has spread amongst us and I’m almost at
172 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


    death’s door. I lie delirious and helpless as some Carlists enter my
    room and rob me of everything they can lay their hands on. The
    care of the village doctors saves me from death, but the same cannot
    be said of several other ofﬁcers who succumb.
  1 November 1837 We leave Arnés for Orta, eating nothing more than
    miserable half-rations whenever they are given.
  10 November 1837 We are ordered to leave at 11 o’clock at night,
    without being given any time even to fetch our blankets, the only
    bedding we possess. No beasts of burden were assigned to transport
    the sick. One ofﬁcer, it is said, was too sick to move and was killed
    by the Carlist guards who were responsible for him, and now they
    are wearing his particulars. Also the widow of the ofﬁcer Mediero
    who had tended to her dying husband until the end is robbed and
    raped.
  11 November 1837 Today and last night more than 40 soldiers have
    been shot who were so weakened by hunger that they couldn’t
    keep up.
  12 November 1837 We sick ofﬁcers who were in the hospital at
    Valderrobles leave for Beceite. Early this morning our men arrived
    here. We were horriﬁed to see them, as they look more like ghosts
    than men. They are put to marching ahead of us whilst we are
    roused from our beds to follow them. We are all starved, but the
    road ahead is littered with the corpses of those captives who were
    executed for straggling.
  14 November 1837 The ofﬁcers’ hunger is assuaged somewhat by some
    occasional half-rations. But the men are not given anything, with
    the result that at least 8–10 soldiers are dying each day from the
    combined effects of hunger and cold.
  25 November 1837 Mortality continues. Ofﬁcers receive some 3,000
    reales generously donated by the National Militia garrison of
    Tortosa, which equates to 37 reales for each individual.
  4 December 1837 Horrendous mortality. The men haven’t had bread
    for more than 12 days and are reduced to eating a ration of one
    pound-and-a-half of raw potatoes per day. They are forced to strip
    the rooﬁng which leaves them terribly exposed to the cold. They
    crowd on to the balcony to beg for help from local inhabitants. The
    balcony gives away killing 15 prisoners and leaving several more
    injured.
  8 December 1837 Mortality continues. Another overcrowded ﬂoor
    holding prisoners gives away. More than 50 are killed or injured.
                                              Carlist Failure, 1837–39   173


  10 December 1837 Delivery arrives from Alcañiz of 306 blankets which
    are distributed amongst the men.
  19 December 1837 Constant daily mortality. Word reaches us from
    Cabrera that our exchange is in train, and that Pellicer should give
    us full rations. Nonetheless, we continue to receive only half.
  28 December 1837 No ration whatsoever today. 21 soldiers die. Such is
    the hunger and misery that anyone with even a couple of maravedis
    (ochavo) in his possession risks being murdered just for this puny
    sum. Any soldier ordered to leave for work on fortiﬁcations would
    pick up bones to be ground into bonemeal to be eaten. The corpses
    of dead comrades are hidden away to be picked over and cooked
    at night by lantern light. Such is the state of the unhappy prison-
    ers of Herrera! History teaches us about the sufferings of ancient
    heroes, and about martyrs in Spain’s bloody wars, but can anyone
    have suffered as much as we imprisoned heroes of Espartero?
  29 December 1837 One-ﬁfth rations today. But a subscription arrives
    from Teruel at the invitation of its prefect of some 2,000 reales
    which are distributed amongst both ofﬁcers and men.
  30 December 1837 One-fourth rations. Ofﬁcers from the regiment of
    Badajoz province garrisoning Tortosa send us 2,000 reales which are
    distributed amongst both ofﬁcers and men. Our thanks are eternal
    and our hearts warm to know that we have not been forgotten by
    our comrades.
  31 December 1837 No rations today. Mortality rises to 13. Only 500
    soldiers are left in their depot, which means that around 600 have
    fallen victim to our appalling situation.

Military solutions to this barbarity were virtually impossible. The
Cristino government hoped that the Expedition had denuded the Carlist
north of forces, yet it had also drawn Cristino forces away from the Army
of the North in pursuit (15 battalions, nine cavalry squadrons and sub-
stantial artillery pursuing the 16 battalions and eight squadrons of the
Carlist expedition). Yet the Carlist Royal Army in the Basque country
retained 31 battalions, four squadrons and almost all its ﬁeld artillery,
against which the Cristinos could mount for campaign 28 battalions, 11
squadrons and rather less artillery. The Carlists thus retained defensive
superiority against Espartero’s Army of the North, even while their Expe-
dition was wreaking havoc with Oráa’s Army of the Centre.70 Espartero
was weakened even further when Portuguese politics robbed him of Das
Antas’s Legion, which Lisbon ordered to redeploy from the Navarrese
174 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


Ribera towards Portugal’s frontier. In June 1837, disaffected Portuguese
civil war veterans – one-third of whom were pardoned miguelistas –
revolted, and Lisbon dared not risk them in battle.71 Also, Espartero’s
forces were absorbed by another Carlist expedition, smaller in number
but also more mobile and one that traversed pro-Carlist areas of Old
Castile. Juan Antonio Zaratiegui, a young royalist during the Triennium
and Inspector of Royalist Volunteers in his native Navarra thereafter,
excited exculpatory post-war writings indignant at his ‘scapegoating’ for
the failure of the Royal Expedition.72
   In July 1837, Zaratiegui launched an expedition of some 280 cavalry
and ﬁve infantry battalions imbued with the same religious liberation
ethos as the Royal Expedition. After defeating Das Antas’s Portuguese
on the Ebro, Zaratiegui pushed into Old Castile towards Madrid.73 Most
of the defenders, including the teenage cadets from the military col-
lege, ﬂed to Madrid, even though several college professors offered their
services to the occupying Carlists. News in the capital of Zaratiegui’s
approach caused panic, and a state of siege was imposed on 6 August,
decreeing a wide range of pro-Carlist activities and opinions to be pun-
ishable by martial law. The capital’s Militia came out in a show of force
and was supplemented by the formation of some companies of ‘distin-
guished citizens’ to patrol suspect districts of the capital (although both
Carlist and Peninsular War experience with these units had revealed
them to be ceremonial rather than warlike in their usefulness).74 Key
military facilities in the Carlists’ path, the Military College at Segovia,
the Artillery College at Alcalá and the Engineers School at Guadalajara,
were evacuated to the capital.75 Zaratiegui suffered the usual Carlist vain-
glory at having conquered a substantial city, and began minting coins
embossed with Don Carlos, and publicising his conquest in the language
of the Christian soldier, thanking Divine Providence for triumphing
over the ‘faithless’ anarchy of the Madrid government and all its false
philosophy.76
   Zaratiegui’s victory speech was untimely, as the pursuit of Espartero’s
battle-hardened Army of the North obliged him to abandon Segovia,
although not before he had emptied the city of its volunteers and
artillery, sabotaging those guns that could not be transported. Yet con-
quests like Segovia and others across Old Castile had served the purpose
of destabilising Cristino defences while the main Carlist invasion of
Cabrera and the Royal Expedition approached the capital from the east.
War- and revolution-weary Cristino garrisons had surrendered often
after a symbolic exchange of ﬁre and Zaratiegui got new recruits, both
volunteers and conscripts. Valladolid was occupied in a negotiated,
                                                Carlist Failure, 1837–39   175


almost gentlemanly manner. Its war-weary municipal delegation rode
out to meet Zaratiegui and Liberal members of the local government
resigned and departed whilst pro-Carlist administrators took their place
and awaited Zaratiegui’s instructions. This accommodation spared the
city from pillage and atrocities. The delegation performed this handover
in reverse when the Army of the North arrived to reoccupy the city days
later.77 Some communities, like those on the Ribera del Duero, showed
the same degree of public enthusiasm for the Carlists as they had pre-
viously for the Liberals. Partisan ideology was negligible in this. Rather,
communities were so terriﬁed by the prospect of violent occupations
and counter-occupations that they resolved to ‘manage’ their occupiers
by staging public welcomes which, in reality, kept the soldiers as much
as possible at arm’s length.78 Local authorities across Old Castile dis-
covered their Carlist sympathies. Or rather, perhaps, they rediscovered
them: the Pretender’s ideas of theocratic monarchy were far from being
alien to men who, under Cristino occupation, would opt for moderan-
tismo, but who would also, without much difﬁculty, move from one
camp to another.
   The disarray of the Cristino war effort led Espartero to ride ahead of
his forces to Madrid in order to reassure the Queen-Regent in person.
A spate of military indiscipline in units around the capital was dealt
with in a lenient fashion by Madrid’s military governor, San Miguel,
who admitted that the gravity of the Carlist invasion outweighed the
need to punish indiscipline.79 The main threat came from Don Carlos’s
expedition in the wake of its victory at Herrera. Eventually the Carlists
occupied Arganda and stood before Madrid’s city walls on 10 September
1837. The capital, which had already been placed under a state of siege,
was now in a state of suspended animation. Contemporaries poignantly
associated this crisis with dust being settled: quite literally, as Madrid,
which was already known as the ‘city of dust’, was choked by the dust
thrown up by the demolition of nationalised church property under-
way since the revolution of 1836.80 As the Carlists neared the walls, the
capital fell deathly quiet, with its shops and industry paralysed and the
streets emptied of people apart from the patrols of the National Militia.
Isolated attempts by Carlist agents to whip up a ﬁfth column failed. San
Miguel judiciously sent one general, whose sympathies were suspect,
away from the walls and to the centre of the capital. Now the Radicals
demanded defence à la outrance, but nothing could have been further
from the minds of the well-to-do ‘distinguished citizens’ who were
happy to preside over glittering street patrols by day – especially when
the Queen-Regent came to inspect – but shirked the more dangerous
176 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


night watches.81 Meanwhile, the capital’s underground Carlist junta
made a clever ploy by circulating a promise not to reintroduce the Inqui-
sition or despotism, and suggesting an honourable Italian exile for the
‘widow of Ferdinand VII’ and the marriage of her child Isabella to the
Carlist Crown Prince. Given this tense atmosphere of plots and counter-
plots, it was remarkable how the crisis was defused: not with conquest
or siege, but with the unmolested withdrawal of the Carlist army from
the capital’s walls.82
   In the midst of this crisis, on 15 September 1837, Captain-General
Quiroga seconded the strident revolutionary rhetoric of Governor
Seoane by condemning the Carlists as vainglorious murderers: brave
words given that many on both sides suspected that a negotiated peace
(transacción) would now take place. Even the session of the ‘besieged’
Cristino Cortes was remembered for being quiet and sombre rather than
inﬂamed and belligerent, in stark contrast to the Patriots’ parliament in
Cádiz during the Peninsular War. Admittedly, Carlist takeover was not
the only threat on the deputies’ minds. Espartero had been drawn into a
stand-off with the capital’s revolutionary dictatorship of General Seoane
over the political leadership of the revolution, which, since 18 July 1837,
had seen its Constitution of 1812 moderated by a bicameral legislature
and other brakes on radicalism that were part of the Constitution of
1837.83 A moderado army insurrection in Pozuelo de Aravaca, to the west
of the capital, had Espartero’s blessing and so the Left-Liberal Calatrava
administration fell. Now Seoane’s ‘revolutionary dictatorship’ had lost
its political support. Espartero’s mobile forces overawed Seoane’s gar-
rison largely thanks to the Carlists’ decision to withdraw in the face
of Espartero’s march. This series of events allowed Espartero – and the
army, as opposed to Seoane’s Militia – to be seen as the saviour of the
capital and the Liberal patria.84 Both men were leftists of the ‘Ayacucho’
clan, but Espartero’s victory consolidated all power in his hands.85
   The expedition’s retreat from the capital opened a series of mis-
fortunes for Carlism from which it would never recover. Redeploying
to Alcalá in a bid to fall on Espartero’s ﬂank, the Carlist army then
received an order to retreat further still, causing outrage in the ranks.
Rumours ran that Don Carlos had ordered the retreat to a more salu-
brious location in order to hear Mass. Others asserted that he wanted
to avoid bloodshed. Both probably played contingent roles, and spoke
of Don Carlos’s piety being his worst enemy.86 But the shifting diplo-
matic situation was the main cause. When the Calatrava administration
expelled the Carlist Marquess Grúa, the Carlist agent carried a letter
from the Queen-Regent to her brother, Ferdinand II of Naples, proposing
                                                 Carlist Failure, 1837–39   177


a compromise peace based on Don Carlos’s accession in return for a
general amnesty and the marriage of the infant Isabella to the Pre-
tender’s son. All depended, especially Metternich’s vital approval, on
Don Carlos’s occupation of Madrid. But the downfall in August of the
Radical Calatrava ministry, and the accession of the moderado, Eusebio
Bardají Azara, meant that when the Carlists reached the gates of the
capital on 12 September, the Queen-Regent no longer felt herself to be a
prisoner of the revolution. Hedging her bets even more, María Cristina
sent Espartero a letter begging him urgently to rescue the capital, a let-
ter that the Carlists intercepted, confusing them even further.87 Never
a decisive commander, Don Carlos thus began an initial retreat which
turned into one of general proportions, whilst sheer luck made Espartero
appear the saviour of the capital and the Liberal revolution. Carlist histo-
rians condemned the Queen-Regent’s spite and duplicity, and regretted
that Don Carlos did not force the issue.88 Madrid was now free of exter-
nal threats. Antonio Quiroga made a triumphalist victory declaration
couched in terms of popular defence and rebranding the Queen-Regent
as a constitutional Patriot.89
   The Carlist retreat, meanwhile, was easy prey for Espartero’s van-
guard: hundreds from the Carlist rearguard at Aranzueque were killed
or captured on 19 September. In desperation, the King invited Cabrera
to replace Moreno as the Carlist Commander-in-Chief but Cabrera,
who had wanted a violent pincer attack on the capital, refused due to
thinly disguised disgust at the failure of the King’s leadership. German
Carlists – anticipating the frustration of their great-grandchildren with
Franco’s slow offensive 100 years later – were exasperated with the
absence of the strategic vision worthy of Cabrera. Lichnowsky was baf-
ﬂed: ‘It was as if no-one had given any thought to descending on
Madrid.’90 The artillery expert, Wilhelm von Rahden, did not under-
stand why Carlist victories were squandered with decorations and
festivals of grace instead of a purposeful thrust.91 Now it was too late: the
Expedition was retreating northwards and the initiative was Espartero’s.
Although Espartero could not prevent the Carlist forces of Zaratiegui
and the King regrouping in their retreat, he was ruthless in his pace
and in the way he treated civilian populations in his path, sometimes
promising the death penalty against hoarders of food and drink. The
Castilians had shown little resistance to the Carlists, and Espartero’s
hand was forced by the perennial problem of hunger and insufﬁcient
and inadequate (cardboard-soled) shoes being delivered by asentistas.
On 25 October, despite his victory against the retreating Expedition
at Retuerta two weeks earlier, Espartero offered his resignation in
178 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


frustration at the general neglect of his men. He found time to ask his
wife and friends to send him money on his own authority, giving the
Generalísimo a reputation for paternalism as well as heroism. The imme-
diate crisis passed when Espartero reached Miranda de Ebro on 28 Octo-
ber to ﬁnd that the Expedition had retreated back across the Ebro.92
   In some respects this was a strange victory for Cristino Spain. Despite
all the revolutionary rhetoric of the previous two years concerning the
popular struggle of the Militia and citizens against Carlism, the Expe-
dition had been defeated in a fashion more akin to Early Modern than
Modern warfare: a dynastic misunderstanding, a carnivalesque armed
‘pilgrimage’, a cabinet war across seemingly apathetic civilian popu-
lations. Yet beneath the high politics of Madrid and Estella, the low
politics of desertion, mutiny, even economic militancy had continued
to grow. While the Expedition approached Madrid, the Army of the
North had been paralysed by mutiny. In August 1837, General Ceballos
Escalera, commanding the Cristino lynchpin of Miranda de Ebro, was
lynched by his own regiment, his soldiers subsequently seizing con-
trol of the streets and daring ofﬁcers to come out of hiding. News of
this insurrection quickly spread to Vitoria, the Cristino capital of Alava,
which was blockaded by the Carlists right up to its walls. Whilst its
Cristino citizens dared not leave, a muleteer used to running blockades
by both sides carried the news. In Vitoria, too, the Cristino garrison
lynched its military governor, the elderly Cortes deputy, Cano, the pres-
ident of the Cristino deputation, and the editor of the ofﬁcial provincial
bulletin, all before levying a ﬁne of 40,000 duros against the munici-
pality. This was the downtrodden, brutalised Cristino soldier asserting
his honour in solidarity with his comrades against both Carlists and
against Cristino elites.93 In both cases, the caretaker authorities which
the garrisons permitted to enter the power vacuum were prevented from
taking anything more than token measures against the perpetrators of
the violence.
   The last two-and-a-half years of the war would see a surging popu-
lar radicalism in the Cristino zone that was irrelevant, and ultimately
hostile, to María Cristina’s throne. Even the Carlist zone would reject its
king in a bid to advance the secular rights of Basque autonomy and army
veterans. In other words, the dynastic dispute of 1833, upon which Don
Carlos had made his obsolescent calculations in 1837, had turned into
a war whose meaning was transformed into secular concerns of Cristino
liberty and Basque fueros.
   Don Carlos’s humiliating retreat across the Ebro set in motion the
secularisation of Carlist politics. At ﬁrst, it was the brutish, apostólico
                                               Carlist Failure, 1837–39   179


wing of Carlism which won the argument, and the cabinet posts,
in the wake of the defeat. On 29 October 1837, the so-called Mani-
festo of Arceniega saw apostólicos capture their King and launch public
proclamations describing the Expedition’s failure as the work of the
transaccionistas. Certainly, few Basques were fooled by the King’s procla-
mation at Arceniega that he had ‘returned only momentarily’ and –
bizarrely – that the Royal Expedition was a promising dress rehearsal
for a future offensive of ‘national liberation’ (rather than the conclu-
sive march on Madrid spoken about only weeks earlier).94 Moderates
were purged from the cabinet and many exiled, whilst such apostólicos
as Teijeiro and Guergué took their place and thereafter maintained a
stranglehold on Carlist politics which would antagonise the army under
Maroto and ultimately result in a civil war within the civil war. That
Maroto would win, and thus clear the way for peace, could not have
been predicted by anyone in late 1837, not as Don Carlos gave three
key ministries (State, Finance and War) to the belligerent José Arias
Teijeiro, a Galician of impoverished noble background, Royalist War vet-
eran and subsequent chamberlain to Don Carlos’s household, whose
promotion into the Carlist nobility of service offended both the older
nobility of birthright and such eighteenth-century military nobles as
Maroto.95
   The only strategic achievement of the Royal Expedition was to lurch
Carlism eastwards. The fact that most of the Expedition’s victories had
been won on Aragonese territory consolidated Cabrera’s leadership in
the Maestrazgo and created a functioning and centralised Carlist state
in the region. An offensive which stalemated the Basque country ener-
gised the Carlist Maestrazgo.96 Moreover, the propaganda value of local
Carlist victories (especially at Huesca, Barbastro and Cherta) encouraged
recruitment into Carlist rather than Cristino ranks (and, further, was
stimulated by the Liberals’ property revolution which created insecurity
in the pre-modern landownership rights that dominated Carlist areas
of Aragón).97 The ‘Supreme Royal Governing Junta of Aragón, Valencia
and Murcia’, set up in 1837, gave Cabrera an effective power of veto on
all matters: whereas he could vote on any matters he saw ﬁt, the junta
was forbidden from interfering in any way with military matters. These
arrangements were similar to those of the Junta of Berga in Catalonia,
whose territory was generally coterminous with that of Cabrera’s after
late 1837. All Cabrera would now need was a run of major victories in
order to make his position unassailable. This would come in 1838, and
so change the centre of Carlism’s gravity eastwards from the stalemate
in the north.
180 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


   The Carlist domination of the Catalan far west and wide areas of
Aragón repeated the deep war of Cristino areas of Castile. The rav-
ages of Carlist bands forced agriculture and proto-industry into sudden
slumps, and these in turn fed recruitment. Recruitment into the Cristino
National Militia rose from 1837 due to the salary active service offered.
But as mobile Militia were routinely deployed away from their homes,
this opened up local communities to further depredations at the hands
of the Carlists. Equally, precisely in order to prevent hardship swelling
the ranks of the Cristino Militia, Carlist commanders frequently forced
local elites under their sway to hire hands in order to starve the Cristino
of recruits.98 This economic war was enough to give the Carlists domina-
tion of the Catalan countryside in the interior but could not generally
break the Cristino stranglehold on the towns and cities. Thus, it was
not just the lack of an Eliot Treaty in this part of Spain that made the
civil war particularly devastating for civilian lives and livelihoods: it was
also the total economic war which the nature of the combat here made
logical. This feature of Catalan Carlism has been acknowledged but not
adequately contextualised in war by historians.99
   Consequently, war in the east underwent ‘privatisation’ just as in Cas-
tile. The industrious small town of Valls was ruined when local Carlists
set up a toll gate along the main road leading to the town.100 When
Tristany, Catalonia’s principal Carlist guerrilla leader, saw a royal envoy
from the Basque country pay villagers for some supplies, his remark
showed surprise: ‘Well, I simply take the best of what they have and
pay them with my best wishes.’101 In western Catalonia, where Carlist
control by this time was virtually complete, a two-tiered requisitioning
system operated. Families of known royalist or apolitical backgrounds
were compensated in full and those with Liberal backgrounds, generally
with family members serving in the National Militia, were robbed.102
The privatisation of warfare was sometimes mitigated by community
solidarity. In March 1837, the southernmost Carlist guerrilla leader oper-
ating in the east, Forcadell, occupied Orihuela (Alicante), whose inhabi-
tants were divided between Cristino and Carlist sympathisers (including
its virtually Carlist bishop). Orihuela offered a potential civil war within
a civil war which had nonetheless remained peacefully coexistent until
Forcadell’s occupation. Ardent Liberals left for the safety of Cartagena
ahead of the occupation, but even the civic Carlists who remained
protested to Forcadell about one of his captives, a constitutional judge
of nearby Almansa, Juan Pablo Clemente, a popular local ﬁgure whom
Forcadell wanted to execute. But the judge’s extensive friendship circle
prevailed upon Forcadell to revoke the sentence, even while Orihuela
itself turned Carlist, as some 500 draftees previously earmarked for
                                                Carlist Failure, 1837–39   181


Cristino conscription enlisted in Forcadell’s force instead. Civil wars fos-
ter virtuous community cohesion as well as vicious denunciations. The
small towns and villages that comprised most Spanish demography cre-
ated networks of social interaction which at their best forged solidarity
and at their worst terror and denunciation. Forcadell told Clemente: ‘if
I had been in your power I’m sure I would be dead, but I grant you your
freedom in recognition of the good you have done to others’.103
   Local mercies, however, barely altered the general terror of warfare
in a region without the Eliot Treaty. In early February 1837, a Carlist
detachment surprised a platoon-sized detachment of Cristino mobile
militia which had been planning to intercept Carlists at San Pedro de
Torelló. Instead, a rapid deployment of Carlist cavalry surprised the
Cristinos, who ﬂed and barricaded themselves in a house where they
refused to surrender and were instead all burnt alive.104 On 18 February,
a particularly gruesome tragedy overtook a sharpshooter column from
Málaga, that southern bastion of radical liberalism which immortalised
its victims as martyrs to liberty. Tristany surprised the column whose
commander, Colonel Francisco Antonio de Oliver, was cut down after
his men refused to follow his death-or-glory charge against the sur-
rounding Carlists. The prisoners, however, fared even worse. Some
280–300 were executed in cold blood, whilst one of their hapless num-
ber managed to take refuge behind a church altar. Although Tristany’s
men searched the building he managed to escape, naked and starv-
ing, to the Cristino stronghold of Calaf.105 But the terror did not end
there. Calaf soon fell to Tristany, some 70 of its houses being burnt and
several wives of militiamen murdered. Tristany was halted at Solsona,
whose besieged defenders underwent the most signal trials (including
the drinking of urine, whose saltiness was extracted by an apothecarist),
and were indemniﬁed by a special Cortes motion.106
   The passage of the Royal Expedition through Catalonia convinced
the King of the need to regularise the chaotic war in the east, so he
made the controversial appointment on 27 June 1837 of an outsider,
the 34-year-old Guipúzcoan, Antonio Urbiztondo, as the Comandante
General of the Carlist Army of Catalonia. A month later, the town of
Berga fell to the Carlists, and this became the seat of the Junta of Berga,
a civilian council which, like its Maestrazgo counterpart to the south,
could not assert itself against Carlist martial law. Whereas Urbiztondo’s
campaign against Cristino fortiﬁed centres bore fruit in the summer –
Gironella and Ripoll fell soon after Berga – his Basque suppression of
Catalan wrongdoing was a controversial centralisation of power over
the bandit-guerrillas (latrofacciosos) and exterminating angels who had
characterised the Catalan far west hitherto.107 The Royal Government
182 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


agreed that regularisation was necessary. In February 1837, the Carlist
representative in Paris, Pedro Gómez Labrador, made his King aware of
the damage Catalan border violations and terrorism was doing both to
Spain’s richest province and to support for the cause amongst legitimists
in France:108

   The letters of very sensible and royalist people leave no doubt of
   the frightful excesses being committed in Catalonia at the hands
   of guerrillas who call themselves ‘Carlists’, because in reality they
   are nothing more than bandits laying the country waste, rustling
   entire ﬂocks, even those belonging to respected local Carlists, looting
   country houses and farmsteads, levying ruinous contributions, seiz-
   ing hostages amongst those who cannot pay and ransoming them for
   exorbitant sums. Many country houses are now closed and the inhab-
   itants of small villages have ﬂed for the safety of forts and fortiﬁed
   places, preferring to live amongst usurping Liberals than to lay them-
   selves open to the constant robberies and violence of the so-called
   defenders of throne and altar. It is imperative that the strictest orders
   be given to our commanders in Catalonia that they impose military
   discipline on those bands of rogues who are laying to waste the most
   industrious province of Spain.

Urbiztondo’s imposition of military discipline was difﬁcult enough.
A biscuit-store bound for Carlist soldiers protecting the Junta of Berga
was violently robbed en route. Battalion commander, Pau Mañó, had
been conducting a private war abducting and extorting local civilians,
imprisoning them in caves to be ransomed. The money families had to
pay for ransom depleted available resources for the Carlist war effort.
Urbiztondo’s siege of Pont d’Armentera was delayed by his need to dis-
cipline a battalion whose ofﬁcers had absconded to sell looted goods
and whose leaderless men had broken ranks.109 But the opposition
of respectable Catalan Carlists made Urbiztondo’s mission impossible.
When he quartered a new, locally raised battalion from the Mondoñedo
area in the monastery of San Francisco (itself inhabited by only 14 friars
and therefore targeted for dissolution by the Cristinos), the men started
stripping an organ to be melted down to make bullets. The Bishop
of Mondoñedo protested, claiming that Don Carlos never stooped so
low in the Basque country and that there was no Papal approval for
such drastic war measures. Urbiztondo, exasperated by Catalan bandit-
priests, replied that he would strip churches of all metal and ‘even force
the slippers off his Holiness if he thought they were made of lead’.
                                                Carlist Failure, 1837–39   183


But Mondoñedo, despite his episcopacy, was a powerful virtual Carlist
whose hitherto quiet hopes for a Carlist victory would soon explode
into a vehement sermon against the Cristino regime and its ‘war against
religion and its opinion’, turning him into a schismatic like León. Mon-
doñedo’s authority proved greater than Urbiztondo’s and the military
power lost to the religious, the troops being billeted elsewhere.110
   The onward march of the Royal Expedition denuded Urbiztondo of
forces and this soon began to tell as the Cristino counter-insurgency
initiative passed to Cristino Captain-General Meer from August 1837.
Urbiztondo’s defeat at San Juan de las Abadesas forced him to do what
he least desired, order his men to change their shirts and resort to guer-
rilla warfare. He complained to Berga at its deliberate neglect of his men,
who had been reduced to scavenging potatoes, but the junta was con-
temptuous in its reply and had in any case been lobbying Don Carlos
for Urbiztondo’s removal. Intelligence reached Urbiztondo of a plot to
kill him, and the junta got the King to promote Urbiztondo’s subordi-
nate, Benito Tristany, a Catalan exterminating angel, to command the
best local forces (his mountain corps). Finally, on 14 November, Berga
presented Urbiztondo with Don Carlos’s note dismissing him. This pro-
duced a stormy junta session from which the deposed comandante was
lucky to escape to Andorra with his life. He was followed into exile by
the Carlist intendant, whose unpopular policing of Carlist mountain vil-
lages, suppression of desertion and heavy taxation of the clergy had also
subjected him to death threats.111 The Barcelona press rejoiced: ‘Berga
is a veritable Babylon, and Urbiztondo has failed in his mission. We are
doing well, very well.’112
   The Urbiztondo controversy had overshadowed the division of the
Junta of Berga, between an aristocrática faction (Catalan nobility led by
Count Fonollar) and universitaria faction (comprising clerics expelled
from the Cristino-occupied University of Cervera). The clerics were
the most intransigent, but the aristocrats had been happy to fol-
low their lead over Urbiztondo, and to persuade Don Carlos to pro-
vide José Segarra as a more amenable replacement comandante. But
Segarra’s command was marred by a vigorous counter-insurgency by
Cristino Captain-General Meer, who reconquered Ripoll and boxed
the Carlists into unyielding mountain areas. By March 1838, Meer’s
counter-insurgency had borne fruit: the Carlist forces had shrunk from
some 13,000 to 7,000.113 Thus Segarra’s command was exposed as
incompetent, and he was replaced in July 1838 by a greater ﬁgure,
Count España, a legitimist of French origin who had been purged from
Catalonia’s captaincy-general in 1832. The Berga aristocrats hoped that
184 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


‘Count’ España would be their ally, but in fact he silenced them ﬁrst, and
most of their junta members either ﬂed to France or remained to rubber-
stamp military policy. Then España turned against the universitarios, but
not before he had met, and exceeded, their demands for the most dra-
conian military and most reactionary cultural policy.114 In the latter
regard, for example, España showed particular contempt for ‘geographi-
cally Cristino’ priests. The predicament of the Priest of Balsareny showed
the dangers facing clerics near front lines who made accommodations
with the ‘usurping’ Cristinos. Balsareny was strongly garrisoned enough
to make its priest feel safe from Carlist reprisals by both complying with
the Cristino abolition of the tithe (since 1837), and, consequently, refus-
ing to hand over these monies to the Carlists, thereby breaking with
this wartime tradition of covert clerical ﬁnancing of Carlists in front-
line areas. But one day, the priest went to visit a neighbouring priest on
the occasion of a religious feast. The latter had him seized and carried
into Carlist territory where España set him before an ecclesiastical tri-
bunal. On España’s instructions, the local Carlist paper Restaurador spun
this story in a dastardly fashion. The newspaper reported that the priest
had ﬂed Balsareny to reach loyal territory where he altruistically handed
over not only the tithe but also other donations as well. This marked the
man as a true Carlist. España was unmoved by the fact that this made
the poor fellow a marked man, as the Cristinos would doubtless tar-
get him for his ‘betrayal’. But España was unmoved: ‘a revolutionary
cleric is either a madman or a monster who deserves neither pity nor
sympathy’.115
   España’s military ruthlessness carved out for him the title ‘Tiger’ in
the enemy press, a title hitherto used only to describe Cabrera further
south.116 His Catalan soldiers nicknamed him the ‘decapitator’ (Trenca-
caps), and the count was famed for keeping a gallows outside his sleeping
quarters that was always busy hanging some unfortunate victim of his
wrath. España perfected the role of exterminating angel, renaming all
battalions as ‘Catholic and Royal’ (deliberately imitating the counter-
revolutionary Vendée), marrying it – during 1838 at least – with the role
of successful military strategist.117
   Whereas the Carlist east reached its apogee, the cradle of Carlism,
the Basque country, never escaped from the stagnation caused by the
failed Royal Expedition. Nothing more imaginative than yet another
expedition was proposed late in 1837. Even though the expedition led
by Basilio Antonio García on 28 December 1837 succeeded in break-
ing into Castile, it did not alter the Carlists’ stagnation. Furthermore,
weary Cristino civilians proved both far less willing to collaborate with
                                                Carlist Failure, 1837–39   185


yet another Carlist invasion and far more willing to oppose it. Two
graphic episodes illustrate how Cristino citizens had learnt from bitter
years of war to mobilise popular resistance against invasion. García’s
Carlists passed through the eastern Andalucían village of Nerpio on
13 February 1838; the village’s resentful inhabitants were rallied by
the local sheriff and murdered some stragglers who had fallen behind
the invaders’ rearguard. But Basilio, unaware of this atrocity, returned
Nerpio whose inhabitants, fearing the worst, deserted their village en
masse. The Carlists did not suspect anything sinister until the bloodied
clothes and powder of their unfortunate comrades were found in the
loft of the sheriff’s house. Outraged, the expedition burnt the house,
and then the whole village, to the ground. Whilst other villages did
not fare so badly, they nonetheless experienced routine ransacking and
wastage: whatever was not useful to the Carlists was destroyed.118 And
even innocent villages sometimes paid the ultimate price for resistance.
When the expedition swung north into New Castile it made an example
of Calzada de Calatrava. Some 166 of its inhabitants – militiamen and
their women and children – were either burnt or crushed to death after
the church they had fortiﬁed was set on ﬁre, weakening its roof, which
collapsed onto the defenders. This catastrophe was made possible by the
defection to the invaders of the local prior. When Army of the Centre
Commander-in-Chief Narváez arrived to liberate Calzada, he bowed to
popular pressure and had the cleric shot.119
  The Basque war effort never recovered from the defeat of the Royal
Expedition. The Royal Police were gone, but the Royal Police state
remained and became more irksome on a population exhausted by the
war. A law passed on 22 January 1838 banned any interaction by any
persons other than designated men with Cristino prisoners-of-war. This
was very hard on families caught between lines and sides. On 19 Febru-
ary 1838, a quarantine was placed on all foreign and Cristino vessels
trying to enter Carlist ports. The ﬁnancial demands of war were exhaust-
ing even the stoic ability of the Basques to give. Total Carlist tax
revenue raised in Navarra, Guipúzcoa, Vizcaya and Alava in the year
up to November 1838 amounted to some 40,188,886 reales, plus some
3,400,000 reales raised at customs. But this 43,588,886 total fell short
of the 46,355,000 expenditure for the same period, leaving a deﬁcit of
2,766,114 reales. This had to be bridged by yet more indirect taxes, ‘vol-
untary’ contributions from clerics, ﬁnes on Liberals and by grants from
foreign powers whose sympathy was wearing thin. In May, the King
of Sardinia promised some 800,000 francs to Carlist Spain, offering to
increase this donation if Russia, Prussia and Austria followed suit.120 But
186 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


the chronic shoestring nature of Carlist ﬁnances gave a keen edge to the
war-weariness and outright conspiracies which grew in the north dur-
ing 1838. Sometimes Don Carlos managed to ride out this disaffection
by judicious changes in policy. In March 1838, the Bishop of León suc-
cessfully prevailed upon his King to dismiss the unpopular José Arias
Teijeiro y Correa (who had always been regarded as a vulgar and brutal
upstart) as his War Minister and to have him replaced by friends of the
popular Prince Sebastián. But other conspiracies, whipping up hatred of
the ojalateros as a convenient vehicle for protest, grew in frequency and,
as the Muñagorri phenomenon would show, began to break with the
Carlist regime.121
   Despite this strategic and political malaise, in early 1838 yet another
expedition invaded Castile, led by one of the most controversial ﬁgures
in Carlist politics. Count Negri was a middle-aged Peninsular and
Royalist War veteran and twice-fugitive from Cristino captivity. His skills
as a courtier won him promotion and the scorn of fellow Carlist diplo-
mats like Marquess Labrador who judged: ‘chamberlains, chaplains and
lawyers should stick to their assigned duties and keep out of the affairs
of military strategy and commanding armies’.122 Negri’s incompetence
in setting out with a badly provisioned force across unyielding rocky ter-
rain led one later, admittedly biased, anti-Marotista (Negri was Maroto’s
protégé) traditionalist to suspect that the whole operation was designed
to sabotage the Carlist cause.123
   Negri’s expedition during early 1838 was poorly provisioned even
from the outset. It marched across the Ebro and around northern Spain,
including a westward march towards Galicia, and then south-east via
Burgo de Osma and Segovia towards La Granja. Strategic assistance came
from Merino in the mountains around Burgos and from Basilio’s expedi-
tion in La Mancha, both of whom diverted Cristino troops. Yet Espartero
was hard on Negri’s heels, not allowing him time to regroup or take
proper rations. Negri’s men went months without pay. A modest victory
at Bendejo strengthened Negri’s hand in negotiating prisoners’ treat-
ment with the enemy, and the Eliot Treaty was agreed to be respected
by both sides during the campaign. Meanwhile, Negri went about the
usual Carlist invasion tactic of setting up improvised Carlist juntas in
the settlements he occupied, disarming the National Militia and taking
on recruits who proved to be more of a burden than anything else. Once
again, some communities ‘managed’ Carlist occupations: for example,
by town-hall delegations riding out to hand over the ‘keys’ to their
towns. Other settlements barricaded themselves in defensible points,
such as the army and militia garrison of Burgo de Osma, which held
out in the town’s fort and cathedral tower. Negri ‘poached’ conscripts
                                                Carlist Failure, 1837–39   187


for his own forces and set up workshops on the approach to Segovia
which employed craftsmen with the loot of surrounding villages to pro-
duce badly needed berets and clothes. Negri took Segovia where, like
Zaratiegui before him, he wooed Catholics by hearing a Palm Sunday
Mass and by issuing a 7 April 1838 proclamation to Castilians asking
them to renounce the usurping Cristinos and to rally to his side in the
name of religion.124
   This was clever propaganda for communities which had faced three
years of drastic secularisation measures tempered only by local inertia
and the incompetence of the Madrid regime. But the time for win-
ning over civilians’ religiosity was over. The latter were exhausted by
repeated demands and outrages from both sides, and were in no mood
to receive Espartero’s reprisals. Espartero, for his part, conducted his
most successful counter-insurgency campaign. Appalling weather and
lack of footwear burdened Negri’s march, his numbers dwindling by the
end of April 1838 to 1,800 infantry and 100 cavalry. Since having been
forced to quit Segovia on 10 April, Negri had not had a moment’s respite,
his forces at one point having to perform a 40-mile forced march. Set-
tlements in his path bolted their doors to his men and increasingly
offered resistance, such as the militiamen and peseteros of Aguilar de
Campo, who barricaded themselves on 25 April 1838, resisting Negri’s
Carlists until Espartero’s vanguard arrived. While Negri extricated his
precious artillery, he was less successful at stopping the desertion of his
men. Finally, on 27 April at Brújula, Espartero routed Negri. Espartero
attributed his triumph to ‘free men’ defeating the forces of despotism.
Even though Negri escaped with his bodyguard, and would ﬁght again
in the defence of Morella, this was the ﬁrst unambiguous defeat of a
Carlist expedition since the Royal Expedition. Cristino military morale,
now virtually personiﬁed by the ﬁgure of Espartero, was recovering.125
So was Cristino military materiel. The 163 higher ofﬁcers, 1,662 ofﬁcers,
23,426 troops, 654 cavalry and over 1,000 artillery ofﬁcers and men
counted by the Carlist Royal Army in the Basque country in September
1838 were outnumbered three times by the Cristino forces in theatre.126
Moreover, the Cristino arms production, hitherto massively dependent
upon French and, especially, British factories, promised to improve
markedly when cannon production from foundries relocated from the
Basque country to Seville ﬁnally got underway in early 1839.127
   The Carlists, by contrast, faced a bleak future. Ever since the defeat of
the Royal Expedition, hostility towards Don Carlos in Carlist ranks had
passed its tipping-point. In particular, the King’s treatment of Prince
Sebastián, the hero of Oriamendi, rankled (a ﬂeeting act of insubordi-
nation had been turned by the apostólicos into grounds for dismissing
188 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


him); equally so did the imprisonments and suspended death sentences
handed down to such popular leaders as Zaratiegui and Elío. The latter
two were championed by a mutiny by troops in the Carlist capital, dur-
ing which demands for their release and the removal of Don Carlos were
uttered. The ringleader, a certain Lieutenant-Colonel Urra, was executed,
but the signs were clear that a civil war was starting to engulf Carlist
ranks from within.128 Manuel Lassala, who, since the July 1836 revolu-
tion had been the secretary to the provincial deputation of Zaragoza,
leading the city’s defence in 1838 against Cabañero’s Carlists, was well
placed to judge the mood of Carlists in the surrounding countryside.129
He observed the growing inﬂuence of Carlist transaccionismo, or maro-
tismo, dynastically loyal enlightened absolutism hostile to the theocratic
‘brutes’, or apostólicos, and Basque fueristas who dominated the ranks
of non-Castilian Carlism. The loose bag of absolutists, foralists and
medievalists of 1833 was splitting into an absolutist and apostólico wing,
with Basque foralists between the two factions. For the moment, the
dynastic loyalty to the person of Don Carlos made both factions priori-
tise the war over their own divisions. But as the compromise peace offers
from the Cristinos improved, Carlism’s last commander-in-chief, the
absolutist, Rafael Maroto, would make peace over the heads of his King
and his theocratic entourage. Don Carlos, after all, had become at best a
mere cipher and at worst the object of ridicule. His time-wasting Masses
during the Royal Expedition, his ﬂat refusal even to consider recognis-
ing Cristino debts and his insistence on sharing the military ‘advice’ he
regularly received from a nun all made the marotista faction see him as
a liability.130 The increasing extremism of the Carlist Royal Government
also sapped the willingness of Basque villagers to sustain the ﬁght. The
prohibition placed on ofﬁcers and men marrying any woman with rel-
atives who were Cristinos was particularly resented, as was the clique
of religious extremists who ‘worked towards’ the King’s poorly articu-
lated will by launching ill-conceived expeditions and leading cultural
crackdowns. Manuel Lassala observed that a growing clamour rose for
moderation on the Carlist side:131

   Don Carlos was too fanatical, bloody and superstitious. True Carlists
   did not volunteer for this, and did not mean to sacriﬁce their lives
   and goods in order to hand over Spain to the hangman and the
   backward times of Philip I. Those were Carlists never worthy of the
   name; no, a thousand times, no! – supposing all Carlists wanted to
   reignite the bonﬁres of the Inquisition is as unjust as supposing that
   all Liberals were partisans of the guillotine and lamp-post lynchings.
                                                Carlist Failure, 1837–39   189


   This grassroots discontent could not fail to play out on the battle-
ﬁeld. The Madrid government, thanks to growing Carlist defection to
the Cristinos and to the cross-border intelligence networks of Spymaster
Aviraneta, was keenly aware of the divisions in Carlist ranks and sought
to exploit them. José Antonio de Muñagorri, a native of Verástegui
(Alava), began the war as the backbone of Carlist war-related industry,
owning six factories in Guipúzcoa and Navarra. He has been repre-
sented as a treacherous buffoon in traditionalist scholarship.132 After
his repeated petitions to the Carlist court for a compromise peace fell
on deaf ears, he emigrated to the French side of the Pyrenees where
he contacted Cristino agents and forged a plan to raise a guerrilla
force of Spanish Basques. By November 1837, Muñagorri had become
a salaried Cristino ofﬁcer, and on 18 April 1838 he raised his standard in
Verástegui calling for peace and fueros (paz y fueros), designed to divide
the fuerista Basques against the transaccionista Castilians. Muñagorri’s
gambit reﬂected the growing willingness of both many fueristas and
many disenchanted absolutists (who would soon be captained by
Maroto) to forge a compromise peace sacriﬁcing Don Carlos in return for
Basque autonomy.133 In fact, Muñagorri’s military operations proved to
be tragicomic, and Cristino generals were so vexed by the desertion his
‘free corps’ attracted from conscripts that they refused to cooperate with
him, and his whole force was interned in France the following year.134
   The sudden Cristino rediscovery of the Basque fueros was as much
for its own security as for the division of the enemy. On 27 May 1838,
the Cristino free corps in Pamplona rose in rebellion, killing General
Sarsﬁeld and Colonel Mendívil, and occupying the city for more than a
month before Espartero’s paciﬁcation action.135 The motives for this ris-
ing betrayed the divided loyalties of Basques serving the Cristino cause:
for its leaders, the Peninsular War guerrilla veteran, Colonel León Iriarte,
and Comandante Pablo Barricart, were motivated by the anti-foralismo
of Madrid, and proclaimed the independence of Navarra.136 But the
fueros were still peripheral to Cristino strategy, whereas for the Carlists,
except in Aragón and the Catalan far west, they were part of its termi-
nal crisis. An unlikely understanding of two Ayacucho veterans, now
opposing one another as rival commanders-in-chief, would soon bring
the war to an end over the heads of crowned princes and even elected
governments.
8
Stalemate and Cristino Victory,
1838–40




The defeat of the Royal Expedition passed the strategic initiative to the
Cristino army. Whilst Espartero’s Army of the North tightened the noose
around the Basque country in the north, Narváez’s Army of Reserve
suppressed both remaining Carlist pockets and radical liberalism in
the south. Cristino tactics, strategy and supplies improved. States of
siege were imposed in a growing number of Cristino provincial capi-
tals, Revolutionary Militia units were purged, militarised or disbanded.
The moderado government (elected in 1837) justiﬁed counter-revolution
in terms of the Carlist threat, eroding constitutional liberties and
governance in order to streamline the war effort. But the progresista
opposition rallied by mobilising disbanded militia, press campaigns and,
above all, their resilient power bases of popularly elected local govern-
ment. They also enlisted the support of the Cristino war hero, General
Espartero. Espartero brokered a victorious peace settlement with his
Carlist opposite number, Rafael Maroto, which outlawed Don Carlos,
allowed Carlists to join Cristino ranks, and promised that the Basque
ancient laws (fueros) would be respected. Rafael Maroto’s command of
the Carlist army from June 1838 excluded the Apostólico faction from
power, culminating in his execution of several of their leading ﬁgures,
and prepared the way for a compromise peace with the Cristinos. The
August 1839 Treaty of Vergara was negotiated by commanders-in-chief
from both sides, demonstrating the degree to which the civil war had
emasculated civilian authority. It orphaned Cabrera’s achievements in
the east, whence the following year he left for exile, ending the First
Carlist War.
   The year 1838 saw the Madrid Cortes conduct debates about how to
end the conﬂict. Whereas moderados proved willing to entertain some
sort of negotiated peace on Cristino dynastic terms – a ‘transaction’ –

                                  190
                                  Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   191


progresistas argued that both national honour and revolutionary logic
demanded unconditional victory. Moderado Finance Minister, Alejandro
Mon, complained about San Miguel’s appeal for yet another levy of
100,000 men, as it was clear that the previous batch had largely gone
over to the facciosos. In the end, it was agreed that a quinta of 40,000
men be added to the nominal Cristino strength of 207,000 infantry
and 14,000 cavalry (a number which casualties and desertions in fact
rendered much lower). Progresista San Miguel observed that only a war
to the death could end civil wars, transacción being a canard; he was
answered by Toreno that history proved the opposite. San Miguel coun-
tered that he meant that only Carlist idea could be crushed forever, not
its defenders. The rareﬁed nature of Spanish Cortes debates, involving
Europe’s most elegant political addresses, was almost obscenely divorced
from reality. This was symbolised by Cristino Spain’s last prime minister
to have commanded a civilian power base (juntas, militia), Mendizábal,
who grew tired of the complaints of senior army ofﬁcers in the wake
of the La Granja revolution. His Cortes speech that ‘each ofﬁcer had a
girdle of gold’ which they might divert from their pleasures to pay their
men, following the brazen claim in the same speech that no Cristino
units were owed more than four months’ pay arrears, was met with rage
by the ofﬁcer corps and demands for personal satisfaction.1 Leftist politi-
cians could not see beyond their ingrained anti-militarism: Mendizábal
answered Espartero’s complaints in a tactless fashion, implying soldiers
were little more than beasts of burden (‘mercenaries’, as Argüelles had
slandered them in 1813) and that Espartero’s soldiers had consumed
40,000 rations per day for ten weeks after his relief of Bilbao even as his
army stood idle.2
   With the crucial exception of Espartero, most senior Cristino ofﬁcers
were aligned with the authoritarian moderados, especially as this fac-
tion endorsed the tendency of regional captain-generals, after 1837, to
subordinate National Militia rivals to army power, usually through the
imposition of states of siege. Yet the Liberal parties had limited impact
on the civil war: indeed, despite some consistent constitutional disagree-
ments (suffrage, municipalities, prerogatives of the Crown), the Liberal
parties themselves barely amounted to more than patronage networks
held together by subscriptions to rival newspapers and occasional ral-
lies disguised as banquets, and as such they rode rather than made the
waves of the civil war. Rather, the last three years of the war saw the
militarisation of the politics of both ‘home fronts’, most enduringly
in the victorious side of Cristino Spain, where the years 1837–40 have
been seen as the advent of praetorian politics in Spain.3 On the one
192 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


hand, there was a participatory dimension to praetorianism as soldiers
and – in the case of the underpaid Cristino naval base at Cartagena –
sailors were paid to ‘ﬁx’ election results to favour the moderados (pro-
gresistas during 1835–37 had done the same using militiamen), either
by being unconstitutionally enrolled in the electoral roll, or by being
deployed to exercise a range of ‘moral’ as well as violent pressure. The
phenomenon of soldiers making and breaking elections, which Romero
Alpuente denounced during the Triennium, was repeated during the
Carlist War on a greater scale.4 The praetorian domination of minis-
terial politics turned Spain under Espartero at the end of the war into
the exact opposite of what Cea Bermúdez, Spain’s last civilian absolutist
prime minister at the start of the war, had desired (Table 8.1).
   Whereas the ministerial politicisation of Spain’s hundreds of thou-
sands of serving soldiers was a dramatic consequence of the Carlist War,
its signiﬁcance was pedestrian in comparison to the two other polit-
ical legacies of the conﬂict. The ﬁrst legacy was the rise to power of
a Bonapartist ‘people’s choice’, General Espartero, at the end of the
war, for reasons which were almost entirely to do with the conﬂict
itself rather than structural shifts. The second legacy both accounted for
the ﬁrst (popular support for the ‘peace dividend’ revolution of 1840)
and stood apart, sometimes opposed, to it, namely the radicalisation of
popular, non-ministerial politics (economic militancy and support for
double regime radicalism) which, again, was either caused or at least
shaped by the war. None of this radicalisation assisted the formal war
aims of the Cristino faction, namely the national and international
recognition of the legitimacy of Princess Isabella’s succession and the
regency of her mother, and, as a corollary to this, the institution of a
constitutional civilian rule of law in Spain. Rather seven long years of
war had changed its popular meaning from a dynastic (and consubstan-
tially, religious) dispute, into a war in which hundreds of thousands of
soldiers and militiamen, and hundreds of thousands of civilians besides,
had been schooled by violence into exercising political consciousness
for the ﬁrst time. In the previous chapters we have explored this devel-
opment largely in the context of mobilisations, battles, sieges, invasions
and occupations. But the war also caused, or at least inﬂuenced, social
protests and class struggles in a manner poorly explored by the existing
historiography.
   Some of this radicalisation may be linked to the highbrow concerns
of the Cortes with regard to the virtual bankruptcy caused by the war.
Mon’s despair at the state of Cristino ﬁnances exposed him to ridicule
when he was taken in by a Swiss adventurer that some treasure allegedly
buried in Santiago in 1809 could be unearthed. Excavations were begun,

                                                                                                                                                                   193
194 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


but all that was uncovered were the latrines of the San Roque hospi-
tal, which stank out the unfortunate town.5 Cristino Spain’s foreign
policy during 1838 was dominated by its ﬁnancial crisis. Government
expenditure exceeded receipts by almost double: 838 million reales were
received by the Treasury against outgoings of 1,546 million. Seeing the
ﬁnancial plight, the British minister, Villiers, again pressed a plan which
he had been formulating for four years, namely an Anglo-Spanish free
trade treaty. Britain would lend Spain money in return for the sus-
pension of import duties on cotton. When this proposal reached the
Cortes it was bitterly opposed and defeated, Catalan deputies and sen-
ators leading the resistance.6 Catalonia’s cotton industry was advanced
by Spanish standards but could not possibly hope to compete in a free-
trade environment with that of Britain. Catalan cotton magnates thus
acted as good Spanish nationalists, and a certain strand of Anglophobia
was revived amongst the otherwise generally Anglophile progresistas.
More radical-republican secret societies in Barcelona turned the protec-
tion of ‘national industry’ into doctrine. When, soon after the civil war,
Espartero signed the free trade treaty, the republican ‘Society of Spanish
Regenerators’ alleged that the British want to ‘turn Iberia into a new
India, have no principles beyond their own egotism, and want to keep
us poor and ignorant so that we might work for them as slaves’.7
   An attempt by several moderado Cortes deputies to reintroduce the
tithe in order to assuage the crisis of secular clergy was blocked by
progresista deputies who were undoubtedly expressing the popular will
(which included episodes of popular violence against tithe-proctors,
especially in the Liberal south and east).8 The Count of Ofalia, moderado
prime minister from December 1837 until September 1838, symbolised
the rightward shift in ministerial liberalism, not least for being ‘soft’ on
the Catholic Church.9 But the persistence of tithing was wormwood and
gall to a Cristino population which had come to see the ‘Carlist’ Church
as the source of its suffering. During the revolutionary summer of 1836,
as the Gómez expedition tore into southern Spain, tithe-proctors in the
province of Toledo were jeered at and assaulted, and local authorities
could not (or would not) offer them protection.10 When the central gov-
ernment sent troops, this only served to inﬂame radicalism even further.
Thus, General Peón’s use of force in collecting the culto y clero in the
province of Málaga during the summer of 1838 left bitter memories.11
The following year, citizens in this province mobilised in a mass tithe
strike, which began in Motril (Granada) before spreading to other pueb-
los. Central government despatched an infantry battalion to protect the
proctors but the strike held.12
                                  Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   195


   The broadest and deepest networks of popular radicalism had evolved
in Liberal port-cities, in particular, Barcelona, Málaga and Cádiz.13 Meer’s
crackdown on radicalism in Barcelona was mirrored elsewhere on the
Cristino home front in accordance with the moderado election victory
