of 1837, by disarming and reforming the militia of Barcelona, Reus
and Tarragona.14 Meer’s successful counter-insurgency against Segarra’s
forces, including his capture of Solsona on 27 July 1838, earned him
political capital to suppress the Radicals.15 The Radical press cast the
moderado Catalan as an honorary member of the ‘Angels of Andalucía’,
the satirical title given to dictatorial captain-generals who imposed mar-
tial law in provincial capitals. Meer imposed a 10,000-reales bond on
all people in Barcelona who could not satisfy the authorities of the
reasons for their stay. The city was full of secret societies and mainly
French expatriate Radicals.16 But the crackdowns by Meer, and other
moderado captain-generals were limited in their effectiveness, for the
progresista press campaign of the late 1830s made heroes out of those
Radicals imprisoned and deported to the garrison colonies in Spanish
North Africa.17
   In Málaga the moderados imposed a state of siege in response to local
authorities’ refusal to cooperate in the judicial investigation into Don-
adío’s and SanJust’s assassinations. Málaga occupied a strategically vital
position: it triangulated Cristino control over the only enduring Carlist
insurgency in Andalucía, the Serranía de Ronda to the west, and its role
as a port grew in importance from 1838 as a series of Carlist prison-
ers’ insurrections in the North African garrison colonies made the city
the staging point for punitive expeditions. Certainly, its importance was
appreciated by allied forces: in October 1836 the commander of a Royal
Navy ship stationed in the port threatened to suspend all British aid
deliveries unless the ‘revolutionary chaos’ came to a halt.18 The risings
of the Militia in 1835 and 1836, as we have seen, started here (whilst
Málaga would either be the ﬁrst, or one of the ﬁrst, provinces to spawn
future revolutions in 1840, 1843, 1854 and 1868).19 Between 1812 and
1835, the name of Málaga’s main square alternated between ‘Royal’ and
‘Constitution’ no fewer than six times. By 1835, the town decided to
save money on stonemasonry by leaving two plaques bearing the alter-
nate names at opposite corners of the square, in the hope that either
might serve the political demands of the day. During the 1836 rev-
olution, however, revolutionaries destroyed the ‘Royal’ plaque and it
was never reinstated.20 Málaga’s radical liberalism can be explained by
several factors. Its modest iron-and-steel industry expanded through-
out the 1830s, to make the city second only to Barcelona in industrial
196 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


importance by 1850, yet with classically Liberal free-trade elites instead
of Catalonia’s protectionists. Málaga, during the Carlist War, was the
only Cristino port to register a positive trade balance with the out-
side world, even though this success was tempered by a deﬁcit in
Spanish cabotage and by rampant smuggling, which was afforded local
protection.21 If statistics could have computed the activity of the so-
called mochileros (‘backpackers’), the smugglers who moved contraband
from Gibraltar via the lawless Serranía de Ronda, then Málaga would
have conformed more closely to the Spanish trade deﬁcit.22
   It is hard to explain much of Málaga’s radicalism because its exas-
perated governors did not risk committing their intelligence to paper.
In March 1838 Captain-General Palarea wrote to Madrid saying that he
was successfully penetrating the ‘juntas and afﬁliations of Andalucía’
but the delicate nature of this task obliged him to keep most details
secret.23 That said, a great part of the radicalism was certainly an expres-
sion of the elaborate local economic interests arising from support for
free trade. In 1837 the Secretary of the Treasury professed his disgust
at the fact that only 5 per cent of the contribution owed by Málaga
to help fund the costs of the central government had so far been paid,
an unpatriotic meanness all the more outrageous because ‘[Málaga] has
swamped the nation with illicit goods which have considerably reduced
tax receipts’.24 Whenever carabineers tried to impound contraband, they
were met with all kinds of resistance. In October 1839, vendors went on
strike in protest at a violent anti-contraband operation in one of the
main shopping streets, which forced the head of carabineers to resign.25
Ofﬁcial protection for contraband was matched by that for political
extremism. The moderado state of siege here was the least effective of
all the provincial capitals. The fact that the Militia used the occasion of
the Queen-Regent’s birthday in 1839 to hold political meetings and to
publish satires which ‘scandalously abused the precious right of the free
press’ suggests that the citizens’ force was not cowed as in other Cristino
cities.26 Military governors frequently resigned, sometimes in response
to death threats,27 and always under the relentless pressure of progresista
newspapers which used Málaga as proof that the moderados were more
interested in persecuting their political enemies hundreds of miles from
the front lines than in vanquishing the mortal enemy of liberalism. The
shabby treatment of the widows of two murdered Radicals underlined
this charge.28 By October 1838, the ‘Widows of Comares’ campaign had
borne fruit as controversy forced the Military Governor of Málaga to end
the transportations of Radicals to African garrison prisons and to return
to liberty all those surviving prisoners.29
                                  Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   197


   It was one of the war’s peculiarities that desertion from Cristino ranks,
and subsequent banditry, continued at a high rate even in 1839 when
the Cristino side was in sight of victory.30 Some of this may be explained
by a growing pragmatic indulgence shown by a victorious army, which,
during the revolutions and defeats years earlier, had been prone to more
condign punishment of offenders. Other explanations lie in the pop-
ular anger at the way that Church properties nationalised under the
revolution were not being parcelled out to Cristino veterans, as had
been proposed by some ‘social Liberals’, but were being auctioned off to
local oligarchs to remedy disastrous state ﬁnances. This ‘missed chance’
of popular landownership was explained by Marxists as a failed ‘bour-
geois revolution’. New property owners, who were few, unlike during
the French Revolution, were too weak to advance their ‘historic mission’
of advancing capitalism.31 This ‘failure’ school was partially revised by
post-Cold War scholarship, which presented a more optimistic, but still
mixed, picture of the Liberal property revolution.32 In fact, the 1830s
property revolution was hybrid in nature. Whereas several new buyers
did become property owners for the ﬁrst time, they generally did not
drive radical capitalism but were ‘feudalised’ by the social and cultural
hegemony of the landed nobility, even though the legal separateness of
the nobility had been abolished for good. The undynamic rentier land-
lord with strong contacts with local authorities was the social category
that beneﬁted most.33
   Of course, this property revolution had already been underway in ﬁts
and starts since the 1790s, with Jovellanos’s free-market philosophy,
Godoy’s conﬁscation of a portion of Church property a decade later,
and that of the Liberals in the early 1820s.34 But the greatest nineteenth-
century programme of land nationalisation in Spain happened during
Spain’s greatest civil war, when half-a-million Spaniards were under
arms, and when an 1839 Cortes motion to reward Carlist War veterans
with nationalised land was rejected by Liberal deputies who had spent
six years exhorting ‘the people’ to accept sacriﬁces in the ﬁght against
Carlism.35 Even veterans lucky enough to afford nationalised property
found the auctions rigged in favour of vested interests, as intendants
designated the lands to be offered for auction with the assistance of
local ‘experts’ who were usually the agents of existing landowners. And
the intendants themselves were easily bribed to rig auctions in advance,
despite the theoretical risk of sanctions against such abuses.36 The struc-
tural grievances of Spanish landownership were at their plainest in the
large estates in the south and south-west, which were worked by landless
and (controversially) largely Galician migrant labour, and the wartime
198 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


property revolution energised these like never before. A progresista elec-
tion campaign in Córdova six months after the end of the war went
awry when a hired mob of veterans ended up targeting migrant work-
ers instead of moderados. The veterans were reminded ‘not to hit the
Galicians’, but to attack ‘the fat people’.37
   The consubstantiation of war and revolution provoked economic mil-
itancy across the Cristino ‘home front’. Both desertion and banditry
remained high towards the end of the war as virtual reserve armies of
bandits roamed the Cristino countryside. The generalised states of siege
in Andalucía from 1837 allowed Captain-Generals to make short work
of bandits, especially whenever there was the slightest hint that the
bandits might in fact be ‘Carlists’ as well as mere criminals. Thus, the
Captain-General of Granada in November 1838 was glad to make public
proclamations of the execution of 13 insurgents who had been preying
on rural communities.38 Even though civil authorities were emascu-
lated under states of siege in matters of crime and punishment, by the
following spring the Civil Governor of Granada had concluded that
economic grievances were motivating at least some of this wrongdo-
ing. He parcelled out fallow municipal lands to landless labourers, in a
desperate bid to end the violent lawlessness around Guadix caused by
draft-dodgers and deserters.39
   In Cristino cities Spain’s female tobacco workers gained a reputation
for militancy. Smoking was a key part of Spanish popular culture: a for-
eign traveller was surprised to discover that even women smoked, and
that the only smoke-free places were the Cortes and the judicial bench.40
Spain pioneered cigarettes, displacing snuff and requiring dainty ﬁngers
for production. This changed the sex of the workforce as women work-
ers (known as tabacaleras or cigarreras) vastly outnumbered men workers
by the time of the Carlist War. Tobacco had long been a state monopoly
produced in factories at Madrid, Cádiz, Valencia, Barcelona, La Coruña,
Alicante and – the biggest – in Seville; all of these were in Cristino hands,
and the ﬁnished product reached the front line bearing such images of
Liberal patriotism as Espartero and Princess Isabella. Tobacco for troops
ranked in importance alongside food and munitions, which turned its
production into a patriotic and political matter, even more than it had
already been before the war (when the Jacobin, Romero Alpuente, had
campaigned to remove tax from tobacco).41 The war inﬂected grievances
about conditions and management of the state monopoly. When José
Laﬁta, a Cristino desk ofﬁcer with powerful connections to the govern-
ing moderado party, was appointed director of the Cádiz factory, he was
                                  Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   199


lampooned by the opposition Eco because ‘although he is completely
untested commanding men on the ﬁeld of battle, he may nonetheless
prove some use commanding women’.42 One hundred and ﬁfty male
snuffbreakers who had been conscripted into the Cristino army returned
to Seville after the war protesting their patriotic right to regain their
jobs. Insult was added to injury when the government offered them
alternative jobs in public works projects (which had been notorious for
employing Carlist prisoners).43
   The greatest wartime economic militancy came from the strikes and
smuggling of the tobacco women. Demands for increased wartime pro-
duction were worsening their working conditions which, in addition to
an eight-hour day, pensions and free overalls, had included the provi-
sion of crèches and wet nurses.44 On 2 August 1838, after suffering four
months’ pay arrears and deteriorating factory food, all 2,000 Seville taba-
caleras went on indeﬁnite strike. The male minority was co-opted into
joining the women, strike-breakers were barred, and the mayor received
a delegation of 20 tabacaleras. The captain-general, who feared the
impact of tobacco shortages on soldiers’ morale, intervened to source a
Chambers of Commerce loan to pay the backlog of wages. The captain-
general conceded this in bad grace, stressing that so many young men
were spilling their blood for liberty and the crown of Isabella whilst
silently suffering pay arrears (a grating and also inaccurate statement,
as at least one conscripted tobacco worker had recently been executed
for turning his musket on an ofﬁcer in the course of a pay dispute).45
Even though the women failed to get their secondary demands met
(removal of the 3-reales weekly levy for medical and burial insurance,
and the right to hold meetings), this ﬁrst great Seville strike passed
without further incident.46 Four more strikes would ensue in as many
years, the May 1842 strike being the most prolonged.47 In July 1839, the
Madrid tabacaleras launched a strike in protest at the continued humili-
ation of having to undergo a body search at the end of shifts. This time
the factory administrators enlisted 40 militiamen to lock the women in
the building, denying them food until they submitted. The authorities
were right to suspect smuggling, but the women did this via a tun-
nel to a secluded street outside. When this was discovered, two weeks
after the strike, the press condemned this ‘sabotage of the state coffers’
at a time when the war for liberty was still ongoing.48 Not only had
Carlist smuggling across the Pyrenees lengthened the war, but Cristino
civilians were doing the same by exercising their ‘moral economy’: in
October 1839, Málaga’s shop owners closed their premises in protest at
200 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


overzealous carabineers.49 Smuggling had long been a way of life along
Spain’s porous borders and coastline, practised in three ways.50 But the
civil war had politicised it like never before.
   The war also inﬂected Spain’s experience of the European trade slump
of 1839 and the default it made on National Debt interest repayments.51
Spain’s ﬁrst strike movement to be coordinated across industries was
broken by government charges of ‘Carlism’. The Cristino abolition in
1837 of the guild system had advantaged wholesalers, who ratcheted
up prices.52 This was answered in Granada by a movement led by a
certain Julián Malaguilla, silk worker artisan, who rallied 200–300 fel-
low silk workers to halt work and ransack the premises of wholesale
silk merchants who had hiked prices. The Militia was overwhelmed and
the city’s shoemakers joined the strike. Three companies of infantry
had to be drafted in to uphold the state of siege. But the strike held
as workshop masters were sent letters threatening them with 50 lashes
if they did not cease production. The movement collapsed only when
Malaguilla was tricked into arbitration at the city hall. He was arrested
and imprisoned upon charges of ‘Carlism’ (letters were produced show-
ing Malaguilla’s appeal for volunteers to join insurgents in the restive
Serranía de Ronda).53 Madrid itself saw signiﬁcant strike action, and
from very proletarian quarters. The water-bearers (aguadores) of Madrid
were mostly Galician migrants, numbering some 900 in the capital in
1840.54 The luckiest of these water-bearers rented licences from the
municipal authorities to carry drinking water to Madrid’s private houses
and businesses; the less lucky might subcontract these licences or work
in small teams as journeymen for the licensee; whilst the least fortu-
nate would work the streets as water-vendors.55 As municipal licences
were hard to acquire, and as most of the water-bearing labour involved
seasonal migration, the luckiest aguadores had the opportunity to sell
their licences for inﬂated prices. By February 1840, it emerged that a
cadre of aguadores in Madrid was doing precisely that, the money being
found by raising the monthly fee levied on water delivery from 6 to 8
reales per household to 12. When consumers complained to the author-
ities, the municipality arrested some of the ‘usurers’ whilst enforcing a
cut in water levies. The response from the aguadores was to launch a
strike, which resulted in no water being carried anywhere in the capital
for a whole day. Militia were dispatched to force the aguadores back to
work. The authorities’ hard line was welcomed by several of the most
recent migrants, who had run the virtual blockade of Carlist banditry to
reach Madrid and had indebted themselves in order to subcontract the
licence.56
                                 Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   201


   Working-class protest came at a sensitive political time. The moderado
government was accused by Radicals of manipulating elections, and a
month after the aguadores’ strike, Fermín Caballero, editor of the Eco del
Comercio and leader of parliamentary progresistas led Olózaga and other
leftists in a walkout of the Cortes.57 By March 1840, Radical political
meetings were taking place in all the Andalucían provincial capitals, and
delegates were dispatched to Seville to deliberate upon a confederation
along the lines suggested by the martyred Catalan Republican, Xaudaró
y Fábregas.58
   Militancies in Cristino society grew while the most savage ﬁghting
shifted eastwards, away from the stagnation of the Basque country and
towards the Carlist ascendancy in Aragón and the Catalan far west. Such
was the pressure that, in February 1838, San Miguel decided to evacu-
ate the entire population of Gandesa rather than continue to waste lives
and supplies in its defence. Refugee civilians from Gandesa were housed
in Zaragoza and a subscription in their aid was opened in Madrid. The
civilians did not return to their destroyed homes until Cabrera left for
France in 1840.59 This Carlist success emboldened Cabrera to launch an
offensive against Zaragoza. Some 2,200 infantry, and 300 cavalry under
the command of the French Carlist, Lespinace, tried to surprise the city
on 5 March 1838. But Zaragoza resisted thanks to its garrison of National
Militia supported by some regular troops and even civilians armed with
such improvised weapons as burning oil and water in imitation of the
greater sieges of the Peninsular War. Carlist casualties were about 20
times those of the Cristinos, so a special medal was awarded to the city’s
National Militia and a laurel added to its emblem.60 Unlike Córdova
in 1836, Zaragoza in 1838 no longer had enough civilians prepared to
act as a ﬁfth column for the besiegers. The failed plot on 27 February
1834 to raise the city for Don Carlos had been answered by months of
ofﬁcial arrests and imprisonments and unofﬁcial anti-clerical outrages.61
By 1838, its streets were indisputably Cristino.
   Cristino operations in Aragón during 1838 were dominated by
attempts to conquer the virtually impregnable Carlist capital of Morella.
Oráa and San Miguel combined an offensive in July which was under-
supplied in food and artillery. The Carlists rounded up cattle in the
approaches to Morella and burnt the ripe corn harvest (both sides cus-
tomarily burnt crops on land that they did not expect to hold), thereby
burdening the Cristinos’ already shoestring supplies. Moreover, Morella
was constructed like a city-tower, its relief and architecture naturally
favouring the defenders. Between the mid-July start of the offensive and
Oráa’s order on 17 August 1838 to retreat, there were some 22 battles
202 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


and two failed Cristino attempts to storm Morella. Oráa was exhausted
by years of war and had long thought a military solution to the conﬂict
was impossible. Moreover, he had had to detach some forces to aid the
Prefect of Murcia to crush some revolutionary National Militia. An inter-
cepted letter revealed to the Carlists that Oráa’s colleagues thought him
old, vacillating, bereft of his earlier speed and unable to leave anything
to chance.62 Cabrera’s defensive victory was followed with an offen-
sive victory at the Battle of Maella against a division led by one of the
Cristino’s most popular generals, Pardiñas, whose élan contrasted with
Oráa’s circumspection. Yet Cabrera won the battle, killing Pardiñas in
combat and massacring most of the several hundred Cristino prisoners
taken.63
   Cabrera’s ascendancy in the Maestrazgo was secured. At the end of
1838 his army numbered some 15,255 infantry, 1,328 cavalry, and 378
artillerymen with some 40 guns. Moreover, Carlist artillery had been
improved by the Prussian Baron von Rahden, who had voluntarily quit
a post in the Basque provinces to come to the Maestrazgo for this
end. A grateful Cabrera made him comandante general.64 The brutal-
ity of Cabrera’s war effort continued to be a source for consternation for
Cristino Radicals:

   To understand the state of alarm and indignation which Liberals in
   Aragón and Valencia reached in the autumn of 1838, it is not enough
   to read the Madrid newspapers. Rather one has to have lived in those
   provinces in, or near, the Maestrazgo; to have heard the fathers, sons
   and brothers of the executed, to have seen the pathetic refugees lucky
   enough to have reached the safety of a fortress or column; to have
   conversed with the well-to-do who lost all their cattle, homes and
   livelihoods. One has to hear the stories of those who witnessed the
   killings and the threats made by Carlists who had done away with
   the lives of even the most distant relatives of the Liberals. If those
   lucky enough never to have experienced a civil war can understand
   this, they would not consider Cabrera a member of the human
   race.65

Towns and villages were being sacked. When Urrea near Zaragoza was
sacked, the smoke billowing from its ruins was visible in the Aragonese
capital, causing panic. Such brutality was defended on the self-fulﬁlling
grounds that residents were defending their properties by ﬁring on
the invaders from houses. A furious exchange of letters between San
Miguel and Cabrera did nothing to assuage the violence. Cabrera, as
                                  Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   203


ever, invoked the execution of his mother as proof that the usurpers had
no lessons to impart on humanitarianism, an attitude which had been
consistently held up in the Carlist press.66 The failed epistolary exchange
took place amidst an escalation of reprisals and counter-reprisals in the
east during late 1838. Oráa’s replacement Commander-in-Chief of the
Army of the Centre, Van-Halen, complained that the Carlists always
retained the upper hand as they could simply rob, kill and snatch
draftees at will. The sovereignty of local municipalities, meanwhile, tied
the hands of Cristino armies and made the ‘nuclear option’ of impos-
ing states of siege necessary not just because of the Carlists but because
of Liberal politics, too. A cycle of riots and assassinations in Valencia
and Zaragoza were directly related to Cabrera’s advances in the sur-
rounding countryside and atrocity stories emanating from them. The
worst ﬁghting and atrocities took place during the Carlist capture of
Caspe. Six Cristinos were shot in cold blood after the town’s capture
on 27 October: three soldiers from the Burgos regiment and three local
militiamen. In reprisal Van-Halen, bowing to the pressure of Cristino
opinion, executed eight Carlist prisoners.67
   Whilst the east went Carlist, Cristino Spain found the threat from the
north far more manageable than during the revolutionary summers of
1835–37. Carlist raids from the provinces during 1838 showed that the
headier days of 1836 and 1837 could no longer be repeated. The elderly
priest Merino, who had been present at the last siege of Bilbao, dur-
ing the Battle of Oriamendi and in the Royal Expedition of Don Carlos,
could not sustain his earlier prowess against stiffened Cristino opposi-
tion. Don Carlos had made him Carlist Captain-General of Old Castile
and the president of its junta, yet Merino’s actions were those of an
insurgent rather than a governor. In 1834 villagers had ﬂocked to him,
but in 1838 he abducted draftees from the countryside around Burgos
and Aranda del Duero and ransomed, even executed, the family mem-
bers of escapees.68 Merino joined forces with Cabrera in a raid during
the summer of 1838 which took Burgo de Osma, threatening Valladolid
on 8 September 1838, whence a prompt campaign by the city’s mobile
militia marched to pursue the Carlist raiders eastwards. Over the fol-
lowing days several villages were invaded. All tailors in Covarrubias
were forced to join the campaign in order to satisfy demands for uni-
forms. Merino was then pursued back across the Ebro. From there,
on the 12 October 1838, Maroto received desperate royal instructions
to order Merino to launch a new raid. But despite the usual speed
and ruthlessness of Merino’s men, they were matched by a Cristino
counter-insurgency equally ruthless with civilians in its path, and which
204 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


defeated Merino, inﬂicting some 800 losses on him. Merino retreated
and never took to the ﬁeld again. After Vergara settlement he joined
the exodus into France where he would become a minor celebrity on
account of his Peninsular War fame and reputation.69 Other minor raids
may have got the royal sanction but barely amounted to more than
organised crime. The raids of Carrión and Cellis are remembered mainly
because of their vindictive atrocities in the village of Villada (Palencia),
whose wealthy residents had recently welcomed a stay by Espartero. The
occupying Carlists imposed ruinous forced contributions on the munici-
pality and even killed the young wife of Lino de Cosío, a wealthy Liberal
landowner, who had only just given birth.70
   The era of Carlist expeditions had passed. The last one, during the
winter of 1839–40, came not from the Basque country (which had
surrendered) but as a desperate move from Cabrera in the east to over-
winter his troops on the food supplies of New Castile.71 It was not only
Carlist exhaustion and demoralisation which ended this offensive strat-
egy but also the decision of the moderado government in early 1838 to
form an established Army of the Reserve in Andalucía, under the com-
mand of General Narváez, who was tasked with both eradicating Carlist
bandit-insurgents and with bringing Radical cities to heel. Lengthy
states of siege were imposed in Málaga, Cádiz, Huelva and Córdova,
whilst Seville was the last provincial capital to follow this example after
a coup attempt in the city by Narváez himself backﬁred in November
1838, sending its instigator into exile in Gibraltar. Narváez had been
locked in rivalry with Espartero, but although he had the support of sev-
eral moderado deputies and ministers in the capital who wanted to see
him replace Espartero, the Queen-Regent, grateful for her rescue from
the Royal Expedition and despite her Generalísimo’s leftist politics, still
trusted Espartero. On 31 October 1838 Espartero wrote her a lengthy
letter that was devastatingly critical of Narváez, and riots followed in
Madrid. Pro-Narváez moderados fought pro-Espartero progresistas as pop-
ular proxies of praetorian politics, and the Radical Eco del Comercio dared
to call its readers ‘to arms, then, against the traitors’. A confused and
sometimes tragicomic few days followed when the new prefect of the
capital, Brigadier Puig, on 8 November 1838, decided to crack down on
the disturbances by ordering the arrest of all men with moustaches who
were not members of the army or Militia. In the end it suited both Lib-
eral factions to describe the disturbances as a Carlist plot, and many
civic Carlists still residing in the capital were arrested, a renewed round
of injustices ensuing only two weeks after the expulsion from the capital
of the wives and children of absent Carlists.72
                                  Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   205


   Defeated in the capital, Narváez joined with the former Commander-
in-Chief, Córdova, in seizing control of the Seville’s garrison and Militia,
and proclaiming a junta.73 But the Captain-General of Andalucía, the
Conde de Cleonard, condemned the rising from Cádiz and the Army
of Reserve failed to rally to its commander. Thus Narváez and Córdova
ﬂed into exile, their parliamentary immunity notwithstanding: Narváez
to Gibraltar and thence to France where he sustained a petitioning cam-
paign to the Cortes and his friend and owner of a moderado press empire,
Andrés Borrego.74 Espartero now reigned supreme, and the Army of the
Reserve – Narváez’s power base – was dissolved. Actually the humilia-
tion and banishment of Narváez would prove a pyrrhic victory. The man
from Loja had been made into a man who, like Louis Bonaparte, would
prove to have greater ambitions than careerism and personal rivalry.
   Narváez had been a visible threat to Espartero, but the last years of
the war saw Radical plots ﬂourish from lower ranks. In November 1838,
prisoners at the Spanish North African garrison at Alhucemas rose in
revolt, even though barely more than a quarter of the rebels were actu-
ally Carlists, pressed a claim to being Spanish ﬁrst before Liberal or
Carlist and promised to defend the colony against any Moroccan attack.
A similar Carlist takeover ensued at Melilla, before the insurgents agreed
to a negotiated peace and to being shipped back to the peninsula on
the condition that they would be free.75 A plot to assassinate Espartero
was centred on Manuel García Uzal, a Radical Cortes deputy, newspa-
per correspondent and, since 1835, a captain serving in a battalion of
‘guides’ (a species of elite unit generally associated with scouting activ-
ities), the fact that he was also battalion adjutant making him ideally
placed to radicalise the troops. Uzal forged a strange conspiracy between
his own circle and Carlist ﬁfth columnists (led by a writer called Vicente
Canseco), but the plot was penetrated by Espartero’s intelligence net-
works and its leaders imprisoned.76 A republican conspiracy in the army
just a few months earlier at León had petered out quickly. Its two cabecil-
las, the standard-bearer of an off-duty battalion, Francisco Doti, and a
lieutenant, Joaquín María Guisasola, seized a caravan of rations as bait
for the troops to second their calls of ‘Long Live the Constitution of
1812’ and ‘Long Live the Republic’. Even though this pronunciamiento
attracted a limited following, after three days, most had deserted, before
the 12 remaining revolutionaries were captured by loyal troops. Doti
and Guisasola were executed.77
   The war had created a radical constituency in the army and society
which had digested the constant diet of ‘liberty’ and was expressing
it on its own terms. Both Spains were weary of conﬂict, and despite
206 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


the misﬁred diplomatic venture of the Royal Expedition, various efforts
came from the Cristino side for peace. In January 1839, Eugenio de
Aviraneta designed a plan to abduct Don Carlos via a naval landing
near his lightly guarded winter quarters at Azcoitia, to be launched
on British vessels from Pasajes. Only the reluctance of Lord Hay to
breach British rules of engagement grounded the abduction mission.78
Any effective diplomatic solution to the Spanish problem could only
come via some accord between Europe’s Liberal and absolutist powers.
Palmerston and Metternich were surprised that they saw eye to eye on
this point from 1838. But Metternich’s Europe still saw Liberal Spain as
the revolution incarnate, an attitude that hardened when Spanish emis-
saries with colourful pasts reached Vienna. Manuel Marliani Cassens, a
cosmopolitan, gaditano friend of Mendizábal and protégé of Calatrava,
was made Spanish consul general to France in September 1836.79 He
forged a plan to impress upon Metternich a dynastic end to Spain’s civil
war via the marriage of the young Princess Isabella to an Austrian prince.
But this plan backﬁred on his mission to Vienna when French agents –
wary of the prospect of a Hapsburg on the Spanish throne – destroyed
Marliani’s credentials by reminding the Austrians of his revolutionary
past in Piedmont in 1821. Metternich refused even to see him, and
Marliani was ordered to leave Vienna within 18 hours, after which the
combined pressure of Austria and Sardinia got him sacked as Spanish
consul to Paris. Even though Marliani rebuilt his career in Spain, his
plan for a dynastic solution to the civil war came to nought.80 Equally
there was no diplomatic breakthrough for the Carlists either. The secret
Carlist junta in Madrid had seen its hopes dashed with the failed Royal
Expedition of 1837. In 1838 the Neapolitan foreign minister, the Prince
of Cassaro, suggested to the Carlists that if they descended on either
Zaragoza or Valencia they would be supported by a naval squadron of
Neapolitan volunteers. However, Cassaro’s enthusiasm was not shared
by his King.81
   Carlist diplomacy made desperate pleas for international help to stave
off defeat. But Don Carlos’s plea on 9 March 1839 for more aid from
friendly sovereigns was met by either cool or business-like responses.
Piedmont-Sardinia refused more money, disgruntled at how money sent
hitherto had been spent on maintaining the Carlist army on home
ground – which was now shrinking – rather than marching on Madrid.
Russia and Austria agreed that to send more money would end up
merely postponing eventual Carlist defeat. The only serious offer came
from a minor absolutist power, the Netherlands, which offered money in
return for Don Carlos’s consent to a Dutch conquest of the Philippines
                                 Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   207


in his name. The Cristino garrison was weak, Amsterdam believed, a ﬂeet
could be launched from Java in the Dutch East Indies, and the Dutch
would be repaid by a stake in Philippine customs revenues. Both Don
Carlos and his cabinet quickly rejected this proposal, but it is unclear
whether they did so out of misplaced imperial pride, realism or supe-
rior knowledge of Philippine affairs. In fact, the Spanish presence in the
Philippines during the 1830s was comparatively strong, as Guillermo
Galvey, amateur ethnographer and Cristino Captain-General of the
colony, led a series of expeditions to expand the colonial frontier in the
Cordillera.82 International supporters of Carlism were disenchanted by
more than just the military situation. Even kings and diplomats openly
upbraided the division and misappropriations of the Carlist court.83
Finally, a plan to sail a 1,500-strong Carlist amphibious force from
Bermeo to attack Cádiz during July 1839 foundered ﬁrst on the lack
of ﬁnancial support from Europe’s absolute powers, and then because
of the Treaty of Vergara.84 Carlist hopes of international aid had col-
lapsed, and Cristino Commander-in-Chief of the Army of the Centre,
Van-Halen, ridiculed Carlists hoping against hope as ‘Jews waiting for
the Messiah’.85
   The Treaty of Vergara ended the war on its main front, and this
came about ﬁrst because of the executive power taken by Espartero,
and more fundamentally, the exhaustion and political divisions of the
Carlist Basque country. The growing clamour for peace in the Carlist
zone in 1839 undermined the Royal Government’s attempts to sustain
the war effort. The demographic losses caused by the war and Cristino
occupation forced the Carlist government, in December 1838, to issue
a blanket amnesty allowing émigrés on the French side of the border
to return to their homes. Meanwhile, the screw of conscription was
tightened still further, and a crackdown was announced on the growing
tide of subversive pamphlets which attacked Don Carlos’s advisers in a
thinly veiled critique of the King himself. Most decisively, the Basque
and Navarrese peasantry was seeing itself reduced to below-subsistence
level by the repeated requisitioning by the Carlist army (and, as 1839
progressed, by the invading Cristinos), which Carlist civilian authorities
never managed to control.86
   The ‘man of Vergara’ was Rafael Maroto. Maroto was a middle-
aged Murcian, veteran of the Spanish-American wars and widower
to a Peruvian wife. Purged from the army in 1833 and imprisoned,
he escaped from Cristino captivity to join his King in Portugal early
in 1834. Maroto was the archetypical ‘Castilian’ hated by apostólicos
and Basque fueristas: dynastically loyal to Don Carlos and loyal to
208 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


nothing else in the Basque country besides. Maroto was also enlight-
ened absolutist in his politics, and was therefore a perfect broker for a
‘transacted’ peace: indeed, during 1839 the Marotista and transaccionista
parties in the Basque country became synonymous. Maroto had few
Basque or apostólico friends and his appointment to commander-in-chief
in 1838 was the result of patient networking and military accomplish-
ments. Once appointed, Maroto knew that time was not on the Carlists’
side. No sooner had he regularised the disorderly and parochial fortress
system which defended the Basque country at local levels, than he had
to ﬁght against court intrigues (especially that of a certain Eustaquio
Laso, placed at his side to spy on him). Even worse, Maroto’s Cristino
enemies now had a ﬂourishing spy network of their own, straddling
both sides of the French border.87 And militarily the Cristinos had noth-
ing more to learn from Carlist territory or tactics. Under Espartero’s
leadership they knew the ground and in September 1837 marched on
the Carlist capital of Estella. Hence, any new Carlist offensive had to be
perfectly coordinated in order to avoid catastrophe.
   Maroto understood that the apóstolico ‘universal minister’, Teijeiro,
would exploit any military defeat to oust him, so he focused on reor-
ganising the army and ﬁghting only local battles rather than bold
offensives. Maroto enjoyed exceptionally good relations with Carlist
agents abroad, which allowed him to increase the always haphazard for-
eign subsidies.88 But hostility towards him grew, and Maroto realised
that only the execution of the conspirators could reassert his author-
ity. He prepared his ground by warning the King at Tolosa. Kissing Don
Carlos’s hand, Maroto theatrically quipped that his King had no desire
to kill him. The surprised King replied in the afﬁrmative. Maroto replied
that in that case it was his duty to request royal approval for the exe-
cution of rebellious ofﬁcers. Don Carlos temporised, assuring Maroto
that the conspiracies were no more than hot air, and that he knew
from experience more about these things than his loyal commander-
in-chief. Maroto made his intention clear, and left for his headquarters
at the Carlist capital of Estella. There he found the streets suspiciously
deserted, and those of its war-weary inhabitants and garrison he did
encounter hurled abuse at him from windows and doorways. Maroto
summoned one of the apostólico conspirators, General Carmona, to meet
him at a café. Maroto’s escort arrested Carmona the moment he arrived,
whilst swift arrests ensued of García, Sanz, Guergué and Intendant Uriz.
Two other generals, the Conde de Negri and Silvestre, quickly rallied to
Maroto’s side. All the captives were imprisoned in the town’s fort and
executed by ﬁring squad two days later, each offering ﬁnal indignant
                                  Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   209


words foretelling the damage that the execution of loyal ofﬁcers of the
King would do to the Carlist cause.89 However, violence, even projected
assassinations, had long characterised the Byzantine world of Carlist
court politics, and Maroto’s ruthlessness was met as much by relief as
indignation. Only the year before a Carlist princess fundraising at Italian
and German courts had been approached by a Royal Government agent
seeking support for a plan to execute certain Carlist generals.90
   Maroto wanted to kill Balmaseda, too, a particularly efﬁcient guer-
rilla comandante in Aragón who was ﬁercely loyal to the King, but a
signed message arrived from Don Carlos in the nick of time ordering this
man to be spared. Don Carlos, shocked by Maroto’s purge and swayed
by Teijeiro, lost little time in condemning his commander-in-chief as
a traitor and dismissing him from command. Knowing that this would
be his death sentence, Maroto received this dismissal using the familiar
ritual of the pronunciamiento. Gathering his men around him, he read
out the letter in order to provoke their indignation. Having secured
his ofﬁcers’ loyalty, the fate of the Carlist War now basically lay in
Maroto’s hands. Maroto marched on Tolosa and overwhelmed the King
into rescinding his order and sacking Teijeiro and the Bishop of León.
Maroto put his friends in leading positions in the army (Zaratiegui,
Eguía, Villarreal and Elío). The King’s humiliation was almost com-
plete, whilst Maroto’s coup certainly was complete, at least as far as
the provinces were concerned.91 His supporters, Generals Elío, Villarreal,
Eguía and Zaratiegui, quickly occupied leading army posts.
   The Frenchman, Saint-Silvain, stepped in as a go-between between
the chastened court and Maroto, thereby saving himself from the list of
apostólicos Maroto had ordered expelled to France, and allowing Don
Carlos himself to save face.92 Saint-Silvain had been the unimpeach-
able face of French legitimism, and Don Carlos’s most active diplomat
in Spain.93 Don Carlos caved in with understandable meekness, giving
ﬁnal proof, as if any more were needed, that the absolute King was
absolutely helpless in the face of the two main Carlist court factions,
the transaccionista one of which had become triumphant. Teijeiro, who
had been subject to personal attacks in a proclamation Maroto made
to his army, led the ﬂight of elites for France, one step ahead of the
assassins. Carlism’s diplomatic agents endorsed the coup. Even the King
of the Two Scillies had found the machinations of the apostólicos dis-
tasteful. The unofﬁcial Carlist ambassador to Naples, José Alvarez de
Toledo, summed up this mood by declaring that ‘some men at court had
been doing more damage to our cause than Espartero and Van Halen
combined’.94 Maroto marched into Tolosa where Don Carlos reviewed
210 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


his army. The King did not even speak about the events of Estella. The
initiative was now Maroto’s.95
   Meanwhile, Espartero had been pondering the best way to take advan-
tage of the civil war within Carlist ranks. Much might have been
achieved by merely awaiting the enemy’s collapse, as the Carlist war
effort was now grinding to an exhausted halt. Overburdened arms
foundries produced inadequate weaponry: shoddy workmanship and
lack of inspection led to tragedy at the fort of Guardamino on 1 May
1839, when its defending Carlist garrison opened ﬁre on the enemy,
only for the shell to explode in the cannon’s bore, igniting the maga-
zine, which killed most of the fort’s garrison outright.96 On 23 March
1839, Espartero wrote to War Minister Alaix, explaining two possi-
ble ways of ending the war, namely, via either a compromise peace,
or transacción, or a scorched-earth policy to devastate the provinces.
Espartero had no qualms about justifying such a policy for the sake of
national integrity: ‘It is best to amputate a diseased member than let it
infect the whole body.’ He was convinced that the civil war could only
be won in the provinces. He hoped an offensive strategy in the north
would capitalise on Carlism’s internal divisions and win the conﬁdence
of sympathetic European powers. But Alaix’s response was unsatisfying.
Whilst he offered Espartero limited and temporary reinforcements from
Galicia, and authorised him to take any action in the north that he saw
ﬁt, he also stressed the self-serving moderado position: that the north-
ern front would have to take its turn for reinforcements along with the
east.97 Espartero was thus ever more inclined to use his military powers
to search for peace above the heads of the politicians.
   Luckily for Espartero, Maroto wanted the same, but for more desperate
reasons. He marched the Royal Army westwards on the back of military
operations towards Santander, a mundane deployment which, in real-
ity, was cover for interviews with Cristino and Quadruple Alliance (Lord
John Hay of the naval squadron at Pasajes) intermediaries. The road
to the Vergara settlement became easier and more comfortable as these
illicit contacts increased. Cristino military superiority obliged Maroto in
any case to approve increasing parleys with the enemy. He negotiated
the surrender of the fort at Guardamino on 12 May 1839: the magazine
explosion of the 1 May combined with a failure to dig in at the crest
surrounding the fort had made the position poorly defensible. For this
feat Espartero was awarded the title Duke of Victory, and Cristino cel-
ebrations embittered the Carlist press, which was at pains to represent
Guardamino as a truce rather than defeat. Maroto was in an unenvi-
able position: he needed to achieve some signiﬁcant victory both in
                                  Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   211


order to stem the growing tide of desertion from Carlist into Cristino
ranks (which had been particularly marked in the lines facing Cristino
Bilbao)98 and to strike a peace from some position of strength. But
the latter was growing more improbable by the day, whilst Espartero’s
encroachments into the Basque–Navarrese heartland heaped suffering
on the already exhausted Carlist home front, which then fed back into
demands for peace.99
   Meanwhile, anti-marotistas may have been cowed but they were not
inactive. Propaganda was spread describing Maroto as a traitor prepared
to sell the King for English gold, and starting rumours that Carlist defec-
tors, contrary to being welcomed into Cristino ranks, were being shot.100
An exchange of letters between the apostólico priest, Juan Echevarría,
and Maroto, revealed a personal antipathy which typiﬁed the mali-
cious gossip that had dominated Carlist court politics over six years.
Maroto, whose impressment into his personal service in 1835 of a cap-
tured adolescent British auxiliary had been the subject of apostólico
gossip and innuendo, now vengefully repaid his tormentors in kind,
waving off Echevarría’s accusations of treason and accusing him of hav-
ing fathered children, and of trying to hide the affair by forcing their
unhappy mother to marry her brother.101 The puritanism of Carlist
political culture was being cast off by its own elites.
   On 9 August, the late Zumalacárregui’s right-hand man, Zaratiegui,
led four battalions in a pro-war pronunciamiento near Pamplona.102 But
this rising was deadlocked: both marotistas and apostólicos competed to
win over the vital legitimacy of Don Carlos, but the King now had grave
doubts about his personal situation in the wake of Maroto’s coup. When
he reviewed some Basque battalions at Elorrio, rather than shouting the
customary ‘Long Live the King’, the men cried ‘Long Live Peace!’103 This
civil war within a civil war was sapping the war effort from within.
General Elío was caught between the Scylla of Cristino invasion and
the Charybdis of the apostólico rising, remarking ‘If I attack the ris-
ing, we are lost, and if I don’t, then I see no hope.’104 This scenario
revived the fortunes of the much-derided Muñagorri who linked up with
Jaúregui on the frontier under the ever-more attractive banner of peace
with fueros without Don Carlos.105 Knowledge was widespread that a
transacción along these lines was the subject of negotiations, especially
at Bayonne.106
   This move towards peace became all the stronger as Espartero’s
summer offensive penetrated ever more into Carlist territory, captur-
ing Estella, impounding villages’ harvests and burning produce that
could not be carried or consumed. This military crisis sharpened the
212 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


political one, and Maroto asked Espartero for a truce, which the latter
refused.107 Meanwhile, Espartero continued invading Navarra, scorching
farmsteads in his wake. Maroto was in an invidious position because of
Espartero’s refusal to agree to a peace which would allow Don Carlos
to participate in the transacción. Espartero was determined that peace
should be brokered by Spaniards alone, and that international medi-
ation should be minimised. Espartero’s attitude hardened when the
French government, in June 1839, launched its proposals for a mediated
peace which were virtually pro-Carlist, and conﬁrmed the long-held
suspicions about King Louis-Philippe’s afﬁliation. Thus both the fueros
and Carlist military commissions and salaries would be respected, whilst
both Don Carlos and María Cristina would be sent into exile, and the
Spanish Bourbon dynasty reunited via the marriage of the Carlist infant
and Isabella. The repeated use of the French raisonnable to describe these
terms stuck in the throats of the Cristinos, and allowed the British to
monopolise the international side of peace-making to which Espartero
did consent.108
   Meanwhile, Espartero maintained the pressure with a calculated,
gradual pace of invasion designed to exact the maximum psychologi-
cal effect on his opponent. Maroto was also under immense pressure
both from the restive Navarrese peasantry and from their King, who
grew suspicious and fearful of his commander-in-chief’s negotiations
and would launch a furious counter-manifesto once the Vergara terms
were proclaimed.109 Both Espartero and Maroto knew that military logic
would dictate peace on Cristino terms. Both men carried out secret nego-
tiations via Bayonne and an elaborate fourfold secret code. Maroto was
desperate to save face by gaining the concession of either a Cristino for-
tiﬁed town (‘victory’) or the marriage of Don Carlos’s son to the young
Isabella (‘honour’). Espartero refused both, but reiterated his promise
not to harm the fueros and his offer of army commissions for Carlist ofﬁ-
cers as long as Don Carlos was either handed over or sent into exile.110
In the end Maroto caved into these terms, but until the last moment
could not actually bring himself to discuss preliminaries at Vergara,
deputising General La Torre to lead the delegation in his stead. What-
ever the anguish of the Carlist high command, the attitude of the Carlist
rank and ﬁle had become both so despondent and enraged with army
elites that a negotiated peace promised to safeguard against something
far worse. When the Carlist generals set out to meet Espartero at Vergara,
they found a group of Guipúzcoan deserters in their path. The desert-
ers damned the generals and detained them. The situation might have
ended disastrously, but the generals convinced the deserters that they
                                  Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   213


were on a mission for peace, and Spanish soldiers’ love of peace in 1839
was greater than their contempt for their leaders.111
  Finally, on 29 August 1839, the Vergara settlement was signed. Carlists
were to switch allegiance to Isabel II in return for continued careers for
ofﬁcers and men in the reuniﬁed army if they desired and Espartero
promised to speak in favour of preserving the fueros at the reuniﬁed
Cortes. Anti-marotista Carlists left for French exile. The King reached
exile safely but not all were so fortunate. General Vicente Moreno, the
‘hangman of Málaga’ and one of Maroto’s great enemies, was caught by
Carlist soldiers at Urdax, just one kilometre from the French frontier,
and murdered.112 Ironically for its eponymous peace settlement, Vergara
was the last Carlist fort to surrender to Espartero’s victorious army. Thus,
the Treaty of Vergara (also known as the ‘Embrace of Vergara’ – Abrazo
de Vergara – for the theatrical embrace that Espartero and Maroto gave
each other as a symbol of peace and reconciliation in front of their men)
was the compromise peace that ended the war in the north.113 Thou-
sands of pro-fueros Carlists, almost all from the mooted loser provinces
of Alava and Navarra, rejected the peace and followed their commanders
into French exile. They were right to suspect Maroto’s verbal assurance
from Espartero that a reuniﬁed Spain would ‘respect the fueros’. Near
Elizondo, the Cristino consul at Bayonne reported that four companies
of the ﬁfth battalion of Navarra honoured the passing into exile of their
King with the cry of ‘Death to Maroto, Long Live the King!’114 Risings
continued into 1840, such as the failed attempt of Balmaseda to reignite
the civil war, but the country was exhausted. Foral privileges were
eroded, which was a matter for the Cristino cities as much as the Carlist
countryside. Vitoria saw its lucrative customs frontier redeployed at the
French and coastal frontier to the beneﬁt of Bilbao and San Sebastián.115
  The nineteenth-century Liberal historian, Antonio Pirala, tried to
rehabilitate Maroto, but to no effect: Maroto remained forever tarnished
with treason in both Romantic eyes and those of the theocratic wing of
Carlism.116 Thus, a persistent ‘Vergara literature’ was spawned, repro-
ducing the myth that Carlism was stabbed in the back but undefeated
on the battleﬁeld, the culmination of a betrayal of popular Carlism
that began with the connivers of Don Carlos’s court and ended with
Maroto’s treachery.117 The 1898-generation ‘Great Man’ novelist, Pío
Baroja, called Don Carlos’s climbdown cowardly.118 Maroto died in exile
in Chile, ending his career where he had begun it, having tried to defend
two lost causes of Spanish monarchism.
  Fighting continued in the east, but not even Cabrera’s genius could
hope to withstand the 50,000 battle-hardened Cristino troops bearing
214 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


down on Aragón. Even before the Vergara settlement there were signs
that Carlism was beginning to lose its grip on Catalonia and Aragón.
The year 1839 saw the tide turn even against Count España’s Carlists in
Catalonia. His failure in February 1839 to take the small Cristino town of
Balsareny marked the turn of the tide, and turned the town into a martyr
of Liberal resistance: troops, militia, Belgian and even women volun-
teers had all died in its defence and practically every house had its roofs
blown off by artillery, some 8,000 sacks being ordered after the battle to
provide makeshift shelter. In late May, Carlist fortunes brieﬂy returned
when España successfully besieged Ripoll. Overwhelmed by the odds
facing them, the Cristino governor accepted a negotiated surrender, his
men and arms going into captivity whilst he rejected Carlist guarantees
and shot himself in the head. During the negotiations, Carlist emis-
saries encountered the town’s women and children solemnly gathering
in the beautiful church of San Pedro. The Carlists removed their berets
in respect and let the civilians leave the church so as to avoid being tar-
geted in the event of hostilities restarting. After the takeover, the town
was burnt to the ground, and those buildings which had survived the
ﬂame were blasted into rubble. Even though Ripoll would rise again,
España had achieved what Espoz had done with Castellfullit in 1822.
He ordered a memorial plaque to be constructed in the town’s ruins
saying ‘Here was Ripoll’.119
   España hoped that the downfall of Meer (a victim of the Radical
press campaign), and his replacement as captain-general by Jerónimo
Valdés, might improve matters. But Valdés was both an experienced
counter-insurgent and, crucially, for Radical opinion, politically to the
Left of Meer. When city magnates’ refused him a loan he imprisoned
them for ransom in the Ciudadela, a dictatorial abuse of the state of
siege which nonetheless pleased radical opinion.120 The ﬁnancial statis-
tics certainly justiﬁed his ruthlessness: Catalonia yielded an annual tax
income of 6 million reales whilst military expenditures alone amounted
to 8 million. And yet it was the wealthiest Cristino city. Workers had
tacitly accepted the reintroduction of the machines since the Bonaplata
riots of 1835 because production in general had been booming – as
shown by the more than doubling of raw cotton imports to Barcelona
between 1824 and 1840 – and the capitalist class was awash with cash.121
And, now that peace had broken out in the north, until Valdés could
get reinforcements he was conﬁned to little more than operations of
active defence, such as the bitter battle to resupply Solsona against
Carlist attacks during November 1839.122 Finally, the end of the war in
the north allowed Espartero to reinforce Valdés (only a third of whose
                                  Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   215


23,000 troops in Catalonia had so far been available for campaigning)
with the forces of General Antonio Azpiroz (four veteran battalions, one
excellent cavalry squadron, one company of sappers and artillery). These
forces arrived at Lérida on 7 November 1839.123
   España’s notorious theocracy and military discipline had become
objectionable because he had stopped winning battles. España’s strate-
gic defeat revived the Junta of Berga and gave his enemies the chance
to take revenge. España was abducted and forced to appear before the
junta, which dismissed him from his command on 26 October 1839.
España again tried to temporise, this time literally at knifepoint, but it
was to no avail. He pronounced himself happy that his predecessor, José
Segarra, was to be appointed his successor but demanded a chance to
delay handing over his command until he had conversed with Segarra
about conﬁdential military matters which were not appropriate for mere
civilians to hear. The junta was unmoved and España began his forced
journey into exile in Andorra. Although staying at safe houses en route,
travelling by night and undergoing the indignity of being dressed in
peasant clothes, España did not have to endure this humiliation for
long. After crossing the River Segre close to Orgaña España was strangled
by his own escort, and his body dumped in the river.124 His assassins,
who were operating with uncertain connivance of higher authority,
would justify their actions by claiming outrageously that España was a
Mason (i.e. Liberal) and about to turn like Maroto. In reality, the Carlists
knew that once they had bested this fanatical reactionary, they would
have to kill him. Carlos de España, after all, had bloodied Barcelona as
its captain-general during the Ominous Decade, and then subjected the
Carlist war effort in the Principality to the most unforgivingly draco-
nian discipline since. If he lived he could wreak the most abject revenge
upon his enemies, even from exile. Nevertheless, España’s assassination
caused great controversy.125
   The whole of the eastern Carlist zone had been traumatised by the
events in the Basque country. España had never shown much respect
either for the person of Don Carlos or for his Byzantine court. When
desperate diehards in Navarra had suggested placing the Carlist Crown
Prince at the head of the war effort in the east, España’s reply was full of
sententious scorn:126

   Princes, just like all kings, are either too good to be true that we lose
   them, or are so bad because they are worked on by a rabble of ﬂat-
   terers. And do you believe that if our prince came here or to Aragón
   he would not be accompanied by a court worse than Satan’s? Haven’t
216 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


   you seen what’s been happening and it still happening in Navarra?
   Haven’t I told you several times that Navarra on its own is enough to
   lose us this war?

But the reinforcement of enemy troops made the situation bleak, even
for the Tiger of the Maestrazgo. Lord John Hay operated as a conduit for
negotiations between Van-Halen and Cabrera to instate the Eliot Treaty
in this region. Hay had greater obstacles to overcome in committing
Cabrera to the agreement, as the Count of Morella continued to com-
mit atrocities even after his signature had been secured in March 1839.
Cabrera was reeling from the loss of a shipment of arms which he had
procured in Britain. The Cristino coastguard intercepted a British ves-
sel carrying weapons bound for a remote beach under Carlist control,
impounding the cargo in Barcelona. The Cristinos did not know that
these arms were detailed for nothing less than a 22,000-strong expedi-
tion which Cabrera planned to lead against Madrid in the summer.127
Cabrera tried to take this strategic calamity in his stride, reverting to the
mystique of the guerrilla:

   War has secrets which are not explained in books. Any general who
   does not know more than what is written down may make an excel-
   lent writer of military books and lose all battles at the same time.
   In the War of Independence we saw how Blake hardly won a single
   action in spite of being brave and a great tactician . . . Napoleon’s gen-
   erals who came to Spain were the same, and our guerrillas defeated
   them . . . Now all the generals of the Army of the Centre who are ﬁght-
   ing me think they have technical superiority over me, and I don’t
   mean to debate this . . . but war gives results, not theories. I repeat
   that the science of war has its secrets.128

This ‘people’s war’ theme, inherited from the Peninsular War, was
a desperate but logical response to enemy superiority. But people’s
war requires civilian support, and this was ebbing among the war-
weary communities of the east. Cabrera found civilians feared Cristino
strength whereas once they had feared his own. Cabrera ordered that
Segura (Teruel) organise its own Carlist garrison in order to free up his
sorely needed troops for deployment elsewhere. Fearing the wrath of
the Cristinos, its inhabitants declined, so Cabrera tore down Segura’s
impressive church, other public buildings and some 350 houses. A total
of 1,600 inhabitants were thus turned into homeless refugees, seeking
shelter in nearby settlements and the caves of Los Pinares.129
                                  Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   217


   The pressure on Cabrera to agree to a more humanitarian conduct of
the conﬂict grew unbearable once Don Carlos added his name to the
criticism. The King’s censure was wide-ranging, and included criticism
of Cabrera’s zeal in suppressing even the most moderate Liberal tracts
which made it into Carlist territory. Don Carlos did not think it wrong
to censor, only that this was the proper duty of the civilian junta, not
the military. But Don Carlos’s most pressing critique was on humani-
tarian grounds. Finally, early in April, both Cabrera and Van-Halen put
their names to a version of the Eliot Treaty in the eastern zone, Cabrera
signing at his headquarters at Segura on 1 April 1839, whereupon the
emissary took it for Van-Halen’s signature at Lécera on 3 April 1839.
Cabrera had initially been piqued that such an important negotiation
had ﬁrst been opened up only verbally when Van-Halen had despatched
to him a Carlist prisoner as an emissary. The Treaty of Lécera (known by
Carlists as the Treaty of Segura) also excited controversy besides, by civil-
ian politicians and journalists. George Villiers defended Van-Halen’s and
British government policy in this matter from an attack in the House
of Lords by Lord Londonderry on 23 July 1839.130 And the interna-
tional controversy paled in comparison to that raging in the press of
Cristino Spain. In particular, the treaty’s provisions to protect the lives
of captured Carlists who had previously served in Cristino ranks, the
insistence that both able-bodied and wounded prisoners be given treat-
ment equal to those of serving soldiers, and the ban placed on deporting
prisoners overseas all excited the ire of the Liberal press, which pointed
out that these points privileged the Carlists more than the Cristinos.131
Moreover, problems remained as civilians would still be subject to the
rigours and excesses of armies, even though local communities now felt
encouraged to strike bargains with local Carlist forces in order to be able
to work their lands unmolested (deals which local Cristino forces were
obliged to tolerate out of sheer necessity). Also, local commanders were
still prone to ignoring or distorting the provisions. Prisoners numbering
more than 300, for example, were to be left in open towns devoid of
all war-related material and to be quarantined up to a distance of two
leagues’ radius from any troops. The Carlist deputy comandante general
of Aragón interpreted this provision as excluding everyone on pain of
death.132
   Meanwhile, pressure was mounting on Van-Halen to inﬂict a
decisive defeat on Cabrera. Cabrera still dominated large areas of
Aragón,Valencia and Catalonia. Carlist domination included an armed
river and coastal ﬂotilla on the Ebro estuary, the only instance in the
war where Carlist naval power matched that of the enemy.133 Van-Halen
218 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


marched on Segura, but he found the town in ashes as Cabrera had
levelled it rather than spend resources on its defence. A series of engage-
ments in March and April 1839 ﬁnally wore out Van-Halen’s momentum
and he was forced to retreat. This sent shock waves through the Lib-
eral government, which had banked on Segura being an easy victory.
Bowing to the inevitable, Van-Halen offered his resignation, which was
accepted.134 War Minister Alaix replaced him with Nogueras, who was
appointed on 16 April 1839 but whose ill health required the temporary
nomination of General Bartolomé Amor in the interim. On 27 April
1839, War Minister Alaix gave both Amor and Nogueras special instruc-
tions to seek out any offensive opportunity against Cabrera in Aragón
and Valencia, lest the northern side of the Ebro end up turning into
Carlist-dominated territory with all the adverse diplomatic connotations
that would produce. In order to streamline this effort, he instructed
them not to prioritise payments to the unpopular asentistas except in the
case of food deliveries.135 The latter stipulation was a shrewd response to
the renewed crisis of morale in the Army of the Centre, with hunger, lack
of footwear and desertion taking their toll.136
   Banditry and insecurity continued to blunt the by-now nominally
massively superior Cristino forces, some 31,556 infantry and 2,136 cav-
alry in the Army of the Centre in April 1839. Furthermore, this army
was riven with unimaginative and timorous commanders who failed to
progress from the inﬂexible tactics of halting their infantry to ﬁre long-
distance volleys at Carlists, even though rushing the enemy from the
outset would have yielded better results. The army was also chronically
undersized by desertion and overstaffed by ofﬁcers – brigades compris-
ing only 12 companies, each of these commanded by comandantes,
being a common phenomenon.137 In short, the Cristino offensive was
a laborious affair. Thus, the suffering continued to devolve onto civil-
ians. Montalbán (Teruel) had been devastated by Carlists in 1836; now
tragedy overtook it again when its National Militia commander, Jaime
Vicente, requested from Cabrera a temporary truce so that medicines
might replenish the exhausted supplies of the town’s hospital which, as
stipulated by the Treaty of Lécera, was treating both Cristino and Carlist
casualties on an equal basis. Cabrera refused, but suggested the defend-
ers hoist a ﬂag on the roof of the hospital so that the Carlist artillery
would be able to spare it their bombardment. As soon as the ﬂag was
hoisted, Carlist artillery rained down a withering ﬁre on the building.
In one day some 66 shells fell on the building, reducing it to ruins and
killing its inmates, including some 13 of Cabrera’s own men. After 50
days of bombardment, the Cristinos abandoned the town, which had
                                 Stalemate and Cristino Victory, 1838–40   219


now been reduced to ruins. Against Cabrera’s terrorism the Cristinos
constructed a narrative of heroic defence, including that of 22-year-
old woman, Manuela Cirugeda, who distinguished herself ﬁghting as
a soldier in the town’s defence.138
   The Cristinos launched assassination attempts against Cabrera, but he
became aware of these plots throughout 1839 and 1840, and took pre-
cautionary measures. No one, except trusted comrades, could get close
to him. He randomly changed his routines, calling on villages for food
and accommodation, in order to either remain anonymous or minimise
the chance of malicious plots coming to fruition. A special police force
was set up to ﬁlter defectors from the Cristinos army for possible assas-
sins and he built up a counter-espionage network which soon foiled
Cristino plots. An elaborate plot involving a poisoned dagger hidden in
the baggage of a ‘Carlist horse-trader’ who claimed to be ﬂeeing Madrid
was thwarted, the unfortunate assassin being caught, tried and decap-
itated. More than ever Cabrera began issuing verbal orders rather than
committing anything to paper, and subordinates obeyed them down the
line of military hierarchy.139 Cabrera imposed a reign of terror against
civilians who threatened to compromise Carlist positions and morale
in any way. In Rossell (Castellón de la Plana) an elderly local notable
with whom Cabrera was billeted pleaded with him to realise that the
Carlist cause was lost. Making an immediate example of this defeatism,
Cabrera marched the old man outside, ordered him to kneel and had
him shot from behind by his escort, Cabrera giving the execution order
himself.140 Any sign of communication with the Cristinos could likewise
spell death for the civilian involved, however innocent and helpless.
A nine-year-old girl from Pobla de Claramunt (Barcelona) who had been
sent to carry chocolates to the fortiﬁed Carlist weapons factory at Forts
del Turó was stopped and searched on her way by some facciosos from
nearby Igualada. In her basket they discovered a letter from her brother,
which conﬁrmed the fall of the Carlist stronghold of Morella. The girl
was executed on the spot.141
   The Treaty of Vergara ended the war on its main front and promised,
ﬁnally, to produce Cristino victory in the east. Cabrera was fortunate
to receive news of the northern ‘treachery’ – the northern Carlists had
tried to ‘prolong their miserable existence by selling out to the revolu-
tionary power’ – in the wake of two local Carlist victories at Carboneras
and Chulilla. His men were thus well-blooded when he summoned a
meeting to give his ofﬁcers the choice of release from service and safe
conduct to their homes, or the strictest discipline and subordination
to continue the ﬁght. Clutching at straws, he thought that the coming
220 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


of winter would buy them time. He promised the most draconian disci-
pline, including the death penalty for anyone even suspected of desiring
transacción with the enemy.142 By giving his ofﬁcers this sole and ﬂeet-
ing opportunity to abandon the Carlist cause, Cabrera showed that he
retained control of the situation. His army, after all, peaked at some
30,000 at the end of 1839, some eight times larger than what he had
found in 1835, whilst he cherished hopes that the diehard apostólico
opponents of Maroto might yet be rallied along the French border for
a fresh attempt to reignite the northern front. But Cabrera would need
to strain every nerve to resist the 50,000-strong army and overwhelm-
ing artillery launched from Zaragoza in October. Moderados accused
Espartero of delaying his descent on the east in order to maximise his
political power, but his gradual rather than rapid offensive still left no
one in doubt as to the hopelessness of the Carlists’ strategic situation.
As we have seen, the immobilisation of Catalan Carlism caused by the
España affair gave Cabrera no relief from the north. Cabrera’s active
defence began well but turned for the worse in mid-December when
he fell gravely ill. In February 1840, the Valencia press reported popular
manifestations in the Maestrazgo willing Cabrera’s recovery, evidence
of resilient morale, perhaps, but also evidence of how personalised the
leadership of the Carlist east remained.143 Whilst Espartero picked off
fortiﬁed points one by one, Cabrera could not even rise from his bed
until May 1840.144
   Cabrera knew that his line could only hold if the war were reignited
in the north. But the French police frustrated most plots and prevented
suspicious Spaniards from concentrating on the border. Despite these
measures, it was remarkable that Balmaseda still managed to invade the
Spanish Basque country in June 1840 with some 510 men and 470 cav-
alry. But this failed to reignite the war. Seeing this, Cabrera, on 5 July
1840, crossed the French frontier at the head of some 4,600 infantry
and 300 cavalry.145 Cristino forces now occupied all national territory.
But the victorious Liberals did not take any chances. The major Carlist
arms foundries at Villarluengo were destroyed in order to dissuade local
Carlists from restarting the war. The First Carlist War was over.146
Conclusions




At the war’s end in 1840, the Catalan priest and ‘common sense’
philosopher, Jaime Balmés, posited an apposite question: ‘if the Carlists
had been nothing more than a gang of thieves and outlaws, why could
the Cristino armies not destroy them?’1 Of course, thieves and outlaws
may have used Carlism as a ﬂag of convenience, but this was largely
outside the safe zones of the Basque country, and to a lesser extent,
Catalonia and the Maestrazgo. But Balmés was correctly parodying the
self-assured arrogance that made Spanish Liberals view opposition to
their transition and regime as a symptom of rural, priest-ridden igno-
rance. The Cristino denigration of their enemy as ‘bandits’, caribes and
facciosos was as facile as the Carlists’ ﬁght against negros and ‘usurpers’.
Yet the fact that Carlist wars would resurface (in 1846, in 1872 and again
in 1936), albeit on nothing like the scale of the ﬁrst war, showed that
there was no peace and reconciliation, and the persistence of belligerent
language represented far deeper tensions.
  For one thing, the peace of 1840 was really no peace at all. Spain
would remain in a revolutionary versus counter-revolutionary crisis
until 1844 at least, when a draconian moderado crackdown and the insti-
tution of the ﬁrst rural gendarmerie (Civil Guard) illegalised most open
recourses to popular radicalism (i.e. deﬁnitively abolishing the veterans’
Militia) while also presiding over the ﬁrst real economic boom of the
nineteenth century. The immediate weeks and months after the con-
ﬂict saw violence persist. The Vergara settlement had positive effects in
Asturias and Galicia where ‘Carlists’ had to revert to banditry and were
therefore targeted by united local communities.2 But it was powerless
in other areas to arrest violence. The area around Ciudad Real (New
Castile) was particularly disﬁgured by conﬂicts between ‘Liberals’ and
‘Carlists’, which quickly turned into a cycle of blood vendettas, in which

                                    221
222 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


women, even pregnant ones, and children, and sometimes whole fami-
lies, were massacred. On 25 August 1839, four days before the northern
peace, families of Carlists began to be held as hostages to be targeted
in reprisals for atrocities from the other side. Gradually this draconian
peace began to work, as villages could see themselves reconnected with
each other and the outside world, and ‘Carlist’ ﬁghters who failed to
submit to the Peace Treaty could now be hunted down as criminals
instead of legitimate enemies. On several occasions local communities,
freed from the fear of organised reprisals, took the initiative in hunting
down their persecutors. But as so many Carlists continued to be active
as brigands beyond the law after the Treaty of Vergara, some unfortu-
nates continued to be held and killed several months after the formal
end of hostilities. Francisco Martín, for example, was the four-year-old
son of a wanted Carlist. He was held hostage and remained in deten-
tion despite the frantic efforts of the residents of his home village of
Fuente el Fresno to retrieve him. On 4 July 1840, the prison comman-
ders ordered a captive to be executed in reprisal for a Carlist outrage and
young Francisco drew the fatal lot. Being led out to face his ﬁring squad,
Francisco was too young to understand the fate that was about to befall
him. He thought the soldiers were going to play with him: ‘Will you buy
me some oranges, and you won’t hurt me, will you soldiers? Nor my
father or mother either?’ The soldiers were beside themselves with grief
about their orders. They temporised frantically, hoping against hope
for their orders to be countermanded, but to no avail. In desperation
they retrieved an orange and threw it in front of the ﬁring squad and
encouraged the boy to fetch it. The soldiers opened ﬁre and Francisco
fell, clasping his stomach wounds before crawling towards the soldiers
pleading ‘Don’t kill me, don’t hurt me.’ Obeying orders the soldiers let
loose a second volley which killed the child outright. The child’s father
later gave himself up and carried on serving in the newly uniﬁed army
under Liberal command. Conditions in Espartero’s army grew so bad
that the man joined a ‘Carlist’ rebellion which killed some ofﬁcers at
Fernán Caballero, a village ten miles away from where his son had been
executed. He was caught and executed by slow strangulation in Madrid
on 9 February 1843. His wife, Francisco’s mother, survived.3
   Perpetrators of terrible atrocities remained at large and were effectively
amnestied by the Vergara settlement. Carlist exterminating angels were
exonerated with the restoration of full diplomatic relations between
the Spanish Church and the Holy See via the Concordat of 1851. José
Lorente, the priest who had ordered the massacre of 73 Cristino prison-
ers by a roadside in October 1836, lived a long and quiet life, and was
                                                           Conclusions   223


still practising in his home parish 30 years later, being paid a stipend
from the Liberal state he had tried to overthrow.4
   But the real legacy of Spain’s bloodiest civil war was embodied in
the thousands of individual anonymous tragedies, beyond the ken of
historians. Often these were given a radical political meaning. Wid-
ows guarded their martyred menfolk’s memory, cherishing images of
Espartero as the people’s war hero, hanging veterans’ militia uniforms
in a pride of place. When militia veterans presided over the 1841
exhumation of the massacred victims of the 1835 Carlist atrocity at
Rubielos, they did so not just to remember these martyrs to liberty,
but also to reafﬁrm the claim of the citizenry-in-arms to a stake in
Spain’s Liberal future.5 The suppression of the militia in 1844 did not
remove it as an actual force. Disbanded veterans retained their own net-
works which sometimes sprang into life during later revolutions. Neither
Cristino militia nor soldiers remained silent about their harsh treatment
imposed upon them as a consequence of the peace treaty, the failure
of land redistribution or even of decent conditions of service in the
peacetime army.
