   The Cristino victory was attributed to Espartero, leftist war hero and
‘Duke of Victory’. His success was a cause for both national and sec-
tarian celebration. Whereas his victory parade through Barcelona was
so rapturous that he had to dismount and allow himself to be carried
aloft by the crowd, in Seville, where Espartero’s presence was substituted
by a giant portrait, thousands marched against the moderados and sev-
eral hundred hotheads burnt to the ground the ofﬁce of the rightist El
Conservador newspaper.6 Espartero was leading a groundswell of veter-
ans’ opinions against the socially regressive centralisation of political
power inherent in the moderado abolition of the autonomy of all but
the smallest municipalities. Both militia and army veterans joined this
‘peace dividend’ revolution, and the situation in Granada, where the
army took no time at all in following the lead of the militia, was typical.7
Hopes were brieﬂy raised by the ‘peace dividend’ revolution of October
1840, caused by moderado attempts to abolish municipal autonomy, and
which forced the abdication of that obsolescent symbol of the conﬂict,
María Cristina, and her replacement the following year as sole regent
by the war hero, Baldomero Espartero. The revolution was started by
hundreds of thousands of militia and army veterans, and it was their
common identity as veterans of a struggle for liberty which united
them. Many veterans were swept up in the class conﬂict that reached
an unprecedented scale in Barcelona during 1842 and again in 1843,
and it ended up ousting Espartero from power. The economic tensions
224 Spain’s First Carlist War, 1833–40


behind the conﬂict exploded into violence in city barricades manned by
Carlist War veterans.
   The peace was cruel to the losers, especially in the Basque country
which saw a spike in emigration in the early 1840s, in response to the
stripping away of the economic privileges vested in the fueros, and an
economic slump made worse by the devastation and a triumphalist
esparterista elite. As for the vanquished Basque provinces, the Vergara
settlement did nothing to stop the ongoing concentration of power in
the hands of local elites; in fact, it actually encouraged it. Whilst most of
the fueros were left intact, the deﬁnitive removal of the Spanish customs
frontier to the Pyrenees privileged the liberal, commercial oligarchies
of San Sebastián and Bilbao at the expense of the paternalistic, socially
harmonious Carlist heartland of Vitoria.8 As for returning Carlist sol-
diers, little was done for them. The Basque armaments industry was
prevented from supplying the Liberal army, despite the fact that the
largest factory, that of Eibar, had been longer in Cristino hands during
the previous seven years than in those of the Carlists. Workers made
redundant, along with demobilised Carlist soldiers, were thus driven
to emigrate to Cuba and the River Plate (Montevideo). The winter and
spring of 1841 witnessed a surge in emigration from Spain’s northern
ports, whilst newspapers related depressing coverage of the haemor-
rhage of youth, many of the latter ending up shipwrecked, indentured
or exploited upon arrival in Cuba and Spain’s former colonies.9
   The years 1833–40 had submerged Spain in a collective trauma which
killed between 2 and 4 per cent of the 1833 population, and damaged
the lives of hundreds of thousands of survivors. The signiﬁcance of the
First Carlist War is more than what has traditionally been attributed
to it (namely, praetorianism in Liberal politics, proxy for Liberal revo-
lution and the deﬁance of Europe’s most enduring legitimism). It also
created new patterns of popular politics which owed their origins to
the sacriﬁces of communities (army, militia and civilian) that had been
exposed to the conﬂict. This phenomenon shaped the progresista revolu-
tion of 1840. Above all, the length and scale of the conﬂict proved to be
an enduring motor for Spain’s political modernity and, in particular, a
model for plebeian action in Liberal politics. Thus, this book argues that
the First Carlist War shaped Spain’s post-Napoleonic crisis more than it
was shaped by it.

11. The complex jurisdiction and generally low productivity of Spanish agricul-
    ture is a vast question. As Islamic Spain was gradually ‘reconquered’ over
    the course of the Middle Ages, vast swathes of land were given as entail
    (which could be neither bought nor sold) in perpetuity to the victorious
    nobility, Church and military orders. These bodies enjoyed considerable
    degrees of economic and jurisdictional autonomy (fueros) under the Crown,
    and so provoked the ire of eighteenth-century enlightened absolutist monar-
    chs who wanted to establish uniform state control, and whose ministers
    strove to achieve this end. In 1767, Campomanes began a project aimed
    at settling deserted land by establishing nuevas poblaciones under regal juris-
    diction. The following year, Olavide proposed reforms designed to improve
    access to the land, whilst by the 1790s, Jovellanos was urging the proto-
    Liberal need to introduce a free market in land. Even if these reforms
    achieved little in practice, the intellectual argument for ‘disentailment’
    (desamortización) had been largely won by the time the post-1808 Liberals

144. Liberal Pirala blamed Jaúregui, whose divisions broke a supporting plan of
     movement leaving Espoz exposed: only Espoz’s hasty forgery of a coun-
     termanding order conveyed to Elío drew one of the Carlist pincers away
     and allowed Espoz to retreat (Pirala, Guerra civil, I, 513–522). Traditional-
     ist Jaime del Burgo claimed that Espoz was himself to blame for his retreat,
     and that only the timely intervention of Oráa and Elío’s delay (which Burgo
     did not explain) enabled the viceroy’s retreat (Burgo, Historia de la primera
     guerra carlista, 135). Such detail interests historians who seek to substan-
     tiate Espoz’s image as the swordarm of Radical liberalism during the ﬁrst
     half of the First Carlist War (Iribarren, Espoz y Mina. A recent account dero-
     manticising Espoz (but which stops short of the Carlist War) is given by

161. During the Royalist War of 1820–23, it was the scene of constant skirmishes

                                                                        Notes   245


     1823 reaction, and its staff and students purged. It reopened in May 1827

     ing in Castlian was compulsory, an edict which also alienated local Catalans

  8. The term ojalatero is the adjectivised or nominised form of the common
     subjunctive phrase, ¡Ojalá!, meaning ‘hopefully’, ‘God grant’ or ‘I wish it
                                                                         Notes   253


    were’. Its origin probably lies in General Carlos O’Donnell, father of two
    sons who distinguished Isabeline politics, who, before his death during the
    siege of Pamplona in 1835, had the habit of commenting on his comrades’
    near successes in combat preambling with ¡Ojalá! His audience thus coined
    the term ojalatero (Pirala, Guerra civil, II, 260–265).
 9. The young Luisa Casiñol was ‘inspired by the urge to defend the rights
    of the Queen’, and thus joined the sixth company of the ‘Riumbau’
    battalion; she distinguished herself during an action against the Carlists
    near Zaragoza, exhausting all her ammunition (Eco del Comercio, 23
    September 1837).

31. Holt, Carlist Wars, 157–159.
32. Canal, El carlismo, 86–87. In general the British Legion’s performance was
    mixed. At the end of the summer of 1836, the unit participated in a few
    skirmishes in Hernani and the areas around Vitoria, and contributed to the
    conquest of the pass of Pasajes and to the maintenance of the fortress of
    Mount Urgull of San Sebastian against the Carlists’ attempts to take the
    city. Likewise, in November of 1836, the unit participated in Espartero’s
    liberation of Bilbao during the siege of the city. In 1837, the British Legion
    suffered a great setback at the Battle of Oriamendi, but later helped to delay
    the advance of the Expedición Real in Navarra.

     Guard, Diego López Garrido, judged that ‘praetorianism’ is a misleading

     power, and were faithful thereafter to the civilian politics of their chosen
     political parties (López Garrido, Guardia Civil, 44).

     tributed proclamations heaping insults upon progresistas (Eco del Comercio,
     17 January 1840).
